# Diabetes Clinical Care Management

The Diabetes Clinical Care Standards promote a comprehensive, patient-centered framework for diabetes management, integrating evidence-based practices, technology, education, and social determinants of health to optimize outcomes across diverse populations and settings.

**Key Features:**
- ADA Standards of Care updates: Annual revisions incorporating person-first language, shared decision-making, and individualized care based on patient values, comorbidities, and financial constraints.
- Conflict of interest disclosures: Mandatory transparency of financial relationships among guideline developers, reviewers, and committee members to mitigate bias.
- Pediatric diabetes care guidelines: Child and family-centered care with age-specific A1C targets, nutrition education, and training for school/daycare personnel.
- ADA Standards of Care framework: Continuous risk-reduction strategies beyond glycemic control, emphasizing self-management education and screening in healthcare settings.
- Multimodal diabetes education strategies: Empowering patients through in-depth education, self-care skills training, and fostering positive attitudes toward management.
- ADA diabetes care standards framework: Standardized recommendations for glycemic goals, glucose monitoring, hypoglycemia management, and individualized care plans across settings.
- Diabetes technology integration: Offering CGM, AID, CSII, and connected pens with education and data utilization for therapy adjustments.
- Expert author affiliations: Guidelines authored by globally affiliated experts with advanced degrees and subspecialty certifications in diabetes and related fields.
- Systematic diabetes care improvement: Team-based care integrating SDOH assessment, glycemic evaluation, and identification of care partners and support systems.
- School-based diabetes management: Collaboration with school personnel, staff training, and systematic self-management education for children with diabetes.
- Social determinants of health integration: Addressing racism, discrimination, and community support as drivers of health outcomes in diabetes care plans.
- Individualized discharge planning: Tailored discharge plans with diabetes self-management education, medication reconciliation, and follow-up scheduling.
- Health-promoting behaviors: Encouraging glucose monitoring, medication adherence, physical activity, healthy eating, and social support through multicomponent interventions.

**Notable Details:**
- ADA Standards of Care 2026 emphasizes person-centered, inclusive language and shared decision-making.
- Conflict of interest disclosures include roles such as consultant, institutional research funding, or ownership/partnership.
- Pediatric guidelines require annual reassessment of self-care transfer and training for daycare/school personnel.
- Diabetes technology integration mandates initial and ongoing education for CGM, AID, and CSII users and caregivers.
- SDOH integration includes assessing community and personal network instrumental support in care plans.
- Individualized discharge planning must begin at admission and include medication reconciliation for access and affordability.
- Health-promoting behaviors combine motivational interviewing, goal setting, and problem-solving techniques.
- ADA Standards of Care framework includes screening for diabetes preferably in healthcare settings, with community screening as an alternative.

## Diabetes Clinical Care Standards

The Diabetes Clinical Care Standards emphasize a holistic, patient-centered approach to diabetes management, integrating evidence-based practices, technology, education, and social determinants of health to optimize outcomes across diverse settings and populations.

**Key Features:**
- ADA Standards of Care updates: Annual updates incorporate person-first language, shared decision-making, and individualized care based on patient values, comorbidities, and financial considerations.
- Conflict of interest disclosures: Mandatory disclosure of financial relationships by guideline developers, peer reviewers, and committee members to ensure transparency and mitigate bias.
- Pediatric diabetes care guidelines: Child and family-centered care with age-appropriate A1C targets, comprehensive nutrition education, and training for school/daycare personnel.
- ADA Standards of Care framework: Continuous, comprehensive risk-reduction strategies beyond glycemic control, emphasizing self-management education and screening in healthcare settings.
- Multimodal diabetes education strategies: Empower patients through in-depth diabetes education, self-care skills training, and fostering positive attitudes toward management.
- ADA diabetes care standards framework: Standardized recommendations for glycemic goals, glucose monitoring, hypoglycemia management, and individualized care plans across settings.
- Diabetes technology integration: Offering CGM, AID, CSII, and connected pens based on patient needs, with education and data utilization for therapy adjustments.

- School-based diabetes management: Close collaboration with school personnel, staff training, and systematic self-management education for children with diabetes.
- Social determinants of health integration: Addressing racism, discrimination, and community support as drivers of health outcomes in diabetes care plans.
- Individualized discharge planning: Tailored discharge plans with diabetes self-management education, medication reconciliation, and follow-up scheduling.
- Health-promoting behaviors: Encouraging glucose monitoring, medication adherence, physical activity, healthy eating, and social support through multicomponent interventions.

**Topics in this group:**

### ADA Standards of Care updates

ADA Standards of Care 2026: Person-centered, evidence-based diabetes care. Annual updates to Standards of Care incorporate person-first and inclusive language, emphasizing shared decision-making based on individual values, preferences, prognoses, comorbidities, and informed financial considerations.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Standards of Care 2026: Person-centered, evidence-based diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Annual updates to Standards of Care incorporate person-first and inclusive language, emphasizing shared decision-making based on individual values, preferences, prognoses, comorbidities, and informed financial considerations.
- Health systems adopt continuous quality improvement with benchmarking programs and interprofessional team engagement to improve care quality and outcomes.
- Digital self-management tools, community health workers, and telehealth are integrated to support diabetes management, particularly in underserved communities.
- Comprehensive medical evaluations include assessment of social determinants of health, structural barriers, and individualized care plans with ongoing reassessment.
- Obesity management is prioritized as a primary treatment goal alongside glycemic control, with weight loss targets of 5–7% for intermediate benefits and >10% for sustained remission and cardiovascular benefits.
- Minor updates to figures and tables to align with current accessibility standards.
- Health systems should facilitate in-person and virtual team-based care, include those knowledgeable and experienced in diabetes management as part of the team, and utilize patient registries, decision support tools, proactive care planning, and community involvement to meet needs of individuals with diabetes.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diagnosis confirmation requires two abnormal results from different tests (e.g., A1C and FPG) or the same test at two different time points in the absence of unequivocal hyperglycemia.
- Shared decision-making must incorporate patient-specific factors including life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk, and social determinants of health.
- Comprehensive medical evaluation includes confirmation of diabetes diagnosis, assessment of glycemic status, evaluation for complications, and identification of care partners and resources.
- Weight management interventions require individualized approaches based on medical history, life circumstances, and preferences, with accommodations for privacy and nonjudgmental communication.
- Hospitalized individuals with diabetes require A1C testing at admission if no result is available from the prior 3 months and structured order sets for glycemic management.
- In people with prediabetes, monitor for the development of diabetes at least annually; modify frequency of testing based on individual risk assessment.
- In people with presymptomatic type 1 diabetes, monitor for disease progression using A1C approximately every 6 months and 75-g oral glucose tolerance test (i.e., fasting and 2-h plasma glucose) annually; modify frequency of monitoring and consider augmenting with other glycemic assessment tools such as continuous glucose monitoring metrics based on individual risk assessment incorporating age, number and type of autoantibodies, and glycemic metrics.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults are no longer recommended; individualized targets based on patient-specific factors are emphasized.
- Non-judgmental language replaces terms like 'noncompliance' or 'nonadherence' to avoid blaming patients for suboptimal self-management outcomes.
- Population-based screening programs for type 1 diabetes have not been established as cost-effective in the U.S., though they are implemented outside the country.
- Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) is not currently recommended for screening or diagnosis of prediabetes or diabetes due to insufficient evidence.
- Structured, very-low-calorie meal interventions (800–1,000 kcal/day) should only be prescribed by trained practitioners in medical settings with close monitoring.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recommendations emphasize the role of community health workers in supporting management of kidney disease risk factors, diabetes, and cardiovascular disease risk in underserved communities.
- New guidance includes monitoring postprandial or random plasma glucose in individuals on long-term glucocorticoid therapy and counseling on hyperglycemia risk with immune checkpoint inhibitors and PI3Kα inhibitors.
- Annual oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) starting at age 10 is the preferred screening test for cystic fibrosis-related diabetes when feasible, with A1C as an alternative two-step strategy.
- SGLT inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists are now considered fundamental elements of cardiovascular and kidney risk reduction in type 2 diabetes due to demonstrated benefits.
- Recommendation 2.8 was divided into two components to emphasize the importance of prompt evaluation for stage 3, overt type 1 diabetes in people with one or more islet autoantibodies.
- Recommendation 2.9 was added to highlight that people with a confirmed single IA-2 autoantibody should be monitored similarly to people with stage 2 type 1 diabetes but negative for IA-2 autoantibodies, as they have a comparable risk of progression to stage 3.
- Recommendation 2.18 was added to reinforce monitoring of postprandial or random plasma glucose in the setting of recurrent or long-term treatment with glucocorticoids.

### Source 1: 2026 ACC-AHA-AACVPR-ABC-ACPM-ADA AGS-APhA-ASPC-NLA-PCNA Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Conflict of Interest Disclosures in Guideline Development

**Key Clinical Actions:**
- Peer reviewers disclose financial relationships with industry entities (e.g., pharmaceutical companies, universities, or research institutions).
- Disclosures include roles such as consultant, institutional research funding, or ownership/partnership.
- Reviewers with no conflicts report 'None' in relevant categories (e.g., speakers bureau, expert witness).
- Financial benefits are categorized by type (e.g., personal research, institutional support, ownership).
- Disclosures are explicitly listed for each reviewer in a standardized format.
- Reviewers disclose specific monetary amounts or ranges for financial relationships (e.g., * for <$10,000, † for $10,000–$50,000, ‡ for >$50,000)

**Patient Preparation / Diagnostic Criteria:**
- Mandatory disclosure of all financial relationships with industry or other entities for peer reviewers.
- Transparency in reporting roles (e.g., consultant, speaker, researcher) and monetary sources.
- Reviewers must declare conflicts in categories such as ownership, research funding, or institutional benefits.
- Guideline development process requires at least one reviewer with no industry ties to serve as an independent arbiter for conflict resolution

### Source 2: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Conflict of Interest Disclosures in Guideline Development

**Core Recommendations:**
- Committee members disclosed financial or dualities of interest covering the 12 months prior to December 2025
- Disclosures include employment, research grants, speakers bureau, honoraria, ownership interest, consultant/advisory roles, and other dualities
- Research grants and consultant roles span multiple pharmaceutical and medical device companies (e.g., Novo Nordisk, Sanofi, Medtronic, Dexcom)
- Some members hold editorial or board positions in diabetes-related organizations or journals
- Disclosures are systematically documented and published alongside the guideline
- Disclosures explicitly include thresholds for financial conflicts (e.g., *≥$10,000 per year from company to individual, #grant or contract to university or other employer*)
- Some disclosures note editorial roles in journals (e.g., *Editor-in-Chief, BMI Open Diabetes Research and Care*)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Disclosure of financial conflicts of interest is mandatory for all committee members
- Disclosures must cover the 12 months prior to guideline publication
- Disclosures must include specific categories (e.g., research grants, honoraria, consultancy)

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of detailed conflict-of-interest disclosures for all committee members in the published guideline

### Source 3: American Thyroid Association.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Conflict of interest disclosures

**Key Clinical Findings:**
- Disclosure of financial relationships with pharmaceutical companies or research funding sources is mandatory for guideline contributors.
- Consultant roles and committee memberships must be transparently reported.
- Mitigation measures are required if conflicts are identified to ensure unbiased guideline development.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- All contributors must disclose any financial conflicts of interest (e.g., research support, consulting fees).
- Disclosures must be reviewed and addressed to prevent bias in guideline recommendations.


### pediatric diabetes care guidelines

Pediatric diabetes care guidelines (Type 1 and Type 2). Child and family-centered care with ongoing reassessment of self-care transfer and training for daycare/school personnel. Comprehensive nutrition education at diagnosis and annually, tailored to growth, eating patterns, and risk factors pediatric diabetes care guidelines. Pediatric diabetes care guidelines. Adopt individualized A1C targets for children and adolescents based on age, pubertal status, and hypoglycemia risk.. Implement structured education programs for pediatric patients and families to enhance self-management skills.. Monitor growth, development, and nutritional status regularly in pediatric diabetes care.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetes care guidelines (Type 1 and Type 2)

**Core Recommendations:**
- Child and family-centered care with ongoing reassessment of self-care transfer and training for daycare/school personnel
- Comprehensive nutrition education at diagnosis and annually, tailored to growth, eating patterns, and risk factors
- ≥60 min/day moderate-to-vigorous physical activity with bone/muscle strengthening ≥3 times/week
- Routine psychosocial screening (e.g., food insecurity, diabetes distress, depression, anxiety) starting at age 7–8 years
- Use of CGM and AID systems for Type 1 diabetes management with school support for diabetes technology
- Education on strategies to mitigate hypoglycemia risk and minimize hyperglycemia associated with exercise in children and adolescents with type 1 diabetes or those using insulin
- Confidential time for adolescents with providers at developmentally appropriate ages during clinical visits

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Interprofessional pediatric diabetes team including physician, CDCES, dietitian, and behavioral health specialist
- Developmentally appropriate DSMES for child/adolescent and family at diagnosis and routinely thereafter
- Individualized A1C goals based on hypoglycemia risk, technology access, and nonglycemic factors
- Screening for microalbuminuria, retinopathy, and neuropathy with updated timing/frequency per SEARCH/TODAY/T1D Exchange evidence
- Assessment of social determinants of health (e.g., food insecurity, housing stability) and family dynamics
- Interprofessional team must include a behavioral health specialist or social worker for children and adolescents with type 2 diabetes to address comorbidities such as obesity, dyslipidemia, hypertension, and microvascular complications
- Initial therapy for new-onset diabetes in children and adolescents with overweight or obesity should address hyperglycemia and metabolic derangements irrespective of ultimate diabetes type, with adjustment after metabolic compensation and islet autoantibody results become available

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized consideration
- Premature transfer of self-management tasks to children/adolescents without ongoing reassessment
- Reliance solely on adult diabetes care models for pediatric management
- Exclusion of behavioral health professionals from routine diabetes care teams
- Limited or non-inclusive nutrition education without cultural/financial considerations

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Expanded pharmacotherapy options (GLP-1 RAs, SGLT2 inhibitors, dual GIP/GLP-1 RA) with pediatric FDA approvals
- Inclusion of AI-based retinal screening tools and updated lipid screening protocols
- Structured transition programs from pediatric to adult care with digital tools for continuity
- Emphasis on ≥60 min/day physical activity and strategies to prevent exercise-related hypoglycemia/hyperglycemia
- Metabolic/bariatric surgery outcomes with ≥10-year follow-up data for pediatric Type 2 diabetes
- Figure 14.1 provides an approach to the initial treatment of new-onset diabetes in children and adolescents with overweight or obesity with clinical suspicion of type 2 diabetes
- Referrals to behavioral health and social work experts for complex social/environmental contexts surrounding pediatric diabetes management

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetes care guidelines

**Core Management Principles:**
- Adopt individualized A1C targets for children and adolescents based on age, pubertal status, and hypoglycemia risk.
- Implement structured education programs for pediatric patients and families to enhance self-management skills.
- Monitor growth, development, and nutritional status regularly in pediatric diabetes care.
- Screen for psychological comorbidities (e.g., depression, anxiety) and provide integrated mental health support.
- Ensure continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) use in pediatric patients to improve glycemic control and reduce hypoglycemia.
- Incorporate screening for substance use (e.g., alcohol, recreational drugs) in adolescents with diabetes, particularly those with poor glycemic control or unexplained hypoglycemia.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes confirmed via autoantibody testing and clinical criteria.
- Baseline evaluation including HbA1c, lipid profile, thyroid function, and celiac screening.
- Family engagement and support systems in place for effective diabetes management.
- Access to a multidisciplinary care team (endocrinologist, dietitian, psychologist, nurse educator).
- Regular follow-up visits every 3–6 months for children under 12 years and more frequently for adolescents.
- Ensure insulin storage and handling education for families, including use of thermochromic vial monitors for insulin stability verification.


### ADA Standards of Care framework

Diabetes care requires continuous, comprehensive risk-reduction strategies beyond glycemic management.. Ongoing diabetes self-management education and support are critical for empowering patients and preventing complications. ADA Standards of Care framework. ADA Standards of Care screening framework. Screening for diabetes should preferably be conducted in a healthcare setting. Community screening is an acceptable alternative if healthcare setting screening is not feasible. Screening should be performed using standardized diagnostic criteria (e.g., HbA1c, FPG, or 2-h PG after 75-g OGTT)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Standards of Care framework

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetes care requires continuous, comprehensive risk-reduction strategies beyond glycemic management.
- Ongoing diabetes self-management education and support are critical for empowering patients and preventing complications.
- Recommendations are based on rigorous evidence evaluation and updated annually with emerging clinical considerations.
- Guidelines are applied with clinical judgment, adjusted for individual preferences, comorbidities, and person-specific factors.
- Shared decision-making between healthcare professionals and individuals with diabetes is emphasized.
- The Standards of Care includes discussion of evidence and clinical practice recommendations intended to optimize care by assisting healthcare professionals and individuals in making shared decisions about diabetes care.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Annual updates by the ADA Professional Practice Committee for Diabetes (PPC).
- Evidence must be systematically reviewed, graded (A, B, C, or E), and approved by 80% consensus of the PPC.
- Recommendations must align with patient-specific factors such as age, comorbidities, and values.
- External peer review, ADA leadership review, and Board of Directors approval are required.
- Living standards are updated immediately if new evidence or regulatory changes (e.g., medication approvals) warrant it.
- The Standards of Care subcommittee for each section creates an initial list of relevant clinical questions reviewed and discussed by the PPC, with systematic literature searches conducted in consultation with a systematic review expert and librarian.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Previously published ADA scientific documents on clinical topics within the Standards of Care purview are superseded.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults are no longer recommended.
- Guidelines that preclude clinical judgment or ignore individual circumstances are outdated.
- Recommendations without evidence grading (e.g., ungraded expert opinions) are now explicitly categorized (E-level).
- Comprehensive treatment plans for specific complications (e.g., diabetic retinopathy) are no longer provided; only screening and referral guidance remain.
- The Standards of Care no longer provides comprehensive treatment plans for diabetes-associated complications (e.g., diabetic retinopathy or diabetic foot ulcers), offering only screening, referral guidance, and management in primary care settings.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Living standards allow immediate updates based on new evidence or regulatory changes (e.g., medication or technology approvals).
- Explicit grading system (A, B, C, E) for all recommendations to clarify evidence strength.
- Consumer-facing plain language summaries for people with diabetes to improve accessibility.
- Inclusion of psychosocial and behavioral health screening guidance in primary care settings.
- Expanded audience coverage to include payors, policymakers, and advocacy groups for policy alignment.
- The Standards of Care includes a consumer-facing plain language summary for people with diabetes to improve accessibility and understanding of guidelines.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Standards of Care screening framework

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Screening for diabetes should preferably be conducted in a healthcare setting
- Community screening is an acceptable alternative if healthcare setting screening is not feasible
- Screening should be performed using standardized diagnostic criteria (e.g., HbA1c, FPG, or 2-h PG after 75-g OGTT)

**Prerequisites:**
- Screening should be performed in a setting that ensures follow-up care and diagnostic confirmation
- Community screening programs must have protocols for referral to healthcare providers for confirmatory testing and follow-up
- Screening should be conducted in a setting with trained personnel capable of performing and interpreting diagnostic tests
- Community screening programs must ensure access to confirmatory testing within a defined timeframe (e.g., 2 weeks)


### multimodal diabetes education strategies

Empower people with diabetes with knowledge and tools to become active partners in their management. Provide in-depth information about diabetes, its complications, and treatment. Teach appropriate self-care skills and resources. Foster a positive attitude toward diabetes management. Train in self-monitoring skills multimodal diabetes education strategies. multimodal diabetes education strategies. Education should be delivered in diverse settings (clinic, schools, camps, social media, virtual platforms). Group-based education (physical or virtual) is more effective and cost-efficient than one-on-one sessions

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** multimodal diabetes education strategies

**Core Management Principles:**
- Education should be delivered in diverse settings (clinic, schools, camps, social media, virtual platforms)
- Group-based education (physical or virtual) is more effective and cost-efficient than one-on-one sessions
- Use age- and culture-appropriate educational materials (books, apps, websites, games) for repeated self-review
- Messages from the diabetes care team and peer groups must be consistent to avoid confusion
- Digital tools should be monitored by the diabetes care team to ensure accuracy and remove myths
- Messages from the diabetes care team and peer groups must be culturally sensitive and contextually relevant to avoid confusion

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Education should be imparted by trained professionals (endocrinologist, diabetes educator, dietician, psychologist)
- Peer education (parents, older patients in self-help groups) should be integrated where feasible
- Group sessions must be supervised by the diabetes care team to validate information
- Digital tools should be personalized or generalized based on patient needs
- Education should be reinforced at key transition points (diagnosis, sick days, provider changes)
- Digital tools must be validated or endorsed by the diabetes care team before use by patients

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** multimodal diabetes education strategies

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Empower people with diabetes with knowledge and tools to become active partners in their management
- Provide in-depth information about diabetes, its complications, and treatment
- Teach appropriate self-care skills and resources
- Foster a positive attitude toward diabetes management
- Train in self-monitoring skills
- Incorporate culturally tailored educational content to address diverse regional and community contexts in India

**Prerequisites:**
- Compliance of people with diabetes is essential for effective management
- Education programmes must be delivered by trained healthcare professionals or certified diabetes educators


### ADA diabetes care standards framework

Provides standardized recommendations for diabetes care across hospital and ambulatory settings. Includes glycemic goals, glucose monitoring, and hypoglycemia management protocols. Emphasizes individualized care plans for special situations and transitions

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Diabetes Care Standards Framework

**Core Recommendations:**
- Provides standardized recommendations for diabetes care across hospital and ambulatory settings
- Includes glycemic goals, glucose monitoring, and hypoglycemia management protocols
- Emphasizes individualized care plans for special situations and transitions
- Promotes medical nutrition therapy and self-management education in hospital settings
- Focuses on preventing admissions and readmissions through structured care pathways
- Includes a dedicated section on 'Standards for Special Situations' to address diabetes management in patients with critical illness, pregnancy, or perioperative care
- Promotes the use of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) in hospitalized patients when clinically appropriate

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Healthcare professionals must adhere to ADA Standards of Care for diabetes management
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., comorbidities, life expectancy) must inform glycemic targets
- Hospitalized patients require continuous glucose monitoring and hypoglycemia risk assessment
- Transition plans must be established for patients moving from hospital to ambulatory care
- Nutrition therapy and self-management education must be integrated into hospital care
- Glycemic targets in hospitalized adults must account for severity of illness, nutritional status, and risk of hypoglycemia
- Hospitalized patients with diabetes must receive a nutrition assessment by a registered dietitian or qualified healthcare professional

**Previous Recommendations:**
- One-size-fits-all glycemic targets in hospitalized patients
- Routine use of sliding-scale insulin without individualized adjustments
- Lack of structured transition protocols from hospital to outpatient care
- Ignoring hypoglycemia risk in diabetes management plans
- Exclusion of medical nutrition therapy in hospital diabetes care
- Routine use of sliding-scale insulin as the sole method for glycemic control in hospitalized patients
- Discontinuation of all oral diabetes medications in hospitalized patients without individualized assessment of their appropriateness

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Standardized glycemic goals tailored to hospitalized adults with diabetes
- Mandatory glucose monitoring protocols in hospital settings
- Integration of self-management education during hospitalization
- Structured transition plans to reduce readmissions
- Emphasis on preventing admissions through proactive diabetes care
- Mandatory use of CGM in ICU settings for patients with diabetes or hyperglycemia when available and clinically feasible
- Standardized protocols for perioperative glycemic management in surgical patients with diabetes


### diabetes technology integration

Diabetes technology integration (CGM, AID, CSII, connected pens). Diabetes devices should be offered to people with diabetes based on individualized needs, circumstances, preferences, and skill level.. Initial and ongoing education/training must be provided for CGM, AID, and CSII users and caregivers, including data utilization for therapy adjustments.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes technology integration (CGM, AID, CSII, connected pens)

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetes devices should be offered to people with diabetes based on individualized needs, circumstances, preferences, and skill level.
- Initial and ongoing education/training must be provided for CGM, AID, and CSII users and caregivers, including data utilization for therapy adjustments.
- Healthcare professionals must stay updated on available technologies and seek additional support when needed.
- Reasonable accommodations for device use should be provided in school and work settings, including time to respond to glucose alerts.
- Standardized reports (e.g., single-page CGM summaries) must be utilized for all diabetes devices.
- Training on CGM may be self-initiated by users with company-provided materials (e.g., OTC CGM brands), though underresourced, younger, or older individuals may require more repetition and in-person support.
- Healthcare teams must assess the need for additional education periodically, particularly if treatment goals are not being met.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individualized selection of devices based on patient needs, preferences, and caregiver capabilities.
- Initial and ongoing education/training for users and caregivers on device use and data interpretation.
- No prerequisites (e.g., C-peptide level, islet autoantibodies, or insulin duration) for initiating CSII or AID.
- Schools and workplaces must support device use with trained staff and backup treatment plans.
- Healthcare teams must have periodic education to keep up with rapidly evolving technology.
- Backup treatment plans must be included in the student's diabetes medical management plan (DMMP) for potential device failure.
- Healthcare professionals should be aware of device updates and how to access resources needed for their use, including periodic education to stay informed.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed one-size-fits-all approaches to diabetes technology use.
- Requiring C-peptide levels, islet autoantibodies, or insulin duration before initiating CSII or AID.
- Limiting device access based on age or A1C levels.
- Assuming all users can self-initiate complex devices without training.
- Relying solely on device availability without education and support.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Early initiation of CGM, CSII, and AID at diagnosis based on patient/caregiver needs and preferences.
- Mandated standardized device reports (e.g., weekly CGM summaries) for clinical decision-making.
- Defined competencies (basic to advanced) for healthcare team members using diabetes technologies.
- OTC CGM brands enabling self-initiation with company-provided training materials.
- ADA’s diabetes technology guide (diabetes.org/living-with-diabetes/treatment-care/diabetes-technology-guide) for device selection.
- Defined competencies (basic, fundamental, intermediate, advanced) for healthcare team members specific to their roles in diabetes technology use.
- Instructions for device use must be outlined in the student's diabetes medical management plan (DMMP) with school support.

### systematic diabetes care improvement

Systematic diabetes care improvement + team-based care + SDOH integration. Assess glycemic status and prior/current treatments at initial and follow-up visits. Identify care partners and support systems at visits

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Systematic diabetes care improvement + team-based care + SDOH integration

**Core Recommendations:**
- Assess glycemic status and prior/current treatments at initial and follow-up visits
- Identify care partners and support systems at visits
- Screen for social determinants of health (food insecurity, housing insecurity, financial barriers, health insurance access, environmental factors, social capital)
- Use interprofessional team-based care models to improve A1C, blood pressure, LDL cholesterol, and ASCVD risk
- Implement quality improvement (QI) initiatives using patient registries, EHR tools, and benchmarking
- Screen patients for financial hardship of treatment, cost-related barriers to medication use, and rationing of essential services due to medical costs
- Assess and address gaps in diabetes care and health outcomes by stratifying clinical quality data by factors such as insurance status, race, ethnicity, preferred language, disability, and SDOH

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient with type 1 or type 2 diabetes
- Access to primary or specialty care (endocrinology/diabetes care)
- EHR or patient registry for tracking metrics
- Interprofessional team including clinical champion, QI/data specialist, and patient representative
- Insurance coverage or financial support systems to minimize cost barriers
- Screening for financial barriers to care and cost-related medication nonadherence
- Access to community health workers or lay health coaches for self-management support

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C numeric targets without individualized consideration
- Routine use of high-deductible health plans without cost-sharing mitigation
- Exclusion of MASLD/MASH from treatment algorithm decisions
- Lack of SDOH screening during clinical encounters
- Visit-based billing models without value-based payment alignment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RA with MASH/MASLD benefits for type 2 diabetes with overweight/obesity
- Osteoporosis drug therapy for older adults with T-score ≤-2.5 or -2.0 to -2.5 with fracture risk
- Vaccination recommendations extended to adolescents
- FIB-4 index >2.67 management by liver specialist
- Preventive drug lists with reduced cost-sharing for diabetes medications
- Preventive drug lists that reduce or eliminate out-of-pocket cost sharing for diabetes medications and related therapies
- Capped out-of-pocket payments for insulin at $35 per month for Medicare beneficiaries (Part D Senior Savings Model and Inflation Reduction Act)


### school-based diabetes management

Close communication with school personnel is essential for optimizing diabetes management, safety, and access to school-sponsored opportunities.. Training and cooperation of school staff are required for effective diabetes care in school settings. school-based diabetes management. school-based diabetes management. Diabetes education is the backbone of diabetes care, with systematic self-management education emphasized as essential for effective control.. Parents and children are taught hands-on skills immediately, including self-testing, insulin injection, and hypoglycemia management, to restore a sense of control.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** school-based diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Close communication with school personnel is essential for optimizing diabetes management, safety, and access to school-sponsored opportunities.
- Training and cooperation of school staff are required for effective diabetes care in school settings.
- Optimal use of diabetes technologies should be integrated into school-based care plans.
- Behavioral health considerations must be addressed in school-based diabetes management.
- Emergency situation protocols and clinical trial participation guidance should be established for school settings.
- Legal protections and accommodations for children with diabetes must extend to childcare settings (e.g., preschools, camps) for young children aged <5 years.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- School personnel must receive appropriate training and resources for diabetes management.
- A communication system between school staff, parents/guardians, and healthcare professionals must be in place.
- Written policies and procedures for diabetes management in schools must be developed.
- Access to necessary diabetes supplies and equipment must be ensured in school settings.
- Legal protections for children with diabetes in school settings must be upheld.
- Written policies and procedures for diabetes management in schools must address security needs, transfers, and access to medical personnel in detention facilities.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of advanced diabetes technologies into school-based care plans is now emphasized.
- Behavioral health considerations are explicitly included in school-based diabetes management guidelines.
- Emergency situation protocols for schools are newly detailed in the updated guidelines.
- Guidance for diabetes care during clinical trial participation in school settings is introduced.
- Legal protections and accommodations for children with diabetes in schools are reinforced.
- Guidance for diabetes care in detention facilities is newly introduced, including policies for empowering diabetes self-management and addressing unique security challenges.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** school-based diabetes management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Diabetes education is the backbone of diabetes care, with systematic self-management education emphasized as essential for effective control.
- Parents and children are taught hands-on skills immediately, including self-testing, insulin injection, and hypoglycemia management, to restore a sense of control.
- School staff (teachers, medical room staff, bus drivers) are educated on diabetes care, emergency responses, and must have access to emergency kits and identification cards.
- Children are encouraged to develop age-appropriate independence in diabetes management, including self-testing, insulin administration, and decision-making.
- Psychosocial care is integrated into diabetes management, addressing family dynamics, mental health, and stigma to prevent deterioration in care.
- Teach safe disposal of sharps from the beginning of diabetes education to prevent injuries and environmental contamination.
- Encourage the entire family to adopt lifestyle modifications (e.g., healthy eating, physical activity) to normalize diabetes care and reduce the child's sense of exclusion.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes in childhood or adolescence requiring intensive insulin therapy and frequent glucose monitoring.
- Stabilization of the child's clinical condition post-diagnosis before outpatient education begins.
- Family willingness and ability to participate in hands-on training and ongoing reinforcement of diabetes management skills.
- Access to a multidisciplinary diabetes care team including educators, psychologists, and healthcare providers.
- School and community support systems to ensure safe diabetes management during school hours and extracurricular activities.
- Stabilization of the child's clinical condition (e.g., resolution of DKA) before outpatient education begins, unless the diagnosis is made early without florid symptoms.
- Family willingness to participate in hands-on training and ongoing reinforcement of diabetes management skills, with consideration for socioeconomic barriers (e.g., cost of SMBG supplies).


### social determinants of health integration

Social determinants of health integration in diabetes care. Integrate social support assessment (community and personal network instrumental support) into diabetes care plans. Address SDOH such as racism and discrimination as potential drivers of adverse health outcomes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Social determinants of health integration in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Integrate social support assessment (community and personal network instrumental support) into diabetes care plans
- Address SDOH such as racism and discrimination as potential drivers of adverse health outcomes
- Leverage community resources and health care community linkages for nutrition and physical activity recommendations
- Utilize CHWs, community paramedics, peer supporters, and lay leaders to deliver DSMES services, particularly in underserved communities
- Delegate limited clinical tasks to CHWs under direct supervision of a health professional and in compliance with state laws
- Identify and document specific SDOH (e.g., racism, discrimination, food insecurity) during patient encounters to inform tailored diabetes care plans

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient engagement in self-care behaviors and medication use may be influenced by SDOH, necessitating tailored interventions
- Community-based care delivery requires collaboration with organizations like the American Medical Association and AHRQ
- CHWs must be part of an evidence-based strategy to improve diabetes and cardiovascular risk management in underserved communities
- Community paramedics require training in chronic disease monitoring, medication management, and SDOH
- Delegated clinical tasks to CHWs must follow state health care laws and be supervised by a licensed health professional
- Community paramedics must operate under the direction of a medical director with documented training in chronic disease monitoring, medication management, and SDOH

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine clinical practice that does not address SDOH such as racism and discrimination
- Exclusive reliance on traditional clinical interventions without community resource linkages
- Unsupervised delegation of clinical tasks to non-clinical personnel

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of CHWs as trusted frontline public health workers to provide social support and basic health education
- Use of community paramedics for diabetes education, medication management, and care coordination
- Adoption of health care community linkages to translate clinical recommendations into practice
- Implementation of peer supporters and lay leaders to enhance DSMES services in underserved populations
- Delegation of limited clinical tasks to CHWs under direct supervision and state-compliant protocols
- Use of community paramedics for wound care in diabetes management as part of expanded scope of practice


### individualized discharge planning

individualized discharge planning for diabetes. Tailor discharge plans to individual needs, capabilities, and preferences. Begin discharge planning at admission and update as needs change. Include diabetes self-management education prior to discharge. Conduct diabetes medication reconciliation with attention to access, affordability, and follow-up scheduling

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** individualized discharge planning for diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Tailor discharge plans to individual needs, capabilities, and preferences
- Begin discharge planning at admission and update as needs change
- Include diabetes self-management education prior to discharge
- Conduct diabetes medication reconciliation with attention to access, affordability, and follow-up scheduling
- Schedule outpatient follow-up within 1 month (earlier if glycemic management is suboptimal)
- Provide clear communication to outpatient healthcare professionals via hospital discharge summaries or direct contact to ensure safe transitions of care

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient-specific factors such as diabetes type, severity, illness effects on blood glucose, and individual circumstances
- Consideration of discharge setting capabilities (home, assisted living, rehabilitation, skilled nursing)
- Glycemic management medications changed or suboptimal glucose control at discharge
- Hyperglycemia and/or hypoglycemia during hospitalization
- Facility capabilities for diabetes management if not discharged to home

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Discharge algorithm for glycemic medication adjustment based on admission A1C, pre-admission medications, and insulin use
- Structured discharge communication including root cause of hyperglycemia, complications, and recommended treatments
- Frequent post-discharge contact to adjust therapy and avoid hyperglycemia/hypoglycemia
- Minimum discharge plan components: medication reconciliation, structured communication, and patient education
- Shared decision-making in creating a safe and effective discharge plan
- Discharge algorithm for glycemic medication adjustment based on admission A1C, pre-admission medications, and insulin usage during hospitalization to guide treatment decisions


### health-promoting behaviors

Health-promoting behaviors in diabetes self-management. Support glucose monitoring. Encourage medication adherence. Promote use of diabetes technologies. Engage in regular physical activity. Adopt healthy eating habits. Facilitating opportunities for social support. Multicomponent interventions combining motivational interviewing, goal setting, and problem-solving

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Health-promoting behaviors in diabetes self-management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Support glucose monitoring
- Encourage medication adherence
- Promote use of diabetes technologies
- Engage in regular physical activity
- Adopt healthy eating habits
- Facilitating opportunities for social support

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient willingness to participate in self-management
- Access to diabetes education or certified diabetes care and education specialists (DCES)
- Availability of trained healthcare professionals or community health workers
- Appropriate tools for tracking or self-monitoring health behaviors
- Supportive social environment or opportunities for social support
- Prior training in evidence-based methods (e.g., motivational interviewing) for diabetes care professionals delivering behavior change strategies

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Rigid, one-size-fits-all behavioral interventions
- Overreliance on single-component behavior change strategies
- Exclusive use of financial incentives without evidence-based counseling
- Ignoring patient-specific barriers to behavior change
- Lack of tailoring interventions to individual or population needs

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Multicomponent interventions combining motivational interviewing, goal setting, and problem-solving
- Family-based or multisystem behavioral interventions for children and adolescents
- Digital health tools for delivering behavior change strategies
- Training for diabetes care professionals in evidence-based methods like motivational interviewing
- Use of behavioral economics approaches (e.g., social norms messaging) for short-term motivation
- Use of behavioral activation as an evidence-based behavioral strategy


### health literacy interventions in diabetes

Health literacy and numeracy interventions in diabetes care. Healthcare professionals and diabetes care specialists should provide easy-to-understand information and reduce unnecessary complexity in care plans.. Interventions addressing low health literacy in diabetes populations improve A1C, diabetes knowledge, and self-management behaviors.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Health literacy and numeracy interventions in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Healthcare professionals and diabetes care specialists should provide easy-to-understand information and reduce unnecessary complexity in care plans.
- Interventions addressing low health literacy in diabetes populations improve A1C, diabetes knowledge, and self-management behaviors.
- Health numeracy is essential for tasks like interpreting glucose readings, decoding food labels, and understanding medication doses.
- Standardized screening measures should be used to assess health literacy and numeracy if treatment decision understanding appears limited.
- Adjunctive education and support are indicated if limited health literacy and numeracy are barriers to optimal care decisions.
- Healthcare professionals should assess and address both health literacy and numeracy barriers simultaneously when developing care plans for patients with diabetes.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals with diabetes or prediabetes who require numeric tasks (e.g., interpreting glucose readings, medication dosing).
- Patients demonstrating limited understanding of treatment decisions or self-management concepts.
- Populations with low health literacy or numeracy identified through screening.
- Diabetes technologies users (e.g., insulin pumps) requiring applied and interpretive health numeracy skills.
- Patients with prediabetes or diabetes who perform numeric tasks (e.g., interpreting glucose readings, medication dosing) must be evaluated for both health literacy and numeracy deficits before finalizing treatment plans.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of standardized screening measures to assess health literacy and numeracy barriers in diabetes care.
- Integration of health numeracy training for patients using diabetes technologies like insulin pumps.
- Emphasis on reducing unnecessary complexity in care plans to accommodate low health literacy.
- Adjunctive education and support strategies tailored to patients with identified health literacy or numeracy barriers.
- Primary numeric skills, applied health numeracy, and interpretive health numeracy should be explicitly integrated into health literacy interventions for patients using diabetes technologies like insulin pumps.

Produced under the auspices of the ADA by invited experts. Does not contain clinical practice recommendations. May provide a scientific rationale for clinical practice recommendations in the Standards of Care

**Core Recommendations:**
- A balanced systematic review and analysis of literature on a scientific or medical topic related to diabetes
- Produced under the auspices of the ADA by invited experts
- Does not contain clinical practice recommendations
- May provide a scientific rationale for clinical practice recommendations in the Standards of Care
- May include task force and expert committee reports
- May include meta-analyses or pooled analyses of clinical trial data to strengthen evidence synthesis
- Often undergoes external peer review by independent experts before publication


### school and daycare diabetes care

School and daycare diabetes care training and legal compliance. Training of school or daycare personnel and athletic coaches to provide care in accordance with the individualized diabetes medical management plan. Ensuring safe access to all school- or daycare-sponsored opportunities for children and adolescents with diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Training of school or daycare personnel and athletic coaches to provide care in accordance with the individualized diabetes medical management plan
- Ensuring safe access to all school- or daycare-sponsored opportunities for children and adolescents with diabetes

### hospital discharge planning

Hospital discharge planning for diabetes care. Identify healthcare professionals responsible for post-discharge diabetes care. Assess patient understanding of diabetes diagnosis, glucose monitoring, and home glucose goals. Provide education on healthy food choices and refer to outpatient dietitian or diabetes educator if needed

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hospital discharge planning for diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Identify healthcare professionals responsible for post-discharge diabetes care
- Assess patient understanding of diabetes diagnosis, glucose monitoring, and home glucose goals
- Provide education on healthy food choices and refer to outpatient dietitian or diabetes educator if needed
- Review medication administration, including insulin and noninsulin injectables
- Review sick-day management and proper use/disposal of diabetes supplies
- Document patient's baseline functional status and any barriers to self-care (e.g., vision impairment, dexterity issues) to tailor discharge education and support needs

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have a confirmed diabetes diagnosis requiring post-discharge care
- Healthcare team must coordinate follow-up care before discharge
- Patient or caregiver must demonstrate ability to perform glucose monitoring and medication administration
- Prescriptions for medications and supplies must be provided at discharge
- Education must be tailored to patient's health literacy and cultural background
- Patient or caregiver must demonstrate proficiency in at least one method of glucose monitoring (fingerstick or CGM) before discharge
- Healthcare team must verify patient's insurance coverage for prescribed diabetes supplies and medications to prevent post-discharge access barriers

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Discharging patients without confirmed follow-up care arrangements
- Providing generic discharge instructions without patient-specific education
- Assuming patients understand medication administration without verification
- Not providing prescriptions for necessary diabetes supplies at discharge
- Ignoring patient's cultural or literacy needs in education
- Relying solely on verbal discharge instructions without written or digital reinforcement of key education points

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Mandatory coordination with outpatient diabetes care team before discharge
- Patient-specific sick-day management plans reviewed at discharge
- Provision of durable medical equipment and supplies at discharge
- Inclusion of CGM sensors or test strips in discharge prescriptions
- Standardized education on proper disposal of sharps and diabetes supplies
- Include a written or digital action plan for hypoglycemia management in discharge materials
- Provide a 30-day supply of diabetes medications and supplies at discharge to ensure immediate continuity of care


### hospital diabetes self-management policy

Diabetes self-management in the hospital may be appropriate for select individuals who wish to continue self-care while acutely ill.. Candidates must successfully perform diabetes self-management at home and have cognitive/physical skills not compromised by acute illness or delirium.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hospital diabetes self-management policy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetes self-management in the hospital may be appropriate for select individuals who wish to continue self-care while acutely ill.
- Candidates must successfully perform diabetes self-management at home and have cognitive/physical skills not compromised by acute illness or delirium.
- Patients must have adequate oral intake and stable insulin requirements.
- Daily agreement between the person with diabetes and care team is required to continue self-management during hospitalization.
- Hospital policies may allow personal medication use (home or hospital-dispensed) under pharmacy verification and prescriber order, supervised by a registered nurse.
- Children with parental supervision may be considered for diabetes self-management in the hospital if they meet all other criteria.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Successful diabetes self-management at home.
- Cognitive and physical ability to self-administer insulin and perform glucose monitoring.
- Adequate oral intake.
- Stable insulin requirements.
- Daily reassessment by care team to confirm appropriateness of self-management.
- Consensus-based guidelines must be followed for insulin pump or CGM device use during hospitalization, including protocols for changing insulin infusion sites and CGM glucose sensors.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Guidelines for wearing insulin pumps or CGM devices during hospitalization, including insulin infusion site and CGM sensor changes.
- Pharmacy exception process for personal medication use in the hospital.
- Requirement for prescriber order and registered nurse supervision for self-administration of medications.
- Policy must include a pharmacy exception process for personal medication use on a case-by-case basis, determined in consultation with the care team.


### evidence grading system

ADA evidence-grading system and Standards of Care development process. Public comment is actively solicited during a year-long period to identify gaps in care and improve transparency.. Feedback is reviewed by the Professional Practice Committee (PPC) and considered for urgent updates published as living standards.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA evidence-grading system and Standards of Care development process

**Core Recommendations:**
- Public comment is actively solicited during a year-long period to identify gaps in care and improve transparency.
- Feedback is reviewed by the Professional Practice Committee (PPC) and considered for urgent updates published as living standards.
- External peer reviewers, endorsing societies (e.g., ACC, AGS, NKF), and ADA leadership review the Standards of Care for methodological rigor, accuracy, and implementation.
- The ADA adheres to the CMSS Principles for Clinical Guideline Development, ensuring multi-stakeholder review and approval.
- Final approval requires consensus from healthcare professionals, scientists, and other stakeholders before publication.
- The ADA Medical Affairs scientific team reviews the Standards of Care for methodological rigor, accuracy, clarity, and implementation before external peer review.
- The Standards of Care undergoes a sequential review process involving external peer review, ADA leadership review, and ADA Board of Directors review and approval.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Evidence must be derived from well-conducted randomized controlled trials, meta-analyses, cohort studies, or case-control studies with minimal methodological flaws.
- Public feedback must be systematically compiled and addressed by the PPC during the annual update cycle.
- External peer review and endorsing society input are mandatory before final approval.
- Living standards updates may be published between annual revisions based on urgent evidence or stakeholder feedback.
- Methodological rigor, clarity, and implementation feasibility are prerequisites for guideline approval.
- Evidence for Level A must come from well-conducted, generalizable randomized controlled trials that are adequately powered.
- Level A evidence specifically includes evidence from a well-conducted multicenter trial or a meta-analysis that incorporated quality ratings in the analysis.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Evidence from poorly controlled or uncontrolled studies (e.g., case series with historical controls) is no longer considered high-quality support.
- Recommendations based on conflicting evidence with weak supporting weight are deprioritized.
- Major methodological flaws in randomized trials (e.g., inadequate power, ungeneralizable populations) invalidate high-level evidence claims.
- Evidence from randomized clinical trials with one or more major methodological flaws that could invalidate the results is categorized as Level C.
- Evidence from observational studies with high potential for bias, such as case series with comparison with historical controls, is categorized as Level C.
- Conflicting evidence where the weight of evidence supports the recommendation is categorized as Level C.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Introduction of living standards updates to incorporate urgent evidence or feedback between annual revisions.
- Expanded public comment period to include people with diabetes, caregivers, and broader clinical stakeholders.
- Mandatory adherence to CMSS Principles for guideline development, enhancing methodological transparency.
- Structured multi-tiered review process involving external peer reviewers, endorsing societies, and ADA leadership.
- Year-long public comment period to systematically address gaps in care and improve guideline relevance.
- The list of endorsing societies providing feedback has been expanded to include ASBMR, ISPAD, and TOS in addition to ACC, AGS, and NKF.
