### population health collaboration framework

Population health collaboration framework for diabetes care. Engagement and collaboration between people with diabetes, caregivers, interprofessional healthcare teams, health systems, community partners, payors, policymakers, and public health agencies

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Population health collaboration framework for diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Engagement and collaboration between people with diabetes, caregivers, interprofessional healthcare teams, health systems, community partners, payors, policymakers, and public health agencies
- Implementation of evidence-based diabetes care recommendations to improve individual and population health outcomes
- Addressing social determinants of health (e.g., food insecurity, housing insecurity, financial toxicity) to eliminate health disparities
- Integration of telemedicine, digital health interventions, and mHealth tools to enhance access and self-management support
- Use of community health workers, peer support, and culturally tailored interventions to improve diabetes outcomes
- Addressing health literacy and numeracy barriers to improve patient understanding and self-management
- Implementing food prescription programs and addressing food insecurity as part of diabetes care

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Existence of interprofessional healthcare teams trained in diabetes management
- Availability of community resources and partnerships to address social needs
- Access to telemedicine infrastructure and digital health tools for underserved populations
- Implementation of standardized screening protocols for social determinants of health
- Adoption of value-based care models and payment reforms to support population health initiatives
- Availability of validated screening tools for food insecurity and housing instability
- Presence of trained community health workers or community paramedics within the care network

**Previous Recommendations:**
- One-size-fits-all A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors
- Fragmented care models that do not integrate community and public health resources
- Reliance solely on traditional in-person care without leveraging telemedicine or digital tools
- Ignoring social determinants of health in diabetes management plans
- Lack of collaboration between healthcare systems, payors, and policymakers

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of social determinants of health screening into routine diabetes care
- Adoption of telemedicine and digital health interventions to improve access and outcomes
- Implementation of community health worker programs to support diabetes self-management
- Use of value-based care models and alternative payment strategies to incentivize population health improvements
- Culturally tailored diabetes education and support programs to address health disparities
- Integration of food prescription programs into routine diabetes care plans
- Deployment of community paramedics for diabetes self-management education and support


### psychosocial care protocols

Psychosocial care protocols in diabetes. Provide routine psychosocial care using a collaborative, person-centered, culturally informed approach by trained healthcare professionals.. Implement annual or change-triggered screening for psychosocial concerns using age-appropriate, standardized, and validated tools.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Psychosocial care protocols in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Provide routine psychosocial care using a collaborative, person-centered, culturally informed approach by trained healthcare professionals.
- Implement annual or change-triggered screening for psychosocial concerns using age-appropriate, standardized, and validated tools.
- Integrate brief, person-centered psychosocial interventions into routine care (e.g., motivational interviewing, CBT techniques, problem-solving, emotion regulation strategies).
- Refer to behavioral health professionals with diabetes expertise for further assessment and treatment when psychosocial concerns are identified.
- Monitor and address SDOH, diabetes distress, suicidality, and family/caregiver psychosocial concerns as part of routine care.
- Health care professionals can integrate brief, person-centered psychosocial interventions by creating a supportive environment for emotional disclosure, validating experiences, and employing empathetic listening.
- Practical examples of interventions include using 'importance' and 'confidence' rulers, conducting week-long behavioral experiments, demonstrating breathing exercises for mindfulness, setting SMART goals, and teaching grounding techniques.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening should occur at diagnosis, routine visits, hospitalizations, transitions in care, or when glycemic goals or self-management are not met.
- Screening tools must be age-appropriate, standardized, and validated (e.g., diabetes distress, fear of hypoglycemia, depression, anxiety).
- Referrals require access to behavioral health professionals with specialized diabetes training or embedded within diabetes care teams.
- Psychosocial interventions should be tailored to patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, cognitive function, cultural background).
- SDOH screening should be incorporated to identify barriers to self-management (e.g., financial, social, emotional resources).
- Screening for suicidality may be appropriate given elevated rates of suicidality among people with diabetes.
- When caregivers or family members play a significant role in diabetes care, their psychosocial concerns should be assessed and addressed by appropriate professionals.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine reliance on informal verbal inquiries without standardized screening tools.
- Waiting for overt psychological deterioration before addressing psychosocial concerns.
- Excluding family/caregiver psychosocial assessments in diabetes care plans.
- Using non-validated or non-age-appropriate psychosocial screening methods.
- Limiting psychosocial care to crisis intervention rather than proactive, routine monitoring.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Mandatory annual psychosocial screening with validated tools (e.g., diabetes distress, suicidality) for all people with diabetes.
- Integration of brief CBT and mindfulness-based interventions into routine diabetes care.
- Embedding behavioral health professionals within diabetes care teams for collaborative care.
- Digital health platforms for delivering psychosocial interventions (e.g., telehealth, group-based appointments).
- Preventive psychosocial interventions (e.g., resilience training, behavioral family interventions) even in absence of positive screeners.
- Preventive psychosocial interventions (e.g., resilience-promoting interventions, behavioral family interventions) are indicated even in the absence of positive psychosocial screeners.
- Psychosocial interventions can be delivered via digital health platforms or integrated into group-based or shared diabetes appointments.


### ADA Standards of Care author team

ADA Standards of Care author team composition and governance. Co-chaired by Mandeep Bajaj, MBBS, FRCP and Rozalina G. McCoy, MD, MS. Includes multidisciplinary experts (endocrinologists, pharmacists, methodologists, and patient advocates). Follows CMSS principles for clinical practice guideline development

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Standards of Care author team composition and governance

**Core Recommendations:**
- Co-chaired by Mandeep Bajaj, MBBS, FRCP and Rozalina G. McCoy, MD, MS
- Includes multidisciplinary experts (endocrinologists, pharmacists, methodologists, and patient advocates)
- Follows CMSS principles for clinical practice guideline development
- Incorporates external peer review from academic and clinical specialists
- Acknowledges ADA Presidents and Presidents-elect for governance alignment
- Explicit acknowledgment of ADA Presidents and Presidents-elect (e.g., A. Enrique Caballero, Joshua J. Neumiller) for governance alignment
- Inclusion of specific external peer reviewers from diverse disciplines (e.g., cardiology, nephrology, endocrinology) to validate recommendations

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Adherence to CMSS code for interactions with companies to ensure transparency
- Use of evidence-based methodology led by Chief Methodologist (Raveendhara R. Bannuru, MD, PhD)
- Inclusion of patient-specific factors in guideline development (e.g., life expectancy, cognitive function)
- Regular updates to reflect new clinical evidence (annual revisions)
- External peer review by recognized experts in diabetes care and related fields

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Prior versions of guidelines without CMSS-aligned methodology
- Non-evidence-based recommendations from outdated consensus statements
- Guidelines not incorporating patient-centered outcomes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of CMSS principles for guideline development in 2026 Standards of Care
- Expanded multidisciplinary author team including methodologists and patient advocates
- Inclusion of new external peer reviewers for enhanced validity
- Updated governance structure with ADA Presidents and Presidents-elect alignment
- Annual review cycle to ensure timeliness and relevance of recommendations


### ADA Standards of Care committee composition

ADA Standards of Care committee composition and governance. The PPC is an interprofessional expert committee comprising physicians, nurse practitioners, pharmacists, diabetes care and education specialists, registered dietitian nutritionists, behavioral health scientists, and others with diverse expertise.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Standards of Care committee composition and governance

**Core Recommendations:**
- The PPC is an interprofessional expert committee comprising physicians, nurse practitioners, pharmacists, diabetes care and education specialists, registered dietitian nutritionists, behavioral health scientists, and others with diverse expertise.
- Appointment to the PPC is based on excellence in clinical practice and research, with attention to demographic, geographic, work setting, or identity representation.
- A PPC chair or co-chairs oversee the committee, and designated subject matter experts support content development but do not vote on recommendations.
- All members comply with ADA duality of interest policy, disclosing financial, intellectual, or other conflicts annually and before meetings.
- Members with conflicts pertinent to specific recommendations are prohibited from voting on those recommendations.
- The ADA adheres to section 7 of the Council of Medical Specialty Societies 'Code for Interactions with Companies' regarding committee interactions.
- Full disclosure statements from all committee members are solicited and reviewed during the appointment process.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Committee members must have expertise in areas such as endocrinology, epidemiology, public health, cardiovascular risk management, nephrology, ophthalmology, podiatry, and diabetes technology.
- Members must disclose conflicts for a time frame including 1 year prior to appointment until publication of the Standards of Care.
- Potential dualities of interest are evaluated by a panel of experts and the ADA Legal Affairs Division based on financial amount and relevance.
- The majority of the PPC and chairs must remain unconflicted for the subject area.
- Chairs must remain unconflicted for 1 year after publication of the Standards of Care.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- No PPC members or subject matter experts were employees of pharmaceutical or medical device companies during the development of the 2026 Standards of Care.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- New to the 2026 Standards of Care, the National Kidney Foundation (NKF) reviewed and approved section 11, 'Chronic Kidney Disease and Risk Management,' and the International Society for Pediatric and Adolescent Diabetes (ISPAD) reviewed and approved section 14, 'Children and Adolescents.'
- A guideline methodologist with expertise in evidence-based medicine and guideline development methodology oversees methodological aspects and serves as a statistical analyst.
- An information specialist (librarian) is consulted as necessary for systematic literature reviews to identify the latest scientific evidence.
- The American Society for Bone and Mineral Research (ASBMR) reviewed and approved the 'Bone Health' subsection in section 4, 'Comprehensive Medical Evaluation and Assessment of Comorbidities.'
- The Obesity Society (TOS) reviewed and approved section 8, 'Obesity and Weight Management for the Prevention and Treatment of Diabetes.'
- The American College of Cardiology (ACC) reviewed and approved section 10, 'Cardiovascular Disease and Risk Management.'
- The American Geriatrics Society (AGS) reviewed and approved section 13, 'Older Adults.'


### ADA Standards of Care supplement

The supplement contains the official ADA position on diabetes care.. It is authored by the ADA under the guidance of the Professional Practice Committee (PPC).. It provides all current ADA clinical practice recommendations in one consolidated source.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA Standards of Care supplement

**Core Recommendations:**
- The supplement contains the official ADA position on diabetes care.
- It is authored by the ADA under the guidance of the Professional Practice Committee (PPC).
- It provides all current ADA clinical practice recommendations in one consolidated source.


### ADA endorsement process

ADA endorsement process for clinical guidelines. Formal review by the Professional Practice Committee (PPC) for alignment with ADA’s mission, Standards of Care, and evidence-based principles. Endorsement signifies scientific accuracy, relevance, and value to healthcare professionals caring for people with diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA endorsement process for clinical guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- Formal review by the Professional Practice Committee (PPC) for alignment with ADA’s mission, Standards of Care, and evidence-based principles
- Endorsement signifies scientific accuracy, relevance, and value to healthcare professionals caring for people with diabetes
- Endorsement does not extend to every specific statement or phrasing in the endorsed document
- ADA representatives may be involved in document development but endorsement does not reflect the official ADA position
- Type and level of endorsement vary based on document type and ADA involvement in development
- ADA representatives may be involved in document development, but their involvement does not guarantee endorsement of the final document

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Document must align with ADA’s mission and Standards of Care
- Document must adhere to evidence-based principles
- Document must demonstrate scientific accuracy and relevance to diabetes care
- Document must be a scientific document, such as a clinical guideline or consensus report, to be eligible for endorsement


### ADA position statement

ADA position statement characteristics. ADA statements represent ADA's point of view or position on advocacy, policy, economic, or medical issues related to diabetes. Statements undergo formal review including external peer review. Review involves ADA Professional Practice Committee for Diabetes. Review includes ADA clinical leadership and ADA scientific team

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ADA position statement characteristics

**Core Recommendations:**
- ADA statements represent ADA's point of view or position on advocacy, policy, economic, or medical issues related to diabetes
- Statements undergo formal review including external peer review
- Review involves ADA Professional Practice Committee for Diabetes
- Review includes ADA clinical leadership and ADA scientific team
- Board of Directors review is conducted as warranted


### Chronic Care Model implementation

Transition from reactive to proactive care delivery using a team-based approach. Utilize clinical information systems and registries for person-specific and population-based support. Engage collaborative interprofessional teams including primary care clinicians, CDCES, and RDNs

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Chronic Care Model implementation

**Core Recommendations:**
- Transition from reactive to proactive care delivery using a team-based approach
- Utilize clinical information systems and registries for person-specific and population-based support
- Engage collaborative interprofessional teams including primary care clinicians, CDCES, and RDNs
- Prioritize timely modification of behavior change interventions and pharmacologic therapy to avoid therapeutic inertia
- Assess and address social determinants of health and barriers to safe diabetes management
- Engage endocrinology teams with advanced diabetes management expertise for cases of high clinical complexity, multimorbidity, or difficult-to-manage hyperglycemia/hypoglycemia
- Develop individualized diabetes medical management plans in collaboration with school professionals and caregivers for pediatric patients

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Care team must include primary care clinician, CDCES, RDN, and other specialists as appropriate
- Programs should include four or more core CCM elements to maximize A1C reduction benefits
- Interventions must be tailored to subpopulations such as adults on intensive insulin or children/adolescents
- Health systems must foster a quality-oriented culture and utilize value-based payment models
- School professionals and caregivers must be engaged for pediatric patients to ensure safe management
- Care teams for adults on intensive insulin therapy must include clinicians experienced in advanced diabetes management, including technology use
- Pediatric care teams must include school professionals, behavioral health professionals (as needed), and parent(s)/caregiver(s)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fragmented delivery systems lacking clinical information capabilities
- Reactive care delivery models without coordinated longitudinal planning
- Case management and clinician education alone without integrated team approaches
- Low-risk Alternative Payment Models with limited financial risk assumption
- Interventions that do not address health-related social needs or community resources

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Patient-Centered Medical Home (PCMH) model for comprehensive primary care
- Accountable Care Organizations (ACOs) supporting CCM implementation
- Accountable Health Communities Model for identifying health-related social needs
- High-risk Alternative Payment Models (e.g., capitated payment) for improved care processes
- Integrated use of technology and advanced diabetes management expertise for complex cases
- Accountable Health Communities Model for addressing health-related social needs (with noted limitations in resource availability)


### DSMES integration in Standards of Care

Advise all people with diabetes to participate in developmentally and culturally appropriate DSMES to facilitate informed decision-making and self-care behaviors.. Provide DSMES at diagnosis, annually, when not meeting treatment goals, when complicating factors develop, and during transitions in life and care.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DSMES Integration in Standards of Care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Advise all people with diabetes to participate in developmentally and culturally appropriate DSMES to facilitate informed decision-making and self-care behaviors.
- Provide DSMES at diagnosis, annually, when not meeting treatment goals, when complicating factors develop, and during transitions in life and care.
- Use behavioral strategies such as motivational interviewing, goal setting, and problem-solving to support DSMES and engagement in health behaviors.
- Communicate DSMES participation with the diabetes care team and ensure culturally and socially appropriate delivery in group or individual settings.
- Assess and weigh social determinants of health (SDOH) to guide the design and delivery of DSMES, leveraging creative delivery options like telehealth.
- Assess and address barriers to DSMES participation at multiple levels (health system, payor, clinic, healthcare professional, and individual) to improve uptake and engagement.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Initial comprehensive health evaluation and assessment of comorbidities.
- Person-centered collaborative care guided by shared decision-making in treatment plan selection.
- Evaluation of medical and behavioral health outcomes, particularly during periods of changes in health and well-being.
- Consideration of the person's burden of treatment, level of self-efficacy, and degree of social and family support.
- Availability of qualified providers such as Diabetes Care and Education Specialists (DCES) or other team members with specialized clinical knowledge.
- Institutional leadership support is required for successful DSMES implementation, including advocacy and resource allocation.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Incorporation of digital health technologies (e.g., mobile apps, websites, digital coaching, SMS) as effective modes of DSMES intervention delivery.
- Explicit inclusion of community health workers, peer educators, peer support, and lay leaders in providing ongoing diabetes self-management support.
- Integration of SDOH screening and referrals to social needs support as part of a multilevel diabetes care intervention.
- Emphasis on ongoing, continuous DSMES rather than one-time occurrence, with a recommended duration of >10 hours over 6–12 months.
- Use of positive, strengths-based language to avoid judgmental words that may increase feelings of shame and guilt.
- Documented evidence that DSMES interventions with >10 hours over 6–12 months yield better outcomes, including A1C reduction and improved quality of life.


### DSMES reimbursement programs

DSMES reimbursement and telehealth eligibility. Medicare reimburses DSMES when services align with National Standards and are recognized by ADA or ADCES.. Medicare Part B initial DSMT is a 'once-in-a-lifetime' benefit covering up to 10 hours of individual and group training.. Two hours of follow-up DSMT are allowed annually after the initial benefit.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DSMES reimbursement and telehealth eligibility

**Core Recommendations:**
- Medicare reimburses DSMES when services align with National Standards and are recognized by ADA or ADCES.
- Medicare Part B initial DSMT is a 'once-in-a-lifetime' benefit covering up to 10 hours of individual and group training.
- Two hours of follow-up DSMT are allowed annually after the initial benefit.
- Programs recognized by ADA and accredited by ADCES are included on the list of telehealth professionals approved by CMS.
- As of October 1, 2025, Medicare recipients must be in a U.S.-based rural office or medical facility for most telehealth services.
- Ongoing support after formal DSMES is instrumental for improving outcomes and is reimbursable as part of follow-up DSMT (2 hours annually).
- Medicare Part B covers Medical Nutrition Therapy (MNT) separately, with 3 hours in the first year and up to 2 hours annually thereafter, with additional hours available for treatment changes.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Services must be provided in accordance with the National Standards for DSMES.
- Programs must be recognized by the American Diabetes Association through the Education Recognition Program.
- Programs must be recognized by the Association of Diabetes Care & Education Specialists (ADCES).
- For telehealth services, the patient must be located in a U.S.-based rural office or medical facility.
- Individual encounters must be billed within the first 10 hours for the initial benefit.
- For Medicare Part B MNT, additional hours beyond the standard benefit are available if a subsequent referral identifies a change in treatment.
- MNT must be delivered and billed by Registered Dietitian Nutritionists (RDNs) only.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Telehealth professionals approved by CMS via the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2023.
- Restriction requiring patients to be in a U.S.-based rural office or medical facility for most telehealth services as of October 1, 2025.
- Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2023 inclusion of ADA/ADCES-recognized programs on CMS telehealth professional approval lists.


### behavioral DSMES and weight management recommendations

Behavioral DSMES and Weight Management. Use behavioral strategies to support diabetes self-management education and support (DSMES) and engagement in positive health behaviors.. Provide overweight or obesity treatment plans including nutrition, physical activity, and behavioral health support to aim for at least 5–7% weight loss.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Behavioral DSMES and Weight Management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use behavioral strategies to support diabetes self-management education and support (DSMES) and engagement in positive health behaviors.
- Provide overweight or obesity treatment plans including nutrition, physical activity, and behavioral health support to aim for at least 5–7% weight loss.
- Counsel and monitor individuals pursuing intentional weight loss for adequate nutrition intake.
- Encourage increases in physical activity levels to meet activity guidelines.
- Screen for anxiety symptoms at least annually and refer for fear of hypoglycemia intervention when clinically appropriate.
- Provide or refer individuals for combination treatment for tobacco and e-cigarette/vaping cessation, including counseling and pharmacologic therapy

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- DSMES participation must be culturally and socially appropriate based on personal preferences and needs.
- DSMES engagement must be communicated with the diabetes care team.
- Screening for anxiety and fear of hypoglycemia should occur at least annually or when clinically appropriate.
- Referral to a qualified behavioral health professional is required if diabetes distress is not adequately addressed during the medical appointment.
- Comprehensive prefasting risk assessment must be used to generate a risk score for the safety of religious fasting.
- Screening for sleep health must be performed in people with diabetes and those at risk for diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Routine assessment and avoidance of tobacco and e-cigarette/vaping use with referral for combination treatment.
- Screening for sleep health in people with diabetes and those at risk for diabetes.
- Updated comprehensive prefasting risk assessment criteria for people with diabetes seeking to fast during Ramadan.
- Updated narrative discussion on the role of physical activity during obesity treatment.
- Recommendation for screening individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia for fear of hypoglycemia.
- Updated criteria for comprehensive prefasting risk assessment for people with diabetes seeking to fast during Ramadan (Table 5.3)


### anxiety management interventions

Anxiety management interventions in diabetes. Implement collaborative care models for adults with type 2 diabetes, depression, and anxiety to achieve clinically significant anxiety reduction.. Utilize Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) to reduce health anxiety, depressive symptoms, and diabetes distress in type 2 diabetes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Anxiety management interventions in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Implement collaborative care models for adults with type 2 diabetes, depression, and anxiety to achieve clinically significant anxiety reduction.
- Utilize Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) to reduce health anxiety, depressive symptoms, and diabetes distress in type 2 diabetes.
- Deploy diabetes technologies (real-time CGM, sensor-augmented pumps, AID) to reduce fear of hypoglycemia in type 1 diabetes independent of frequency reduction.
- Apply CBT-based interventions for young adults with type 1 diabetes to reduce fear of hypoglycemia and improve self-management behaviors.
- Ensure specialized behavioral interventions are delivered by qualified professionals as a positive adjunct to diabetes technology.
- Address injection-related anxiety in diabetes care plans using targeted behavioral interventions.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 2 diabetes with comorbid depression and anxiety for collaborative care protocols.
- Patient must have type 1 diabetes for technology-based fear of hypoglycemia interventions.
- Interventions must be delivered by qualified professionals.
- Evidence supports use in adults and young adults with specific anxiety profiles.
- Interventions should target injection-related anxiety, fear of hypoglycemia, or general anxiety associated with diabetes.
- Patient must have clinically significant anxiety symptoms (e.g., health anxiety, fear of hypoglycemia, or general anxiety) to qualify for CBT or collaborative care interventions.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Collaborative care models for anxiety management in type 2 diabetes.
- CBT as a primary intervention for health anxiety and diabetes distress.
- Real-time CGM, sensor-augmented pumps, and AID for reducing fear of hypoglycemia.
- CBT-based interventions specifically for young adults with type 1 diabetes.
- Integration of specialized behavioral interventions with diabetes technology.
- Integration of obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) symptom screening in diabetes care for patients with injection-related anxiety.


### diabetes transition care planning

Implement transition preparation programs beginning in early adolescence and at least 1 year before anticipated transfer.. Provide interprofessional support and resources for adolescents, young adults, and families prior to and during the transfer process.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes transition care planning

**Core Recommendations:**
- Implement transition preparation programs beginning in early adolescence and at least 1 year before anticipated transfer.
- Provide interprofessional support and resources for adolescents, young adults, and families prior to and during the transfer process.
- Engage in shared decision-making for the timing of transfer to an adult diabetes specialist without an age-specific cutoff.
- Utilize transition coordinators and technology to support communication and address young adult needs.
- Include a multidisciplinary team including CDEs, nurses, behavioral health professionals, dietitians, and social workers.
- Provide resources to enhance social and peer support during the transition process
- Include clinicians certified as diabetes care and education specialists (CDEs) in the transition team

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be an adolescent or young adult with type 1 or type 2 diabetes.
- Transfer must be planned collaboratively between the young person, caregivers, and pediatric specialists.
- Assessment of readiness, preferences, and concerns regarding self-management responsibilities.
- Recognition of the critical period of emerging adulthood involving major life transitions.
- Availability of resources to enhance social and peer support during the transition.
- Assessment of readiness, preferences, and concerns regarding self-management responsibilities must include evaluation of financial preparedness for healthcare costs post-transfer (e.g., insurance coverage, prescription costs)
- Prior to transfer, ensure the young person has demonstrated ability to schedule and attend medical appointments independently

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Relying solely on abrupt transfer without comprehensive planning beginning in early adolescence.
- Using a fixed age cutoff to determine transfer to adult diabetes care.
- Fragmented health care delivery lacking coordinated planning between pediatric and adult teams.
- Excluding behavioral health professionals and social workers from the transition team.
- Failing to address the shift in responsibility for self-management from parents to the young person.
- Assuming all adolescents are ready for transfer at a fixed age (e.g., 18 years) without individualized readiness assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of transition coordinators to facilitate the transfer process.
- Implementation of technology to support communication with young adults.
- Adoption of comprehensive transition tools developed by the Endocrine Society, ADA, and other organizations.
- Inclusion of emerging adults in the decision-making process regarding transfer timing.
- Focus on addressing psychosocial, emotional, and behavioral challenges specific to the transition period.
- Use of transition tools developed by the Endocrine Society, ADA, and other organizations to standardize transition planning
- Implementation of interventions to promote attendance at follow-up appointments and reduce hospitalizations during the transition period


### discharge communication protocols

Accurately and promptly communicate information on medication changes, pending tests, and follow-up needs to outpatient health care professionals.. Transmit discharge summaries to the primary care health care professional as soon as possible after discharge.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Discharge communication protocols

**Core Recommendations:**
- Accurately and promptly communicate information on medication changes, pending tests, and follow-up needs to outpatient health care professionals.
- Transmit discharge summaries to the primary care health care professional as soon as possible after discharge.
- Schedule follow-up appointments prior to discharge with patient agreement on time and place to increase attendance likelihood.
- Include patient/caregiver education on new medications, self-monitoring instructions, and warning signs requiring immediate attention in the discharge communication.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient has diabetes and is being discharged from an acute care setting.
- Outpatient health care professional is identified and available to receive the discharge summary.
- Patient agrees to the scheduled time and place for follow-up appointments.
- Patient or caregiver must demonstrate understanding of discharge instructions and follow-up plan before discharge.
- Outpatient healthcare professional must confirm receipt and review of the discharge summary within 48 hours of transmission.


### culturally and linguistically appropriate diabetes education

Health systems and professionals must develop or offer educational programs and materials in culturally appropriate languages for patients with limited English proficiency.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Culturally and linguistically appropriate diabetes education

**Core Recommendations:**
- Health systems and professionals must develop or offer educational programs and materials in culturally appropriate languages for patients with limited English proficiency.
- Professional language assistance (e.g., trained interpreters) must be provided to individuals with limited English proficiency or other communication needs at no cost.
- Use of untrained interpreters, including family members, should be avoided to prevent miscommunication or inaccurate information conveyance.
- Written materials should be in the patient's language and at a sixth-grade reading level or lower for accessibility.
- Healthcare professionals should improve cultural competency and address health literacy per National CLAS Standards.
- Health systems should integrate professional language assistance services as a standard of care for all patient interactions involving individuals with limited English proficiency or other communication needs.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients with limited English proficiency or other communication needs must be identified.
- Access to professional language assistance must be ensured at no cost to the patient.
- Culturally appropriate educational materials must be available in the patient's preferred language.
- Health literacy levels of patients must be assessed to tailor written materials appropriately.
- Healthcare professionals must adhere to National CLAS Standards for culturally and linguistically appropriate care.
- Healthcare professionals must assess patients' preferred language and communication needs at the point of care before delivering diabetes education.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of untrained interpreters, including family members, for conveying medical information.
- Relying solely on English-language educational materials for non-English-speaking patients.
- Assuming all patients have the same health literacy level without assessment.
- Using complex medical jargon in written materials without simplification.
- Ignoring cultural differences in diabetes education and care delivery.
- Relying on ad-hoc or informal translation methods (e.g., untrained staff, community volunteers) for conveying critical diabetes education materials.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of professional language assistance services as a standard of care for limited English proficiency patients.
- Development of culturally tailored diabetes education materials in multiple languages.
- Adoption of National CLAS Standards to guide culturally and linguistically appropriate care.
- Use of medical translation tools to address language barriers, with ongoing evaluation of their efficacy.
- Emphasis on health literacy assessment to ensure patient understanding of diabetes education.
- Health systems should evaluate and validate the efficacy of medical translation tools in diabetes care contexts before widespread adoption.


### diabetes psychosocial care guidelines

Screen all patients with diabetes for psychosocial distress, depression, anxiety, and disordered eating at diagnosis and annually, or when clinically indicated.. Integrate diabetes distress assessment into routine care using validated tools (e.g., PAID, T1-DDAS, T2-DDAS) to guide treatment planning.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes psychosocial care guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen all patients with diabetes for psychosocial distress, depression, anxiety, and disordered eating at diagnosis and annually, or when clinically indicated.
- Integrate diabetes distress assessment into routine care using validated tools (e.g., PAID, T1-DDAS, T2-DDAS) to guide treatment planning.
- Provide patient-centered communication that avoids stigmatizing language and fosters shared decision-making.
- Implement collaborative care models linking mental health professionals with diabetes care teams to address comorbid conditions.
- Offer evidence-based psychological interventions (e.g., CBT, mindfulness, motivational interviewing) tailored to patient needs and cultural context.
- Screen for disordered eating behaviors and eating disorders (e.g., binge eating, night eating syndrome) in patients with diabetes, particularly those with type 1 diabetes, using validated tools (e.g., mSCOFF, Binge Eating Scale, Night Eating Questionnaire) at diagnosis and annually.
- Assess fear of hypoglycemia using validated tools (e.g., Hypoglycemia Fear Survey-II) in patients with diabetes, especially those on insulin therapy, to guide targeted interventions.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diagnosis of type 1 or type 2 diabetes mellitus.
- Patient consent for psychosocial screening and intervention.
- Availability of trained mental health professionals or referral pathways.
- Access to validated psychosocial screening tools (e.g., PHQ-9, GAD-7, PAID).
- Integration of psychosocial care into multidisciplinary diabetes care plans.
- Training for healthcare providers in culturally tailored communication and interventions to address disparities in mental health outcomes among diverse patient populations.
- Access to digital mental health tools (e.g., telehealth platforms, mobile apps) for patients with limited access to in-person mental health services.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of unvalidated psychosocial screening tools without follow-up intervention.
- Dismissing diabetes distress as a normal part of living with diabetes without addressing it.
- Using punitive approaches to address non-adherence without exploring underlying psychosocial factors.
- Assuming all patients with diabetes require the same psychosocial interventions without individualized assessment.
- Delaying mental health referrals until severe symptoms or crises occur.
- Relying solely on unstructured or non-validated psychosocial screening tools without follow-up interventions or referrals.
- Assuming that all patients with diabetes will benefit equally from the same psychosocial interventions without individualized assessment of their specific needs and cultural context.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adoption of structured diabetes distress assessment tools (e.g., T1-DDAS, T2-DDAS) for routine screening.
- Integration of digital mental health interventions (e.g., telehealth, mobile apps) for accessible psychosocial support.
- Emphasis on culturally tailored psychosocial interventions to address disparities in mental health outcomes.
- Inclusion of family-focused interventions to support adolescents and young adults with diabetes.
- Use of automated insulin delivery systems to reduce diabetes distress related to glycemic variability.


### device education and training guidelines

Education and training recommendations are now device-specific (e.g., CGM, AID systems, CSII).. Healthcare professionals must provide tailored support for device initiation and use.. Schools and workplaces must accommodate device management (e.g., time for glucose monitoring, device adjustments).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Device education and training guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- Education and training recommendations are now device-specific (e.g., CGM, AID systems, CSII).
- Healthcare professionals must provide tailored support for device initiation and use.
- Schools and workplaces must accommodate device management (e.g., time for glucose monitoring, device adjustments).
- CGM use is recommended at diabetes onset and anytime thereafter for insulin users and those on hypoglycemia-inducing therapies.
- AID systems are the preferred insulin delivery method for type 1 diabetes and certain type 2 diabetes cases.
- Healthcare professionals should receive device-specific training to support patients effectively, including understanding operational complexities and failure modes of CGM, CSII, and AID systems.
- Education and training should address the unique risks associated with each device type, such as hypoglycemia management with AID systems and device integration into daily life.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Device initiation should not require C-peptide levels, islet autoantibodies, or duration of insulin treatment for CSII or AID.
- Individuals using CGM must also have access to blood glucose monitoring.
- Children and adolescents in school settings require support for diabetes technology use.
- Adults (≥18 years) in workplaces need reasonable accommodations for device management.
- AID systems may be considered for type 2 diabetes on basal insulin not meeting glycemic goals.
- Individuals using CGM must have access to blood glucose monitoring for calibration or confirmation of readings, even if CGM is the primary monitoring method.
- Children and adolescents in school settings require structured support for diabetes technology use, including training for school staff on device management and emergency protocols.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) is no longer discussed as a primary device type.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets are no longer emphasized for device initiation decisions.
- Intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) is no longer endorsed as a primary device type, though it may still be referenced as an older option.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Real-time CGM (rtCGM), over-the-counter CGM (OTC-CGM), and professional CGM are now the defined CGM device types.
- Recommendation 7.3a: Guidance for prescribing, initiating, and following CGM devices.
- Recommendation 7.3b: Guidance for prescribing, initiating, and following AID systems.
- Recommendation 7.8a: No prerequisites for CSII or AID initiation based on C-peptide, autoantibodies, or insulin duration.
- Recommendation 7.25a: AID systems are preferred for type 1 diabetes and select type 2 diabetes cases.
- Recommendation 7.15: CGM use is recommended at diabetes onset and anytime thereafter for insulin users, those on hypoglycemia-inducing therapies, and any diabetes treatment where CGM aids management, regardless of age, sex, education, income, or baseline diabetes characteristics.
- Recommendation 7.25a: AID systems are the preferred insulin delivery method for type 1 diabetes and for adults and children with type 2 diabetes on multiple daily injections, CSII, or sensor-augmented pump therapy, as well as other forms of insulin-deficient diabetes.


### diabetes care journal

Diabetes Care journal mission and scope. Promotes better patient care by serving the expanded needs of all health professionals committed to diabetes care. Increases knowledge, stimulates research, and promotes better management of people with diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes Care journal mission and scope

**Core Recommendations:**
- Promotes better patient care by serving the expanded needs of all health professionals committed to diabetes care
- Increases knowledge, stimulates research, and promotes better management of people with diabetes
- Publishes original research across multiple categories including Clinical Care, Epidemiology, Emerging Technologies, and Pathophysiology
- Disseminates ADA statements, consensus reports, review articles, and health news to clinically oriented professionals
- Targets physicians, researchers, epidemiologists, psychologists, diabetes educators, and other health professionals
- Publishes letters to the editor and points of view to foster dialogue among clinicians and researchers
- Provides a platform for ADA statements and consensus reports to disseminate official guidelines and recommendations

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Original research must focus on human studies
- Manuscripts must align with the journal's categories: Clinical Care/Education/Nutrition/Psychosocial Research, Epidemiology/Health Services Research, Emerging Technologies and Therapeutics, Pathophysiology/Complications, and Cardiovascular and Metabolic Risk

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of Cardiovascular and Metabolic Risk as a distinct research category


### diabetes population health systems

Systems to Support Diabetes Population Health. Tailoring treatment strategies to address social determinants of health (e.g., socioeconomic status, access to care, food security). Implementing structured care coordination across multidisciplinary teams (e.g., endocrinologists, primary care, community health workers)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Systems to Support Diabetes Population Health

**Core Recommendations:**
- Tailoring treatment strategies to address social determinants of health (e.g., socioeconomic status, access to care, food security)
- Implementing structured care coordination across multidisciplinary teams (e.g., endocrinologists, primary care, community health workers)
- Using data-driven approaches to identify high-risk patient cohorts for targeted interventions
- Integrating patient-reported outcomes and social risk screening into routine diabetes care
- Promoting shared decision-making with patients to align treatment plans with their social and cultural contexts
- Engaging family members or caregivers in diabetes care planning to address social and cultural contexts
- Implementing culturally tailored health literacy interventions to improve patient understanding of diabetes management

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Availability of electronic health records (EHRs) with population health management capabilities
- Access to community resources (e.g., nutrition programs, transportation assistance) to address social needs
- Established partnerships with local organizations (e.g., food banks, housing services) for referrals
- Trained staff (e.g., community health workers, social workers) to conduct social risk assessments
- Interoperable data systems to track patient outcomes and care gaps across settings
- Integration of diabetes care plans with local public health initiatives (e.g., diabetes prevention programs, vaccination campaigns)
- Availability of interpreter services or multilingual staff to support diverse patient populations

**Previous Recommendations:**
- One-size-fits-all diabetes management protocols without consideration of social context
- Reliance solely on clinical metrics (e.g., A1C) without addressing social barriers to care
- Fragmented care delivery models that do not coordinate between primary and specialty care
- Screening for social needs without follow-up interventions or resource linkages
- Passive patient education without active engagement in shared decision-making
- Routine hospitalization for glycemic control without addressing outpatient social determinants of health

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Standardized social risk screening tools (e.g., PRAPARE, THRIVE) integrated into EHR workflows
- Community-based diabetes self-management programs tailored to cultural and linguistic preferences
- Automated risk stratification algorithms to prioritize patients for intensive case management
- Use of telehealth and remote monitoring to bridge gaps in access to care for underserved populations
- Policy-level interventions (e.g., housing support, food assistance) as part of diabetes care plans
- Use of artificial intelligence-driven predictive analytics to forecast diabetes complications and resource needs
- Implementation of community health worker-led diabetes education programs in non-clinical settings (e.g., places of worship, community centers)

## Diabetes Screening and Management

This collection of guidelines and protocols addresses comprehensive diabetes screening, management, and complication prevention across diverse populations, emphasizing evidence-based practices for glycemic control, risk stratification, and early intervention.

**Key Features:**
- Diabetic retinopathy management: Prioritize glycemic, blood pressure, and lipid control to reduce progression, with initial dilated eye exams at 5 years for type 1 diabetes and at diagnosis for type 2 diabetes.
- Diabetes screening protocols: Use 3-year intervals for average-risk adults, shorter intervals for high-risk individuals, and universal screening for those >30 years.
- Pediatric diabetes screening: Screen overweight/obese children with additional risk factors after puberty or age 10, whichever is earlier.
- Diagnostic criteria for diabetes: Require confirmation via repeat testing (FPG, OGTT, A1C) unless symptomatic hyperglycemia or hyperglycemic crisis is present.
- ICMR type 2 diabetes guidelines: Emphasize individualized glycemic targets, lifestyle modifications as first-line therapy, and metformin as initial oral agent.
- Prediabetes screening guidelines: Use risk factor assessment or validated calculators for asymptomatic adults.
- Complication screening protocols: Monitor FPG/2hr PPG monthly, HbA1c every 6–12 months (3 months if targets unmet), and conduct clinical exams every 3 months.

**Notable Details:**
- ETDRS classification is research-focused; International Classification of Diabetic Retinopathy is recommended for clinical use.
- Avoid incretin-based therapies (GLP-1 RAs, DPP-4 inhibitors) in pancreatitis-related diabetes due to pancreatitis risk.
- ICMR guidelines revised based on the latest decade of type 2 diabetes management research and expert consensus.
- Panretinal photocoagulation (PRP) remains the gold standard for proliferative diabetic retinopathy with high-risk characteristics.
- Screen for diabetes 3–6 months post-acute pancreatitis and annually thereafter; annually for chronic pancreatitis.
- Severe non-proliferative retinopathy criteria include microaneurysms ≥ standard photograph 2A, cotton-wool spots, venous beading, or IRMA.
- Diabetes prevalence in India is rising in rural areas and among youth, with higher insulin resistance in the 'Asian Indian phenotype'.
- ICMR guidelines address the disproportionate impact of SARS-CoV-2 on people with diabetes in India.

Fundus photography is recommended for documenting DR status and monitoring progression over time.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic retinopathy management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Implement strategies to help people with diabetes reach glycemic, blood pressure, and lipid goals to reduce the risk or slow the progression of diabetic retinopathy.
- Perform initial dilated and comprehensive eye examinations based on diabetes type and duration (5 years after type 1 diabetes onset; at diagnosis for type 2 diabetes).
- Use retinal photography with remote reading or FDA-approved AI algorithms for screening, with pathways for timely referral to ophthalmologists.
- Refer individuals with diabetic macular edema, moderate or worse nonproliferative diabetic retinopathy, or proliferative diabetic retinopathy to an ophthalmologist.
- Use intravitreal anti-VEGF injections as first-line treatment for most diabetic macular edema involving the fovea and for proliferative diabetic retinopathy.
- Assess retinopathy status when intensifying glucose-lowering therapies (e.g., GLP-1 RAs) due to risk of initial worsening of retinopathy with rapid A1C reductions.
- Use macular focal/grid photocoagulation or intravitreal corticosteroid injections for persistent diabetic macular edema despite prior anti-VEGF therapy or in non-candidates for anti-VEGF.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Glycemic goals must be individualized based on patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, hypoglycemia risk).
- Blood pressure and lipid management should be optimized to reduce retinopathy progression.
- Retinal photography or AI screening requires validation and pathways for referral to comprehensive eye exams.
- Pregnant individuals with preexisting diabetes require pre-pregnancy eye exams and trimester-specific monitoring.
- Prompt management of infections and use of advanced therapies (e.g., WIFI staging) for diabetic foot complications.
- Retinopathy screening in children and adolescents with type 1 or type 2 diabetes (see section 14, 'Children and Adolescents').
- Adjust screening intervals based on specific risk factors (e.g., not meeting glycemic goals, advanced retinopathy, or diabetic macular edema) for more frequent examinations.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults.
- Reliance solely on retinal photography without pathways for comprehensive eye exams.
- Use of panretinal laser photocoagulation as the only treatment for proliferative diabetic retinopathy without considering anti-VEGF alternatives.
- Aspirin contraindication in individuals with retinopathy due to retinal hemorrhage risk.
- Monotherapy with laser for center-involved diabetic macular edema without considering anti-VEGF agents.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Expanded discussion of GLP-1 RA effects on eye health, including potential risks (e.g., mild worsening of retinopathy) and benefits (e.g., reduced intraocular pressure, lower glaucoma risk).
- FDA-approved AI platforms (AEYE-DS, EyeArt, LumineticsCore) for diabetic retinopathy screening.
- Use of intravitreal anti-VEGF agents as alternatives to panretinal laser photocoagulation for proliferative diabetic retinopathy.
- Emphasis on holistic, multifactorial approaches to eye health in diabetes, including adjunctive therapies.
- Inclusion of emerging technologies (e.g., self-monitoring foot temperatures) for early identification of diabetic foot complications.
- FDA pregnancy category C classification for anti-VEGF medications, warranting caution in pregnant individuals with diabetes due to theoretical fetal vascular risks.
- Use of fenofibrate in individuals with dyslipidemia to slow retinopathy progression, particularly with early diabetic retinopathy at baseline.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetic retinopathy screening and diagnostic work-up in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Screening for diabetic retinopathy (DR) is essential even in asymptomatic patients due to early microaneurysm presence.
- Fundus photography is recommended for documenting DR status and monitoring progression over time.
- Fundus fluorescein angiography (FFA) is used to identify hyperfluorescent (leaking microaneurysms/neovascularization) and hypofluorescent (ischemic/hemorrhagic) areas.
- Optical Coherence Tomography (OCT) is increasingly used for non-invasive evaluation of diabetic macular edema (DME), its severity, and treatment response.
- Ultra-wide field imaging (fundus photography and FFA) allows visualization of peripheral retinal changes.
- B-Scan Ultrasonography is included as part of the detailed investigative work-up for DR to assess complications like retinal detachment or vitreous hemorrhage.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Screening must be performed even in asymptomatic patients to detect early DR changes.
- FFA requires resuscitation equipment due to risks of dye extravasation and allergic reactions.
- FFA is contraindicated in patients with history of hypersensitivity to fluorescein.
- OCT is required for assessing DME severity and guiding treatment decisions.
- Portable, low-cost fundus photography tools are recommended for accessible screening.
- Resuscitation equipment must be available when performing FFA due to risks of dye extravasation and allergic reactions.

### Source 3: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic retinopathy screening and management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Record visual acuity and perform retinal examination via direct ophthalmoscopy in all diabetic patients
- Obtain retinal photography whenever possible
- Refer patients to a retina specialist for evaluation, treatment, and follow-up when services are available
- Educate patients on the risk of blindness from diabetes and the need for regular eye examinations and blood glucose control
- Compare retinal findings with standard DR images or grading charts if specialist services are unavailable
- Screen patients for other causes of visual loss (e.g., cataract, glaucoma, macular degeneration) alongside diabetic retinopathy assessment

**Prerequisites:**
- All diabetic patients must undergo baseline eye examination at diagnosis (Type 2) or within 5 years of diagnosis (Type 1)
- Pregnant women with diabetes require retinal examination prior to conception and during pregnancy, with early referral to a retina specialist
- Patients with long diabetes duration, poor glycemic control, hypertension, or nephropathy are at higher risk and require closer monitoring
- Visual acuity testing and intraocular pressure measurement should precede retinal examination
- Patients with impaired vision (<6/60) should undergo low vision evaluation and rehabilitation
- Vision testing and intraocular pressure measurement must precede retinal examination in all patients


### diabetic retinopathy classification

diabetic retinopathy classification in T1DM. ETDRS classification is complex, requires comparison with standard photographs, and is difficult to apply in clinical settings but best suited for research studies.. International Classification of Diabetic Retinopathy and Diabetic Macular Edema is recommended for simpler, clinically useful classification.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetic retinopathy classification in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- ETDRS classification is complex, requires comparison with standard photographs, and is difficult to apply in clinical settings but best suited for research studies.
- International Classification of Diabetic Retinopathy and Diabetic Macular Edema is recommended for simpler, clinically useful classification.
- DR prevalence in T1DM increases with disease duration (17% at <5 years to 97.5% at ≥15 years).
- Intensive glycemic control reduces DR risk by 76% and slows progression by 54%, with lasting benefits despite HbA1c equalization.
- DR burden is higher in T1DM compared to adult-onset T2DM (77.3% vs. 25.2% for any DR, 32.4% vs. 3.0% for PDR).
- ETDRS classification uses Modified Airlie House classification to label fundus picture areas from 1 to 7 for detailed grading of DR severity.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Fundus photography (e.g., four-field digital retinal color photography) is preferred for accurate DR diagnosis.
- Glycemic control (HbA1c targets) is critical for DR prevention and progression.
- Regular screening for DR is essential, especially in long-duration T1DM.
- Differential diagnosis must exclude retinal vein occlusion, ocular ischemic syndrome, sickle cell retinopathy, and radiation retinopathy.
- Use of standardized classification systems (ETDRS or International Classification) for consistent grading.
- Use of four-field digital retinal color photography is preferred for accurate DR diagnosis in Indian clinical settings, as demonstrated in studies like Rajalakshmi et al.


### diabetes screening protocols

Diabetes screening protocols and settings. Screening intervals are typically 3 years to balance false positives and timely detection of complications. Shorter screening intervals may be used for high-risk individuals (e.g., near-diagnostic cut points or on high-risk medications) diabetes screening protocols. Diabetes screening protocols in high-risk populations. Screening should be performed in all individuals >30 years of age.. Screening should be carried out at an earlier age in adults with risk factors (e.g., family history, overweight/obese, hypertension, dyslipidaemia).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes screening protocols and settings

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screening intervals are typically 3 years to balance false positives and timely detection of complications
- Shorter screening intervals may be used for high-risk individuals (e.g., near-diagnostic cut points or on high-risk medications)
- Screening should ideally occur within healthcare settings to ensure follow-up and treatment access
- Community screening is generally discouraged unless a robust referral system is established
- Dental settings may be explored for screening due to the bidirectional relationship between periodontal disease and diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening should be conducted in settings with established follow-up and treatment pathways
- High-risk individuals may require shorter screening intervals based on prior test results or medication use
- Referral systems must be in place for positive tests in community screening scenarios
- Screening in dental settings requires further research to confirm feasibility and cost-effectiveness
- Screening should target populations at higher risk of undiagnosed diabetes or prediabetes
- Screening in community settings requires an adequate referral system established beforehand for positive tests
- Community screening must target high-risk groups and avoid testing low-risk individuals or those already diagnosed

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Community screening without a referral system for positive tests
- Screening in settings lacking follow-up care pathways
- Routine screening in low-risk populations or those already diagnosed

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Exploration of dental settings for diabetes screening due to periodontal disease-diabetes link
- Consideration of shorter screening intervals for high-risk individuals
- Emphasis on healthcare setting-based screening for follow-up and treatment access

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes screening protocols in high-risk populations

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Screening should be performed in all individuals >30 years of age.
- Screening should be carried out at an earlier age in adults with risk factors (e.g., family history, overweight/obese, hypertension, dyslipidaemia).
- Screening can be done via fasting plasma glucose, OGTT, random plasma glucose, or HbA1c (with limitations in India).
- Retesting should be done after 3 years for normal glucose tolerance and annually for prediabetes.
- Diagnosis requires confirmation by a repeat test on a different day (unless unequivocal hyperglycemia).
- Screening should include assessment of cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk factors alongside diabetes screening.

**Prerequisites:**
- Age >30 years for general screening.
- Risk factors such as family history, overweight/obese, hypertension, or dyslipidaemia for earlier screening.
- Symptoms present for random plasma glucose testing.
- Confirmation of diagnosis via repeat testing unless unequivocal hyperglycemia.
- HbA1c limitations in India due to local constraints.
- History of gestational diabetes or macrosomia (birth weight > 3.5 kg) as a risk factor for earlier screening.
- History of polycystic ovarian syndrome and/or acanthosis nigricans as a risk factor for earlier screening.


### pediatric diabetes screening

Pediatric diabetes screening and risk-based screening protocol. Screen children and adolescents with overweight (BMI ≥85th to <95th percentile) or obesity (BMI ≥95th percentile) and one or more additional risk factors for diabetes after the onset of puberty or after 10 years of age, whichever occurs earlier. pediatric diabetes screening. Pediatric diabetes screening criteria. Screen children and adolescents aged 18 years and below who are overweight (weight >120% of ideal body weight).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetes screening and risk-based screening protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen children and adolescents with overweight (BMI ≥85th to <95th percentile) or obesity (BMI ≥95th percentile) and one or more additional risk factors for diabetes after the onset of puberty or after 10 years of age, whichever occurs earlier.
- Repeat screening at minimum 2-year intervals if normal, or more frequently if BMI is increasing.
- Use fasting plasma glucose, 2-h plasma glucose during a 75-g oral glucose tolerance test, elevated random glucose with symptoms of hyperglycemia, and/or A1C for testing prediabetes or type 2 diabetes in children and adolescents.
- Test pancreatic autoantibodies in children and adolescents with overweight or obesity and hyperglycemia to exclude autoimmune type 1 diabetes.
- Screen for tobacco/nicotine vaping, substance use, and alcohol use at diagnosis and regularly thereafter, with appropriate referrals to cessation programs as needed.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Children and adolescents must have overweight (BMI ≥85th to <95th percentile) or obesity (BMI ≥95th percentile) and at least one additional risk factor for diabetes.
- Screening should occur after the onset of puberty or after 10 years of age, whichever occurs earlier.
- A1C is not recommended for diagnosis in children with cystic fibrosis or symptoms suggestive of acute type 1 diabetes.
- Only A1C assays without interference should be used in children and adolescents with hemoglobinopathies.
- Comorbidities such as hypertension, dyslipidemia, albuminuria, neuropathy, and retinopathy should be assessed at diagnosis.
- A1C is not recommended for diagnosis in children and adolescents with cystic fibrosis or symptoms suggestive of acute type 1 diabetes.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Using fasting glucose alone for diabetes diagnosis in children and adolescents, as it may overdiagnose diabetes.
- Relying solely on A1C for diagnosis in certain high-risk pediatric populations due to limited validation in pediatric outcomes.
- Assuming A1C is universally superior to other diagnostic methods in all pediatric ethnic groups without consideration of context.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Incorporation of risk-based screening protocols tailored to pediatric populations with overweight or obesity and additional risk factors.
- Recommendation to test pancreatic autoantibodies in hyperglycemic children and adolescents to differentiate type 1 from type 2 diabetes.
- Emphasis on screening for tobacco/nicotine vaping, substance use, and alcohol use in pediatric diabetes care.
- Inclusion of comprehensive comorbidity assessment at diagnosis, including blood pressure, lipid panel, albumin-to-creatinine ratio, foot examination, and dilated eye examination.
- Discouragement of cannabis use in adolescents with diabetes due to risks of hyperglycemic ketosis-cannabis hyperemesis syndrome.
- Consideration of hyperglycemic ketosis-cannabis hyperemesis syndrome in adolescents with type 1 diabetes presenting with hyperglycemic emergencies (pH ≥7.4 and bicarbonate >15 mmol/L in the presence of ketosis).
- Routine discouragement of recreational cannabis use in all forms for adolescents with diabetes.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetes screening criteria

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Screen children and adolescents aged 18 years and below who are overweight (weight >120% of ideal body weight).
- Screen if any of the following risk factors are present: family history of type 2 diabetes in first-degree relatives, signs of insulin resistance (acanthosis nigricans), hypertension, dyslipidemia, or polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS).
- Screen children and adolescents with a history of maternal gestational diabetes during pregnancy.

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be aged 18 years or younger.
- Patient must be overweight (weight >120% of ideal body weight).
- Presence of at least one risk factor: family history of type 2 diabetes, acanthosis nigricans, hypertension, dyslipidemia, or PCOS.
- Screening should be performed using fasting plasma glucose, HbA1c, or oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) as per standard diagnostic criteria.


### diagnostic criteria for diabetes

Diagnosis requires confirmation via repeat testing in asymptomatic individuals using FPG, 2-h PG (OGTT), or A1C.. Random plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL (11.1 mmol/L) with classic hyperglycemia symptoms or hyperglycemic crisis is sufficient for diagnosis without repeat testing. diagnostic criteria for diabetes. Diagnostic criteria for diabetes and prediabetes. Diabetes diagnosis requires confirmation with a second test if using FPG ≥ 126 mg/dl, 2-hour OGTT ≥ 200 mg/dl, or HbA1c ≥ 6.5%. Symptoms of diabetes (e.g., polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, fatigue) plus casual plasma glucose ≥ 200 mg/dl can confirm diabetes without fasting

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diagnostic criteria for diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diagnosis requires confirmation via repeat testing in asymptomatic individuals using FPG, 2-h PG (OGTT), or A1C.
- Random plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL (11.1 mmol/L) with classic hyperglycemia symptoms or hyperglycemic crisis is sufficient for diagnosis without repeat testing.
- A1C testing is used to assess chronicity of hyperglycemia but is not diagnostic alone in asymptomatic individuals.
- FPG or A1C is preferred for routine screening due to ease of administration, while 2-h PG (OGTT) is more sensitive and recommended for high-risk conditions (e.g., cystic fibrosis-related diabetes).
- Carbohydrate intake of at least 150g for 3 days prior to OGTT is required to avoid false-positive results.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Classic symptoms of hyperglycemia (polyuria, polydipsia, unexplained weight loss) or hyperglycemic crisis for random plasma glucose diagnosis.
- Repeat testing required for confirmation in asymptomatic individuals regardless of test used.
- Nonpregnant individuals for routine screening with FPG or A1C.
- Mixed eating pattern with ≥150g carbohydrates for 3 days prior to OGTT.
- Prompt sample processing for glucose testing to avoid falsely low results due to glycolysis.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of single-test results without confirmation for diagnosis in asymptomatic individuals.
- Relying solely on A1C for diagnosis in conditions with altered red blood cell turnover (e.g., hemoglobinopathies).
- Carbohydrate restriction prior to OGTT due to risk of false-positive results.
- Use of OGTT without standardized carbohydrate intake preparation.
- Diagnosis based on isolated glucose measurements without clinical correlation.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- A1C, FPG, or 2-h PG criteria applicable for diagnosing diabetes in children and adolescents, with risk-based screening recommendations.
- OGTT preferentially recommended for high-risk conditions (e.g., cystic fibrosis-related diabetes, post-transplantation diabetes mellitus).
- Emphasis on prompt glucose sample processing to prevent glycolysis-related falsely low results.
- Standardized carbohydrate intake (≥150g for 3 days) prior to OGTT to ensure accurate results.
- Incorporation of A1C testing to assess chronicity of hyperglycemia in symptomatic individuals.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diagnostic criteria for diabetes and prediabetes

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Diabetes diagnosis requires confirmation with a second test if using FPG ≥ 126 mg/dl, 2-hour OGTT ≥ 200 mg/dl, or HbA1c ≥ 6.5%
- Symptoms of diabetes (e.g., polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, fatigue) plus casual plasma glucose ≥ 200 mg/dl can confirm diabetes without fasting
- Prediabetes is defined by IFG (FPG 100-125 mg/dl), IGT (2-hour OGTT 140-199 mg/dl), or HbA1c 5.7-6.4%
- Random plasma glucose 140-199 mg/dl warrants OGTT for further evaluation
- Testing requires normal diet (≥200g carbohydrate/day for 3 days), overnight fast (8-10 hours), and standardized OGTT protocol (75g glucose load)
- More than half of all patients with diabetes may be asymptomatic, requiring laboratory confirmation for diagnosis

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be on a normal diet (≥200g carbohydrate/day) for at least 3 days prior to testing
- Fasting plasma glucose testing requires an 8-10 hour overnight fast
- OGTT requires 75g glucose load dissolved in ≥250ml water, consumed within 5 minutes
- Second blood sample collected exactly 2 hours post-glucose load with patient resting and refraining from smoking/eating
- Confirmation of diabetes diagnosis with a second test if using FPG, OGTT, or HbA1c criteria
- Osmotic symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia), weight loss despite polyphagia, tiredness, generalized pruritus, recurrent urogenital infections, or delayed wound healing must be present alongside casual plasma glucose ≥ 200 mg/dl to confirm diabetes without fasting

Presence of at least one microaneurysm. Hemorrhages/microaneurysms ≥ standard photograph 2A. Cotton-wool spots, venous beading, or IRMA definitely present. Criteria not met for more severe retinopathy. Total number of prepubertal years contributing to DR risk

**Core Management Principles:**
- Presence of at least one microaneurysm
- Hemorrhages/microaneurysms ≥ standard photograph 2A
- Cotton-wool spots, venous beading, or IRMA definitely present

- Poor glycemic control

### prediabetes screening guidelines

Screening for prediabetes and type 2 diabetes risk should be done in asymptomatic adults using risk factor assessment or validated risk calculators.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Prediabetes screening guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screening for prediabetes and type 2 diabetes risk should be done in asymptomatic adults using risk factor assessment or validated risk calculators.
- Testing should be considered in adults of any age with overweight/obesity and one or more risk factors (e.g., first-degree relative with diabetes, high-risk race/ethnicity, hypertension, dyslipidemia, polycystic ovary syndrome).
- Screening should begin at age 35 for all adults without risk factors.
- Repeat screening is recommended at minimum 3-year intervals, sooner with symptoms or risk changes (e.g., weight gain).
- Screening should be considered in children/adolescents with overweight/obesity and additional risk factors (e.g., maternal history of diabetes/GDM, family history of type 2 diabetes).
- Screening for prediabetes/diabetes should be considered in adults of any age with overweight/obesity and one or more risk factors (e.g., first-degree relative with diabetes, high-risk race/ethnicity, hypertension, dyslipidemia, polycystic ovary syndrome).
- Screening should begin at no later than age 35 years for all adults without risk factors.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Asymptomatic adults require risk factor assessment or validated risk calculator for screening.
- Adults with overweight/obesity (BMI ≥25 kg/m² or ≥23 kg/m² in Asian ancestry) must have one or more risk factors for testing.
- Children/adolescents must have overweight (BMI ≥85th percentile) or obesity (BMI ≥95th percentile) plus one or more risk factors.
- People with prediabetes (A1C 5.7–6.4%, IGT, or IFG) should be tested annually.
- People with a history of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) should be tested every 1–3 years.
- For OGTT screening, adequate carbohydrate intake (at least 150 g/day) should be assured for 3 days prior to testing.
- Screening for prediabetes/diabetes in people with HIV should use plasma glucose criteria (FPG preferred) due to A1C underestimation in this population.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fasting plasma glucose (FPG) as the sole screening method for glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia (postprandial or random glucose preferred).
- Reliance on A1C alone for screening in people with HIV (plasma glucose criteria preferred due to underestimation).
- Screening intervals based solely on fixed numeric targets without consideration of risk changes.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Screening for prediabetes/diabetes in people on high-risk medications (e.g., statins, thiazide diuretics, some HIV medications, second-generation antipsychotics).
