- For second-generation antipsychotics, screen at baseline, 12–16 weeks post-initiation, and annually thereafter.
- For people with HIV, screen with FPG before/during antiretroviral therapy (ART) and 3–6 months after ART initiation/switch; annual FPG if normal.
- Monitor postprandial or random glucose in people on long-term glucocorticoids (not fasting glucose).
- Use ADA risk test or similar tools to guide screening decisions.
- Screening for prediabetes/diabetes should be performed in people treated with glucocorticoids, statins, thiazide diuretics, some HIV medications, and second-generation antipsychotic medications due to increased risk of glucose abnormalities.
- For people on glucocorticoids, check postprandial or random glucose levels (not fasting glucose) due to glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia typically resulting from insulin resistance.


### diabetes epidemiology

diabetes epidemiology and risk profile in India. Diabetes is no longer confined to older, affluent, or urban populations in India. Rural prevalence of diabetes is increasing rapidly. Type 2 diabetes is now affecting children and adolescents. The 'Asian Indian phenotype' shows higher insulin resistance and diabetes prevalence for any given BMI compared to white Caucasians diabetes epidemiology. diabetes epidemiology in young Indians. Increasing burden of diabetes in children and adolescents in India. Type 1 diabetes is the commonest form in children and adolescents. Type 2 diabetes is becoming more common in children. MODY and FCPD may be misdiagnosed as type 1 diabetes in younger patients

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetes epidemiology in young Indians

**Core Management Principles:**
- Increasing burden of diabetes in children and adolescents in India
- Type 1 diabetes is the commonest form in children and adolescents
- Type 2 diabetes is becoming more common in children
- MODY and FCPD may be misdiagnosed as type 1 diabetes in younger patients
- Improved awareness and early diagnosis are contributing to rising type 1 diabetes detection
- Diabetic ketoacidosis remains a significant problem in rural and peripheral healthcare centers despite improvements in urban management

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Accurate differential diagnosis to distinguish type 1 diabetes from MODY, FCPD, and other forms
- Access to precise blood glucose monitoring tools for children with type 1 diabetes
- Early diagnosis and treatment to reduce diabetic ketoacidosis in rural and peripheral centers
- Equitable access to advanced diabetes care tools (e.g., CGM, precise glucometers) across rural and peripheral centers to reduce disparities in type 1 diabetes management

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetes epidemiology and risk profile in India

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Diabetes is no longer confined to older, affluent, or urban populations in India
- Rural prevalence of diabetes is increasing rapidly
- Type 2 diabetes is now affecting children and adolescents
- The 'Asian Indian phenotype' shows higher insulin resistance and diabetes prevalence for any given BMI compared to white Caucasians
- Type 2 diabetes accounts for 90-95% of all diabetes cases in India

**Prerequisites:**
- Use ICMR-INDIAB study data for state-wise and urban-rural prevalence assessment
- Classify diabetes into Type 1, Type 2, gestational, and other types for accurate diagnosis
- Differentiate Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes based on clinical features (e.g., age at diagnosis, family history, insulin dependence)
- Differentiate Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes based on clinical features including age at diagnosis, family history, and insulin dependence
- Classify diabetes into Type 1, Type 2, gestational, and other types (e.g., monogenic, pancreatic, drug-induced) for accurate diagnosis


### ICMR guideline revision

ICMR guideline revision process. Guidelines revised based on latest developments in type 2 diabetes management over the past decade. Expert group constituted by ICMR to review and modify old guidelines. Document circulated for feedback from extended group of researchers and practitioners. Final guidelines published after multiple rounds of iteration and expert workshop

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ICMR guideline revision process

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Guidelines revised based on latest developments in type 2 diabetes management over the past decade
- Expert group constituted by ICMR to review and modify old guidelines
- Document circulated for feedback from extended group of researchers and practitioners
- Final guidelines published after multiple rounds of iteration and expert workshop
- Feedback solicited from readers for future guideline improvements

**Prerequisites:**
- Expert group formation by ICMR for guideline revision
- Multiple rounds of iteration and expert review
- Workshop held to finalize changes (ICMR Guidelines for Management of Type 2 Diabetes 2018)
- Document placement on ICMR website for public feedback
- Ratification by extended group of researchers and practitioners
- Ratification of final guidelines by circulation to an extended group of researchers and practitioners drawn from all over India


### pancreatic diabetes management

Screen for diabetes within 3–6 months following acute pancreatitis and annually thereafter; screen annually for chronic pancreatitis. Avoid incretin-based therapies (GLP-1 RAs, dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs, DPP-4 inhibitors) in pancreatitis-related diabetes due to pancreatitis risk

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pancreatic diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for diabetes within 3–6 months following acute pancreatitis and annually thereafter; screen annually for chronic pancreatitis
- Avoid incretin-based therapies (GLP-1 RAs, dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs, DPP-4 inhibitors) in pancreatitis-related diabetes due to pancreatitis risk
- Initiate insulin therapy early in pancreatic diabetes to achieve glycemic goals
- Use OGTT for posttransplantation diabetes mellitus (PTDM) diagnosis due to immunosuppressant effects
- Implement interprofessional team-based management for posttransplantation diabetes
- Consider screening individuals with acute and chronic pancreatitis for exocrine pancreatic insufficiency by measuring fecal elastase
- Use insulin pump therapy, including automated insulin delivery (AID) systems, for cystic fibrosis–related diabetes when appropriate

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Documented episode of acute pancreatitis or diagnosis of chronic pancreatitis
- Evidence of pancreatic exocrine insufficiency (e.g., low fecal elastase) or pathological pancreatic imaging
- Absence of type 1 diabetes-associated autoimmunity
- Pretransplant OGTT for diabetes risk assessment in transplant candidates
- eGFR ≥45 mL/min/1.73m² for metformin initiation
- eGFR ≥30 mL/min/1.73m² for metformin continuation (discontinue if eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73m²)
- Stable kidney function for sulfonylurea use in early postoperative periods posttransplantation

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets without individualized consideration
- Use of incretin-based therapies in pancreatitis-related diabetes
- Reliance on A1C or fasting plasma glucose alone for PTDM diagnosis
- Passive lifestyle interventions without behavior therapy components
- Sulfonylureas as first-line therapy in early postoperative periods posttransplantation

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Annual diabetes screening post-acute pancreatitis and in chronic pancreatitis
- Early insulin initiation in pancreatic diabetes management
- OGTT as preferred diagnostic tool for PTDM
- Consideration of islet autotransplantation in selected pancreatectomy patients
- Use of GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors in PTDM with caution for UTI risk
- Perform early OGTT at 3 months posttransplantation and late OGTT at 1 year and onward for PTDM diagnosis
- Consider DPP-4 inhibitors in the immediate posttransplant period for mild hyperglycemia or impaired glucose tolerance due to potential to decrease progression to PTDM


### laser photocoagulation in diabetic retinopathy

Grid/focal laser photocoagulation is primarily used for diabetic macular edema (DME) but indications are diminishing due to pharmacotherapy availability.. Panretinal photocoagulation (PRP) is the gold standard for proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR) with high-risk characteristics.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** laser photocoagulation in diabetic retinopathy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Grid/focal laser photocoagulation is primarily used for diabetic macular edema (DME) but indications are diminishing due to pharmacotherapy availability.
- Panretinal photocoagulation (PRP) is the gold standard for proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR) with high-risk characteristics.
- PRP involves administration of 1200-2000 burns in 1-2 visits, with retreatment possible if vessel regression is incomplete.
- Focal/grid laser is a one-off treatment that can be repeated if needed, though its use is diminishing in favor of intravitreal VEGF/steroid injections.
- Intravitreal VEGF injections are increasingly preferred for DME and PDR due to higher efficacy and lower collateral retinal damage.
- PRP may cause transient loss of central vision due to macular edema as a side effect.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- PRP is indicated for PDR with high-risk characteristics or severe non-proliferative DR when close follow-up is not feasible.
- Focal/grid laser is indicated for DME, with preference in pregnancy.
- Intravitreal VEGF injections may be used in combination with PRP (one week before or immediately before) to reduce DME.
- Laser treatment is non-invasive for PRP and one-off for focal/grid, with retreatment allowed if necessary.
- PRP is preferred for PDR or severe NPDR when close follow-up is not feasible.


### complication screening protocols

Monitor blood glucose (FPG and 2 hr PPPG) at least once a month, more frequently if targets are not met. Measure HbA1c at least every 6-12 months, every 3 months if targets are not met or tight control is attempted. Perform clinical examination during every visit, minimum every 3 months

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** complication screening protocols

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Monitor blood glucose (FPG and 2 hr PPPG) at least once a month, more frequently if targets are not met
- Measure HbA1c at least every 6-12 months, every 3 months if targets are not met or tight control is attempted
- Perform clinical examination during every visit, minimum every 3 months
- Screen for long-term complications (retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy, PVD, CAD) at least annually, more often if needed
- Optimize weight, waist circumference, blood pressure, and lipids

**Prerequisites:**
- Dilated retinal examination by trained ophthalmologist at diagnosis and annually thereafter
- Kidney function assessment at diagnosis and annually using urine albumin-creatinine ratio, serum creatinine, and eGFR
- Comprehensive foot examination at diagnosis and annually focusing on neuropathy and peripheral arterial disease
- Urine examination for ketones if blood glucose exceeds 400 mg/dl
- Urine examination for estimation of ketones if blood glucose exceeds 400 mg/dl


### diabetes distress screening

Diabetes distress screening and management. Screen for diabetes distress at least annually in people with diabetes, caregivers, and family members, and repeat screening when treatment goals are not met, at transitional times, or in the presence of diabetes complications diabetes distress screening. Diabetes distress screening

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes distress screening and management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for diabetes distress at least annually in people with diabetes, caregivers, and family members, and repeat screening when treatment goals are not met, at transitional times, or in the presence of diabetes complications
- Address diabetes distress by initiating conversations about feelings and beliefs in a respectful, sensitive, and direct manner, validating emotional aspects, and linking emotions to self-management behaviors
- Use diabetes-specific validated measures appropriate for each person or population (e.g., age and diabetes type) for routine monitoring of diabetes distress
- Introduce brief coping strategies (e.g., values affirmation, mindful breathing, cognitive reframing) and highlight past successes to reinforce resilience
- Refer to qualified behavioral health professionals, ideally with diabetes expertise, for further assessment and treatment if distress is not adequately addressed during medical appointments
- Counseling about expected diabetes distress during new diagnoses, treatment changes, or life stressors
- Use of digital interventions (e.g., smartphone apps, video visits) to reduce diabetes distress

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes distress screening is required at least annually, during transitions, or when treatment goals are not met
- Screening should be performed using validated, diabetes-specific measures tailored to age and diabetes type
- Referral to behavioral health professionals is required if distress is not adequately addressed during routine care
- Interventions should be tailored to the individual's life stage and level of diabetes progression
- Screening should include caregivers and family members of people with diabetes
- Assessment of intentional omission or underdosing of insulin/noninsulin medication to cause weight loss before addressing distress
- Evaluation of disordered eating behaviors or disrupted eating patterns prior to standard distress screening

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of non-diabetes-specific screening tools for diabetes distress
- Assuming diabetes distress is equivalent to depression or anxiety without further assessment
- Ignoring diabetes distress in routine care despite its high prevalence and impact on outcomes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of brief coping strategies (e.g., values affirmation, mindful breathing) into routine diabetes care
- Use of digital interventions (e.g., smartphone apps, video visits) to reduce diabetes distress
- Counseling about expected diabetes distress during new diagnoses, treatment changes, or life stressors
- Evidence supporting DSMES, CBT, mindfulness-based therapies, and motivational interviewing for reducing diabetes distress
- AID systems may reduce diabetes distress in adults with type 1 diabetes and caregivers of children with type 1 diabetes
- Use of AID systems to reduce diabetes distress in adults with type 1 diabetes and caregivers of children with type 1 diabetes
- Integration of mindfulness-based therapies and mindful self-compassion training into diabetes care

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes distress screening


### diabetes-associated cognitive decline

Diabetes-associated cognitive decline and dementia risk. Diabetes significantly increases risk of all-cause dementia (43%), Alzheimer's dementia (43%), and vascular dementia (91%)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes-associated cognitive decline and dementia risk

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetes significantly increases risk of all-cause dementia (43%), Alzheimer's dementia (43%), and vascular dementia (91%)
- Diabetes-related dementia is distinct: slow progression, no typical Alzheimer/vascular neuroimaging findings, high A1C, long diabetes duration, insulin use, frailty, and sarcopenia/dynapenia
- Higher A1C correlates with lower cognitive function; intensive glycemic control (<6.0–6.5%) shows no cognitive benefit vs standard control
- Severe hypoglycemia is linked to cognitive decline and dementia; recurrent episodes increase psychomotor/mental function decline risk
- Cognitive capacity affects diabetes self-management (attention, memory, executive function) and requires routine assessment
- Episodes of severe hypoglycemia are independently associated with cognitive decline and are a risk factor for accelerated decline
- Early-onset type 1 diabetes is associated with long-term deficits in intellectual abilities, especially when accompanied by repeated episodes of severe hypoglycemia

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Presence of diabetes or prediabetes for risk stratification
- Documented cognitive impairment or dementia diagnosis
- Assessment of hypoglycemia history and A1C levels
- Evaluation of self-management ability and need for caregiver support
- Consideration of developmental stage for children/adolescents
- Assessment of developmental stage for children/adolescents to determine self-management capacity and need for caregiver support
- Evaluation of diabetes-specific factors (e.g., younger age at diagnosis, longer disease duration, recurrent DKA, microvascular complications) in type 1 diabetes for cognitive decline risk stratification

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Intensive glycemic management (A1C <6.0–6.5%) for improving cognitive outcomes
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized adjustment
- Reliance on standard neuroimaging alone for diabetes-related dementia diagnosis
- Unmonitored complex treatment plans for individuals with cognitive decline
- Assumption of consistent cognitive decline trajectory
- Reliance on fixed A1C targets (e.g., <6.0–6.5%) without individualized adjustment for cognitive outcomes in type 2 diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recognition of diabetes-related dementia as a distinct clinical entity
- Routine cognitive capacity assessment in diabetes care, including executive function evaluation
- Introduction of lay care professionals for daily monitoring in cognitively impaired patients
- GLP-1 receptor agonists associated with reduced dementia risk in meta-analysis
- Exercise as a potential nonpharmacologic treatment for cognitive impairment in older adults with type 2 diabetes
- GLP-1 receptor agonists are associated with a statistically significant reduction in dementia risk compared to control therapy in meta-analysis
- Exercise is a potential nonpharmacologic treatment pathway for cognitive impairment in older adults with type 2 diabetes


### peripheral artery disease screening in diabetes

Peripheral artery disease (PAD) screening in diabetes. Screen all people with diabetes aged ≥50 years using ankle-brachial index (ABI) and repeat every 5 years if normal

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Peripheral artery disease (PAD) screening in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen all people with diabetes aged ≥50 years using ankle-brachial index (ABI) and repeat every 5 years if normal
- Perform initial PAD screening with history of leg fatigue, claudication, rest pain, and physical examination including lower-extremity pulses, capillary refill, rubor on dependency, pallor on elevation, and venous filling time
- Use toe systolic blood pressure (<30 mmHg) for more accurate PAD diagnosis in people with diabetes due to noncompressible vessels affecting ABI accuracy
- Refer individuals with abnormal pulse volume recordings and toe pressures <30 mmHg with foot ulcers for formal vascular evaluation and angiography
- Intensify pharmacologic therapies (e.g., LDL-lowering, low-dose rivaroxaban, GLP-1 RA) in people with diabetes and PAD to improve cardiovascular and limb outcomes
- Assess individuals' understanding of foot care education and their physical ability to conduct proper foot surveillance and care, with assistance from family members or caregivers if needed
- Use advanced wound therapy (e.g., topical oxygen therapy) for chronic DFUs failing standard care after 4 weeks of appropriate wound management (50% reduction in wound size not achieved)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes diagnosis with age ≥50 years
- Presence of microvascular disease, clinical foot complications, or end-organ damage from diabetes
- Duration of diabetes ≥10 years
- History of smoking, hypertension, dyslipidemia, or prior cardiovascular disease
- Symptoms of leg fatigue, claudication, or rest pain, or abnormal physical examination findings
- Presence of microvascular disease (e.g., neuropathy, retinopathy) as a risk factor for adverse outcomes in PAD
- History of prior cardiovascular disease (CVD) as a risk factor for PAD in diabetes

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on classical claudication symptoms for PAD diagnosis due to high prevalence of asymptomatic PAD in diabetes
- Use of ABI alone without corroborating clinical findings or toe pressures in people with diabetes
- Routine screening for PAD without subsequent intensification of pharmacologic therapies
- Self-care education alone without reinforcement or assessment of patient understanding and physical ability
- Use of canagliflozin in individuals with significant lower-limb complications due to increased risk of amputation
- Routine use of hyperbaric oxygen therapy for nonhealing DFUs without ischemia and/or infection due to lack of robust supporting evidence

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Incorporation of toe systolic blood pressure (<30 mmHg) as a more accurate diagnostic tool for PAD in diabetes
- Recommendation for screening all people with diabetes aged ≥50 years using non-invasive arterial studies, with repeat screening every 5 years if normal
- Use of GLP-1 RA agents (e.g., semaglutide) for PAD management in diabetes, showing benefits in reducing major adverse limb events and improving walking capacity
- Adoption of the Wound Ischemia foot Infection (WIFI) staging system for diabetic foot lesions to stage PAD severity and predict DFU healing
- Implementation of advanced wound therapies (e.g., topical oxygen therapy) for chronic DFUs failing standard care, with preference for grade A evidence-based products
- Adoption of smart mats, smart insoles, or socks for self-monitoring of foot temperatures as part of an interactive prevention protocol for early identification of impending ulceration
- Use of FDA-approved living bioengineered skin substitutes (e.g., Apligraf, Dermagraft) and growth factor products (e.g., becaplermin) for advanced wound care in chronic DFUs


### immune checkpoint inhibitor-induced diabetes

Immune checkpoint inhibitor-induced diabetes + PI3K/mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia + glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia. ICI-induced diabetes presents as rapid-onset, severe hyperglycemia or DKA, requiring lifelong insulin therapy due to irreversible β-cell destruction.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Immune checkpoint inhibitor-induced diabetes + PI3K/mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia + glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia

**Core Recommendations:**
- ICI-induced diabetes presents as rapid-onset, severe hyperglycemia or DKA, requiring lifelong insulin therapy due to irreversible β-cell destruction.
- PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia occurs within 2 weeks, with risk factors including preexisting dysglycemia, BMI >25 kg/m², and age >65 years; metformin is first-line, with pioglitazone or SGLT2 inhibitors as alternatives.
- mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia is managed with metformin as first-line, pioglitazone as second-line, and insulin reserved for refractory cases.
- Glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia timing and extent vary by dose, duration, and route; insulin is most frequently used, with sulfonylureas/meglitinides for type 2 diabetes or no prior diagnosis.
- ICI treatment should not be discontinued for severe hyperglycemia, as β-cell destruction is irreversible.
- ICI-induced diabetes is most common after exposure to PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitors (e.g., nivolumab, pembrolizumab, durvalumab, avelumab) and less commonly with CTLA-4 inhibitors (e.g., ipilimumab).
- Hyperglycemia due to ICIs can occur at any time after initiation, ranging from 1 week to up to 12 months after the first dose.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- ICI-induced diabetes requires confirmation of insulinopenia for prandial insulin initiation.
- PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia risk factors include preexisting dysglycemia, BMI >25 kg/m², and age >65 years.
- Glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia management must account for timing and extent of glucose elevations, not just morning monitoring.
- PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia management prioritizes metformin, with pioglitazone or SGLT2 inhibitors as alternatives due to potential ketoacidosis risk.
- mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia management prioritizes metformin, with pioglitazone as second-line and insulin reserved for refractory cases.
- Confirmation of insulinopenia is required before initiating prandial insulin for ICI-induced diabetes.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Sulfonylureas and insulin as first-line for PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia due to potential reactivation of PI3K pathway.
- GLP-1 RAs for PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia due to uncertain effects on PI3K inhibitor efficacy and potential nausea/vomiting.
- Fixed A1C targets for glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia without considering timing and extent of glucose elevations.
- Discontinuation of ICI therapy for severe hyperglycemia, as β-cell destruction is irreversible.
- Routine morning glucose monitoring alone for glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia without accounting for afternoon/evening peaks.
- Sulfonylureas and insulin as first-line agents for PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia due to potential reactivation of the PI3K pathway.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Lifelong insulin therapy is generally required for ICI-induced diabetes due to irreversible β-cell destruction.
- Basal insulin initiation should be considered for ICI-induced hyperglycemia with blood glucose >250 mg/dL.
- Metformin is first-line for PI3K and mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia due to efficacy and safety.
- Pioglitazone may be considered as second-line for PI3K or mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia.
- Insulin is the most frequently used agent for glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia, with sulfonylureas/meglitinides for type 2 diabetes or no prior diagnosis.
- Pioglitazone may be considered as monotherapy or in combination with metformin for PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia, though its slow onset of action may limit effectiveness.
- GLP-1 RAs are not recommended for PI3K inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia due to uncertain effects on PI3K inhibitor efficacy and potential adverse effects (e.g., nausea, vomiting).


### housing insecurity impact on diabetes self-management

Directly associated with reduced ability to maintain appropriate diabetes self-management. Coexists with food insecurity, lack of health insurance, cognitive impairment, behavioral health concerns, and low literacy/numeracy skills

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Housing insecurity impact on diabetes self-management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Directly associated with reduced ability to maintain appropriate diabetes self-management
- Coexists with food insecurity, lack of health insurance, cognitive impairment, behavioral health concerns, and low literacy/numeracy skills
- Requires secure storage for diabetes medications and supplies, including refrigerator access for insulin
- Assessed using validated brief risk assessment tools (e.g., for veterans)
- Healthcare professionals should connect patients to housing resources or social workers to improve diabetes care
- Prevalence of diabetes among people experiencing housing insecurity is estimated to be around 8% in the U.S.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have diabetes and experience housing insecurity
- Assessment of coexisting barriers (e.g., food insecurity, insurance status)
- Access to secure medication storage and refrigeration for insulin
- Use of validated housing insecurity risk assessment tools
- Availability of social work or housing support resources
- Healthcare professionals must be familiar with local resources to support housing-insecure individuals or have access to social workers

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of housing insecurity risk assessment into diabetes care workflows
- Routine screening for housing insecurity in diabetes management plans
- Collaboration with social workers to address housing as part of diabetes care
- Provision of secure medication storage solutions for housing-insecure patients
- Education on insulin storage requirements for patients with unstable housing


### sexual dysfunction screening guideline

Sexual dysfunction screening in diabetes. Screen men with diabetes/prediabetes for erectile dysfunction (ED), prioritizing those with high cardiovascular risk, retinopathy, CVD, CKD, neuropathy, long diabetes duration, depression, hypogonadism, or suboptimal glycemic control.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Sexual dysfunction screening in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen men with diabetes/prediabetes for erectile dysfunction (ED), prioritizing those with high cardiovascular risk, retinopathy, CVD, CKD, neuropathy, long diabetes duration, depression, hypogonadism, or suboptimal glycemic control.
- Inquire about sexual health (e.g., low libido, ED) in men with diabetes/prediabetes; screen for hypogonadism with morning serum total testosterone if symptoms/signs are present.
- In women with diabetes/prediabetes, screen for desire, arousal, and orgasm difficulties, particularly in those with depression/anxiety or recurrent UTIs.
- Evaluate cardiovascular and endocrine risk factors in men presenting with ED, including glycemic assessment, lipid profile, and morning testosterone levels.
- Encourage lifestyle interventions (e.g., Mediterranean diet, exercise, smoking cessation) and glycemic optimization to improve sexual function in women with diabetes.
- In men with diabetes/prediabetes, screen for ED in those with hypertension, dyslipidemia, metabolic syndrome, obesity, or use of medications such as antidepressants and opioids.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Men with diabetes/prediabetes and high cardiovascular risk, retinopathy, CVD, CKD, neuropathy, long diabetes duration, depression, hypogonadism, or suboptimal glycemic control must be screened for ED.
- Symptoms/signs of hypogonadism in men with diabetes/prediabetes require morning serum total testosterone screening.
- Women with diabetes/prediabetes and depression/anxiety or recurrent UTIs should be screened for sexual dysfunction.
- Men newly presenting with ED require glycemic assessment, lipid profile, and morning total/free testosterone if indicated.
- Women with diabetes/prediabetes should have psychosocial factors (e.g., depression, anxiety) addressed as part of sexual health screening.
- Men newly presenting with ED require evaluation of physical activity levels as part of cardiovascular and endocrine risk assessment.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of high-dose PDE5Is as first-line therapy without optimizing comorbidities or considering daily therapy in non-responders.
- Assuming obesity is the sole cause of low testosterone in men with diabetes without evaluating hypogonadism.
- Ignoring sexual health discussions in women with diabetes due to perceived lack of guidelines or patient reluctance.
- Relying solely on numeric A1C targets to assess sexual dysfunction risk without considering broader cardiovascular and endocrine factors.
- Overlooking the bidirectional relationship between ED and depression in men with diabetes.
- Assuming vascular disease is the sole primary risk factor for ED in men with diabetes without considering the multifactorial interplay of obesity, poor nutrition, and lack of exercise.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Morning serum total testosterone screening for men with diabetes/prediabetes and hypogonadism symptoms/signs.
- Glycemic assessment, lipid profile, and testosterone evaluation in men newly presenting with ED.
- Screening women with diabetes/prediabetes for desire, arousal, and orgasm difficulties, especially with depression/anxiety or recurrent UTIs.
- Lifestyle interventions (e.g., Mediterranean diet, exercise) and glycemic optimization to improve sexual function in women with diabetes.
- Consideration of FDA-approved treatments (e.g., flibanserin, bremelanotide) for hypoactive sexual desire disorder in women with diabetes.
- Consider testosterone replacement therapy in men with diabetes and symptomatic hypogonadism, with caution due to potential increased coronary artery plaque volume in older men.


### sleep health screening recommendation

Sleep health screening in diabetes. Screen for sleep health in people with prediabetes or diabetes. Screen for sleep disorders and diabetes-related sleep disruptions. Refer to sleep medicine specialists and/or qualified behavioral health professionals or diabetes care team as indicated

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Sleep health screening in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for sleep health in people with prediabetes or diabetes
- Screen for sleep disorders and diabetes-related sleep disruptions
- Refer to sleep medicine specialists and/or qualified behavioral health professionals or diabetes care team as indicated
- Screen for sleep disorders commonly associated with diabetes, such as obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), insomnia, restless legs syndrome, and circadian rhythm disorders

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- People with prediabetes or diabetes
- Those at risk for diabetes
- Screening should be performed at initial diabetes diagnosis and periodically thereafter, particularly in patients with poor glycemic control or unexplained daytime symptoms


### hyperglycemia monitoring in cancer therapy

Educate patients starting cancer therapy (ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, mTOR inhibitors) about hyperglycemia risks, symptoms, and crises.. Test fasting or random plasma glucose before initiating ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, or mTOR inhibitors.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hyperglycemia monitoring in cancer therapy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Educate patients starting cancer therapy (ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, mTOR inhibitors) about hyperglycemia risks, symptoms, and crises.
- Test fasting or random plasma glucose before initiating ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, or mTOR inhibitors.
- Monitor fasting or random plasma glucose at each visit during ICI, PI3Kα inhibitor, or mTOR inhibitor therapy.
- For PI3Kα inhibitors, test random plasma glucose weekly for the first 2 weeks, then every 4 weeks; consider A1C every 3 months.
- Promptly refer patients with ICI-induced autoimmune diabetes to an endocrinologist and provide diabetes self-management education.
- Provide education and counseling on hyperglycemia symptoms and glucose testing tools for patients treated with chemotherapy or glucocorticoids, in addition to ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, and mTOR inhibitors.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients starting ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, or mTOR inhibitors must be assessed for hyperglycemia risk.
- Symptoms or signs of hyperglycemia during or after treatment cessation warrant immediate glucose testing.
- ICI-induced autoimmune diabetes requires lifelong insulin therapy and urgent endocrinology referral.
- PI3Kα inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia peaks early (median 13 days), necessitating frequent initial monitoring.
- mTOR inhibitor-induced hyperglycemia lacks a clear temporal pattern, requiring ongoing monitoring.
- Patients treated with chemotherapy or glucocorticoids must be assessed for hyperglycemia risk and educated about symptoms and crises.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on A1C for early detection of hyperglycemia in PI3Kα inhibitor therapy (due to early peak onset).
- Fixed screening intervals without consideration of symptom-driven testing for hyperglycemia.
- Assuming hyperglycemia risk is uniform across all cancer therapies without tailored monitoring strategies.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Weekly random plasma glucose testing for the first 2 weeks of PI3Kα inhibitor therapy, then every 4 weeks.
- Consider A1C testing every 3 months during PI3Kα or mTOR inhibitor therapy.
- Mandatory patient education on hyperglycemia symptoms and crises for ICIs, PI3Kα inhibitors, and mTOR inhibitors.
- Immediate glucose testing for any symptoms or signs of hyperglycemia during or after treatment cessation.
- Recognition of ICI-induced autoimmune diabetes as a distinct entity requiring lifelong insulin therapy.
- Consider HLA-based risk prediction scores for targeted screening strategies in ICI-induced autoimmune diabetes.


### earliest sign of NPDR

Presence of microaneurysms as small deep-red dots (25–100µm) within the retina. Microaneurysms are outpouchings from retinal capillaries due to pericyte loss. Disease classified as mild non-proliferative diabetic retinopathy (NPDR) at this stage. Presence of 'featureless retina' as a sign of severe retinal hypoxia in advanced NPDR

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** earliest sign of NPDR

**Core Management Principles:**
- Presence of microaneurysms as small deep-red dots (25–100µm) within the retina
- Microaneurysms are outpouchings from retinal capillaries due to pericyte loss
- Disease classified as mild non-proliferative diabetic retinopathy (NPDR) at this stage
- Presence of 'featureless retina' as a sign of severe retinal hypoxia in advanced NPDR
- Appearance of venous beading (sausage-like or beaded retinal veins) as a marker of worsening NPDR
- Development of intra-retinal microvascular abnormalities (IRMA) as abnormal fine vascular patterns within the retina

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Dilated fundus examination for retinal assessment
- Stereo fundus photography or slit lamp biomicroscopy for detailed evaluation
- Evaluation for diabetic macular edema (DME) requires three-dimensional assessment using dilated slit lamp biomicroscopy and/or stereo fundus photography
- Definition of clinically significant macular edema (CSME) based on ETDRS criteria: retinal thickening within 500 μm of the macula center, hard exudates within 500 μm with adjacent thickening, or retinal thickening within one-disc diameter of the macula center


### eating disorder screening in diabetes

Screen for disordered or disrupted eating using validated screening measures (e.g., DEPS-R for type 1 diabetes, mSCOFF, QEWP-5, Binge Eating Scale, Night Eating Questionnaire for type 2 diabetes).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Eating disorder screening in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for disordered or disrupted eating using validated screening measures (e.g., DEPS-R for type 1 diabetes, mSCOFF, QEWP-5, Binge Eating Scale, Night Eating Questionnaire for type 2 diabetes).
- Reevaluate treatment plans for individuals with symptoms of disordered eating, in consultation with qualified professionals trained in diabetes and eating behaviors.
- Monitor for insulin omission, binge eating, night eating syndrome, and emotional distress in people with diabetes.
- Address eating disorders in the context of diabetes treatment, avoiding stigmatizing language and rigid carbohydrate counting.
- Consider technology use cautiously, with more research needed on risks and benefits.
- Review the medical treatment plan to identify potential treatment-related effects on hunger/caloric intake as part of disordered eating screening.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals with type 1 diabetes (higher risk for eating disorders and insulin restriction).
- Individuals with type 2 diabetes (higher risk for binge eating, night eating syndrome, and intentional insulin omission if treated with insulin).
- Presence of diabetes distress, depression, anxiety, or fear of hypoglycemia.
- Elevated A1C or inconsistent glycemic control.
- Household food insecurity (associated with increased disordered eating behaviors in youth).
- Presence of body image concerns and emotional distress in individuals with type 1 diabetes, which are associated with disordered eating behavior.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Rigid focus on A1C targets without considering underlying eating behaviors or psychological factors.
- Intensive weight monitoring that may reinforce maladaptive patterns.
- Labeling individuals with diabetes as having a diagnosable eating disorder without comprehensive evaluation.
- Assuming disordered eating is solely psychological without considering physiologic disruptions in hunger/satiety cues.
- Relying solely on non-diabetes-specific screening tools without diabetes context.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of diabetes-specific screening tools (e.g., DEPS-R, mSCOFF) to assess for intentional insulin omission and disordered eating.
- Inclusion of GLP-1 receptor agonists (e.g., incretin therapies) in treatment plans for disrupted eating behaviors, given evidence of reduced binge eating and emotional eating.
- Interprofessional care teams including professionals trained in diabetes and disordered eating behaviors.
- CBT and diabetes education programs for individuals with type 1 diabetes and disordered eating showing improvements in symptoms.
- Pilot self-guided online programs for type 2 diabetes and binge eating disorder showing promise in symptom reduction.
- Consider the use of incretin therapies (GLP-1 RAs and dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs) for treating disrupted or disordered eating behaviors, given evidence of reduced binge eating and emotional eating.


### vitrectomy indications for diabetic retinopathy

Vitrectomy is indicated for non-resolving vitreous haemorrhage (typically present for more than 3 months). Vitrectomy is required for tractional retinal detachment involving or threatening the macula

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** vitrectomy indications for diabetic retinopathy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Vitrectomy is indicated for non-resolving vitreous haemorrhage (typically present for more than 3 months)
- Vitrectomy is required for tractional retinal detachment involving or threatening the macula
- Vitrectomy is indicated for tractional rhegmatogenous detachment or proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR) unresponsive to aggressive laser PRP
- Surgical outcomes depend on patient-related factors such as macular perfusion and duration of macular detachment
- Blood glucose must be adequately controlled prior to vitrectomy or intravitreal injections to reduce infection risk
- Vitrectomy outcomes are influenced by the duration of macular detachment, with longer durations correlating with poorer visual recovery

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Non-resolving vitreous haemorrhage for more than 3 months
- Tractional retinal detachment involving or threatening the macula
- Tractional rhegmatogenous detachment or PDR unresponsive to laser PRP
- Adequate blood glucose control prior to surgical procedures
- Aggressive laser photocoagulation (PRP) must be attempted prior to considering vitrectomy for proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR) unresponsive to treatment


### food insecurity screening protocol

Food insecurity screening should occur at all levels of the healthcare system.. Any member of the healthcare team can screen using the Hunger Vital Sign.. Households are considered at risk if they answer either or both Hunger Vital Sign questions as 'often true' or 'sometimes true'.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** food insecurity screening protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Food insecurity screening should occur at all levels of the healthcare system.
- Any member of the healthcare team can screen using the Hunger Vital Sign.
- Households are considered at risk if they answer either or both Hunger Vital Sign questions as 'often true' or 'sometimes true'.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening must include the two Hunger Vital Sign questions.
- Screening should be conducted at all healthcare system levels.
- Screening must be conducted using the exact two Hunger Vital Sign questions as specified in the protocol.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of the Hunger Vital Sign as the standardized screening tool for food insecurity.
- Positive screening results must trigger referral to appropriate food insecurity programs and resources.


### food insecurity impact on diabetes

Food insecurity impact on diabetes management. Food insecurity is associated with increased risk of type 2 diabetes and higher-than-recommended glycemia.. Individuals facing food insecurity have lower engagement in self-care behaviors and medication use.. Food insecurity is linked to higher rates of depression, diabetes distress, and worse glycemic management.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Food insecurity impact on diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Food insecurity is associated with increased risk of type 2 diabetes and higher-than-recommended glycemia.
- Individuals facing food insecurity have lower engagement in self-care behaviors and medication use.
- Food insecurity is linked to higher rates of depression, diabetes distress, and worse glycemic management.
- Older adults with food insecurity are more likely to have emergency department visits and hospitalizations.
- Hyperglycemia risk in food-insecure individuals may stem from consumption of inexpensive carbohydrate-rich processed foods, binge eating, financial constraints, anxiety, depression, and poor sleep.
- Food insecurity disproportionately affects historically marginalized groups, low-income households, and households headed by single mothers.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening for food insecurity using a validated two-item tool (e.g., statements about food running out or not lasting).
- Assessment of patient-specific factors such as socioeconomic status, household composition, and access to community resources.
- Consideration of local food prescription programs or community resources to address food insecurity.
- Evaluation of medication adherence and barriers to filling prescriptions due to financial constraints.
- Monitoring for severe hyperglycemia or hypoglycemia in patients with diabetes and food insecurity.
- Use of a validated two-item screening tool with specific statements about food running out or not lasting within the past 12 months.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Assuming fixed A1C targets without considering food insecurity-related barriers to self-care.
- Relying solely on medication adjustments without addressing underlying food insecurity.
- Ignoring the impact of socioeconomic factors on diabetes outcomes.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of food prescription programs into primary care settings to address food insecurity.
- Use of validated screening tools to identify food insecurity in clinical practice.
- Prioritizing mitigation of severe hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia in patients with diabetes and food insecurity.
- Considering local resources and community partnerships to support food access for patients with diabetes.
- Integration of food prescription programs into primary care settings to address food deserts in underserved communities.


### post-transplantation diabetes screening

Post-transplantation diabetes screening and management. Screen for hyperglycemia after organ transplantation.. Use OGTT as the preferred diagnostic test for PTDM.. Diagnose PTDM once the patient is stable on maintenance immunosuppression (typically 3 months) and free of acute infection.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Post-transplantation diabetes screening and management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for hyperglycemia after organ transplantation.
- Use OGTT as the preferred diagnostic test for PTDM.
- Diagnose PTDM once the patient is stable on maintenance immunosuppression (typically 3 months) and free of acute infection.
- Treat hyperglycemia appropriately regardless of immunosuppression type, prioritizing insulin in the hospital setting.
- Use noninsulin glucose-lowering therapies for long-term management based on side effects, drug interactions, and cardiovascular/kidney benefits.
- Exclude pretransplant undiagnosed diabetes from PTDM classification when using the term 'new-onset diabetes after transplantation (NODAT)'.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be stable on an immunosuppressive plan before formal PTDM diagnosis.
- Absence of acute infection at the time of screening.
- Screening should occur as early as 3 months post-transplant.
- Use of immunosuppressive plans optimized for graft survival and patient outcomes.
- Consider FPG and/or A1C for initial high-risk identification before OGTT.
- Screening for high-risk individuals using FPG and/or A1C before OGTT to reduce unnecessary testing burden.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine diagnosis of PTDM during early post-transplant period (e.g., first few weeks) when hyperglycemia is often stress- or glucocorticoid-induced and transient.
- Use of fixed numeric A1C targets for PTDM diagnosis without considering timing or clinical stability.
- Exclusive reliance on FPG or A1C for definitive PTDM diagnosis without confirmatory OGTT.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Formal definition of PTDM as diabetes in the post-transplant setting irrespective of onset timing.
- OGTT as the preferred diagnostic test for PTDM.
- Emphasis on individualized treatment of hyperglycemia based on immunosuppression interactions and patient-specific risks.
- Recognition of PTDM as a significant risk factor for cardiovascular disease and chronic kidney disease in transplant recipients.
- Use of insulin as the agent of choice for hyperglycemia management in the hospital setting post-transplant.
- Recognition of transplant-specific risk factors for PTDM beyond general diabetes risks (e.g., immunosuppressant use).
- Acknowledgment that early posttransplant hyperglycemia (~90% prevalence) is often transient and resolves by discharge, necessitating delayed formal PTDM diagnosis.


### national guideline development

national guideline development process. Developed by ICMR as a joint effort of academic and research institutions. Expert contributions allocated based on experience. Draft reviewed in a workshop (10th January 2018, Chennai). Public feedback incorporated via ICMR website viewing period. Aims to provide uniform care across tertiary, secondary, and primary care levels

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** national guideline development process

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Developed by ICMR as a joint effort of academic and research institutions
- Expert contributions allocated based on experience
- Draft reviewed in a workshop (10th January 2018, Chennai)
- Public feedback incorporated via ICMR website viewing period
- Aims to provide uniform care across tertiary, secondary, and primary care levels

**Prerequisites:**
- Consensus guidelines required due to high prevalence of Type 2 Diabetes mellitus
- Expert contributions from multiple institutions
- Workshop review and incorporation of suggestions
- Public feedback integration
- Endorsement by ICMR and allied health professionals


### pediatric diabetes immunization guidelines

Follow the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) Recommended Child and Adolescent Immunization Schedule for Ages 18 Years or Younger.. Ensure routine vaccinations are up to date, including influenza, pneumococcal, and hepatitis B for children with diabetes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetes immunization guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- Follow the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) Recommended Child and Adolescent Immunization Schedule for Ages 18 Years or Younger.
- Ensure routine vaccinations are up to date, including influenza, pneumococcal, and hepatitis B for children with diabetes.
- Prioritize annual influenza vaccination for all children with diabetes aged 6 months and older.
- Administer pneumococcal vaccination based on age and risk factors, including children with diabetes at higher risk for infections.
- Screen for and administer hepatitis B vaccination in unvaccinated children with diabetes, particularly those at risk for exposure.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Children with diabetes should receive all routine childhood vaccinations as per AAP guidelines.
- Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for all children with diabetes aged 6 months and older.
- Pneumococcal vaccination is recommended for children with diabetes who are at increased risk for pneumococcal infections.
- Hepatitis B vaccination is recommended for unvaccinated children with diabetes, particularly those at risk for exposure.
- Vaccination status should be reviewed at each clinical visit to ensure compliance with immunization schedules.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of live attenuated vaccines in severely immunocompromised children with diabetes is no longer recommended without specific risk assessment.
- Delaying vaccinations due to mild illness is no longer considered necessary; vaccinations should proceed unless the child is severely ill.
- Routine use of live attenuated vaccines in children with diabetes who are not severely immunocompromised is no longer contraindicated; individualized risk assessment is recommended.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Increased emphasis on annual influenza vaccination for all children with diabetes, including those with type 1 and type 2 diabetes.
- Expanded recommendations for pneumococcal vaccination in children with diabetes, particularly those with additional risk factors.
- Integration of vaccination status checks into routine diabetes care visits to improve compliance.
- Consideration of hepatitis B vaccination for all unvaccinated children with diabetes, regardless of risk factors.
- Updated guidance on the use of live attenuated vaccines in children with diabetes, with individualized risk assessment.
- Consideration of meningococcal vaccination (including serogroup B) for children with diabetes, particularly those with additional risk factors such as asplenia or complement deficiencies.
- Integration of vaccination status checks into routine diabetes care visits to include documentation of vaccine receipt in electronic health records.


### pediatric psychosocial screening

Pediatric psychosocial screening in diabetes care. Screen children and adolescents with type 1, type 2, and other forms of diabetes for psychosocial concerns (e.g., diabetes distress, depressive symptoms, anxiety, disordered eating behaviors) using age-appropriate standardized and validated tools.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric psychosocial screening in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen children and adolescents with type 1, type 2, and other forms of diabetes for psychosocial concerns (e.g., diabetes distress, depressive symptoms, anxiety, disordered eating behaviors) using age-appropriate standardized and validated tools.
- Assess family factors, social determinants of health (e.g., food insecurity, housing stability, health literacy, financial barriers), and social adjustment (peer relationships, school performance).
- Encourage developmentally appropriate family involvement in diabetes management to prevent diabetes distress and deterioration in glycemia.
- Offer adolescents private time with healthcare professionals during visits.
- Incorporate reproductive health and preconception counseling into routine visits for individuals of childbearing potential.
- Screen for diabetes distress using validated tools as early as 7 or 8 years of age in children with diabetes.
- Inquire about diabetes-specific family relationships, including family teamwork and conflict, during visits.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening should occur at diagnosis and during routine follow-up care.
- Screening for depression should begin at age 12 years and continue thereafter.
- Screening for anxiety should begin at age 8 years and above.
- Screening for fear of hypoglycemia should be considered in children experiencing severe or frequent hypoglycemia or substantial hyperglycemia.
- Screening for disordered eating behaviors should be conducted when signs or symptoms are present.
- Referral to a behavioral health professional with experience in pediatric diabetes when psychosocial concerns are identified.
- Provide education about contraception to prevent unplanned pregnancy in individuals of childbearing potential at the onset of puberty or at diagnosis of diabetes.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of non-validated or non-standardized psychosocial screening tools.
- Premature or unsupportive transfer of diabetes care responsibility to children without considering developmental readiness.
- Exclusion of family involvement in diabetes management for children and adolescents.
- Lack of consideration for social determinants of health in treatment decisions.
- Absence of private adolescent healthcare visits.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of behavioral health professionals as integral members of the pediatric diabetes interprofessional team.
- Use of decision aids and self-care contracts to engage children and adolescents in shared decision-making.
- Developmentally tailored education and gradual transfer of self-care responsibilities with parental guidance.
- Incorporation of reproductive health and preconception counseling for individuals of childbearing potential.
- Routine screening for food insecurity, housing stability, and other social determinants of health.
- Use of decision aids to engage children and adolescents in shared decision-making about treatment options.
- Creation of self-care contracts to facilitate shared decision-making and enhance care.


### pancreas transplantation indications

Pancreas and islet transplantation indications. Pancreas transplantation should be reserved for people with type 1 diabetes undergoing simultaneous kidney transplantation, following kidney transplantation, or for those with recurrent ketoacidosis or severe hypoglycemia despite optimized glycemic management.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pancreas and islet transplantation indications

**Core Recommendations:**
- Pancreas transplantation should be reserved for people with type 1 diabetes undergoing simultaneous kidney transplantation, following kidney transplantation, or for those with recurrent ketoacidosis or severe hypoglycemia despite optimized glycemic management.
- Allogenic islet transplantation is indicated for adults with type 1 diabetes who are unable to reach their A1C goals due to repeated episodes of severe hypoglycemia despite intensive diabetes management and education.
- Pancreas transplantation may be considered for individuals with type 1 diabetes and hypoglycemia unawareness despite optimized glycemic management, even in the absence of recurrent ketoacidosis or severe hypoglycemia

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 1 diabetes diagnosis
- Undergoing simultaneous kidney transplantation or following kidney transplantation
- Recurrent ketoacidosis or severe hypoglycemia despite optimized glycemic management
- Inability to reach A1C goals due to repeated severe hypoglycemia despite intensive diabetes management and education (for islet transplantation)
- Documented hypoglycemia unawareness (for pancreas transplantation consideration)

**Updated Recommendations:**
- First allogeneic islet cell therapy (donislecel) approved in 2023 for specified type 1 diabetes cases
- Regulation of allogenic islet transplantation as cell therapy in the U.S.
- 2021 ADA/EASD consensus report on β-cell replacement therapy indications (Fig. 9.3) as a simplified reference for clinical decision-making


### panretinal photocoagulation for proliferative retinopathy

PRP involves delivering 1800 laser burns to peripheral retinal tissue to destroy photoreceptors and retinal pigment epithelium.. PRP converts hypoxic retina (releasing VEGF) into anoxic retina via laser ablation.. PRP is typically administered in 2 to 3 sessions.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** panretinal photocoagulation for proliferative retinopathy

**Core Management Principles:**
- PRP involves delivering 1800 laser burns to peripheral retinal tissue to destroy photoreceptors and retinal pigment epithelium.
- PRP converts hypoxic retina (releasing VEGF) into anoxic retina via laser ablation.
- PRP is typically administered in 2 to 3 sessions.
- Side effects include decreased peripheral field of vision and night vision.
- PRP may be performed using either argon green or diode laser, with diode laser preferred in eyes with media opacities like cataract or vitreous hemorrhage

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR) diagnosis as the primary indication for PRP.
- Assessment of retinal hypoxia and VEGF release prior to PRP.
- PRP should be deferred in patients with active DME until DME is stabilized or treated, as PRP can exacerbate macular edema
- PRP is contraindicated in patients with advanced glaucoma or significant media opacities that preclude adequate laser delivery


### macular edema management in PRP candidates

PRP may exacerbate pre-existing macular edema. Focal treatment of macular edema (clinically significant or not) should be considered in patients who may require PRP in the future. PRP should be deferred in patients with active macular edema until edema is controlled

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** macular edema management in PRP candidates

**Core Management Principles:**
- PRP may exacerbate pre-existing macular edema
- Focal treatment of macular edema (clinically significant or not) should be considered in patients who may require PRP in the future
- PRP should be deferred in patients with active macular edema until edema is controlled

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Assessment of macular edema status before PRP
- Consideration of focal treatment for macular edema prior to PRP
- Detailed fundus fluorescein angiography (FFA) or optical coherence tomography (OCT) to assess macular edema severity before PRP


### pregnancy contraindications and risks

pregnancy contraindications and risks in T1DM. Active proliferative retinopathy, severe nephropathy (GFR <30 ml/min/1.73 m²), severe autonomic neuropathy, or severe coronary heart disease increase pregnancy risks and require detailed evaluation and stabilization before conception.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pregnancy contraindications and risks in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Active proliferative retinopathy, severe nephropathy (GFR <30 ml/min/1.73 m²), severe autonomic neuropathy, or severe coronary heart disease increase pregnancy risks and require detailed evaluation and stabilization before conception.
- Pregnancy increases short-term risk of proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR), especially if baseline non-proliferative retinopathy (NPDR) is moderate to severe; risk persists up to 1 year postpartum.
- Women with chronic kidney disease (CKD) have higher adverse pregnancy outcomes; preconception screening of urine ACR, serum creatinine, and eGFR is mandatory.
- ACE inhibitors/ARBs must be discontinued preconception; intensive glucose and blood pressure control are required, with frequent monitoring and consideration of insulin analogues, CGMS, or pumps.
- Autonomic neuropathy (e.g., CAN, gastroparesis) and hypoglycemia unawareness increase adverse pregnancy outcomes; advanced glucose monitoring technologies are recommended.
- Pregnancy outcome is favorable in women with modest elevations in serum creatinine (below 1.4 mg/dl), proteinuria less than 1 g/24 h, and normal blood pressure; however, serum creatinine above 2 mg/dl, severe hypertension, nephrotic-range proteinuria (≥3 g/24 h), or pre-existing cardiovascular disease are associated with high risk of poor maternal and fetal outcomes

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Detailed ocular assessment preconception (if not done in last 6 months) and in first and third trimesters; monthly follow-up for severe retinopathy.
- Preconception renal function assessment (urine ACR, serum creatinine, eGFR) for women with CKD.
- Discontinuation of ACE inhibitors/ARBs preconception; switch to progesterone-only contraceptives in nephropathy.
- Intensive glucose and blood pressure control with frequent monitoring (e.g., serum creatinine and ACR every 4 weeks till 32 weeks, then biweekly).
- Use of advanced technologies (e.g., CGMS, sensor-augmented insulin pumps) for high-risk cases (e.g., hypoglycemia unawareness).
- Involvement of an expert retina specialist for individualized decisions regarding diabetic macular edema (DME) management during pregnancy


### ICI-induced autoimmune diabetes

Fulminant onset of autoimmune diabetes with DKA and low/undetectable C-peptide levels. Fewer than half of cases have islet autoantibodies. Most cases do not experience a phase of partial remission. Majority occurs in individuals with high-risk HLA susceptibility haplotypes for type 1 diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ICI-induced autoimmune diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Fulminant onset of autoimmune diabetes with DKA and low/undetectable C-peptide levels
- Fewer than half of cases have islet autoantibodies
- Most cases do not experience a phase of partial remission
- Majority occurs in individuals with high-risk HLA susceptibility haplotypes for type 1 diabetes
- Risk cannot be predicted by family history or autoantibodies
- ICI-induced diabetes typically presents with severe hyperglycemia and rapid progression to DKA, often within weeks of ICI initiation

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Healthcare professionals administering ICIs or caring for exposed individuals must be mindful of this adverse effect
- All exposed individuals should be educated and monitored appropriately
- Prompt initiation of insulin therapy is required upon diagnosis, as β-cell destruction is irreversible and endogenous insulin production is permanently lost

**Updated Recommendations:**
- ICI-induced diabetes occurs in 0.6–1.4% of treated individuals
- Higher frequency with PD-1/PD-L1 pathway blockade, alone or combined with CTLA-4 inhibitors
- Consider baseline HbA1c and fasting glucose monitoring prior to ICI initiation in high-risk patients (e.g., those with pre-existing type 2 diabetes or high-risk HLA haplotypes)


### A1C target optimization in youth

A1C target optimization in youth with type 1 diabetes. Set A1C goal of <7% (<53 mmol/mol) for most children and adolescents with type 1 diabetes. Encourage goal of <6.5% (<48 mmol/mol) in those who can achieve it without adverse effects, especially with access to CGM and AID systems

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** A1C target optimization in youth with type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Set A1C goal of <7% (<53 mmol/mol) for most children and adolescents with type 1 diabetes
- Encourage goal of <6.5% (<48 mmol/mol) in those who can achieve it without adverse effects, especially with access to CGM and AID systems
- Monitor glucose multiple times daily using CGM whenever possible; finger-stick glucose monitoring remains essential if CGM is inaccessible or inaccurate
- Provide initial and ongoing education and behavioral support for CGM use to patients, parents, and caregivers
- Avoid fixed numeric A1C targets in young children (<6 years) due to hypoglycemia risk and inability to recognize/manage hypoglycemia
- Use CGM soon after type 1 diabetes diagnosis to improve A1C outcomes
- Offer behavioral support to improve ongoing CGM use in youth and caregivers

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 1 diabetes diagnosis in children or adolescents
- Access to CGM or blood glucose monitoring capability
- Consideration of patient-specific factors such as age, hypoglycemia awareness, and phase of diabetes (e.g., honeymoon phase)
- Availability of diabetes education and support systems for CGM use
- Exclusion of severe hypoglycemia risk in young children (<6 years) or those unable to manage hypoglycemia
- Consider residual β-cell function (honeymoon phase) when setting A1C targets
- Evaluate patient age and hypoglycemia awareness (especially in <6 years) before setting lower A1C goals

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors
- Reliance solely on finger-stick glucose monitoring without CGM when available
- Use of older CGM data standards requiring 14 days of data for reliable glycemic assessment
- Avoidance of lower A1C goals (<6.5%) due to perceived hypoglycemia risk without evidence-based assessment
- Ignoring the honeymoon phase as a window for achieving lower A1C goals
- Reliance on 14 days of CGM data as the sole standard for glycemic assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Encouragement of A1C <6.5% (<48 mmol/mol) in eligible youth with type 1 diabetes using modern AID systems and CGM
- Use of ≥7 days of CGM data for reliable glycemic assessment in children and adolescents
- Emphasis on CGM Time in Range (TIR) metrics for glycemic assessment alongside A1C
- Recognition of the honeymoon phase as an opportunity to achieve lower A1C goals
- Awareness of the relationship between higher CGM TIR and improved neurocognitive outcomes in youth
- ≥7 days of CGM data may be sufficient for reliable glycemic assessment in children and adolescents
- Higher CGM TIR is associated with better structural brain development and neurocognitive outcomes in youth


### anxiety screening guidelines

Anxiety and fear of hypoglycemia screening in diabetes. Screen for anxiety symptoms at least annually in people with diabetes. Screen individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia or with severe/frequent hypoglycemia for fear of hypoglycemia at least annually and when clinically appropriate

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Anxiety and fear of hypoglycemia screening in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for anxiety symptoms at least annually in people with diabetes
- Screen individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia or with severe/frequent hypoglycemia for fear of hypoglycemia at least annually and when clinically appropriate
- Address anxiety symptoms within the scope of practice or refer to a qualified behavioral health professional if symptoms interfere with diabetes self-management or quality of life
- Conduct screening for both diabetes distress and anxiety at least annually in people with diabetes, followed by further clinical assessment to ensure accurate identification

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Anxiety symptoms may overlap with diabetes distress and/or hyperglycemia symptoms, requiring careful differentiation
- History of nocturnal hypoglycemia, co-occurring psychological concerns, and sleep disturbances contribute to fear of hypoglycemia
- Excessive diabetes self-management behaviors beyond prescribed needs may indicate underlying anxiety
- Prioritize screening for anxiety in individuals with a history of nocturnal hypoglycemia, co-occurring psychological concerns, or sleep disturbances
- Conduct further clinical assessment to differentiate anxiety from diabetes distress or hyperglycemia symptoms when overlapping symptoms are present

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Annual screening for fear of hypoglycemia in high-risk individuals or those with severe/frequent hypoglycemia
- Integration of anxiety and diabetes distress screening into routine diabetes care
- Refer individuals with fear of hypoglycemia to a trained healthcare professional for evidence-based intervention (e.g., cognitive behavioral therapy)


### diabetic macular edema management strategies

Conservative management is recommended for DME with preserved visual acuity; treatment is considered only if vision loss progresses.. Treatment for DME should be initiated only after excluding ischemic maculopathy, as inappropriate treatment can worsen vision loss.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetic macular edema management strategies

**Core Management Principles:**
- Conservative management is recommended for DME with preserved visual acuity; treatment is considered only if vision loss progresses.
- Treatment for DME should be initiated only after excluding ischemic maculopathy, as inappropriate treatment can worsen vision loss.
- Intravitreal Anti-VEGF agents (Ranibizumab, Aflibercept, Bevacizumab) are the primary treatment for DME, offering better functional and structural outcomes than laser therapy.
- Intraocular corticosteroids (e.g., triamcinolone acetonide, dexamethasone implant, fluocinolone acetonide implant) are used for DME unresponsive to other treatments, with risks of cataract and increased intraocular pressure explained to patients.
- Close monitoring of DR and DME is required during pregnancy, with dilated retinal examinations preconception, each trimester, and postpartum for up to 12 months.
- Posterior sub-tenon injection of triamcinolone acetonide (PST) is a viable corticosteroid option for DME with a duration of action of 4 to 6 weeks.
- Intra-vitreal triamcinolone acetonide (IVTA) is used for DME with a duration of action of 4 to 6 weeks.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Exclusion of ischemic maculopathy before initiating DME treatment.
- Stable and effective control of retinopathy and maculopathy for at least three months prior to cataract surgery.
- Dilated retinal examination preconception for women with T1DM planning pregnancy.
- Collaboration between endocrinologist/diabetologist, ophthalmologist, and obstetrician for optimal outcomes in pregnant women with T1DM.
- Use of non-invasive investigations such as OCT for accurate diagnosis and treatment of DR.
- Stable and effective control of retinopathy and maculopathy for at least three months prior to cataract surgery is required to minimize postoperative worsening of DR.


### diabetic retinopathy progression timing

Diabetic retinopathy progression timing and screening optimization. Retinopathy most commonly occurs after puberty and 5–10 years of diabetes duration. Low risk of vision-threatening retinal lesions prior to 12 years of age. Lower frequency of eye examinations recommended in adolescents with A1C near goal range

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic retinopathy progression timing and screening optimization

**Core Recommendations:**
- Retinopathy most commonly occurs after puberty and 5–10 years of diabetes duration
- Low risk of vision-threatening retinal lesions prior to 12 years of age
- Lower frequency of eye examinations recommended in adolescents with A1C near goal range
- Autonomous AI screening increases access to routine diabetic retinopathy screening
- Referrals to eye care professionals with diabetic retinopathy expertise for pediatric patients
- Retinopathy risk increases with longer diabetes duration and post-puberty status due to cumulative hyperglycemic damage to retinal microvasculature

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes duration of 5–10 years for retinopathy risk
- Post-puberty status for retinopathy development
- Age under 12 years for low risk of vision-threatening lesions
- A1C near goal range for reduced eye examination frequency
- Access to autonomous AI screening or specialized eye care professionals
- Expertise in pediatric diabetic retinopathy counseling for referred eye care professionals

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Previously recommended annual eye examinations for all adolescents regardless of A1C control
- Routine screening protocols not accounting for age or puberty status
- Exclusive reliance on traditional ophthalmologic screening without AI augmentation

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Reduced eye examination frequency in adolescents with A1C near goal range
- Autonomous AI screening for diabetic retinopathy to improve access
- Referral to eye care professionals with pediatric diabetic retinopathy expertise
- Risk stratification based on age, puberty status, and diabetes duration
- Emphasis on early detection and intervention counseling for pediatric patients
- Use of DCCT adolescent cohort data to justify reduced eye examination frequency in well-controlled adolescents


### cognitive capacity monitoring guideline

Cognitive capacity monitoring in diabetes. Monitor cognitive capacity throughout the life span for all individuals with diabetes. Particular attention to those with documented cognitive disabilities. Monitor those who experience severe hypoglycemia. Monitor very young children. Monitor older adults

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Cognitive capacity monitoring in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Monitor cognitive capacity throughout the life span for all individuals with diabetes
- Particular attention to those with documented cognitive disabilities
- Monitor those who experience severe hypoglycemia
- Monitor very young children
- Monitor older adults

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Documented history of severe hypoglycemia episodes as a trigger for cognitive capacity monitoring


### diagnostic criteria and screening

diagnostic criteria and screening for type 2 diabetes. Use standard diagnostic criteria for type 2 diabetes based on symptoms and laboratory tests. Apply specific criteria for diagnosing prediabetes. Perform Oral Glucose Tolerance Test (OGTT) when indicated. Screen children and adolescents for type 2 diabetes using targeted criteria

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diagnostic criteria and screening for type 2 diabetes

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Use standard diagnostic criteria for type 2 diabetes based on symptoms and laboratory tests
- Apply specific criteria for diagnosing prediabetes
- Perform Oral Glucose Tolerance Test (OGTT) when indicated
- Screen children and adolescents for type 2 diabetes using targeted criteria
- Use HbA1c as an alternative diagnostic criterion for type 2 diabetes when fasting plasma glucose or OGTT is impractical
- Screen high-risk adults (e.g., those with BMI ≥23 kg/m², hypertension, or family history) annually for type 2 diabetes
- Perform random plasma glucose testing in symptomatic individuals to support diagnosis

**Prerequisites:**
- Symptoms of diabetes must be evaluated alongside laboratory confirmation
- Prediabetes diagnosis requires adherence to defined glycemic thresholds
- OGTT must be conducted following standardized protocols
