- Screening in children/adolescents should follow pediatric-specific guidelines
- HbA1c testing must be performed using NGSP-certified methods standardized to the DCCT assay
- Prediabetes diagnosis requires confirmation with a second test (fasting glucose, OGTT, or HbA1c) on a different day unless unequivocal hyperglycemia is present
- Screening in children/adolescents should prioritize those with BMI ≥85th percentile for age/sex or with additional risk factors (e.g., family history, acanthosis nigricans)


### cognitive impairment screening

Cognitive impairment screening in older adults with diabetes. Screen annually for cognitive impairment or dementia in adults ≥65 years at initial visit and as clinically indicated.. Use validated tools (e.g., Mini-Cog, Mini-Mental State Examination, Montreal Cognitive Assessment) for screening.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Cognitive impairment screening in older adults with diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen annually for cognitive impairment or dementia in adults ≥65 years at initial visit and as clinically indicated.
- Use validated tools (e.g., Mini-Cog, Mini-Mental State Examination, Montreal Cognitive Assessment) for screening.
- Screen when significant decline in clinical status (e.g., self-care errors, insulin dosing mistakes, hypoglycemia) is observed.
- Refer individuals screening positive for formal cognitive/neuropsychological evaluation if feasible.
- Simplify diabetes care plans and engage support structures when cognitive dysfunction is identified.
- Screen for delirium using the 4AT tool when acute changes in mental status are suspected.
- Discuss findings of cognitive screening with the individual and their care partners.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Adults ≥65 years with diabetes.
- Presence of geriatric syndromes (e.g., cognitive impairment, depression) or functional decline.
- Significant decline in self-care activities (e.g., insulin dosing errors, skipped meals).
- Prior assessment of hypoglycemia risk, polypharmacy, and frailty.
- Availability of caregiver support for individuals with cognitive impairment.
- Presence of significant decline in clinical status due to increased problems with self-care activities and medication management (e.g., errors in calculating insulin dose, difficulty counting carbohydrates, skipped meals, skipped insulin doses, difficulty recognizing/preventing/treating hypoglycemia).
- Availability of care partners or support systems to assist with care when cognitive dysfunction is identified.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of non-validated cognitive screening tools.
- Rigid A1C targets without consideration of cognitive function.
- Complex insulin regimens without caregiver support in cognitively impaired individuals.
- Ignoring hypoglycemia risk in older adults with cognitive decline.
- Intensive treatment protocols aimed to achieve an A1C <6.0% with complex drug plans in older adults with cognitive impairment or complex health needs.
- Rigid A1C targets without consideration of cognitive function and functional limitations.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Annual cognitive screening for early detection of mild cognitive impairment or dementia.
- Integration of Mini-Cog and other validated tools into routine diabetes care.
- Use of GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors for potential cognitive benefits.
- Simplification of care plans and caregiver engagement for cognitively impaired individuals.
- Consideration of diabetes-related dementia as a distinct clinical entity.
- Recognition of 'diabetes-related dementia' as a distinct clinical entity characterized by slower progression, absence of typical neuroimaging findings, advanced age, elevated A1C, long duration of diabetes, high frequency of insulin use, frailty, sarcopenia, and dynapenia.
- Use of validated hypoglycemia risk calculators (e.g., Kaiser Hypoglycemia Model) to stratify future risk for hypoglycemia.


### cystic fibrosis diabetes screening

Annual screening for cystic fibrosis–related diabetes (CFRD) should begin by age 10 years in all people with cystic fibrosis, preferably using OGTT.. If OGTT is not feasible, A1C can be used as an alternative first-line screening tool with A1C 5.5–6.4% prompting OGTT within 3 months.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Cystic fibrosis diabetes screening

**Core Recommendations:**
- Annual screening for cystic fibrosis–related diabetes (CFRD) should begin by age 10 years in all people with cystic fibrosis, preferably using OGTT.
- If OGTT is not feasible, A1C can be used as an alternative first-line screening tool with A1C 5.5–6.4% prompting OGTT within 3 months.
- A1C ≥6.5% (≥48 mmol/mol) is consistent with a diagnosis of CFRD and requires confirmatory testing.
- Weight loss or failure of expected weight gain in cystic fibrosis patients should prompt screening for CFRD regardless of age.
- Annual monitoring for diabetes complications is recommended beginning 5 years after CFRD diagnosis.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- People with cystic fibrosis starting at age 10 years.
- OGTT preferred if feasible; A1C used as alternative when OGTT is not feasible.
- A1C 5.5–6.4% requires follow-up OGTT within 3 months.
- Weight loss or failure to gain weight in cystic fibrosis patients.
- Confirmation of A1C ≥6.5% with repeat A1C or OGTT.
- A1C of 5.5% provides 98% negative predictive value for CFRD detection, supporting its use to identify patients who do not require further testing.
- A1C of 5.8% provides 95% sensitivity to detect CFRD by OGTT.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Screening for CFRD before age 10 years without established benefit for weight, height, BMI, or lung function.
- Use of FPG alone for diagnosis due to lower sensitivity in people with cystic fibrosis.
- Routine use of CGM for CFRD screening outside research settings due to lack of validated thresholds.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- A1C as a two-step screening strategy alternative to OGTT for CFRD.
- A1C 5.5% threshold with 91% sensitivity and 98% negative predictive value for CFRD detection.
- Confirmation of A1C ≥6.5% with repeat A1C or OGTT for CFRD diagnosis.
- Annual monitoring for diabetes complications starting 5 years after CFRD diagnosis.
- Insulin therapy for people with CFRD to attain individualized glycemic goals.
- Use of A1C as a first-line screening tool to identify patients who should not undergo further testing with OGTT (negative predictive value approach).
- Use of A1C as a first-line screening tool to identify patients who should proceed to OGTT (sensitivity approach).


### depressive symptom screening

Depressive symptom screening in diabetes. Screen for depressive symptoms in all people with diabetes at least annually and more frequently among those with a history of depression

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Depressive symptom screening in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for depressive symptoms in all people with diabetes at least annually and more frequently among those with a history of depression
- Refer to qualified behavioral health professionals or other healthcare professionals with experience using evidence-based treatment approaches for depression in collaboration with the diabetes care team
- Conduct annual screening for depression, anxiety, and diabetes distress to distinguish overlapping symptoms
- Perform a comprehensive clinical assessment following positive screening findings to ensure accurate diagnosis
- Incorporate person-centered integrated care approaches to improve both depression and glycemic outcomes
- Query origins and exacerbating factors for depressive symptoms to distinguish between diabetes distress and depression
- Monitor and potentially adjust the medical treatment plan in response to reduction in depressive symptoms

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- History of depression
- Current depression or antidepressant medication use
- Diabetes-related stigma (particularly in type 2 diabetes)
- Elevated depressive symptoms or depressive disorders
- Patient consent for referral to behavioral health professionals
- Use of age-appropriate validated measures for routine monitoring
- Implementation of multisite screening protocols in diabetes clinics

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Screening for depressive symptoms only in symptomatic patients
- Relying solely on non-validated measures for depression screening
- Isolated treatment of diabetes without addressing comorbid depressive symptoms
- Assuming depressive symptoms are solely due to diabetes distress without further assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of age-appropriate validated measures for routine monitoring of depressive symptoms
- Implementation of collaborative care models integrating behavioral health professionals into the diabetes care team
- Consideration of GLP-1 receptor agonists for potential improvement in depressive symptoms in type 2 diabetes
- Integration of psychological interventions (e.g., CBT via internet, phone, or self-guided) for depressive symptoms
- Lifestyle interventions targeting nutrition and/or physical activity to improve depressive symptoms and A1C
- Use of GLP-1 receptor agonists with potential for improvement in depressive symptoms (noted as mixed evidence in meta-analyses)
- Psychological interventions delivered via internet, phone, or self-guided formats (e.g., CBT)


### T1DM retinopathy screening guidelines

Annual screening for diabetic retinopathy (DR) is recommended in individuals with T1DM aged >10 years with a disease duration of ≥5 years.. Screening can be performed using direct ophthalmoscopy, indirect ophthalmoscopy, slit lamp fundus exam, or fundus cameras (mydriatic or non-mydriatic).

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** T1DM retinopathy screening guidelines

**Core Management Principles:**
- Annual screening for diabetic retinopathy (DR) is recommended in individuals with T1DM aged >10 years with a disease duration of ≥5 years.
- Screening can be performed using direct ophthalmoscopy, indirect ophthalmoscopy, slit lamp fundus exam, or fundus cameras (mydriatic or non-mydriatic).
- Mydriatic retinal imaging with >2 fields of view provides the highest sensitivity for DR detection.
- Direct ophthalmoscopy sensitivity may be lower, especially when performed by non-eye care professionals.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Disease must be an important public health problem with a well-understood natural history and long latent phase.
- Acceptable, cost-effective diagnostic tests and available treatment facilities are required.
- Case finding should be a continuous process, not a one-time project.
- Dilated ophthalmoscopy is the standard for assessing DR severity.
- Treatment for DR must be available and proven to improve patient outcomes.
- Facilities for both diagnosis and treatment must be accessible.
- Case finding must be a continuous process rather than a one-time project.



## Diabetes Pharmacotherapy and Outcomes

This collection synthesizes evidence-based pharmacotherapy strategies and outcomes research for diabetes management, emphasizing individualized glycemic control, cardiovascular and renal protection, and complication prevention across diverse patient populations.

**Key Features:**
- SGLT2 inhibitors: Oral therapy with cardiovascular, renal, and heart failure benefits, but requires careful selection to avoid adverse effects in older adults.
- GLP-1 receptor agonists: Recommended for glycemic management in CKD and type 2 diabetes, with dual GIP/GLP-1 agents preferred for heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF).
- Intensive glycemic control: Reduces microvascular complications by 50–76% in type 1 diabetes (DCCT/EDIC), with long-term macrovascular benefits.
- DPP-4 inhibitors: Low hypoglycemia risk but weaker glucose-lowering effects; useful in older adults or those with low eGFR as metformin alternatives.
- Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors: Target postprandial glucose by delaying carbohydrate absorption; require administration with meals and glucose (not sucrose) for hypoglycemia treatment.
- Cardiovascular risk reduction: GLP-1 RAs or SGLT2 inhibitors prioritized regardless of glycemic goals in patients with heart failure, CKD, or established CVD.
- Diabetes complications research: Focuses on retinopathy (AI/teleophthalmology screening), neuropathy, and foot care with individualized A1C targets.
- SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA risk: Euglycemic DKA risk necessitates patient education, ketone monitoring, and avoidance in type 1 diabetes.
- Oral antidiabetic agent comparison: Selection based on mechanisms targeting gut absorption, insulin secretion, hepatic output, or renal glucose handling.
- Heart failure treatment: Early primary prevention (lifestyle, ACEi/ARBs) and intensive hypertension management in diabetes.
- Glycemic management guidelines: Regular A1C/fasting glucose monitoring, individualized goals, and GLP-1 RA use in CKD after metformin/SGLT2 failure.

**Notable Details:**
- SGLT2 inhibitors reduce MI, stroke, or cardiovascular death with empagliflozin/canagliflozin, while dapagliflozin reduces HF hospitalization/CVD death without MI/stroke reduction.
- Euglycemic DKA (blood glucose <250 mg/dL) can occur with SGLT2 inhibitors, particularly in pregnancy or during acute illness.
- GLP-1 RAs are recommended for type 2 diabetes with CKD after metformin/SGLT2 failure to meet individualized glycemic targets.
- Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors must be taken with the first bite of food and hypoglycemia treated with oral glucose, not sucrose.
- Intensive glycemic control (A1C ~7%) in type 1 diabetes reduces retinopathy, neuropathy, and CKD progression by 50–76% (DCCT/EDIC).

**Topics in this group:**

### SGLT2 inhibitor therapy

Administered orally, convenient for older adults with diabetes. Demonstrates cardiovascular benefits in patients with established ASCVD. Beneficial for people with heart failure and slows progression of chronic kidney disease. Generally well tolerated among older adults, but requires thoughtful selection to avoid adverse effects SGLT2 inhibitor therapy. SGLT2 inhibitor therapy in CKD with or without diabetes. SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended for adults with CKD and eGFR ≥20 ml/min/1.73 m², regardless of diabetes status.. Initiation does not require alteration of CKD monitoring frequency.. Reversible eGFR dip on initiation is not an indication to discontinue therapy. SGLT2 inhibitor therapy. SGLT2 inhibitor therapy. Inhibit SGLT2 in the proximal convoluted tubule to induce glycosuria. Reduce blood glucose, weight, and blood pressure. Provide cardiovascular benefits beyond glucose lowering. Monitor for genital mycotic infections, urinary tract infections, polyuria, and volume depletion in elderly

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT2 inhibitor therapy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Administered orally, convenient for older adults with diabetes
- Demonstrates cardiovascular benefits in patients with established ASCVD
- Beneficial for people with heart failure and slows progression of chronic kidney disease
- Generally well tolerated among older adults, but requires thoughtful selection to avoid adverse effects
- May cause clinically significant volume depletion, higher risk in frail or orthostatic-prone older adults

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Established atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) for cardiovascular benefits
- Presence of heart failure or chronic kidney disease for therapeutic indication
- Assessment of frailty or orthostatic risk before initiation in older adults
- Evaluation for recurrent or severe urinary tract infections before use
- Monitoring for genital mycotic infections and urinary incontinence symptoms

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors without consideration of volume depletion risk in frail older adults
- Ignoring urinary incontinence symptoms before and after initiation
- Overlooking euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis risk in multimorbid or PALTC residents

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Stratified analyses showing older adults may have similar or greater benefits than younger people
- Increased awareness of genital mycotic infections, especially in women
- Small increase in urinary tract infections requiring caution in susceptible individuals
- Euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis as a rare but serious risk, particularly with infection triggers
- Oral administration convenience for older adults with diabetes

### Source 2: Chronic Kidney Disease.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT2 inhibitor therapy in CKD with or without diabetes

**Clinical Actions:**
- SGLT2 inhibitors are recommended for adults with CKD and eGFR ≥20 ml/min/1.73 m², regardless of diabetes status.
- Initiation does not require alteration of CKD monitoring frequency.
- Reversible eGFR dip on initiation is not an indication to discontinue therapy.
- SGLT2 inhibitors reduce risk of kidney failure, AKI, cardiovascular death, and hospitalization for heart failure.
- SGLT2 inhibitors favorably reduce BP, uric acid, fluid overload, and serious hyperkalemia.
- SGLT2 inhibitors reduce the risk of hospitalization from any cause in people with CKD.
- SGLT2 inhibitors reduce the risk of myocardial infarction in people with CKD.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- eGFR ≥20 ml/min/1.73 m² with urine ACR ≥200 mg/g (≥20 mg/mmol), or
- heart failure irrespective of albuminuria level, or
- CKD with high risk of progression (e.g., albuminuria or reduced eGFR).
- No need for routine post-initiation blood test rechecks in adults with CKD.
- Exclusion of adults with polycystic kidney disease from large trial evidence.
- Consideration of initiating SGLT2i when eGFR falls below 20 ml/min per 1.73 m² and continuing until initiation of kidney replacement therapy (KRT), as demonstrated in large CKD population RCTs.
- No significant subgroup interaction by primary kidney diagnosis for kidney disease progression outcomes with SGLT2i use.

### Source 3: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT2 inhibitor therapy

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Inhibit SGLT2 in the proximal convoluted tubule to induce glycosuria
- Reduce blood glucose, weight, and blood pressure
- Provide cardiovascular benefits beyond glucose lowering
- Monitor for genital mycotic infections, urinary tract infections, polyuria, and volume depletion in elderly
- Assess risk of euglycemic ketoacidosis and lower extremity amputations (especially with canagliflozin)

**Prerequisites:**
- Presence of type 2 diabetes mellitus
- Adequate renal function (eGFR criteria as per guideline)
- No history of recurrent genitourinary infections
- No active diabetic ketoacidosis
- No hypersensitivity to SGLT2 inhibitors


### GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation

GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendations across comorbidities. Recommend GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA for type 2 diabetes with symptomatic HFpEF to reduce heart failure events and symptoms. Use GLP-1 RA for glycemic management in advanced CKD with demonstrated benefits GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation. GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation in T2D with CKD. Recommended as a long-acting GLP-1 RA for adults with T2D and CKD who have not achieved glycemic targets despite metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor use, or are unable to use those medications.. Prioritize GLP-1 RAs with documented cardiovascular benefits.. Preferred over insulin in people with T2D and CKD. GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation. GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation. GLP-1 receptor agonists reduce hyperglycemia with minimal risk of hypoglycemia. GLP-1 receptor agonists promote weight loss. GLP-1 receptor agonists can be combined with all anti-hyperglycemic agents except DPP-4 inhibitors. GLP-1 receptor agonists improve cardiovascular outcomes beyond glycemic effects

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendations across comorbidities

**Core Recommendations:**
- Recommend GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA for type 2 diabetes with symptomatic HFpEF to reduce heart failure events and symptoms
- Use GLP-1 RA for glycemic management in advanced CKD with demonstrated benefits
- Prefer GLP-1 RA with MASH benefits for type 2 diabetes with biopsy-proven MASH or high liver fibrosis risk
- Consider dual GIP/GLP-1 RA for MASLD or MASH with overweight/obesity for glycemic control
- Offer CGM at diabetes onset and anytime thereafter for adults on insulin or noninsulin therapies with hypoglycemia risk
- Recommend assessment for insulin therapy in individuals on immunotherapy who develop hyperglycemia to prevent diabetic ketoacidosis
- Consider metformin as first-line treatment for hyperglycemia due to mTOR inhibitors or PI3K inhibitors

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 2 diabetes diagnosis with symptomatic HFpEF for GLP-1 RA recommendation
- Advanced CKD for GLP-1 RA initiation and continuation
- Biopsy-proven MASH or high liver fibrosis risk for GLP-1 RA preference
- Overweight/obesity with MASLD or MASH for dual GIP/GLP-1 RA consideration
- Insulin therapy or noninsulin therapies with hypoglycemia risk for CGM recommendation
- Presence of symptomatic HFpEF for dual GIP/GLP-1 RA recommendation
- Biopsy-proven MASH or high risk for liver fibrosis for GLP-1 RA preference

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Dual GIP/GLP-1 RA for type 2 diabetes with symptomatic HFpEF
- GLP-1 RA for advanced CKD glycemic management
- GLP-1 RA with MASH benefits for biopsy-proven MASH or high-risk patients
- Dual GIP/GLP-1 RA for MASLD or MASH with overweight/obesity
- CGM at diabetes onset and anytime thereafter for adults on insulin or high-risk noninsulin therapies

### Source 2: Chronic Kidney Disease.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation in T2D with CKD

**Clinical Actions:**
- Recommended as a long-acting GLP-1 RA for adults with T2D and CKD who have not achieved glycemic targets despite metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor use, or are unable to use those medications.
- Prioritize GLP-1 RAs with documented cardiovascular benefits.
- Preferred over insulin in people with T2D and CKD.
- Modestly reduces risk of hospitalization for heart failure (HR: 0.89) and all-cause mortality (HR: 0.88).
- GLP-1 RAs are recommended to be prioritized ahead of insulin in people with T2D and CKD, even when glycemic targets are not yet achieved with metformin and SGLT2 inhibitors.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Adults with type 2 diabetes (T2D) and chronic kidney disease (CKD).
- Failure to achieve individualized glycemic targets despite metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor treatment, or inability to use those medications.
- Documented cardiovascular benefits of the chosen GLP-1 RA.
- Documented prior cardiovascular disease (CVD) or high cardiovascular risk is a key consideration when selecting a GLP-1 RA with cardiovascular benefits.

### Source 3: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 receptor agonist recommendation

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- GLP-1 receptor agonists reduce hyperglycemia with minimal risk of hypoglycemia
- GLP-1 receptor agonists promote weight loss
- GLP-1 receptor agonists can be combined with all anti-hyperglycemic agents except DPP-4 inhibitors
- GLP-1 receptor agonists improve cardiovascular outcomes beyond glycemic effects
- GLP-1 receptor agonists should be taken at the same time every day

**Prerequisites:**
- GLP-1 receptor agonists are resistant to DPP-4 degradation, requiring longer half-life consideration
- Monitor for nausea, vomiting, and rare cases of acute pancreatitis
- GLP-1 receptor agonists can be taken at any time of day, but preferably at the same time every day


### diabetes complications research

Diabetes complications research: retinopathy, neuropathy, and foot care. Implement individualized A1C targets based on patient-specific factors such as life expectancy, cognitive function, and hypoglycemia risk.. Use teleophthalmology and AI-based screening for diabetic retinopathy to improve early detection and reduce ungradable image rates. diabetes complications research. Diabetes complications research and management. Acute complications (hypo-/hyperglycaemic emergencies) require immediate glucose monitoring and tailored treatment based on consciousness level.. Chronic complications (microvascular, macrovascular, diabetic foot) necessitate early, proactive screening even in prediabetes due to late-stage symptom onset. diabetes complications research. infection risk and complications in diabetes. Patients with diabetes are at higher risk of pulmonary infections (e.g., pneumococcus, influenza) leading to increased morbidity and mortality.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes complications research: retinopathy, neuropathy, and foot care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Implement individualized A1C targets based on patient-specific factors such as life expectancy, cognitive function, and hypoglycemia risk.
- Use teleophthalmology and AI-based screening for diabetic retinopathy to improve early detection and reduce ungradable image rates.
- Adopt intravitreal anti-VEGF therapies (e.g., aflibercept, ranibizumab) as first-line treatment for diabetic macular edema and proliferative diabetic retinopathy.
- Screen for diabetic peripheral neuropathy using validated tools like the Ipswich Touch Test and prioritize early intervention with intensive glycemic control.
- Apply multidisciplinary foot care teams and offloading strategies to prevent and manage diabetic foot ulcers, including Charcot neuro-osteoarthropathy.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Documented diagnosis of type 1 or type 2 diabetes with confirmed microvascular or macrovascular complications.
- Baseline retinal imaging and neuropathy assessment prior to initiating intensified therapy.
- Patient consent and adherence to follow-up protocols for screening and treatment.
- Access to specialized care including ophthalmology, endocrinology, podiatry, and vascular surgery.
- Integration of patient-reported outcomes and symptom tracking in routine diabetes care.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of fixed A1C numeric targets without individualized adjustment.
- Laser photocoagulation as the sole first-line therapy for proliferative diabetic retinopathy without consideration of anti-VEGF options.
- Reliance on oral hypoglycemic agents alone for neuropathy management without addressing glycemic control and lifestyle factors.
- Use of outdated neuropathy screening tools lacking sensitivity or specificity.
- Empiric opioid therapy for painful diabetic neuropathy without prior evaluation of non-opioid alternatives.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adoption of 3-year screening intervals for sight-threatening retinal lesions in type 2 diabetes patients without retinopathy.
- Integration of GLP-1 receptor agonists and SGLT2 inhibitors in patients with diabetes and cardiovascular or renal risk to reduce complications.
- Implementation of automated teleretinal screening programs to expand access to diabetic retinopathy detection.
- Use of faricimab and other extended-duration intravitreal therapies for diabetic macular edema with dosing up to every 16 weeks.
- Application of digital health technologies, including smart mats and remote monitoring, for early detection of foot ulcers and neuropathy.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** infection risk and complications in diabetes

**Core Management Principles:**
- Patients with diabetes are at higher risk of pulmonary infections (e.g., pneumococcus, influenza) leading to increased morbidity and mortality.
- Diabetes increases susceptibility to infections with organisms like Mycobacterium tuberculosis, Staphylococcus aureus, gram-negative bacilli, and fungi (e.g., Cryptococcus, Candida, Mucor).

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Routine screening for infections in diabetic patients, particularly those with poor glycemic control.
- Vaccination against pneumococcus and influenza recommended for diabetic patients.

### Source 3: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes complications research and management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Acute complications (hypo-/hyperglycaemic emergencies) require immediate glucose monitoring and tailored treatment based on consciousness level.
- Chronic complications (microvascular, macrovascular, diabetic foot) necessitate early, proactive screening even in prediabetes due to late-stage symptom onset.
- Comprehensive initial assessment includes detailed history, examination, and preliminary tests, with additional investigations for high-risk patients.
- Referral to specialists (e.g., retinal specialist) is mandatory for organ-specific complications or high-risk conditions.
- Management emphasizes patient compliance, medication review, and addressing comorbidities (e.g., hypertension, kidney disease).
- Recurrent hypoglycaemia requires checking for hypothyroidism in addition to renal function
- DKA is characterized by metabolic acidosis with abdominal pain, breathlessness, vomiting, altered sensorium, and dehydration

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient must have confirmed diabetes diagnosis or prediabetes for early complication screening.
- Glycemic control history (HbA1C, blood glucose) and medication compliance must be documented.
- High-risk patients (e.g., with hypertension, kidney disease) require additional investigations.
- Consciousness level and glucose monitoring availability dictate hypoglycaemia treatment approach.
- Organ-specific symptoms or abnormal preliminary tests trigger specialist referral.
- Documentation of duration of diabetes, medication doses, and compliance with medication and lifestyle measures during initial assessment
- Assessment of tuberculosis history as part of comprehensive initial evaluation for high-risk patients


### DPP-4 inhibitor use

DPP-4 inhibitor use in diabetes care. DPP-4 inhibitors have few side effects and minimal risk of hypoglycemia. Relatively weak glucose-lowering agents compared to GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors. May be useful in older adults with mild hyperglycemia or high hypoglycemia risk. Alternative to metformin in older adults with low eGFR DPP-4 inhibitor use. DPP-4 inhibitor use. Inhibit DPP-4 enzyme to increase incretin hormones (GLP-1 and GIP), stimulating glucose-dependent insulin secretion and suppressing glucagon release. Do not cause hypoglycemia as monotherapy and are weight-neutral. Used as monotherapy, second-line with metformin, or in combination with other oral antihyperglycemic agents or insulin

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DPP-4 inhibitor use in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- DPP-4 inhibitors have few side effects and minimal risk of hypoglycemia
- Relatively weak glucose-lowering agents compared to GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors
- May be useful in older adults with mild hyperglycemia or high hypoglycemia risk
- Alternative to metformin in older adults with low eGFR
- Linagliptin does not require renal dose adjustment
- Cost may be a barrier to some older adults

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Older adults with mild hyperglycemia
- High risk of hypoglycemia
- Intolerance to GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs
- Low eGFR (for alternative to metformin)
- Patient-specific factors favoring minimal side effects

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DPP-4 inhibitor use

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Inhibit DPP-4 enzyme to increase incretin hormones (GLP-1 and GIP), stimulating glucose-dependent insulin secretion and suppressing glucagon release
- Do not cause hypoglycemia as monotherapy and are weight-neutral
- Used as monotherapy, second-line with metformin, or in combination with other oral antihyperglycemic agents or insulin
- Most administered as a single daily dose except vildagliptin (twice daily)
- Dosage reduction required in renal insufficiency except for linagliptin, teneligliptin, and gemigliptin (with caution in advanced renal disease)
- Gliptins have a duration of action ranging from 3 hours (vildagliptin) to 24 hours (sitagliptin), influencing dosing frequency and patient adherence

**Prerequisites:**
- Presence of type 2 diabetes mellitus requiring glycemic control
- Renal function assessment prior to initiation (except for linagliptin, teneligliptin, gemigliptin)
- Consideration of combination therapy with metformin as preferred second-line option
- Monitoring for renal function during therapy (adjust dose as needed)
- Assessment of hypoglycemia risk when combined with sulfonylureas or insulin
- Assessment of predominant mode of excretion (renal vs. hepatic) for drug selection in patients with mixed hepatic-renal dysfunction


### SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA risk

SGLT inhibitors are not approved for use in people with type 1 diabetes due to increased DKA risk.. Educate at-risk individuals about DKA signs, symptoms, mitigation, and management.. Prescribe accurate tools for ketone measurement in at-risk patients. SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA risk. SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA risk. Euglycemic DKA (blood glucose <250 mg/dl) can occur with SGLT2 inhibitors, particularly in pregnancy, alcohol intake, or reduced carbohydrate intake.. DKA is a leading cause of morbidity and mortality in children with T1DM, with high case fatality rates in severe cases.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA risk

**Core Recommendations:**
- SGLT inhibitors are not approved for use in people with type 1 diabetes due to increased DKA risk.
- Educate at-risk individuals about DKA signs, symptoms, mitigation, and management.
- Prescribe accurate tools for ketone measurement in at-risk patients.
- Individuals who have experienced DKA should not be treated with SGLT inhibition.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Risk assessment for DKA in individuals with type 1 or insulin-deficient type 2 diabetes before SGLT inhibitor therapy.
- Monitoring for very-low-carbohydrate eating patterns, prolonged fasting, dehydration, and excessive alcohol intake.
- Patient education on DKA symptoms and management strategies.
- Recognition that up to a third of SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA cases present with glucose levels <200 mg/dL, necessitating specific education on euglycemic presentation.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of SGLT inhibitors in type 1 diabetes without strict risk mitigation protocols.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Emphasis on ketone measurement tool prescription for at-risk patients.
- Guidance on DKA risk mitigation and management in section 6 of updated guidelines.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT inhibitor-associated DKA risk

**Core Management Principles:**
- Euglycemic DKA (blood glucose <250 mg/dl) can occur with SGLT2 inhibitors, particularly in pregnancy, alcohol intake, or reduced carbohydrate intake.
- DKA is a leading cause of morbidity and mortality in children with T1DM, with high case fatality rates in severe cases.
- Cerebral edema is a major contributor to DKA-related mortality, especially in young children with severe presentations.
- Omission of insulin is the most common cause of recurrent DKA, particularly in adolescents, requiring psycho-social assessment.
- DKA at T1DM diagnosis is more common in children <5 years and those with poor socioeconomic background, with a 28.7% prevalence reported in Indian youth (YDR registry).
- Cerebral edema occurs in 0.5-1% of children with DKA and accounts for 60-90% of all DKA-related deaths in children, with a mortality rate of 20-25%.
- Insulin omission is the most common cause of recurrent DKA, particularly in adolescents, and may be influenced by use of non-injectable 'alternate systems of medicine' in some regions.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- SGLT inhibitor use should be avoided in patients with conditions predisposing to DKA (e.g., pregnancy, alcohol use, low carbohydrate intake).
- Close monitoring for euglycemic DKA symptoms (e.g., nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain) is required in patients on SGLT inhibitors.
- Insulin omission must be addressed with psycho-social support in recurrent DKA cases.
- Detailed psycho-social assessment is required for patients with recurrent DKA due to insulin omission, particularly in adolescents.
- Risk mitigation strategies must be implemented when considering SGLT inhibitor use in patients with high DKA risk (e.g., pregnancy, alcohol use, low carbohydrate intake).


### SGLT2 inhibitor cardiovascular outcomes

SGLT2 inhibitor cardiovascular outcomes and hospital management. SGLT2 inhibitors reduce rates of MI, stroke, or cardiovascular death with empagliflozin and canagliflozin.. Dapagliflozin reduces hospitalization for heart failure or cardiovascular death despite no reduction in MI/stroke.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT2 inhibitor cardiovascular outcomes and hospital management

**Core Recommendations:**
- SGLT2 inhibitors reduce rates of MI, stroke, or cardiovascular death with empagliflozin and canagliflozin.
- Dapagliflozin reduces hospitalization for heart failure or cardiovascular death despite no reduction in MI/stroke.
- Ertugliflozin and bexagliflozin have not demonstrated evidence of cardiovascular risk reduction.
- SGLT2 inhibitors should be initiated or continued during hospitalization for heart failure if indicated and no contraindications exist.
- SGLT2 inhibitors should be stopped 3 days before scheduled surgeries (4 days for ertugliflozin).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 2 diabetes and heart failure for hospital initiation/continuation.
- No contraindications such as severe illness, ketonemia, ketonuria, or prolonged fasting.
- Acute illness must have recovered before initiating SGLT2 inhibitors in the hospital setting.
- Proactive adjustment of insulin and diuretic dosing is required during hospitalization.
- Collaboration with a cardiology/heart failure consultation team is recommended.
- Admission glucose must be <180–200 mg/dL for the use of DPP-4 inhibitors with or without basal insulin in hospitalized patients with mild to moderate hyperglycemia.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors for glycemic management in the hospital setting.
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors during prolonged fasting and surgical procedures.
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors in cases of severe illness.
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors in patients with ketonemia or ketonuria.
- Continuation of saxagliptin and alogliptin in patients who develop heart failure.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Initiation or continuation of SGLT2 inhibitors during hospitalization for heart failure.
- Specific preoperative discontinuation timing: 3 days for most SGLT2 inhibitors, 4 days for ertugliflozin.
- Proactive adjustment of insulin and diuretic dosing during hospitalization and discharge.
- Individualized A1C targets based on patient-specific factors including life expectancy and hypoglycemia risk.
- Use of DPP-4 inhibitors with or without basal insulin for mild to moderate hyperglycemia on admission.


### intensive glycemic control benefits

Intensive glycemic control benefits and individualized A1C targets. Intensive glycemic control (mean A1C ~7%) reduces microvascular complications (retinopathy, neuropathy, chronic kidney disease) by 50–76% in type 1 diabetes (DCCT/EDIC). intensive glycemic control benefits. Intensive glycemic control benefits in T1DM. Early intensive glycemic control reduces long-term macrovascular and microvascular complications in T1DM. Intensive control lowers risk of CVD events by 42% and non-fatal myocardial infarction/stroke/CV death by 57% over 17 years (DCCT/EDIC)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Intensive glycemic control benefits and individualized A1C targets

**Core Recommendations:**
- Intensive glycemic control (mean A1C ~7%) reduces microvascular complications (retinopathy, neuropathy, chronic kidney disease) by 50–76% in type 1 diabetes (DCCT/EDIC).
- Early glycemic lowering in type 2 diabetes reduces microvascular complications, with long-term benefits persisting despite diminished glycemic separation (UKPDS, Kumamoto).
- A curvilinear relationship exists between A1C and microvascular complications; greatest benefits occur when lowering from very high to moderate A1C levels.
- Further A1C reduction from 7% to 6% yields smaller absolute risk reductions but increases hypoglycemia risk with hypoglycemia-prone medications (e.g., insulin, sulfonylureas).
- CGM and automated insulin delivery systems improve glycemic control without increasing hypoglycemia, enabling safer achievement of lower A1C goals.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 1 or type 2 diabetes with sufficient life expectancy to derive long-term benefits from glycemic lowering.
- Low hypoglycemia risk or use of non-hypoglycemic medications (e.g., metformin, GLP-1 RAs, SGLT2 inhibitors).
- Individualized A1C goals based on duration of diabetes, age, life expectancy, comorbid conditions, and hypoglycemia risk (Fig. 6.1).
- Shared decision-making incorporating patient preferences, resources, and support systems.
- Postprandial glucose monitoring if A1C goals are unmet despite preprandial glucose targets.
- Individuals must have sufficient life expectancy to derive long-term benefit from glucose lowering.
- Goals must be individualized based on known cardiovascular disease or advanced microvascular complications.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Near-normalization of A1C (e.g., <6%) in long-standing type 2 diabetes using high-risk hypoglycemic medications (e.g., insulin, sulfonylureas) without cardio-kidney-metabolic benefits.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, hypoglycemia risk).
- Intensive glycemic control in older adults with complex/intermediate or very complex/poor health without individualized goal adjustment.
- Reliance on pre-DCCT/UKPDS glycemic management strategies (e.g., insulin monotherapy without CGM or automated systems).
- Postprandial glucose monitoring timing not aligned with meal-induced peak levels (1–2 hours post-meal).
- Treating diabetes to near-normal A1C goals in people with long-standing type 2 diabetes using medications with a high risk for hypoglycemia and with other adverse effects and no additional cardio-kidney-metabolic benefits.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of CGM and automated insulin delivery systems to achieve glycemic goals with reduced hypoglycemia risk.
- Prioritization of non-hypoglycemic medication classes (e.g., GLP-1 RAs, SGLT2 inhibitors) for intensive glycemic control.
- Individualized A1C targets favoring more stringent goals in patients with short diabetes duration, low hypoglycemia risk, and long life expectancy.
- Emphasis on early glycemic lowering in type 2 diabetes to maximize long-term microvascular benefits.
- Shared decision-making frameworks for glycemic goal setting, incorporating patient preferences and support systems.
- Use of dual GIP and GLP-1 RA as a glucose-lowering medication class unlikely to cause hypoglycemia.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Intensive glycemic control benefits in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Early intensive glycemic control reduces long-term macrovascular and microvascular complications in T1DM
- Intensive control lowers risk of CVD events by 42% and non-fatal myocardial infarction/stroke/CV death by 57% over 17 years (DCCT/EDIC)
- Intensive control reduces carotid intima-media thickness progression, a marker of CVD risk
- Intensive control lowers incident hypertension risk by 24% (HR 0.76) in long-term follow-up
- Hyperglycemia contributes to endothelial dysfunction, oxidative stress, and atherosclerotic CVD progression
- Acute hyperglycemia is associated with poor post-stroke outcomes (both ischemic and hemorrhagic stroke), increasing cerebral lactate production and reducing salvageable penumbral tissue in ischemic stroke, or expanding hematoma and perihematomal edema in hemorrhagic stroke
- Uncontrolled diabetes contributes to dyslipidemia, as shown in the SEARCH for Diabetes in Youth study, which may exacerbate cardiovascular risk in T1DM

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1DM)
- No prior history of coronary artery disease (for macrovascular risk assessment)
- Baseline assessment of glycemic control (HbA1c) and cardiovascular risk factors
- Regular monitoring of blood pressure and kidney function (e.g., albuminuria, GFR)
- Patient eligibility for intensive glycemic control based on age, life expectancy, and hypoglycemia risk
- Assessment of dyslipidemia as part of baseline cardiovascular risk evaluation in T1DM
- Evaluation of stroke risk and acute hyperglycemia management protocols in T1DM patients with cerebrovascular events


### oral antidiabetic agent comparison

oral antidiabetic agent comparison and selection. Select oral anti-hyperglycaemic agents based on their mechanism of action targeting gut glucose absorption, peripheral glucose uptake, hepatic glucose output, pancreatic hormone secretion, incretin effects, or renal glucose handling

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** oral antidiabetic agent comparison and selection

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Select oral anti-hyperglycaemic agents based on their mechanism of action targeting gut glucose absorption, peripheral glucose uptake, hepatic glucose output, pancreatic hormone secretion, incretin effects, or renal glucose handling
- Prefer newer sulfonylureas (Glimepiride, Gliclazide) over older agents due to updated clinical profiles
- Monitor for continued drug efficacy and development of side-effects during therapy
- Consider insulin initiation early in patients with marked hyperglycemia, severe symptoms, acute complications, or long-standing undiagnosed diabetes with complications
- Anticipate reduced efficacy of oral agents over time and plan for poly-pharmacy or timely insulin introduction

**Prerequisites:**
- Metformin is the preferred first-line oral agent unless contraindicated
- If metformin is contraindicated, monotherapy can be initiated with any other class of oral agents
- Assess patient-specific factors such as hypoglycemia risk, weight impact, cost, and comorbidities when selecting agents
- Evaluate renal function and hepatic status before prescribing agents metabolized or excreted via these routes
- Initiate lifestyle modification alongside pharmacotherapy at diagnosis
- Initiate pharmacotherapy alongside lifestyle modification for the majority of patients at the time of diagnosis
- Consider continuous insulin therapy if initial assessment reveals complications such as diabetic retinopathy or nephropathy, indicating long-standing undiagnosed diabetes


### cardiovascular risk reduction therapy

Use GLP-1 RA or SGLT2i irrespective of glycemic goal attainment in patients with HF, CKD, established CVD, or multiple risk factors. Initiate insulin regardless of background therapy if symptoms of hyperglycemia are present or A1C >10% / blood glucose ≥300 mg/dL

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Cardiovascular risk reduction therapy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use GLP-1 RA or SGLT2i irrespective of glycemic goal attainment in patients with HF, CKD, established CVD, or multiple risk factors
- Initiate insulin regardless of background therapy if symptoms of hyperglycemia are present or A1C >10% / blood glucose ≥300 mg/dL
- Continue glucose-lowering agents when initiating insulin therapy to maintain ongoing glycemic and metabolic benefits
- Avoid concurrent use of DPP-4 inhibitors with GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA due to lack of additional glucose-lowering benefit
- Reevaluate medication plans every 3–6 months to adjust for individualized goals and comorbidities
- Reassess the need for and/or dose of medications with higher hypoglycemia risk (e.g., sulfonylureas, meglitinides, insulin) when initiating a new glucose-lowering medication to minimize hypoglycemia and treatment burden

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 2 diabetes with established ASCVD, HF, CKD, or indicators of high CVD risk
- Presence of symptoms of hyperglycemia or very high A1C (>10%) or blood glucose (≥300 mg/dL) for insulin initiation
- Biopsy-proven MASH or high risk for liver fibrosis for consideration of GLP-1 RA or pioglitazone
- Individualized assessment of hypoglycemia risk, treatment burden, and patient preferences
- Absence of contraindications or intolerance to specific glucose-lowering agents
- Presence of symptomatic heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) for consideration of GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA therapy

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualization based on patient-specific factors
- Concurrent use of DPP-4 inhibitors with GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA
- Delaying treatment modification for adults not meeting individualized treatment goals
- Prioritizing weight and glucose management over mitigation of diabetes-related complications in algorithm design
- Use of sulfonylureas or DPP-4 inhibitors as preferred initial therapy over GLP-1 RA in moderate CVD risk

**Updated Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RA and SGLT2i recommended independent of A1C attainment for patients with established or high risk of ASCVD, HF, or CKD
- GLP-1 RA preferred over insulin for initial or add-on therapy in adults without severe hyperglycemia or hyperglycemic crisis
- Combination therapy with GLP-1 RA and insulin recommended for greater glycemic effectiveness and reduced hypoglycemia risk
- GLP-1 RA and dual GIP/GLP-1 RA recommended for glycemic management and MASH treatment in patients with biopsy-proven MASH or high risk of liver fibrosis
- Integration of social determinants of health and shared decision-making tools in glucose-lowering management


### alpha-glucosidase inhibitor therapy

Ingest drug with the first bite of food to ensure presence in the small bowel. Start with minimum dose at any one meal and increase to maximum tolerable dose. Use oral glucose rather than sucrose to treat hypoglycaemia in combination therapy. Utilize agents to decrease post-prandial glucose levels

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** alpha-glucosidase inhibitor therapy

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Ingest drug with the first bite of food to ensure presence in the small bowel
- Start with minimum dose at any one meal and increase to maximum tolerable dose
- Use oral glucose rather than sucrose to treat hypoglycaemia in combination therapy
- Utilize agents to decrease post-prandial glucose levels
- Combine with all other oral and injectable anti-hyperglycaemic agents

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be able to take medication with the first bite of every meal
- Dose titration must begin at the minimum recommended dose (e.g., 25mg for acarbose/miglitol, 0.2mg for voglibose)
- Monitoring for gastrointestinal side effects including bloating, abdominal discomfort, diarrhoea, and flatulence
- Assessment of renal function if using miglitol due to predominant renal excretion
- Evaluation of potential for hypoglycaemia when used in combination with other agents


### heart failure treatment guidelines

Heart Failure Prevention and Treatment in Diabetes. Implement early primary prevention strategies including lifestyle intervention, nutrition, physical activity, weight management, and smoking cessation.. Manage hypertension intensively using ACE inhibitors or ARBs as the preferred strategy, particularly in the presence of albuminuria or coronary artery disease.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Heart Failure Prevention and Treatment in Diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Implement early primary prevention strategies including lifestyle intervention, nutrition, physical activity, weight management, and smoking cessation.
- Manage hypertension intensively using ACE inhibitors or ARBs as the preferred strategy, particularly in the presence of albuminuria or coronary artery disease.
- Treat asymptomatic patients with structural heart disease (e.g., LVEF ≤40%) with ACE inhibitors/ARBs plus β-blockers to prevent progression to symptomatic heart failure.
- Prescribe SGLT2 inhibitors for patients with type 2 diabetes and established HFpEF or HFrEF to reduce hospitalization and cardiovascular death.
- Consider adding finerenone in patients with type 2 diabetes and CKD with albuminuria to improve cardiovascular outcomes and reduce heart failure hospitalization risk.
- Assess susceptibility to diabetic ketoacidosis and provide education regarding risks, symptoms, and prevention strategies for patients on SGLT2 inhibitors.
- Prescribe home monitoring supplies for beta-hydroxybutyrate to minimize the risk of diabetic ketoacidosis in patients using SGLT2 inhibitors.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 2 diabetes and established cardiovascular disease or increased cardiovascular risk for SGLT2 inhibitor initiation.
- Patient must have eGFR ≥ 20 mL/min/1.73 m² and potassium < 5.0 mEq/L for SGLT2 inhibitor use.
- Patient must have symptomatic stage C or D heart failure or history of MI with reduced LVEF for standard guideline-directed medical therapy.
- Patient must have type 2 diabetes and CKD with albuminuria treated with maximum tolerated doses of ACE inhibitor or ARB for finerenone consideration.
- Patient must have symptomatic HFpEF (ejection fraction ≥40%) for finerenone consideration in heart failure outcomes.
- Patient must have symptomatic HFpEF (ejection fraction ≥40%) for finerenone consideration specifically for heart failure outcomes.
- Patient must have eGFR ≥30 mL/min/1.73 m² for the use of SGLT1/2 inhibitors as an alternative to SGLT2 inhibitors.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults without individualization based on life expectancy and hypoglycemia risk.
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors in patients with eGFR < 20 mL/min/1.73 m² without specific alternative protocols.
- Reliance on glucose-lowering activity alone as the primary mechanism for heart failure benefit in SGLT2 inhibitors.
- Exclusion of patients with type 1 diabetes from SGLT2 inhibitor cardiovascular outcome trials without specific risk assessment.
- Use of non-selective MRAs without consideration of nonsteroidal alternatives like finerenone in diabetic kidney disease.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recommendation to assess susceptibility, provide education, and prescribe home monitoring supplies for β-hydroxybutyrate to minimize diabetic ketoacidosis risk with SGLT2 inhibitors.
- Use of sotagliflozin (SGLT1/2 inhibitor) initiated during or shortly after hospitalization for heart failure in people with diabetes.
- Extension of SGLT2 inhibitor benefits to patients with heart failure irrespective of the presence or absence of type 2 diabetes.
- Consideration of finerenone addition in patients with symptomatic HFpEF to improve cardiovascular and kidney outcomes.
- Integration of collaborative approaches with cardiovascular specialists for management of symptomatic stage C and D heart failure in people with diabetes.


### glycemic management guidelines

Comprehensive glycemic management. Assess glycemic status regularly using A1C, fasting glucose, and continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) where appropriate.. Set individualized glycemic goals based on patient-specific factors such as age, comorbidities, and hypoglycemia risk. glycemic management guidelines. GLP-1 RA use in T2D with CKD after metformin/SGLT2 failure. Use long-acting GLP-1 RA in adults with T2D and CKD who fail to meet individualized glycemic targets despite metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor therapy. Select GLP-1 RA agents with documented cardiovascular benefits

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Comprehensive glycemic management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Assess glycemic status regularly using A1C, fasting glucose, and continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) where appropriate.
- Set individualized glycemic goals based on patient-specific factors such as age, comorbidities, and hypoglycemia risk.
- Prevent and manage hypoglycemia with structured education, glucose monitoring, and treatment protocols.
- Adjust diabetes management during intercurrent illness to prevent hyperglycemia and ketoacidosis.
- Diagnose and manage hyperglycemic crises (DKA and HHS) with standardized protocols for fluid resuscitation, insulin therapy, and electrolyte correction.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Baseline glycemic assessment including A1C, fasting glucose, and patient history.
- Evaluation of patient comorbidities, life expectancy, and hypoglycemia risk before setting glycemic goals.
- Patient education on hypoglycemia recognition, prevention, and treatment.
- Sick-day management plan for patients with intercurrent illness.
- Access to emergency protocols for hyperglycemic crises management.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors.
- Routine use of urine glucose testing as the primary method for glycemic monitoring.
- One-size-fits-all glycemic management without individualized adjustments.
- Delayed treatment of hyperglycemic crises without standardized protocols.
- Exclusive reliance on fasting glucose for glycemic assessment without CGM or postprandial glucose monitoring.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Incorporation of CGM data for real-time glycemic trend analysis and hypoglycemia prevention.
- Use of time-in-range (TIR) metrics as a complementary goal to A1C for glycemic assessment.
- Structured hypoglycemia risk stratification and prevention strategies.
- Standardized sick-day management protocols for diabetes patients during illness.
- Evidence-based protocols for hyperglycemic crises management with emphasis on early intervention.

### Source 2: Chronic Kidney Disease.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 RA use in T2D with CKD after metformin/SGLT2 failure

**Clinical Actions:**
- Use long-acting GLP-1 RA in adults with T2D and CKD who fail to meet individualized glycemic targets despite metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor therapy
- Select GLP-1 RA agents with documented cardiovascular benefits
- Consider patient-specific factors (e.g., eGFR, risk of hypoglycemia, gastrointestinal tolerability) when selecting a long-acting GLP-1 RA in CKD patients with T2D

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Adults with type 2 diabetes (T2D) and chronic kidney disease (CKD)
- Failure to achieve individualized glycemic targets despite metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor use
- Inability to use metformin or SGLT2 inhibitors
- Documented failure to achieve individualized glycemic targets despite optimized metformin and SGLT2 inhibitor therapy (or contraindications to these agents)
- Assessment of cardiovascular risk profile to guide GLP-1 RA selection with documented cardiovascular benefits


### pioglitazone safety profile

Low risk for hypoglycemia. Use cautiously in select individuals due to risks for heart failure, fluid retention, weight gain, osteoporosis, and/or macular edema. Adverse effect risks reduced with lower doses and combination therapy. May have beneficial effect of stroke reduction pioglitazone safety profile. pioglitazone safety profile. Weight gain may be significant and dose-related. Edema and cardiac failure are reported, especially when combined with insulin. Other adverse events include anemia and hemodilution. Higher risk of fractures, especially in post-menopausal women. Bladder cancer has been reported with prolonged use

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pioglitazone safety profile

**Core Recommendations:**
- Low risk for hypoglycemia
- Use cautiously in select individuals due to risks for heart failure, fluid retention, weight gain, osteoporosis, and/or macular edema
- Adverse effect risks reduced with lower doses and combination therapy
- May have beneficial effect of stroke reduction
- May increase risk of bladder cancer with long-term use (>1 year), particularly in males with a history of smoking or hematuria
- Associated with increased risk of fractures in postmenopausal women and elderly men
- May exacerbate or precipitate heart failure in patients with NYHA Class III or IV heart failure

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Use in select individuals only
- Consider lower doses to minimize adverse effects
- Use as part of combination therapy to reduce risks
- Baseline assessment of cardiac function (e.g., echocardiogram or BNP levels) in patients with known heart failure risk factors
- Renal function monitoring (e.g., serum creatinine, eGFR) due to potential fluid retention effects
- Baseline and periodic liver function tests (LFTs) to monitor for hepatotoxicity
- Avoid initiation in patients with active bladder cancer or history of bladder cancer

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pioglitazone safety profile

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Weight gain may be significant and dose-related
- Edema and cardiac failure are reported, especially when combined with insulin
- Other adverse events include anemia and hemodilution
- Higher risk of fractures, especially in post-menopausal women
- Bladder cancer has been reported with prolonged use
- Macular edema has been reported, particularly in patients with pre-existing retinopathy

**Prerequisites:**
- Type 1 diabetes exclusion
- History of cardiac failure exclusion
- Pregnancy and lactation exclusion
- Moderate to severe anemia exclusion
- History of bladder cancer or unexplained hematuria exclusion; known risk factors for bladder cancer must be assessed before starting pioglitazone
- Assess liver function tests (ALT, AST) before and periodically during treatment due to potential hepatotoxicity


### DKA prevention in pediatric T1DM

DKA prevention and cerebral edema management in pediatric T1DM. Early contact with healthcare provider during illness to emphasize insulin importance and review blood glucose goals.. Initiation of easily digestible liquid diet containing carbohydrates and salt when nauseated, with plenty of fluids.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DKA prevention and cerebral edema management in pediatric T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Early contact with healthcare provider during illness to emphasize insulin importance and review blood glucose goals.
- Initiation of easily digestible liquid diet containing carbohydrates and salt when nauseated, with plenty of fluids.
- Education of family members on sick day management, including monitoring temperature, blood glucose, ketones, and insulin administration.
- Use of home glucose-ketone meters to enable early recognition of impending ketoacidosis and guide home insulin therapy.
- Counseling families that insulin is the only effective treatment and discouraging the use of non-injectable alternative medications.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Access to medical care and effective communication with healthcare providers during intercurrent illness.
- Availability of medications to suppress fever and treat infections.
- Ability to perform home glucose and ketone testing.
- Financial stability to prevent insulin withholding due to economic reasons.
- Immediate initiation of treatment for cerebral edema pending neuroimaging results.


### weight-beneficial diabetes pharmacotherapy

Prioritize GLP-1 receptor agonists or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists for patients with diabetes and overweight/obesity due to superior weight loss efficacy.. Continue obesity pharmacotherapy beyond reaching weight loss goals to maintain health benefits and prevent weight regain.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Weight-beneficial diabetes pharmacotherapy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Prioritize GLP-1 receptor agonists or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists for patients with diabetes and overweight/obesity due to superior weight loss efficacy.
- Continue obesity pharmacotherapy beyond reaching weight loss goals to maintain health benefits and prevent weight regain.
- Individualize dosing and titration approaches to balance effectiveness, health benefits, and tolerability rather than automatically using maximum approved doses.
- Minimize use of weight-promoting medications for other conditions among adults with diabetes and obesity.
- Modify or intensify treatment with lifestyle programs, metabolic surgery, or alternative agents if weight treatment goals are not reached.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have diabetes and overweight or obesity.
- Patient should have stable insurance coverage for preferred agents.
- Treatment should be clinically appropriate and tailored to individual tolerability.
- Weight treatment goals must be defined and monitored.
- Other weight-promoting medications should be reviewed for potential discontinuation.
- Patient must have stable insurance coverage for preferred agents.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Automatic use of maximum approved doses without individualization.
- Discontinuation of obesity pharmacotherapy once weight loss goals are reached.
- Prioritization of weight-neutral or weight-gain associated agents over weight-loss agents when weight management is a goal.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Semaglutide and tirzepatide as preferred agents for weight loss in diabetes.
- Structured lifestyle management programs and metabolic surgery as additional intensification strategies.
- Recognition of sodium–glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitors, metformin, and amylin mimetics as agents with smaller but meaningful weight loss effects.
- Categorization of dipeptidyl peptidase 4 inhibitors, bromocriptine, α-glucosidase inhibitors, and colesevelam as weight neutral.
- Identification of sulfonylureas, meglitinides, thiazolidinediones, and insulin as agents often associated with weight gain.
- Agents associated with clinically meaningful weight loss include glucagon-like peptide 1 (GLP-1) receptor agonists and dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists, which are the preferred agents in individuals with stable insurance coverage.


### hyperglycemic retinopathy mechanisms

hyperglycemic retinopathy pathophysiology and progression. Chronic hyperglycemia drives retinopathy via polyol pathway accumulation, non-enzymatic protein glycation, PKC activation, and RAAS changes.. Mechanisms induce oxidative stress through increased reactive oxygen species (ROS) and reduced free oxygen clearance.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hyperglycemic retinopathy pathophysiology and progression

**Core Management Principles:**
- Chronic hyperglycemia drives retinopathy via polyol pathway accumulation, non-enzymatic protein glycation, PKC activation, and RAAS changes.
- Mechanisms induce oxidative stress through increased reactive oxygen species (ROS) and reduced free oxygen clearance.
- Endothelial damage and pericyte loss lead to blood-retinal barrier breakdown, leakage, edema, and microaneurysm formation.
- Capillary basement membrane thickening and extracellular matrix deposition cause capillary occlusion and retinal ischemia.
- Ischemia stimulates VEGF production, triggering angiogenesis and neovascularization that result in proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR).
- Hemodynamic changes (e.g., increased blood flow and shear stress) contribute to endothelial dysfunction and vascular damage in retinal capillaries under hyperglycemia.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Recognition of chronic hyperglycemia as the primary risk factor for retinopathy development.
- Monitoring for clinical signs of endothelial damage, pericyte loss, and capillary occlusion.
- Assessment of VEGF-mediated pathways in cases of proliferative changes or neovascularization.
- Evaluation of oxidative stress markers and inflammatory responses in retinal tissue.
- Differentiation between non-proliferative and proliferative stages based on ischemia and neovascularization.
- Monitoring for subclinical inflammation and leukostasis as early indicators of retinal vascular damage in T1DM patients.


### retinopathy risk and metabolic memory

retinopathy risk and metabolic memory in T1DM. Early glycemic control provides long-term benefits on retinopathy (DCCT/EDIC 'metabolic memory'), even if later glycemic control deteriorates.. Intensive blood pressure control is actively pursued for overall cardiovascular and nephropathy benefits, though retinopathy-specific reduction is modest.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** retinopathy risk and metabolic memory in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Early glycemic control provides long-term benefits on retinopathy (DCCT/EDIC 'metabolic memory'), even if later glycemic control deteriorates.
- Intensive blood pressure control is actively pursued for overall cardiovascular and nephropathy benefits, though retinopathy-specific reduction is modest.
- Ophthalmologic screening intervals are tailored by DR severity: annual for absent/mild NPDR, 6 months for moderate NPDR, 4 months for severe NPDR, 3 months for PDR, and monthly for DME.
- Laser treatment is required for PDR with high-risk characteristics (e.g., NVD ≥1/3 disc area or vitreous hemorrhage).
- Vision loss in DR may result from CSME, PDR complications (e.g., pre-retinal hemorrhage), or neovascular glaucoma.
- Early worsening of DR may occur with rapid glycemic control, particularly in patients with higher baseline HbA1c, though serious vision loss is rare and long-term benefits are clear.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Optimal hypertension control should be actively pursued for cardiovascular and nephropathy benefits, even if retinopathy-specific reduction is modest.
- Ophthalmologic follow-up frequency must align with DR severity (e.g., monthly for DME, 3-monthly for PDR).
- Laser treatment must be initiated promptly for PDR with high-risk characteristics to prevent severe vision loss.
- Optimal control of blood pressure, lipids, proteinuria, and smoking cessation should be actively pursued for overall microvascular and cardiovascular benefits, even if retinopathy-specific reductions are modest.


### gliptin therapy adjustments

Dose of sitagliptin, vildagliptin, and saxagliptin should be reduced in renal impairment. Teneligliptin and gemigliptin should be used with caution in advanced renal disease. Linagliptin can be continued at unchanged doses in renal impairment. Saxagliptin should be avoided in cardiac failure

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** gliptin therapy adjustments

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Dose of sitagliptin, vildagliptin, and saxagliptin should be reduced in renal impairment
- Teneligliptin and gemigliptin should be used with caution in advanced renal disease
- Linagliptin can be continued at unchanged doses in renal impairment
- Saxagliptin should be avoided in cardiac failure
- Monitor for acute pancreatitis and allergic reactions to gliptins

**Prerequisites:**
- Renal function assessment required before and during gliptin therapy
- Cardiac evaluation for patients considered for saxagliptin


### glycemic control impact on diabetic retinopathy

Glycemic control remains paramount in management, as established by DCCT.. Controlling hyperglycemia is associated with reduction in the incidence as well as progression of diabetic retinopathy (DR).

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** glycemic control impact on diabetic retinopathy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Glycemic control remains paramount in management, as established by DCCT.
- Controlling hyperglycemia is associated with reduction in the incidence as well as progression of diabetic retinopathy (DR).
- Each 10% proportional decline in HbA1c (e.g., from 9.0% to 8.1%) is associated with a 39% reduction in DR progression.
- HbA1c targets should be individualized based on patient factors such as age, duration of diabetes, and risk of hypoglycemia, with stricter targets (e.g., <7.0%) for those at lower hypoglycemic risk and less stringent targets for older adults or those with limited life expectancy.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- HbA1c monitoring is essential for assessing glycemic control and DR risk.
- Regular retinal screening is mandated at diagnosis for T1DM patients with duration ≥5 years, and annually thereafter if retinopathy is present or progressing.


### perioperative GLP-1 medication management

GLP-1 RAs may cause delayed gastric emptying, increasing residual gastric content despite fasting. FDA added warnings about pulmonary aspiration risk during general anesthesia or deep sedation. Personalized perioperative management is recommended based on drug characteristics, patient factors, and procedure type

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Perioperative GLP-1 medication management

**Core Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RAs may cause delayed gastric emptying, increasing residual gastric content despite fasting
- FDA added warnings about pulmonary aspiration risk during general anesthesia or deep sedation
- Personalized perioperative management is recommended based on drug characteristics, patient factors, and procedure type
- Preoperative gastric ultrasound may be considered to assess gastric contents
- Full stomach precautions and liquid nutrition protocols may be implemented for high-risk patients
- Increased residual gastric content persists despite prolonged fasting (up to 30 days) due to GLP-1 RA's long half-life and delayed gastric emptying effects

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Consider primary indication (diabetes or obesity), glycemic management, drug dose/duration, and gastrointestinal symptoms
- Evaluate type of surgery/procedure, urgency, and anesthesia type
- Assess patient risk for delayed gastric emptying (e.g., nausea, vomiting, dyspepsia)
- Implement alternative glycemic management (e.g., insulin) if GLP-1 RA is held and hyperglycemia is anticipated
- Use gastric ultrasound or other assessments to quantify gastric contents preoperatively
- Assess primary indication (diabetes vs. obesity) as it influences risk stratification and management decisions
- Evaluate urgency of surgery/procedure (elective vs. emergent) to determine appropriateness of personalized vs. conservative approaches

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine holding of GLP-1 RAs on the day of procedure for daily doses and 7 days prior for weekly doses
- Fixed withdrawal periods without considering individual patient factors
- One-size-fits-all fasting protocols without personalized risk assessment
- Withholding GLP-1 RAs for 7 days prior to weekly-dose procedures as a fixed protocol

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Personalized perioperative GLP-1 RA management based on comprehensive risk stratification
- Consideration of continuing GLP-1 RAs in low-risk elective surgery patients
- Implementation of liquid nutrition protocols 24 hours pre-procedure for high-risk patients
- Use of gastric ultrasound to quantify residual gastric content preoperatively
- Full stomach precautions for patients with symptoms of delayed gastric emptying
- Consider continuing GLP-1 RAs in low-risk elective surgery patients based on comprehensive risk assessment


### pioglitazone therapy regimen

Pioglitazone is an insulin sensitizer acting at adipose tissue and skeletal muscle via PPAR-γ binding. Dose range: 7.5 to 30 mg once daily. Onset of action: 2–4 weeks; maximum effect: 8–12 weeks. Can be combined with all antihyperglycemic agents except insulin (use cautiously)

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pioglitazone therapy regimen

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Pioglitazone is an insulin sensitizer acting at adipose tissue and skeletal muscle via PPAR-γ binding
- Dose range: 7.5 to 30 mg once daily
- Onset of action: 2–4 weeks; maximum effect: 8–12 weeks
- Can be combined with all antihyperglycemic agents except insulin (use cautiously)
- May induce ovulation in women with anovulatory cycles; provide contraceptive advice
- Inhibits hepatic glucose output in addition to improving insulin sensitivity at adipose tissue and skeletal muscle

**Prerequisites:**
- No specific clinical prerequisites mentioned beyond standard antihyperglycemic therapy considerations
- Provide contraceptive advice to women with anovulatory cycles due to potential ovulation induction


### pioglitazone dosage and pharmacokinetics

Administer pioglitazone once daily. Maintain daily dosage within 7.5–30 mg range. Expect duration of action between 16–24 hours. Monitor renal function due to partial urinary excretion. Monitor hepatic function due to hepatobiliary excretion pathway

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pioglitazone dosage and pharmacokinetics

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Administer pioglitazone once daily
- Maintain daily dosage within 7.5–30 mg range
- Expect duration of action between 16–24 hours

**Prerequisites:**
- Monitor renal function due to partial urinary excretion
- Monitor hepatic function due to hepatobiliary excretion pathway


### pediatric DKA mortality and predictors

Cerebral edema is a leading cause of mortality in pediatric DKA, with risk factors including younger age, new-onset diabetes, high blood urea nitrogen, severe acidosis, and bicarbonate therapy.. Delayed diagnosis or missed presentation of T1DM increases DKA risk at onset, particularly in younger children.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pediatric DKA mortality and predictors

**Core Management Principles:**
- Cerebral edema is a leading cause of mortality in pediatric DKA, with risk factors including younger age, new-onset diabetes, high blood urea nitrogen, severe acidosis, and bicarbonate therapy.
- Delayed diagnosis or missed presentation of T1DM increases DKA risk at onset, particularly in younger children.
- Hypoglycemia unawareness and counterregulatory hormone defects are associated with increased risk of severe hypoglycemia and DKA.
- Insulin omission or inadequate insulin therapy is a common precipitating factor for DKA in established T1DM.
- Infections and poor adherence to insulin therapy contribute to DKA recurrence and poor outcomes.
- Infections (e.g., mucormycosis, malignant otitis externa, necrotizing fasciitis, and injection-site abscesses) are significant contributors to morbidity and mortality in pediatric T1DM, particularly in poorly controlled diabetes.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Early recognition of DKA symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, vomiting) and immediate fluid resuscitation.
