- Monitoring for cerebral edema during DKA management, including neurological assessment and avoidance of rapid fluid shifts.
- Use of standardized DKA protocols with careful insulin and fluid management to prevent complications.
- Education on sick-day management and insulin adjustment to prevent DKA in children with T1DM.
- Regular follow-up to assess glycemic control and address barriers to insulin adherence.
- Use of standardized Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) for neurological assessment in pediatric DKA to monitor for cerebral edema progression.


### pharmacologic treatment efficacy

Pharmacologic treatment efficacy in diabetes care. Medications are evaluated for glucose-lowering efficacy, hypoglycemia risk, weight effects, cardiovascular (CV) effects, kidney effects, and MASH (metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease) effects.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pharmacologic treatment efficacy in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Medications are evaluated for glucose-lowering efficacy, hypoglycemia risk, weight effects, cardiovascular (CV) effects, kidney effects, and MASH (metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease) effects.
- Dosing adjustments are required based on kidney function for certain medications (e.g., DPP-4 inhibitors like sitagliptin, saxagliptin, alogliptin).
- Insulin therapy requires lower doses with decreased eGFR and careful titration to avoid hypoglycemia, especially in patients with impaired kidney function.
- Postmarketing concerns about pancreatitis with DPP-4 inhibitors, leading to recommendations for discontinuation if suspected

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient-specific factors such as kidney function must be assessed before selecting pharmacologic treatments.
- Hypoglycemia risk must be considered, particularly with insulin or human insulin formulations (NPH or premixed).
- Cardiovascular and kidney effects of medications must be evaluated to guide treatment choices.
- Assessment of heart failure (HF) risk is required when considering DPP-4 inhibitors, particularly saxagliptin, due to potential increased risk of HF hospitalization

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, hypoglycemia risk).
- Use of DPP-4 inhibitors with unadjusted dosing in patients with significant kidney impairment (e.g., sitagliptin, saxagliptin, alogliptin).
- Use of human insulin (NPH or premixed formulations) in patients with impaired kidney function without careful titration and hypoglycemia risk assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Linagliptin (DPP-4 inhibitor) does not require dose adjustment based on kidney function.
- Postmarketing concerns about joint pain and bullous pemphigoid with DPP-4 inhibitors, leading to recommendations for discontinuation if suspected.
- Inhaled regular insulin formulation available for patients requiring non-injectable insulin therapy.
- Inhaled regular insulin formulation available as an alternative for patients requiring non-injectable insulin therapy, with specific device compatibility considerations


### intensive glycemic control cardiovascular outcomes

Intensive glycemic control and cardiovascular outcomes. Intensive glycemic management does not consistently reduce composite cardiovascular disease (CVD) events during intervention periods in type 1 or type 2 diabetes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Intensive glycemic control and cardiovascular outcomes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Intensive glycemic management does not consistently reduce composite cardiovascular disease (CVD) events during intervention periods in type 1 or type 2 diabetes.
- Long-term follow-up of intensive glycemic control shows reduced myocardial infarctions and major CVD events, particularly in type 1 diabetes (e.g., EDIC cohort).
- Severe hypoglycemia is strongly associated with increased cardiovascular events and mortality; aggressive near-normal A1C targets should be avoided if unsafe.
- SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists (including dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs) are recommended for individuals with type 2 diabetes and established CVD, chronic kidney disease, or heart failure, regardless of A1C or metformin use.
- Cardiovascular benefits of SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 RAs are mediated by mechanisms beyond glycemic control (e.g., weight loss, hemodynamic effects, anti-inflammatory changes).
- Blood glucose is a weaker independent cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk factor compared to hypertension or hypercholesterolemia, explaining the mixed results of intensive glycemic control trials.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Established cardiovascular disease, chronic kidney disease, or heart failure in individuals with type 2 diabetes for SGLT2 inhibitor or GLP-1 RA initiation.
- Prior treatment with dual or multiple glucose-lowering therapies before considering switching to SGLT2 inhibitors or GLP-1 RAs.
- A1C goal achievement or individualized A1C target in individuals with CVD for cardiovascular benefit from SGLT2 inhibitors or GLP-1 RAs.
- Vigilance in hypoglycemia prevention when setting glycemic targets.
- Post-trial observational follow-up data supporting long-term CVD benefits of intensive glycemic control.
- Post-trial observational follow-up data (e.g., EDIC, UKPDS, VADT, ADVANCE-ON, ACCORDION) should be considered when evaluating long-term cardiovascular benefits of intensive glycemic control.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Rapid escalation of therapies and early use of large insulin doses in intensive glycemic control (as seen in ACCORD).
- Fixed intensive A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors (e.g., hypoglycemia risk, life expectancy).
- Reliance solely on A1C reduction as a surrogate for cardiovascular risk reduction.
- Use of intensive glycemic control strategies in older adults with long-standing diabetes and high CVD risk without individualized assessment.
- Reliance on composite CVD event reduction as the sole metric for evaluating intensive glycemic control effectiveness, given heterogeneity in trial outcomes (e.g., no reduction in ADVANCE-ON vs. significant reductions in EDIC and VADT follow-ups).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Initiation of SGLT2 inhibitors or GLP-1 RAs (including dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs) in type 2 diabetes with CVD, kidney disease, or heart failure independent of A1C or metformin use.
- Long-term follow-up data demonstrating reduced myocardial infarctions and major CVD events with prior intensive glycemic control.
- Emphasis on hypoglycemia prevention as a critical component of glycemic management to reduce CVD risk.
- Recommendation to switch to SGLT2 inhibitors or GLP-1 RAs in individuals already on dual or multiple glucose-lowering therapies without these agents.
- Post-trial observational data showing heterogeneity in CVD outcomes (e.g., ADVANCE-ON vs. EDIC, VADT, UKPDS follow-ups).
- Initiation of SGLT2 inhibitors or GLP-1 RAs (including dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs) in individuals with type 2 diabetes and obesity, independent of A1C, metformin use, or glycemic targets, for cardiovascular and kidney benefits.


### intensive therapy benefits in type 1 diabetes

Early initiation of intensive insulin therapy (e.g., multiple daily injections or insulin pump) to achieve near-normal glycemia and reduce long-term microvascular and macrovascular complications

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Intensive therapy benefits in type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Early initiation of intensive insulin therapy (e.g., multiple daily injections or insulin pump) to achieve near-normal glycemia and reduce long-term microvascular and macrovascular complications
- Use of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) with automated insulin delivery (AID) systems or hybrid closed-loop technology to optimize glycemic control and reduce hypoglycemia
- Implementation of structured diabetes education programs (e.g., DAFNE) to enhance self-management skills, carbohydrate counting, and insulin dose adjustments
- Adoption of rapid-acting insulin analogs (e.g., lispro, aspart) and basal insulin analogs (e.g., glargine, detemir, degludec) to improve postprandial glucose control and reduce hypoglycemia risk
- Regular monitoring of glycemic outcomes (e.g., A1C, time-in-range) and adjustment of therapy based on CGM data and patient-specific factors
- Use of inhaled technosphere insulin (e.g., Afrezza) as an alternative to rapid-acting injectable insulin for postprandial glucose control in type 1 diabetes, particularly in patients with needle phobia or adherence challenges
- Implementation of predictive algorithms for insulin dose adjustments based on CGM trend arrows and patient-specific factors (e.g., carbohydrate counting accuracy, physical activity levels) to optimize glycemic outcomes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes confirmed by clinical criteria (e.g., autoantibodies, C-peptide levels)
- Patient willingness and ability to engage in intensive self-management, including frequent glucose monitoring and insulin dose adjustments
- Access to healthcare resources for ongoing education, technology (e.g., CGM, insulin pumps), and clinical support
- Absence of contraindications to intensive insulin therapy (e.g., severe hypoglycemia unawareness, active malignancy)
- Baseline evaluation of glycemic control (e.g., A1C), hypoglycemia risk, and comorbidities to guide therapy selection
- Patient willingness to use and maintain continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) technology, including sensor wear time and calibration requirements
- Access to healthcare resources for ongoing education, technology training, and clinical support for intensive therapy implementation

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of neutral protamine Hagedorn (NPH) insulin as basal insulin due to higher hypoglycemia risk and less predictable pharmacokinetics
- Fixed insulin dosing without adjustment for carbohydrate intake or physical activity
- Reliance solely on urine glucose testing or infrequent self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) without CGM integration
- Delayed initiation of intensive therapy in favor of gradual escalation in patients with suboptimal glycemic control
- Use of older, less accurate glucose monitoring methods (e.g., urine strips) without real-time CGM
- Fixed insulin dosing without adjustment for carbohydrate intake, physical activity, or glycemic trends

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of hybrid closed-loop systems (e.g., Control-IQ, MiniMed 780G) for automated insulin delivery to improve time-in-range and reduce hypoglycemia
- Early initiation of CGM within the first year of type 1 diabetes diagnosis to optimize glycemic outcomes and reduce complications
- Use of ultra-rapid insulin analogs (e.g., faster aspart, ultra-rapid lispro) to improve postprandial glucose control and reduce glycemic excursions
- Adoption of predictive low-glucose suspend (PLGS) and automated insulin delivery (AID) systems to mitigate hypoglycemia risk
- Implementation of structured education programs (e.g., DAFNE) to enhance patient empowerment and self-management in intensive therapy


### GLP-1 receptor agonist therapy for HFpEF with obesity

GLP-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists (e.g., tirzepatide) are recommended to improve heart failure symptoms and reduce heart failure events in individuals with HFpEF and obesity.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 receptor agonist therapy for HFpEF with obesity

**Core Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists (e.g., tirzepatide) are recommended to improve heart failure symptoms and reduce heart failure events in individuals with HFpEF and obesity.
- Symptom improvement is measured using the Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire clinical summary score (KCCQ-CSS) and 6-minute walk distance.
- Weight reduction is a secondary benefit observed with GLP-1 RA therapy in this population.
- Therapy should be considered irrespective of glycemic control status or need for additional glucose lowering.
- Metformin may be used for hyperglycemia management in people with stable HFpEF, provided kidney function remains within recommended ranges.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must have established HFpEF with obesity (BMI ≥ 30 kg/m²).
- Approximately 45% of individuals with HFpEF have diabetes, but therapy is not restricted to those with diabetes.
- Cardioprotective agents (e.g., SGLT2 inhibitors) should be considered as part of a comprehensive risk reduction plan.
- Close collaboration between primary and specialty care professionals is recommended to facilitate therapy adoption and minimize adverse effects.
- DPP-4 inhibitors are generally considered safe in heart failure, but saxagliptin should be used with caution due to increased risk of heart failure hospitalization.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Thiazolidinediones should be avoided in people with heart failure due to a strong and consistent relationship with increased risk of heart failure.
- Saxagliptin is associated with increased risk of heart failure hospitalization and should be used with caution in this population.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Semaglutide (GLP-1 RA) demonstrated significant improvement in KCCQ-CSS and weight reduction in the STEP-HFpEF DM trial.
- Tirzepatide (dual GIP/GLP-1 RA) reduced worsening heart failure events and improved KCCQ-CSS in the SUMMIT trial.
- GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA therapy is now recommended for reduction in heart failure events and symptoms in people with HFpEF and obesity.
- GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA therapy is recommended for reduction in heart failure events and symptoms in people with HFpEF and obesity, irrespective of glycemic control status or need for additional glucose lowering.


### GLP-1 receptor agonist cardiovascular benefits

GLP-1 RAs (liraglutide, semaglutide, albiglutide, efpeglenatide, dulaglutide) reduce major atherosclerotic cardiovascular outcomes (MI, stroke, or cardiovascular death).. Oral semaglutide has also demonstrated cardiovascular benefit.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 receptor agonist cardiovascular benefits

**Core Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RAs (liraglutide, semaglutide, albiglutide, efpeglenatide, dulaglutide) reduce major atherosclerotic cardiovascular outcomes (MI, stroke, or cardiovascular death).
- Oral semaglutide has also demonstrated cardiovascular benefit.
- Lixisenatide and extended-release exenatide are not recommended for CVD risk reduction due to lack of superiority to placebo.
- GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors reduce risk of atherosclerotic major adverse cardiovascular events comparably in people with type 2 diabetes and established ASCVD.
- Combination therapy with an SGLT2 inhibitor and a GLP-1 RA may be considered for additive cardiovascular and kidney outcomes benefits.
- SGLT2 inhibitors reduce risk of heart failure hospitalization and progression of kidney disease in people with established ASCVD, multiple ASCVD risk factors, or albuminuric kidney disease

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients must have type 2 diabetes with established ASCVD, multiple ASCVD risk factors, or CKD to qualify for GLP-1 RA cardiovascular benefit recommendations.
- GLP-1 RAs with demonstrated cardiovascular benefit (liraglutide, semaglutide, dulaglutide) are preferred over those without (lixisenatide, extended-release exenatide).
- Combination therapy with SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 RAs is considered for patients with established ASCVD or multiple risk factors.
- Use of GLP-1 RAs in type 1 diabetes is not currently recommended due to increased risk of diabetic ketoacidosis.
- Patients with type 1 diabetes are not currently recommended to receive SGLT2 inhibitors for cardiovascular or kidney outcomes due to increased risk of diabetic ketoacidosis and euglycemic ketoacidosis

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Lixisenatide and extended-release exenatide are no longer recommended for CVD risk reduction.
- Albiglutide is no longer recommended due to market removal (business reasons).
- Fixed A1C targets for cardiovascular risk reduction are no longer emphasized; outcomes-based therapy is prioritized.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Oral semaglutide is now included among GLP-1 RAs with demonstrated cardiovascular benefits.
- Combination therapy with SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 RAs is newly recommended for additive cardiovascular and kidney outcomes benefits.
- Meta-analyses support comparable cardiovascular risk reduction between GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors in established ASCVD.
- Tirzepatide (GIP/GLP-1 RA) cardiovascular outcome trials are ongoing and not yet recommended.
- Albiglutide is noted as removed from the market for business reasons (not solely due to lack of cardiovascular benefit)


### GLP-1 RA treatment in older adults

GLP-1 RAs and tirzepatide are highly effective glucose-lowering medications with low hypoglycemia risk. GLP-1 RAs can be used in older adults with reduced eGFR, including during dialysis. GLP-1 RAs demonstrate cardiovascular benefits in older adults with ASCVD, CKD, or heart failure with preserved ejection fraction

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GLP-1 RA treatment in older adults

**Core Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RAs and tirzepatide are highly effective glucose-lowering medications with low hypoglycemia risk
- GLP-1 RAs can be used in older adults with reduced eGFR, including during dialysis
- GLP-1 RAs demonstrate cardiovascular benefits in older adults with ASCVD, CKD, or heart failure with preserved ejection fraction
- Weight loss from GLP-1 RAs benefits conditions like obstructive sleep apnea, knee osteoarthritis, and MASH
- Injectable administration (except oral semaglutide) requires visual, motor, and cognitive skills; weekly dosing preferred
- GLP-1 RAs may be associated with nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and/or constipation, requiring slow dose titration to minimize gastrointestinal side effects

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Established atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) or high ASCVD risk
- Chronic kidney disease or symptomatic heart failure with preserved ejection fraction
- Adequate protein intake and resistance/strength training to mitigate sarcopenia risk
- Regular monitoring for dehydration and excessive weight loss
- Slow dose titration to minimize gastrointestinal side effects
- Monitoring for dehydration and excessive weight loss to prevent sarcopenia and bone density loss

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets without individualized consideration of life expectancy, cognitive function, or hypoglycemia risk
- Use of GLP-1 RAs in older adults with unexplained weight loss or undernutrition
- Use of GLP-1 RAs in older adults with problematic constipation, significant gastroparesis, recurrent ileus, or bowel obstruction

**Updated Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RAs as preferred agents for older adults with ASCVD or high cardiovascular risk
- Consideration of tirzepatide (GIP/GLP-1 RA) for enhanced glucose-lowering and weight loss
- Shared decision-making for stepwise treatment plan adjustments in older adults
- Emphasis on deintensifying or simplifying regimens based on safety and burden
- Monitoring for sarcopenia and bone density loss with protein intake and strength training
- Stepwise approach for assessing difficulties in the diabetes treatment plan, reevaluating glycemic goals through shared decision-making, deintensifying, simplifying, or modifying the treatment plan, and reassessing the safety and burdens of any interventions


### DKA exclusion in pregnant T1DM

Exclude DKA in all pregnant women with T1DM who become unwell, even if blood glucose is <200 mg/dl (euglycemic DKA can occur in pregnancy).. Maintain a high index of suspicion for DKA in diabetic pregnant women with symptoms like nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, fever, hyperventilation, hypotension, or change in mental status.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DKA exclusion in pregnant T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Exclude DKA in all pregnant women with T1DM who become unwell, even if blood glucose is <200 mg/dl (euglycemic DKA can occur in pregnancy).
- Maintain a high index of suspicion for DKA in diabetic pregnant women with symptoms like nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, fever, hyperventilation, hypotension, or change in mental status.
- Educate all women with pre-existing diabetes (planning or pregnant) about DKA prevention, self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG), medical nutrition therapy, and sick day management.
- Teach patients to perform urine ketone measurements during illness or when glucose levels exceed 200 mg/dl and report positive values promptly.
- Educate patients about predisposing factors for DKA (e.g., infection, insulin omission, β-sympathomimetic/glucocorticoid use, protracted vomiting, starvation, dehydration, undiagnosed diabetes, insulin pump failure, or poor glycemic control) to enable proactive prevention.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Multidisciplinary approach for DKA management in pregnancy, including IV fluid therapy, IV insulin therapy, electrolyte correction, and evaluation of precipitating factors.
- Continuous fetal heart rate monitoring and biophysical tests for fetal wellbeing after 24 weeks' gestation during DKA management.
- Prompt correction of DKA to revert fetal distress patterns (usually within 4–8 hours) before considering delivery.
- Delivery is warranted only if fetal status does not improve or maternal condition deteriorates despite aggressive therapy.
- Monitor maternal and fetal responses continuously during DKA management, including evaluation for bicarbonate administration if severe acidosis (pH <7.0) is present.


### DPP-4 inhibitor cardiovascular outcomes

DPP-4 inhibitors have not shown cardiovascular benefits relative to placebo in cardiovascular outcomes trials.. CAROLINA trial demonstrated noninferiority between linagliptin and glimepiride on cardiovascular outcomes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DPP-4 inhibitor cardiovascular outcomes

**Core Recommendations:**
- DPP-4 inhibitors have not shown cardiovascular benefits relative to placebo in cardiovascular outcomes trials.
- CAROLINA trial demonstrated noninferiority between linagliptin and glimepiride on cardiovascular outcomes.
- DPP-4 inhibitors primarily reduce postprandial glucose by inhibiting degradation of incretin hormones (GLP-1 and GIP), but their cardiovascular effects are likely indirect and insufficient to reduce major adverse cardiac events (MACE)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients with type 2 diabetes considered for DPP-4 inhibitors should be evaluated for cardiovascular risk.
- Cardiovascular risk assessment should include evaluation of prior cardiovascular events, heart failure history, and renal function before initiating DPP-4 inhibitors

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Linagliptin showed lower rates of hypoglycemia compared to glimepiride in the CAROLINA trial.
- Consider GLP-1 receptor agonists or SGLT2 inhibitors with proven cardiovascular benefits in patients with type 2 diabetes and high cardiovascular risk


### cardiovascular outcomes guidance for diabetes drugs

Cardiovascular outcomes trials for diabetes drugs. All new medications for type 2 diabetes must undergo cardiovascular outcomes trials as mandated by FDA guidance issued in 2008.. The 2023 revision updated the cardiovascular outcomes guidance for diabetes drugs.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Cardiovascular outcomes trials for diabetes drugs

**Core Recommendations:**
- All new medications for type 2 diabetes must undergo cardiovascular outcomes trials as mandated by FDA guidance issued in 2008.
- The 2023 revision updated the cardiovascular outcomes guidance for diabetes drugs.
- Pre-2008 diabetes medications were not subject to cardiovascular outcomes trial requirements.
- Cardiovascular outcomes trials must now evaluate both safety and potential cardiovascular benefits (e.g., reduction in major adverse cardiovascular events) for new diabetes drugs.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- New type 2 diabetes medications must demonstrate cardiovascular safety through outcomes trials.
- Compliance with FDA guidance for cardiovascular risk assessment is mandatory for drug approval.
- Drug developers must include patients with established cardiovascular disease or high cardiovascular risk in cardiovascular outcomes trials to ensure robust assessment of cardiovascular outcomes.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Diabetes medications approved prior to 2008 without cardiovascular outcomes trial data.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Revised FDA guidance (May 2023) for cardiovascular outcomes trials in diabetes drugs.
- May 2023 FDA guidance explicitly encourages the use of composite cardiovascular endpoints (e.g., MACE: cardiovascular death, non-fatal myocardial infarction, non-fatal stroke) in trial design.

alternative antihyperglycaemic agents in India. Hydroxychloroquine and bromocriptine are approved as antihyperglycaemic agents in India. Role of hydroxychloroquine and bromocriptine in type 2 diabetes therapeutic algorithm requires further study. Saroglitazar is licensed only for diabetic dyslipidaemia in India, not as an antihyperglycaemic agent

**Clinical Recommendation:** alternative antihyperglycaemic agents in India

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Hydroxychloroquine and bromocriptine are approved as antihyperglycaemic agents in India
- Role of hydroxychloroquine and bromocriptine in type 2 diabetes therapeutic algorithm requires further study

### SGLT2 inhibitor perioperative management

SGLT2 inhibitors should be held for 3–4 days before elective surgery.. Individuals using SGLT2 inhibitors undergoing nonelective surgeries should be closely monitored for DKA after surgery.. Euglycemic DKA should be considered in perioperative patients on SGLT2 inhibitors.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SGLT2 inhibitor perioperative management

**Core Recommendations:**
- SGLT2 inhibitors should be held for 3–4 days before elective surgery.
- Individuals using SGLT2 inhibitors undergoing nonelective surgeries should be closely monitored for DKA after surgery.
- Euglycemic DKA should be considered in perioperative patients on SGLT2 inhibitors.
- Temporary discontinuation of SGLT2 inhibitors at least 3 days prior to surgery is advised by the FDA.
- SGLT2 inhibitors may be restarted in the hospital setting for heart failure indication when nutritional intake is resumed.
- SGLT2 inhibitors should be held for 3–4 days before surgery even if the patient is on a low-dose regimen.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Risk factors for ketoacidosis must be resolved prior to reinitiating SGLT2 inhibitor therapy.
- Nutritional intake must be resumed before restarting SGLT2 inhibitors in the hospital setting.
- Risk factors for ketoacidosis (e.g., prolonged fasting, dehydration, infection, or critical illness) must be assessed and mitigated prior to surgery.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consideration of euglycemic DKA in perioperative monitoring of SGLT2 inhibitor users.
- FDA recommendation for temporary discontinuation of SGLT2 inhibitors at least 3 days prior to surgery.
- Consider holding SGLT2 inhibitors for longer than 3–4 days in patients with renal impairment or those undergoing major surgeries with prolonged fasting periods.

- Sick day education should be imparted to all T1DM patients to mitigate DKA risk
- Education should include management strategies for acute complications like DKA, hypoglycemia, and infections
- Sick day education should emphasize the importance of monitoring blood glucose and ketones (urine or blood) during illness to detect early signs of DKA

## Advanced Diabetes Technology and Monitoring

Advanced Diabetes Technology and Monitoring encompasses a suite of tools and protocols designed to enhance glycemic control, reduce complications, and improve patient self-management through real-time data, automation, and evidence-based practices.

**Key Features:**
- Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) implementation for real-time glucose trend analysis and insulin therapy optimization
- Automated Insulin Delivery (AID) systems integrating CGM, insulin pumps, and algorithms for dynamic insulin adjustment
- Hypoglycemia prevention strategies leveraging CGM and structured education to reduce risks in insulin-treated patients
- Diabetes monitoring protocols for hospitalized patients, including point-of-care and scheduled glucose checks
- Blood glucose monitoring (BGM) protocols emphasizing device access, structured self-monitoring, and accuracy optimization
- Technology-enabled diabetes self-management with two-way communication, individualized feedback, and virtual DSMES
- CGM use in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes to improve A1C, time in range, and maternal-fetal outcomes
- Glycemic status assessment methods combining A1C and CGM metrics (TIR, TBR, TAR, GMI) for nuanced data
- Early diabetes technology initiation post-diagnosis, especially for insulin-requiring individuals
- Standardized CGM data utilization for shared decision-making between patients and healthcare teams
- Glucometer accuracy optimization via control solutions and pre-analytical variable management
- Open-source automated insulin dosing (OS-AID) guidance for transparent, community-driven insulin delivery
- Pain reduction in self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) through site rotation, lancet selection, and technique

**Notable Details:**
- CGM provides glucose data every 5–15 minutes, unlike SMBG which offers intermittent snapshots
- AID systems adjust insulin delivery every 5 minutes based on predicted glucose trends (e.g., 30–60 minutes ahead)
- Hospital monitoring protocols require POC glucose checks before meals and every 4–6 hours in non-eating patients
- CGM in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes improves A1C and time in range without increasing hypoglycemia risk
- OS-AID systems (e.g., OpenAPS, Loop) are supported by real-world studies and randomized controlled trials
- Standardized CGM data should be accessible and analyzable by both patients and clinicians for treatment optimization
- Glucometer accuracy is impacted by expired test strips, improper storage, hematocrit, and unwashed hands
- SMBG pain reduction strategies include using the side of fingers, fresh lancets, and minimal depth settings

**Topics in this group:**

### continuous glucose monitoring implementation

Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) implementation. CGM is recommended at diabetes onset and anytime thereafter for individuals on insulin therapy, noninsulin therapies that can cause hypoglycemia, or any treatment where CGM aids management. continuous glucose monitoring implementation. continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) implementation. CGM provides longitudinal glucose data (every 5-15 minutes) for glycemic trend analysis, unlike SMBG which offers snapshots. Real-time CGM enables immediate patient/caregiver action via alarms for hypoglycemia/hyperglycemia, while retrospective CGM requires clinic review after data download

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) implementation

**Core Recommendations:**
- CGM is recommended at diabetes onset and anytime thereafter for individuals on insulin therapy, noninsulin therapies that can cause hypoglycemia, or any treatment where CGM aids management.
- In people on insulin therapy, CGM devices should be used as close to daily as possible for maximal benefit with uninterrupted access to supplies.
- During pregnancy for individuals with type 1 diabetes, CGM can help achieve glycemic goals and A1C targets.
- People who wear CGM devices should be educated on potential interfering substances and factors affecting accuracy.
- Skin reactions from CGM devices should be assessed and addressed to aid successful use.
- Healthcare professionals should monitor for signs of overbasalization during insulin therapy, such as significant bedtime-to-morning or postprandial-to-preprandial glucose differential, occurrences of hypoglycemia (aware or unaware), and high glycemic variability.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must be on insulin therapy, noninsulin therapies that can cause hypoglycemia, or any diabetes treatment where CGM aids management.
- For pregnancy with type 1 diabetes, CGM is recommended to achieve glycemic goals.
- Healthcare professionals must be aware of factors impairing CGM accuracy, such as oxygen tension, temperature, and interfering substances.
- Individuals should have uninterrupted access to CGM supplies for consistent use.
- Education on CGM use and potential interfering substances is required for successful implementation.
- Glucagon must be prescribed for all individuals taking insulin or at high risk for hypoglycemia, with preferred non-reconstitution preparations.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Historically, CGM devices were categorized as real-time CGM (rtCGM) or intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM), but modern devices often combine features.
- Unmodified glucose dehydrogenase pyrroloquinoline quinone (GDH/PQQ) enzyme methods for glucose monitoring are no longer recommended due to interference risks.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- CGM is now recommended for broader indications, including noninsulin therapies that can cause hypoglycemia and any treatment where CGM aids management.
- Modern CGM devices typically require no swiping and have similar characteristics across brands.
- Glucagon should be prescribed for all individuals taking insulin or at high risk for hypoglycemia, with preferred non-reconstitution preparations.
- Routine assessment for financial obstacles to diabetes management is emphasized to improve access to CGM and other evidence-based care.
- Lower-cost medications (e.g., metformin, sulfonylureas, thiazolidinediones, human insulin) should be considered for adults with cost-related barriers.
- CGM measures interstitial glucose, which correlates well with plasma glucose but may lag during rapid glucose changes (e.g., rising or falling levels).
- Healthy behaviors, diabetes self-management education and support, avoidance of therapeutic inertia, and consideration of social determinants of health are essential components of glucose-lowering management in diabetes.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) implementation

**Core Management Principles:**
- CGM provides longitudinal glucose data (every 5-15 minutes) for glycemic trend analysis, unlike SMBG which offers snapshots
- Real-time CGM enables immediate patient/caregiver action via alarms for hypoglycemia/hyperglycemia, while retrospective CGM requires clinic review after data download
- CGM benefits are maximized when used ≥70% of the time and in motivated patients, particularly those with hypoglycemia unawareness or frequent asymptomatic hypoglycemia
- CGM is most effective when paired with insulin pump therapy (sensor-augmented pump) compared to multiple subcutaneous insulin injections
- CGM accuracy is reduced during rapid glucose changes, hypoglycemia, and early sensor use (day 1), necessitating confirmatory finger-prick glucose for critical decisions
- CGMS sensor insertion is typically painless and rapid, with the sensor remaining in subcutaneous tissue (e.g., upper abdomen) for 3-14 days depending on the model
- CGMS data can be analyzed in detail by feeding sensor data to a computer for comprehensive assessment of glucose variability

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be willing to use CGM ≥70% of the time for meaningful glycemic control improvement
- CGM is recommended for patients with hypoglycemia unawareness, severe hypoglycemia (≥1 episode/year), frequent asymptomatic hypoglycemia (>2 episodes/week), extreme fear of hypoglycemia, or persistent hyperglycemia (HbA1c ≥9%)
- CGM requires calibration with capillary blood glucose (2-4x/day) for most devices, except Dexcom G6 which is calibration-free
- CGM is approved for use in children and adults, with sensor insertion typically in subcutaneous tissue (e.g., upper abdomen) for 3-14 days
- Patient selection should prioritize those with motivation to engage with CGM data and insulin therapy adjustments
- Calibration of most CGMS devices requires capillary blood glucose readings 2-4 times daily, except for Dexcom G6 which is calibration-free
- Calibration should be performed during periods of relatively stable blood glucose (e.g., fasting or pre-meal)


### automated insulin delivery systems

Automated Insulin Delivery (AID) Systems. AID systems integrate insulin pump, CGM, and algorithm to adjust insulin delivery in real time based on glucose levels. Modulates insulin by increasing, decreasing, or pausing delivery every 5 minutes using predicted glucose trends (e.g., 30 min or 1 h ahead)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Automated Insulin Delivery (AID) Systems

**Core Recommendations:**
- AID systems integrate insulin pump, CGM, and algorithm to adjust insulin delivery in real time based on glucose levels
- Modulates insulin by increasing, decreasing, or pausing delivery every 5 minutes using predicted glucose trends (e.g., 30 min or 1 h ahead)
- Provides automated correction doses and allows manual mode (though not recommended due to loss of CGM modulation benefits)
- Adjusts glucose targets dynamically (87–180 mg/dL) with 24-hour or segmented settings (up to 8 segments/day)
- Requires manual carbohydrate entry for meal announcements or qualitative meal estimation for prandial dosing
- Algorithm hosting location varies by system: pump body, insulin pod, or phone app
- AID systems may provide automated correction doses via microdose adjustments every 5 minutes or additional correction boluses with variable frequency and threshold glucose based on algorithm type

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- FDA-approved or -cleared AID systems must be used in individuals with type 1 diabetes capable of safe device use (independently or with caregiver support)
- Prior use of CGM and insulin pump therapy is recommended for optimal AID system integration
- Individual preferences, cost, insulin type, dosing plan, and self-management capabilities should guide AID system selection
- Training, access, and support for AID system use must be available for effective implementation
- Pivotal trials and real-world studies support AID use in individuals ≥2 years old, including older adults
- Individuals must be capable of safe device use independently or with caregiver support (explicitly stated as a requirement in the full text)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Sensor-augmented pumps (SAPs) with predictive low-glucose suspend are no longer commonly used due to superior AID systems
- Manual insulin dosing without CGM modulation is discouraged as it may reduce glycemic benefits
- Routine use of fixed glucose targets without dynamic adjustment is outdated
- Non-automated insulin delivery methods (e.g., multiple daily injections without CGM) are less effective for glycemic control
- Historical reliance on SAPs for hypoglycemia prevention is superseded by AID systems

**Updated Recommendations:**
- AID systems are now the standard of care for type 1 diabetes, replacing SAPs and manual dosing methods
- Real-world studies confirm AID systems' superiority in improving TIR, reducing hypoglycemia, and lowering A1C
- Dynamic glucose targets (87–180 mg/dL) and segmented settings (up to 8/day) are now standard features
- AID systems provide psychosocial benefits and are cost-effective for glycemic management
- AID systems are recommended for Medicare/Medicaid beneficiaries with type 1 or type 2 diabetes based on individual needs
- AID systems have largely replaced other methods of continuous subcutaneous insulin delivery due to their advantages in insulin modulation and minimizing hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia
- Real-world studies have retrospectively analyzed longer duration of AID system use (average 9 months) compared to pivotal trials, confirming sustained glycemic benefits


### hypoglycemia prevention strategy

Use CGM for older adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes on insulin to improve glycemic outcomes, reduce hypoglycemia, and reduce treatment burden. Provide structured education on hypoglycemia prevention and treatment through diabetes self-management education programs or trained specialists hypoglycemia prevention strategy. Hypoglycemia prevention and management strategy. Teach patients and families to avoid hypoglycemia triggers (e.g., delaying/missing meals, exercising without adjusting insulin/calories, insulin overdose). Encourage frequent blood glucose (BG) testing when starting new exercise regimens or unexpected activities

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypoglycemia prevention strategy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use CGM for older adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes on insulin to improve glycemic outcomes, reduce hypoglycemia, and reduce treatment burden
- Provide structured education on hypoglycemia prevention and treatment through diabetes self-management education programs or trained specialists
- Educate individuals at risk for hypoglycemia on definitions, precipitating situations (e.g., fasting, delayed meals, physical activity), blood glucose self-monitoring, avoidance of driving with hypoglycemia, and glucagon use
- Reevaluate diabetes treatment plans for individuals with Level 2 or 3 hypoglycemic events, considering deintensification of therapy
- Offer training to reestablish hypoglycemia awareness for individuals with impaired hypoglycemia awareness
- Provide ketone monitoring tools (urine or blood) to individuals at risk for hyperglycemic crises, particularly those on SGLT2 inhibitors or with type 1 diabetes, and educate on their use during illness or hyperglycemia
- Offer sick day advice and insulin dose adjustment guidance to prevent DKA in individuals on intensive insulin therapy, emphasizing the importance of not stopping basal insulin even when not eating

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must have a documented history of hypoglycemia or risk factors for hypoglycemia
- Structured education should be provided by a diabetes care and education specialist or equivalent trained professional
- CGM is recommended for insulin-treated individuals, especially those using multiple daily insulin injections or continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion
- Training for impaired hypoglycemia awareness should be offered to individuals with type 1 diabetes or those with recurrent hypoglycemia
- Medication adjustments (e.g., sulfonylureas) should be considered when interacting with antimicrobials that increase hypoglycemia risk
- Individuals on SGLT2 inhibitors, particularly those with type 1 diabetes, should be monitored for ketone levels (fasting β-hydroxybutyrate ≥0.8 mmol/L) as a risk factor for DKA
- Pregnant individuals with pregestational diabetes should be counseled on DKA risk and provided with ketone monitoring tools due to elevated risk during pregnancy

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized consideration of hypoglycemia risk
- Non-structured or non-evidence-based hypoglycemia education approaches
- Lack of consideration for medication interactions (e.g., sulfonylureas with fluoroquinolones) in hypoglycemia prevention
- Routine discontinuation of basal insulin during illness or fasting without providing detailed sick day management instructions
- Reliance solely on urine ketone monitoring without consideration of blood ketone monitoring for more accurate DKA risk assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Introduction of CGM for hypoglycemia detection and prevention in insulin-treated individuals
- Standardized terminology for care in post-acute and long-term care (PALTC) settings
- Specific guidance on protein intake (at least 0.8 g/kg body weight/day) in lifestyle management
- Stepwise approach for assessing difficulties in diabetes treatment plans and shared decision-making for glycemic goals
- Emphasis on ketone monitoring tools and
- for hyperglycemic crisis prevention

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypoglycemia prevention and management strategy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Teach patients and families to avoid hypoglycemia triggers (e.g., delaying/missing meals, exercising without adjusting insulin/calories, insulin overdose)
- Encourage frequent blood glucose (BG) testing when starting new exercise regimens or unexpected activities
- Carry a diabetes I-card with hypoglycemia symptoms and a rapid-acting glucose source (e.g., glucose tablets, sugar cubes, juice)
- Follow rapid-acting glucose with a slowly absorbed snack (e.g., milk, peanuts) to sustain recovery
- Ensure family/staff are trained to recognize hypoglycemia symptoms and administer glucagon in emergencies
- Teach patients to recognize behavioral changes (e.g., irritability, lethargy) as potential hypoglycemia symptoms in toddlers or non-verbal individuals
- Encourage patients to analyze CGM glucose trends proactively to prevent hypoglycemia episodes, especially in high-risk scenarios (e.g., nocturnal periods)

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be educated on hypoglycemia symptoms and triggers at diagnosis, with reinforcement during follow-ups
- Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) should be used to detect asymptomatic or nocturnal hypoglycemia
- Glucagon (injectable or nasal) must be available at home, school, or work for severe hypoglycemia treatment
- BG confirmation is required before treating hypoglycemia, especially with CGM to avoid spurious lows
- Family/staff must be familiar with distinguishing persistent hyperglycemia from rebound hyperglycemia
- BG confirmation is required before treating hypoglycemia, particularly when using CGM to avoid spurious lows
- Glucagon must be available at home, school, or work for severe hypoglycemia treatment, with dosing adjusted for age (<12 years: 0.5 mg; ≥12 years: 1 mg)


### diabetes monitoring protocols

Hospital diabetes monitoring protocols + CGM and insulin pump use. Perform point-of-care (POC) blood glucose monitoring before meals in hospitalized individuals with diabetes who are eating. Monitor glucose every 4–6 hours in hospitalized individuals with diabetes who are not eating diabetes monitoring protocols. Diabetes monitoring protocols. Monitoring and follow-up protocols for people with diabetes are structured into self-monitoring with blood glucose (SMBG) and annual check-ups.. Self-monitoring with blood glucose (SMBG) using glucose monitors is a key component of diabetes management.. Annual check-ups are a critical part of ongoing diabetes care and follow-up.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hospital diabetes monitoring protocols + CGM and insulin pump use

**Core Recommendations:**
- Perform point-of-care (POC) blood glucose monitoring before meals in hospitalized individuals with diabetes who are eating
- Monitor glucose every 4–6 hours in hospitalized individuals with diabetes who are not eating
- Use more frequent POC monitoring (every 30 min to every 2 h) for safe intravenous insulin therapy
- Confirm POC glucose results with laboratory testing if results do not correlate with clinical status, especially for asymptomatic hypoglycemia
- Continue personal CGM use during hospitalization if clinically appropriate, with confirmatory POC for insulin dosing and hypoglycemia assessment
- Perform repeat POC glucose testing if initial result does not correlate with clinical status before confirming with laboratory testing
- Review and document CGM and AID data daily (e.g., before meals and at bedtime) with pump setting adjustments as needed

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Use FDA-approved POC hospital-calibrated glucose monitoring systems
- Ensure institutional protocols for CGM validation and accuracy (e.g., 20/20 criterion: ±20% or ±20 mg/dL of POC measurement)
- Provide staff training and ongoing competency assessments for diabetes technology use
- Implement institutional policies for safe use of insulin pumps and AID systems
- Cross-check CGM readings with POC or laboratory blood glucose at least once daily or when clinically appropriate
- Ensure institutional policies for safe use of hospital-owned CGM systems with EHR integration and remote monitoring capabilities
- Confirm CGM accuracy by cross-checking CGM readings with POC or laboratory blood glucose at least once daily or when clinically appropriate

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on capillary (finger-stick) POC glucose monitoring without confirmatory testing when results are inconsistent with clinical status
- Use of non-FDA-approved POC glucose monitoring systems in hospital settings
- Unvalidated CGM use for insulin dosing without confirmatory POC measurements
- Discontinuation of insulin pump or AID systems during hospitalization without clinical necessity

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adoption of hybrid protocols combining CGM with periodic POC monitoring for intravenous insulin titration to reduce hourly testing burden
- Validation criteria for CGM accuracy in hospital settings (20/20 criterion) to ensure safe insulin dosing
- Encouragement of hospital-wide CGM policies with EHR integration and remote monitoring capabilities
- Support for continuation of insulin pumps and AID systems during hospitalization when clinically appropriate and with proper training
- Daily review and documentation of CGM and AID data (e.g., before meals and at bedtime) with pump setting adjustments as needed
- Implementation of remote monitoring (e.g., glucose telemetry systems) for CGM data in hospital settings
- Development of institutional policies for safe use of hospital-owned CGM systems with EHR integration

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes monitoring protocols

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Monitoring and follow-up protocols for people with diabetes are structured into self-monitoring with blood glucose (SMBG) and annual check-ups.
- Self-monitoring with blood glucose (SMBG) using glucose monitors is a key component of diabetes management.
- Annual check-ups are a critical part of ongoing diabetes care and follow-up.
- Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) is recommended as an alternative or adjunct to traditional SMBG for people with diabetes, particularly those on intensive insulin therapy or with hypoglycemia unawareness.
- HbA1c testing should be performed at least twice per year for people with stable glycemic control and quarterly for those with unstable glycemia or recent treatment changes.
- Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) testing is required annually to screen for diabetic kidney disease in people with type 1 diabetes for ≥5 years or type 2 diabetes at diagnosis.
- Dilated eye examination by an ophthalmologist or optometrist is mandated annually to screen for diabetic retinopathy, with more frequent monitoring for those with pre-existing retinopathy.

**Prerequisites:**
- People with diabetes must be actively monitored and followed up according to structured protocols.
- Access to a glucose monitor is required for self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG).
- CGM use requires patient education on device application, data interpretation, and troubleshooting to ensure accurate and actionable glucose data.
- HbA1c testing requires standardized laboratory methods (e.g., NGSP-certified assays) to ensure comparability across clinical settings.
- Annual check-ups must include a comprehensive foot examination to assess for peripheral neuropathy and peripheral arterial disease, with referral to podiatry if abnormalities are detected.


### blood glucose monitoring protocols

People with diabetes should be provided with BGM devices as indicated by circumstances, preferences, and treatment.. People using CGM must also have access to BGM at all times. blood glucose monitoring protocols. Blood glucose monitoring protocols. Self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) is central to diabetes management, with frequency correlating with improved HbA1c and reduced acute complications.. Structured SMBG programs significantly reduce A1C levels in poorly controlled, noninsulin-treated type 2 diabetes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Blood glucose monitoring protocols

**Core Recommendations:**
- People with diabetes should be provided with BGM devices as indicated by circumstances, preferences, and treatment.
- People using CGM must also have access to BGM at all times.
- Insulin users should check blood glucose levels when appropriate, including fasting, pre/post meals, bedtime, during hypoglycemia, after treatment, and before critical tasks like driving.
- Healthcare professionals must use FDA-approved meters with proven accuracy and properly stored, unexpired test strips.
- Glucose monitoring results should guide therapy adjustments, medical nutrition therapy, physical activity, and medication changes.
- Healthcare professionals should be aware of potential interference of medications and substances on glucose levels measured by blood glucose meters when interpreting BGM results.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- BGM devices are indicated based on patient circumstances, preferences, and treatment type.
- Insulin users must check blood glucose levels at specific times relevant to their therapy.
- Only FDA-approved meters with proven accuracy and properly stored test strips should be used.
- CGM users must have access to BGM for calibration, suspected inaccuracies, or rapid glucose changes.
- Glucose monitoring should be integrated into multifactorial interventions for insulin-treated individuals.
- BGM may be helpful in noninsulin therapies when modifying meal plans, physical activity plans, and/or medications (particularly those that can cause hypoglycemia) as part of a treatment adjustment program.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine fixed-frequency BGM schedules without patient-specific tailoring.
- Use of non-FDA-approved or unproven blood glucose meters.
- Reliance solely on CGM without access to BGM for critical situations.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- CGM as a preferred method for glucose monitoring in many settings due to improved outcomes.
- Patient-specific BGM and CGM frequency and timing based on individual needs and goals.
- Awareness of medication and substance interference with glucose meter accuracy.
- BGM use in noninsulin therapies when modifying meal plans, activity, or medications.
- Integration of glucose testing results into therapy adjustments for medical nutrition and physical activity.
- Glucose monitoring (BGM or CGM) results should be integrated into therapy adjustments to guide medical nutrition therapy, physical activity, and medication changes, particularly prandial insulin or correction bolus doses.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Blood glucose monitoring protocols

**Core Management Principles:**
- Self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) is central to diabetes management, with frequency correlating with improved HbA1c and reduced acute complications.
- Structured SMBG programs significantly reduce A1C levels in poorly controlled, noninsulin-treated type 2 diabetes.
- Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) improves glycemic control and quality of life in type 1 diabetes, especially with impaired hypoglycemia awareness.
- Point-of-care β-hydroxybutyrate testing aids in assessing diabetic ketoacidosis severity prior to treatment.
- Alternative site blood glucose monitoring may introduce errors; fingertip testing remains the gold standard.
- HbA1c measurement should be interpreted with caution in patients with variant hemoglobins (e.g., HbS, HbC, HbD) due to potential interference with assay results, requiring confirmatory testing or alternative glycemic markers (e.g., fructosamine) in affected populations.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient education on proper SMBG technique and interpretation is mandatory.
- CGM use requires patient commitment to sensor wear and data review.
- HbA1c measurement must account for variant hemoglobins (e.g., HbF, HbE) that interfere with results.
- Ketone testing is required during hyperglycemia or illness to prevent ketoacidosis.
- Glucose meters must meet performance standards across hypoglycemic, normoglycemic, and hyperglycemic ranges.
- Healthcare providers must ensure patients using CGM systems receive training on sensor insertion, calibration (if required), and data interpretation to optimize glycemic outcomes.


### technology-enabled diabetes self-management

Two-way communication between person with diabetes and healthcare team. Individualized feedback tailored to person's needs. Use of person-generated health data for self-management decisions. Education integrated with technology use. Exploration of virtual environments for DSMES with gamification principles

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Technology-enabled diabetes self-management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Two-way communication between person with diabetes and healthcare team
- Individualized feedback tailored to person's needs
- Use of person-generated health data for self-management decisions
- Education integrated with technology use
- Exploration of virtual environments for DSMES with gamification principles
- Integration of person-generated health data into electronic health records (EHRs) for seamless care coordination
- Use of remote monitoring by healthcare teams to proactively adjust therapy based on real-time data

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Person with diabetes must be engaged in self-management decisions
- Healthcare team must provide individualized feedback
- Access to diabetes technologies (e.g., CGM, AID systems, connected glucose meters)
- Education on technology use and self-management
- Systematic approach for technology assessment and integration into care plan
- Healthcare team must have access to training on interpreting person-generated health data and providing individualized feedback
- Patient must demonstrate willingness and capability to engage with technology-enabled self-management tools

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Traditional DSMES without technology integration
- Non-personalized feedback approaches
- Rigid, non-adaptive diabetes care plans
- Routine reliance on self-reported glucose logs without technology integration for clinical decision-making

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Virtual DSMES environments with avatar-based interaction
- Reimbursement policies to increase DSMES access and use
- CGM combined with individualized DSMES or behavioral interventions
- DSMES plus intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) for improved time in range
- Systematic technology assessment and integration into diabetes care plans
- Standardized protocols for remote monitoring and virtual DSMES delivery in clinical workflows
- Requirements for interoperability between diabetes technologies and EHR systems


### CGM in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes

Use real-time CGM in addition to standard care for pregnant individuals with type 1 diabetes to improve A1C, maternal glucose time in range (TIR), and time above range (TAR) without increasing hypoglycemia CGM in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes. CGM in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes. CGM provides detailed glucose pattern data to optimize diet, lifestyle, and insulin doses. CGM alerts users to impending hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia for earlier corrective action. Sensor-integrated insulin delivery improves time in range compared to stand-alone pump therapy

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use real-time CGM in addition to standard care for pregnant individuals with type 1 diabetes to improve A1C, maternal glucose time in range (TIR), and time above range (TAR) without increasing hypoglycemia
- Monitor CGM-reported mean glucose, lower mean glucose, lower SD, and higher TIR to reduce risks of large-for-gestational-age births and other adverse neonatal outcomes
- Use CGM time in range (TIR) for glycemic outcome assessment in type 1 diabetes pregnancy, though it does not provide actionable data for fasting/postprandial hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia
- Follow pregnancy-specific CGM glucose goals: TIR >70% (63–140 mg/dL), level 1 TBR <4% (<63 mg/dL), level 2 TBR <1% (<54 mg/dL), and TAR <25% (>140 mg/dL)
- Select CGM device based on individual circumstances, preferences, and needs, considering nonadjunctive use and accuracy validated in pregnancy
- Use CGM-reported mean glucose as the primary metric for glycemic assessment in pregnancy, given its superiority over estimated A1C or glucose management indicator (GMI) due to pregnancy-induced changes in red blood cell turnover and plasma volume
- Follow finger stick glucose measurements in specific circumstances: post-hypoglycemia correction (due to plasma-interstitial glucose time lag), symptom mismatch with sensor readings, and first day of sensor use when accuracy may be lower

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Pregnancy with type 1 diabetes diagnosis
- Optimization of pre- and postprandial glucose goals as part of standard care
- Individualized device selection considering accuracy, alarms, and patient preferences
- Use of CGM systems validated for pregnancy (e.g., demonstrated %20/20 agreement rates ≥78.6%)
- Finger stick glucose measurements in specific circumstances (e.g., post-hypoglycemia correction, symptom mismatch, first day of use)
- Pregnancy-specific CGM accuracy validation (e.g., demonstrated %20/20 agreement rates ≥78.6% on day 1, ≥96.3% on days 4–7, and ≥97.3% on day 10) for device selection in type 1 diabetes pregnancy
- Nonadjunctive CGM use in pregnancy requires device accuracy validated for pregnancy (e.g., %20/20 agreement rates ≥96.3% by day 4–7)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on estimated A1C or glucose management indicator without CGM data in pregnancy
- Use of capillary blood glucose monitoring as the primary method for glycemic control in type 1 diabetes pregnancy
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of CGM-derived TIR and pregnancy-specific goals

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adoption of CGM in type 1 diabetes pregnancy as standard care based on RCT evidence (CONCEPTT trial)
- Pregnancy-specific CGM glucose targets (TIR >70%, TBR <4%/<1%, TAR <25%) endorsed by international consensus
- Use of CGM-reported mean glucose as superior to estimated A1C for glycemic assessment in pregnancy
- Nonadjunctive CGM use in pregnancy with validated accuracy (e.g., %20/20 agreement rates ≥96.3% by day 4–7)
- Cost-effectiveness of CGM in pregnancy offset by improved maternal and neonatal outcomes
- International consensus endorses pregnancy-specific CGM glucose goal ranges (TIR >70%, TBR <4%/<1%, TAR <25%) for type 1 diabetes but does not specify device type, accuracy thresholds, or alarm requirements
- CGM systems for pregnancy do not require calibrations and are approved for nonadjunctive use, though finger stick measurements are advised in specific circumstances

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM in pregnancy for type 1 diabetes

**Core Management Principles:**
- CGM provides detailed glucose pattern data to optimize diet, lifestyle, and insulin doses
- CGM alerts users to impending hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia for earlier corrective action
- Sensor-integrated insulin delivery improves time in range compared to stand-alone pump therapy
- CGM reduces neonatal intensive care admissions, large-for-gestational-age births, and neonatal hypoglycemia in T1DM pregnancies
- CGM alerts healthcare providers to glycemic trends for remote monitoring and shared decision-making in pregnancy

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Women with type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1DM) at <14 weeks gestation
- Continuous CGM use from randomization until delivery
- Preconception initiation of insulin therapy for women with recurrent hypoglycemia or hypoglycemia unawareness
- Women with T1DM and a history of recurrent troublesome hypoglycemia or hypoglycemia unawareness should initiate insulin therapy preconception


### glycemic status assessment methods

A1C is the primary tool for assessing glycemic status, reflecting average glycemia over 2–3 months and strongly linked to diabetes complications.. CGM metrics (TIR, TBR, TAR, GMI, coefficient of variation, mean glucose) provide nuanced glucose data for self-management and treatment optimization.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glycemic status assessment methods

**Core Recommendations:**
- A1C is the primary tool for assessing glycemic status, reflecting average glycemia over 2–3 months and strongly linked to diabetes complications.
- CGM metrics (TIR, TBR, TAR, GMI, coefficient of variation, mean glucose) provide nuanced glucose data for self-management and treatment optimization.
- BGM via capillary (finger-stick) devices remains integral for insulin-treated individuals and complements A1C and CGM.
- Glycemic status should be assessed at least twice yearly, with more frequent monitoring (e.g., every 3 months) for unstable glucose levels, recent treatment changes, or hypoglycemia/hyperglycemia.
- A1C testing should be performed routinely at initial assessment and continuing care, with point-of-care testing offering timely treatment adjustments.
- CGM is increasingly important for optimizing treatment in people with diabetes in pregnancy and other forms of diabetes (e.g., cystic fibrosis-related diabetes).
- A1C does not provide a measure of glycemic variability, real-time glucose levels, or hypoglycemia, necessitating complementary BGM or CGM data for comprehensive assessment.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- A1C testing requires NGSP-certified assays for accuracy and should be performed in all people with diabetes.
- Alternative monitoring (e.g., fructosamine, glycated albumin, BGM, CGM) is required when A1C interpretation is compromised (e.g., hemoglobinopathies, anemia, pregnancy).
- CGM wear of 70% or higher is recommended for reliable TIR assessment over 10–14 days.
- Individuals with type 1 diabetes, insulin-treated type 2 diabetes, or high hypoglycemia risk benefit most from CGM.
- Discordant A1C and CGM/BGM results require further evaluation due to potential inaccuracies or high glycemic variability.
- A1C testing should be performed routinely at initial assessment and as part of continuing care in all people with diabetes.
- Alternative glycemic monitoring (e.g., fructosamine, glycated albumin, BGM, CGM) is required when A1C interpretation is compromised due to conditions affecting red blood cell turnover (e.g., hemolytic anemia, anemia, recent blood transfusion, kidney failure, pregnancy) or homozygous hemoglobin variants (e.g., HbSS, HbEE).

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of fructosamine or glycated albumin as primary glycemic monitoring tools without clear indications (e.g., A1C interference).
- Relying solely on A1C for glycemic variability assessment without complementary BGM or CGM data.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors (e.g., hypoglycemia risk, life expectancy).
- Point-of-care A1C testing as a substitute for laboratory assays in all clinical scenarios due to potential accuracy limitations.
- Assuming race/ethnicity as a proxy for A1C interpretation differences.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- CGM metrics (TIR, TBR, TAR, GMI) as standardized tools for glycemic status assessment and clinical decision-making.
- 10–14 day CGM wear periods with ≥70% wear time for reliable TIR and other metric evaluation.
- Remote glucose data analysis to guide real-time diabetes therapy adjustments.
- Integration of GMI and coefficient of variation (>36%) into treatment plans for hypoglycemia risk assessment.
- Emphasis on individualized glycemic monitoring frequency based on treatment stability, hypoglycemia risk, and life stage.
- Reporting of estimated average glucose (eAG) alongside A1C results to provide context for glycemic control interpretation.
- Standardized CGM metric reports with visual summaries to facilitate clinical interpretation and treatment decision-making.


### early diabetes technology initiation

Diabetes technology should be initiated soon after diagnosis and anytime thereafter based on patient needs and wishes. CGM and BGM devices should be started from the outset of diabetes diagnosis, especially in insulin-requiring individuals

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Early diabetes technology initiation

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetes technology should be initiated soon after diagnosis and anytime thereafter based on patient needs and wishes
- CGM and BGM devices should be started from the outset of diabetes diagnosis, especially in insulin-requiring individuals
- Early CGM initiation in children/adolescents with type 1 diabetes reduces A1C and improves parental satisfaction
- AID systems are preferred for type 1 diabetes and insulin-deficient type 2 diabetes on MDI or basal insulin not meeting targets
- Early AID therapy initiation (within 21 days of diagnosis) improves time in range and lowers A1C in youth with type 1 diabetes
- Diabetes technology initiation should be tailored to patient preferences and lifestyle, with shared decision-making between healthcare providers and patients/families

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Insulin requirement for CGM/BGM initiation
- Patient willingness and need for technology use
- Training on CGM alarm/alert settings to prevent alarm fatigue
- Consistent access to CGM devices to avoid treatment interruption
- No requirement for C-peptide, islet autoantibodies, or diabetes duration to determine suitability for AID therapy
- Shared decision-making process between healthcare providers and patients/families to determine technology suitability and goals of care

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Delayed initiation of diabetes technology after diagnosis
- Reliance on fixed A1C targets without individualized assessment
- Exclusion of type 2 diabetes patients from CGM/AID therapy based on insulin production status

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Early CGM use in type 2 diabetes not using insulin with observed glycemic and cost-effectiveness benefits
- AID system initiation within 21 days of type 1 diabetes diagnosis for improved outcomes
- CGM use to reduce burden of frequent BGM in insulin-requiring individuals
- AID therapy consideration for type 2 diabetes on basal insulin not meeting targets
- No data supporting exclusion of patients from technology based on C-peptide or autoantibody status
- Consideration of AID therapy for insulin-deficient type 2 diabetes on basal insulin not meeting targets, regardless of insulin production status


### standardized CGM data utilization

Use of standardized CGM tracing is recommended for people with diabetes and clinicians. Data should be accessible and analyzable by both people with diabetes and healthcare teams. Data analysis should occur between and at clinic visits. Data should inform self-management and medication dose titration

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** standardized CGM data utilization

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use of standardized CGM tracing is recommended for people with diabetes and clinicians
- Data should be accessible and analyzable by both people with diabetes and healthcare teams
- Data analysis should occur between and at clinic visits
- Data should inform self-management and medication dose titration
- Applicable across settings including connected insulin pens, CGM devices, and AID systems
- Standardized CGM data should be used to identify and address glycemic variability patterns, including time in range (TIR), time below range (TBR), and time above range (TAR)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- People with diabetes must have access to CGM devices
- Healthcare teams must have tools to access and interpret CGM data
- Data sharing infrastructure must support between-visit and in-clinic analysis
- Patient consent for data sharing between devices and healthcare systems
- Training for both patients and clinicians on standardized CGM data interpretation
- Healthcare systems must ensure interoperability between CGM devices and electronic health records (EHRs) for seamless data integration

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on intermittent fingerstick glucose monitoring for treatment decisions
- Manual transcription of CGM data without standardized reporting formats
- Use of non-standardized or proprietary CGM data formats in clinical workflows

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of CGM data with connected insulin pens for dose optimization
- Standardized CGM reporting formats for consistent data interpretation
- Use of CGM data in automated insulin delivery (AID) systems
- Remote monitoring capabilities for CGM data between clinic visits
- Patient-facing CGM data dashboards for self-management support
- Use of standardized CGM data to guide dietary and exercise interventions based on glucose trend analysis


### glucometer accuracy optimization

Use control solution (CS) to assess glucometer working status, though storage and stability of CS are concerns.. Address pre-analytical variables like expired test strips, improper storage conditions (temperature/humidity), unwashed hands, and hematocrit/hypotension.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** glucometer accuracy optimization

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use control solution (CS) to assess glucometer working status, though storage and stability of CS are concerns.
- Address pre-analytical variables like expired test strips, improper storage conditions (temperature/humidity), unwashed hands, and hematocrit/hypotension.
- Ensure adequate blood volume (as low as 0.3 µl) for accurate readings.
- Adjust screen/font size or use talking meters for visually impaired patients.
- Consider dexterity limitations in patients performing SMBG.
- Verify glucometer calibration periodically as part of accuracy optimization

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Control solution (CS) usage for glucometer accuracy assessment.
- Proper storage of test strips (avoid temperature/humidity extremes).
- Adequate blood volume (0.3 µl) for testing.
- Screen/font size adjustments or talking meters for visually impaired patients.
- Assessment of patient dexterity for SMBG feasibility.
- Ensure test strips are stored in original containers to maintain stability
- Use glucometers with ISO 15197:2013 certification for clinical accuracy


### open-source automated insulin dosing guidance

Open-source automated insulin dosing (OS-AID) guidance. OS-AID algorithms provide transparent, community-driven code for automated insulin dosing, enabling healthcare professionals and users to understand risks and benefits.. OS-AID systems (e.g., OpenAPS, Loop) are implemented on various platforms and supported by real-world studies and RCTs.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Open-source automated insulin dosing (OS-AID) guidance

**Core Recommendations:**
- OS-AID algorithms provide transparent, community-driven code for automated insulin dosing, enabling healthcare professionals and users to understand risks and benefits.
- OS-AID systems (e.g., OpenAPS, Loop) are implemented on various platforms and supported by real-world studies and RCTs.
- Healthcare professionals should support tuning of algorithm settings (e.g., basal rates, insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios) for safe and effective use.
- Direct management of algorithm aggressiveness is possible through conventional pump settings.
- Backup insulin treatment plans are advisable for all AID systems.
- OS-AID algorithms (e.g., OpenAPS, Loop) are implemented on multiple platforms (e.g., Android smartphones as AndroidAPS, iPhone as iAPS/Trio) and supported by both real-world studies and randomized controlled trials (RCTs).
- Loop algorithm supports direct management of algorithm aggressiveness through conventional pump settings, enabling healthcare professionals to adjust basal rates, insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios, or insulin sensitivity factors.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Use of cleared CGM systems and insulin pump components with OS-AID algorithms.
- Healthcare professional understanding of OS-AID system tuning and settings.
- Patient willingness to engage with community-driven systems and online resources.
- Institutional protocols for technology use in inpatient settings if hospitalized.
- Compliance with data privacy and security standards for digital health solutions.
- Healthcare professionals must be knowledgeable about OS-AID system tuning and settings to provide safe and effective support for patients using these systems.
- Institutional protocols must be established for the use of OS-AID systems in inpatient settings, including training, competency assessments, and resource availability.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of the term 'DIY' to describe open-source systems, as it inaccurately implies individual coding.
- Reliance on non-transparent or proprietary automated glycemic controllers without source code availability.
- Assumption that all users of OS-AID systems have built the software themselves.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recognition of OS-AID as the preferred term for open-source automated insulin dosing systems.
- FDA clearance of Tidepool Loop, a variant of the Loop algorithm, supporting commercial implementation.
- Integration of online or virtual licensed coaching with CGM, insulin pumps, and diabetes apps to improve glycemic outcomes.
- Use of FDA-cleared digital therapeutics ('digiceuticals') for diabetes management.
- Emphasis on data privacy and security compliance (e.g., HIPAA) for cloud-based data aggregators like Tidepool and Glooko.
- Recognition of digital therapeutics ('digiceuticals') as FDA-cleared digital health technologies for diabetes management, with emphasis on their clinical validation and regulatory approval.
- Recommendation to use cloud-based data aggregators (e.g., Tidepool, Glooko) that comply with HIPAA and other data privacy standards for monitoring and sharing diabetes data.


### pain reduction in self-monitoring of blood glucose
