pain reduction in self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG). Rotate fingers to avoid repeated use of the same site. Use the side of fingers rather than the central fat pad for lancing. Use a fresh lancet for each prick to ensure sharpness and reduce pain. Use lancets with a depth gauge and adjust to the minimal setting for an adequate blood drop

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pain reduction in self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG)

**Core Management Principles:**
- Rotate fingers to avoid repeated use of the same site
- Use the side of fingers rather than the central fat pad for lancing
- Use a fresh lancet for each prick to ensure sharpness and reduce pain
- Use lancets with a depth gauge and adjust to the minimal setting for an adequate blood drop
- Use smaller gauge lancets to minimize tissue damage and pain

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Ensure alternate testing sites (palm, forearm, thigh) are used only when blood glucose is stable, not during rapid changes (e.g., after meals, insulin, or exercise)
- Avoid using alternate sites during suspected hypoglycemia due to delayed detection of glucose changes
- Use alternate testing sites (palm, forearm, thigh) only in fasting state or when blood glucose is stable, as accuracy is nearly similar to fingertip in these conditions


### professional CGM for glycemic pattern analysis

Professional CGM devices provide retrospective data (blinded or unblinded) to identify patterns of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia.. Useful for evaluating glucose levels when real-time CGM (rtCGM) or intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) is unavailable or when patients prefer blinded analysis.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** professional CGM for glycemic pattern analysis

**Core Recommendations:**
- Professional CGM devices provide retrospective data (blinded or unblinded) to identify patterns of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia.
- Useful for evaluating glucose levels when real-time CGM (rtCGM) or intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) is unavailable or when patients prefer blinded analysis.
- Particularly beneficial for individuals using hypoglycemia-inducing agents to assess hypoglycemic periods and adjust medication doses.
- Can evaluate hyperglycemia patterns to guide treatment adjustments.
- Intermittent use of CGM (rtCGM or isCGM) in type 2 diabetes on noninsulin or basal insulin therapies may improve A1C levels.
- Professional CGM can be used to assess glycemic variability and time-in-range (TIR) metrics, which are critical for evaluating overall glycemic control beyond just hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia patterns.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals using agents that can cause hypoglycemia (e.g., sulfonylureas, insulin).
- Patients with type 2 diabetes not on intensive insulin therapy for intermittent CGM use.
- Patients who prefer blinded analysis or shorter unblinded data exposure.
- Coupling CGM use with analysis, interpretation, and education to adjust medication and lifestyle behaviors.
- Patients with type 2 diabetes not on insulin at baseline for real-world professional CGM use.
- Professional CGM may be considered for patients with type 2 diabetes who are not achieving glycemic targets despite optimized lifestyle and oral therapy, to guide further treatment intensification.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of professional CGM in type 2 diabetes not on insulin at baseline resulted in lower A1C at 6 months.
- Shift toward greater use of glucose-lowering medications with cardiometabolic benefits (e.g., SGLT2 inhibitors, GLP-1 receptor agonists) after professional CGM use.
- Intermittent CGM use in type 2 diabetes on noninsulin or basal insulin therapies may lead to early and late improvements in A1C levels.
- Professional CGM use in type 2 diabetes not on insulin at baseline may lead to earlier initiation of cardiometabolic-beneficial therapies (e.g., SGLT2 inhibitors, GLP-1 receptor agonists) due to identified glycemic patterns.


### CGM and AID therapy recommendations

CGM use is recommended for adults with diabetes on insulin or noninsulin therapies that can induce hypoglycemia to improve glycemic outcomes and reduce hypoglycemia episodes.. AID systems should be offered to adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes on insulin therapy to improve glycemic management and outcomes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM and AID therapy recommendations

**Core Recommendations:**
- CGM use is recommended for adults with diabetes on insulin or noninsulin therapies that can induce hypoglycemia to improve glycemic outcomes and reduce hypoglycemia episodes.
- AID systems should be offered to adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes on insulin therapy to improve glycemic management and outcomes.
- Technology selection (CGM/AID) should be based on patient/caregiver preference, resource availability for training, and support systems.
- All individuals treated with insulin or at high risk for hypoglycemia should be prescribed glucagon.
- Individuals prescribed glucagon and their close contacts should receive education on glucagon use and administration.
- Clinicians should routinely review individuals' access to glucagon to ensure appropriate prescribing and adherence.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Adults with diabetes on insulin or noninsulin therapies that can induce hypoglycemia for CGM use.
- Adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes on insulin therapy for AID system consideration.
- Availability of resources for training and education for CGM/AID implementation.
- Prescription of glucagon for individuals treated with insulin or at high hypoglycemia risk.
- Education on glucagon use for individuals prescribed glucagon and their close contacts.
- Individuals prescribed glucagon must have education on use and administration provided to them and their close contacts.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- AID systems recommended for adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes on insulin therapy.
- Non-reconstitution glucagon products preferred for ease of administration.
- ADA resource (diabetes.org/living-with-diabetes/treatment-care/diabetes-technology-guide) for technology decision support.


### CGM device classification and usage

CGM devices are categorized as rtCGM, isCGM, OTC-CGM, or professional CGM based on data stream, alarms, and user requirements.. Frequency of sensor use in type 1 diabetes is a predictor of A1C lowering across all age-groups.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM device classification and usage

**Core Recommendations:**
- CGM devices are categorized as rtCGM, isCGM, OTC-CGM, or professional CGM based on data stream, alarms, and user requirements.
- Frequency of sensor use in type 1 diabetes is a predictor of A1C lowering across all age-groups.
- Most rtCGM systems require minimal or no user calibration, but all CGM systems require BGM confirmation in cases of perceived discordance.
- CGM benefits include reduced A1C, hypoglycemia episodes, and acute diabetes complications, with consistent use yielding the greatest improvements.
- CGM use in type 2 diabetes (insulin or noninsulin) improves TIR, reduces hyperglycemia, and enhances user satisfaction, though insulin dose changes are often unnecessary.
- Reductions in acute diabetes complications (e.g., DKA, severe hypoglycemia, diabetes-related coma) and hospitalizations for hypoglycemia/hyperglycemia persist for at least 2 years after CGM initiation in adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes
- isCGM devices show glycemic benefits in type 1 and type 2 diabetes across multiple therapy types (MDI, basal insulin, noninsulin) based on observational, retrospective, and registry data

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Prescription required for rtCGM devices used in diabetes management.
- OTC-CGM devices can be purchased by anyone, including those without diabetes or with prediabetes, but lack alarms and alerts.
- BGM confirmation is required for all CGM systems when sensor readings conflict with clinical perception.
- Integration with digitally connected devices (e.g., insulin systems) depends on device-specific compatibility.
- Patient willingness and ability to regularly wear and interpret CGM data are critical for sustained benefits.
- Healthcare professional oversight is recommended for professional CGM use to ensure proper data interpretation and application
- Behavioral support for parents of young children with type 1 diabetes using CGM is advised to reduce hypoglycemia concerns and diabetes distress

**Previous Recommendations:**
- CGM systems requiring frequent user calibration (e.g., older rtCGM models with mandatory fingerstick calibration).
- Reliance solely on BGM for glucose monitoring in patients eligible for CGM.
- Fixed A1C targets without consideration of CGM-derived metrics (e.g., TIR, time below/above range).
- Use of professional CGM devices without healthcare professional oversight or data interpretation.
- Assumption that CGM benefits are limited to type 1 diabetes or insulin-treated patients.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- OTC-CGM devices for lifestyle assessment in individuals without diabetes or with prediabetes.
- Integration of CGM with automated insulin systems or connected insulin pens as a class II medical device (iCGM).
- Real-world evidence showing reduced acute diabetes complications (DKA, severe hypoglycemia) with CGM use.
- CGM use in type 2 diabetes (insulin or noninsulin) demonstrating improved TIR and reduced hyperglycemia without mandatory insulin dose adjustments.
- Observational data supporting rtCGM benefits in adults with type 2 diabetes not on insulin, including reduced mean glucose and GMI.
- Short-term CGM use in youth with type 2 diabetes may not immediately impact glucose metrics or A1C but can lead to behavioral changes (e.g., increased BGM, insulin administration) and improved diabetes management and quality of life


### CGM implementation in elderly

CGM devices improve glycemic management and are acceptable for older adults with type 1 or insulin-requiring type 2 diabetes. CGM reduces hypoglycemia and improves glycemic outcomes in older adults not treated with insulin. Older adults may require more time to learn and adapt to CGM technology

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM implementation in elderly

**Core Recommendations:**
- CGM devices improve glycemic management and are acceptable for older adults with type 1 or insulin-requiring type 2 diabetes
- CGM reduces hypoglycemia and improves glycemic outcomes in older adults not treated with insulin
- Older adults may require more time to learn and adapt to CGM technology
- Caregiver engagement is beneficial for successful CGM implementation in older adults
- Cognitive and functional capacity for technology use must be assessed before CGM initiation
- CGM use in older adults not treated with insulin reduces hypoglycemia and improves other glycemic outcomes, including mean glucose levels

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Assessment of cognitive and functional capacity for technology use
- Availability of caregiver support or care partner engagement, if appropriate
- Patient resides in a setting where glucose monitoring by a surrogate is required (e.g., group homes, assisted living facilities)
- Physical or cognitive limitations necessitating surrogate glucose monitoring
- Baseline glycemic management (e.g., well-managed blood glucose with mean A1C ~7.4%)
- Baseline well-managed blood glucose (mean A1C ~7.4%) is a prerequisite for advanced insulin delivery device trials in older adults

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Hybrid closed-loop advanced insulin delivery systems improve time in range (TIR) in older adults
- Automated insulin delivery (AID) strategies reduce hypoglycemia in older adults with type 1 diabetes
- AID use in older adults with type 2 diabetes on multiple daily injections increases TIR by 27% over 12 weeks
- Real-world evidence shows hybrid closed-loop insulin delivery improves mean glucose and increases TIR by 10% in Medicare-eligible older adults
- High acceptance rate (91%) of AID systems in older adults aged 65+ years
- AIDE T1D study demonstrated 91% acceptance rate of AID systems in older adults aged 65+ years after crossover trial participation


### CGM indication expansion in pregnancy

CGM indication has expanded to include pregnancy for Dexcom G7, FreeStyle Libre 2, and FreeStyle Libre 3. Enhances care in the pregnant population. Dexcom G7 CGM indication expanded to pregnancy. FreeStyle Libre 2 CGM indication expanded to pregnancy. FreeStyle Libre 3 CGM indication expanded to pregnancy

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM indication expansion in pregnancy

**Core Recommendations:**
- CGM indication has expanded to include pregnancy for Dexcom G7, FreeStyle Libre 2, and FreeStyle Libre 3
- Enhances care in the pregnant population

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Pregnancy status must be confirmed for CGM use
- CGM use in pregnancy must be discussed in section 15, 'Management of Diabetes in Pregnancy'

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Dexcom G7 CGM indication expanded to pregnancy
- FreeStyle Libre 2 CGM indication expanded to pregnancy
- FreeStyle Libre 3 CGM indication expanded to pregnancy


### CGM sensor interference risks

CGM sensor interference risks from medications/substances. Routinely review medications and supplements to identify potential interfering substances with CGM sensors.. Advise patients on the need to use additional blood glucose monitoring (BGM) if sensor values are unreliable due to identified interfering substances.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM sensor interference risks from medications/substances

**Core Recommendations:**
- Routinely review medications and supplements to identify potential interfering substances with CGM sensors.
- Advise patients on the need to use additional blood glucose monitoring (BGM) if sensor values are unreliable due to identified interfering substances.
- Recognize that CGM sensor interference can cause temporary measurement errors, including falsely elevated glucose readings.
- Document and report suspected CGM sensor interference events to the device manufacturer and relevant regulatory bodies to contribute to post-market surveillance data.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be using a CGM device for glucose monitoring.
- Healthcare provider must assess patient's medication and supplement list for known interfering substances.
- Healthcare providers must verify the specific CGM device model in use, as interference risks may vary by manufacturer and sensor generation.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Hydroxyurea identified as a newly recognized interfering substance causing temporary increases in sensor glucose values.
- Mannitol and sorbitol (when administered intravenously or in peritoneal dialysis) identified as substances causing falsely elevated CGM readings.
- Acetaminophen (paracetamol) in high doses (>1000 mg) identified as a potential interfering substance in some CGM systems, causing falsely elevated glucose readings in certain models.


### CGM wear and glycemic metrics

Use ≥14-day CGM wear time for pattern management. Maintain ≥70% active CGM time out of 14 days. Target TIR (70–180 mg/dL) ≥50% for older adults with complex/intermediate health. Limit hypoglycemia (<70 mg/dL) to ≤1% (15 min/day) for older adults. Deintensify therapy if hypoglycemia goals (<4% <70 mg/dL, <1% <54 mg/dL) are not met

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CGM wear and glycemic metrics

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use ≥14-day CGM wear time for pattern management
- Maintain ≥70% active CGM time out of 14 days
- Target TIR (70–180 mg/dL) ≥50% for older adults with complex/intermediate health
- Limit hypoglycemia (<70 mg/dL) to ≤1% (15 min/day) for older adults
- Deintensify therapy if hypoglycemia goals (<4% <70 mg/dL, <1% <54 mg/dL) are not met
- Engage in shared decision-making with patient/family to individualize glycemic goals and adjust therapy based on changing health status

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- CGM device must be worn for ≥14 days for valid pattern analysis
- Patient must be using insulin or sulfonylureas for hypoglycemia risk assessment
- Shared decision-making with patient/family to individualize glycemic goals
- Assess cognitive function, frailty, and comorbidities for older adults
- Prioritize hypoglycemia prevention in high-risk populations
- Assess patient's cognitive function, frailty, and comorbidities when setting glycemic goals for older adults
- Consider patient's health, function, capacity for self-management, and hypoglycemia risk when individualizing glycemic goals

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized consideration
- Routine use of non-CGM-based glycemic monitoring for pattern management
- One-size-fits-all glycemic goals without accounting for hypoglycemia risk
- Ignoring patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, self-management capacity)
- Overly aggressive glycemic targets in frail or high-hypoglycemia-risk individuals
- Routine use of fixed A1C targets without individualized consideration of patient-specific factors

**Updated Recommendations:**
- CGM wear time ≥14 days as standard for valid glycemic pattern analysis
- Active CGM time ≥70% as a minimum threshold for reliable data
- Hypoglycemia thresholds (<70 mg/dL <1%, <54 mg/dL <1%) as actionable CGM metrics
- Individualized TIR goals (e.g., ≥50% for older adults) based on health status
- Therapy deintensification if hypoglycemia metrics are exceeded
- Lower coefficient of variation (CV) target of <33% for additional hypoglycemia protection in insulin or sulfonylurea users
- Less stringent glycemic goals for individuals with significant cognitive/functional limitations, frailty, or severe comorbidities



## Type 1 Diabetes Clinical Management

This collection of clinical management topics for Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM) highlights comprehensive care strategies spanning diagnosis, risk stratification, and management of microvascular complications, with a focus on early intervention, monitoring, and multidisciplinary support to optimize patient outcomes.

**Key Features:**
- T1DM epidemiology and diagnosis — Establish diagnosis via FPG, OGTT, HbA1c, or random plasma glucose with characteristic hyperglycemia symptoms; screen high-risk individuals due to rising global incidence and DKA risk (25–50% at presentation).
- Diabetic foot examination protocol — Perform annual comprehensive foot evaluation including pulse palpation, monofilament and vibration perception testing, and patient education on daily foot care to prevent ulcers and amputations.
- Small fiber neuropathy assessment — Evaluate for positive neuropathic symptoms (burning pain, allodynia) and objective loss of pinprick/thermal sensation; common in T1DM and requires targeted screening.
- Sleep disorders in diabetes — Screen for obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) in symptomatic individuals, as prevalence exceeds 50% in T1DM and impacts glycemic control and cardiovascular risk.
- Peripheral neuropathy management — Optimize glycemic control and footwear; assess proprioception during activity; refer atypical cases for nerve conduction studies to differentiate etiologies.
- Diabetic autonomic neuropathy — Screen for cardiac, blood pressure, and thermoregulatory dysfunction before and during exercise; requires cardiac evaluation for intense activity.
- Type 1 diabetes autoimmune risk assessment — Screen high-risk individuals (e.g., first-degree relatives) for islet autoantibodies (GAD65, IA-2, ZnT8, insulin) to identify preclinical T1DM.
- Diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation — Classify neuropathy as diagnosis of exclusion; up to 50% of cases are asymptomatic, necessitating routine screening.
- Glucose monitoring strategy in adolescent T1DM — Initiate continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) early and maintain uninterrupted access to sustain HbA1c reductions in youth.
- Insulin therapy optimization in T1DM — Require lifelong insulin therapy with multidisciplinary team support for education, dosing, and adherence to achieve glycemic targets.

**Notable Details:**
- DKA presents in 25–50% of new T1DM cases, underscoring the need for early diagnosis and education.
- Annual diabetic foot exams must include monofilament testing and pulse assessment to stratify ulcer/amputation risk.
- CGM initiation within the first year of T1DM diagnosis in adolescents is associated with sustained HbA1c improvements, especially in publicly insured populations.
- Up to 50% of diabetic peripheral neuropathy cases are asymptomatic, necessitating routine screening even in the absence of symptoms.
- OSA prevalence exceeds 50% in T1DM, warranting evaluation in symptomatic patients due to links to poor glycemic control and cardiovascular risk.

**Topics in this group:**

### T1DM epidemiology and diagnosis

T1DM epidemiology, diagnosis, and risk stratification. Increasing incidence and prevalence of type 1 diabetes observed globally.. Acute symptom presentation with markedly elevated blood glucose levels.. 25–50% of T1DM cases present with life-threatening diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA). T1DM epidemiology and diagnosis. T1DM epidemiology and diagnosis. Diagnosis established via FPG, OGTT, HbA1c, or random plasma glucose with characteristic hyperglycemia symptoms. Peak incidence between 10-14 years of age, though can occur at any age. Autoimmune disease with insulin deficiency and hyperglycemia in genetically susceptible individuals

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** T1DM epidemiology, diagnosis, and risk stratification

**Core Recommendations:**
- Increasing incidence and prevalence of type 1 diabetes observed globally.
- Acute symptom presentation with markedly elevated blood glucose levels.
- 25–50% of T1DM cases present with life-threatening diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA).
- Family history increases risk but ~90% of cases occur without known relatives with T1DM.
- Islet autoantibody testing identifies individuals at high risk for progression to T1DM.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Confirmation of first autoantibody-positive test with a second test within 3 months in a laboratory meeting Islet Autoantibody Standardization Program (IASP) standards.
- Screening programs should include counseling on diabetes symptoms, DKA prevention, and referral pathways for autoantibody-positive individuals.
- Teplizumab-mzwv infusion for stage 2 T1DM requires discussion with patients ≥8 years in a setting with trained personnel.
- Genetic risk scores may identify high-risk subsets for targeted autoantibody testing, particularly in populations of European ancestry.
- Screening programs should ensure high-quality assays and resources for follow-up before implementation.
- Confirmation of ≥2 islet autoantibodies (e.g., GAD65, IA-2, insulin, ZnT8) is required to define stage 2 T1DM for teplizumab-mzwv eligibility in individuals ≥8 years.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine reliance on single autoantibody testing without confirmation.
- Fixed A1C targets for T1DM diagnosis without consideration of acute symptom presentation.
- Generalized screening without validated assays or follow-up infrastructure.
- Routine single autoantibody testing without confirmatory retesting within 3 months.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Nationwide screening programs for T1DM in general population (e.g., Italy’s program for ages 1–17 years).
- Teplizumab-mzwv approved to delay stage 3 T1DM in stage 2 patients ≥8 years, with HLA-DR4 and absence of HLA-DR3/antizinc transporter 8 predicting response.
- Use of genetic risk scores in newborn screening to identify high-risk subsets for autoantibody testing.
- Evidence linking higher glycemic index diets and sugar intake to increased progression risk in autoantibody-positive children.
- Moderate to vigorous physical activity associated with reduced progression risk in children with multiple autoantibodies.
- Presence of HLA-DR4 and absence of HLA-DR3/antizinc transporter 8 (ZnT8) antibodies predict better response to teplizumab-mzwv in stage 2 T1DM.
- Genetic risk scores in newborn screening can identify >77% of future T1DM cases within 10% of the general population, prioritizing autoantibody testing in high-risk subsets.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** T1DM epidemiology and diagnosis

**Core Management Principles:**
- Diagnosis established via FPG, OGTT, HbA1c, or random plasma glucose with characteristic hyperglycemia symptoms
- Peak incidence between 10-14 years of age, though can occur at any age
- Autoimmune disease with insulin deficiency and hyperglycemia in genetically susceptible individuals
- Variable presentations: acute in children, gradual in adults
- Autoantibodies present in 70-90% of individuals with T1DM
- Presence of 'honeymoon period' after diagnosis with partial recovery of β-cell function and minimal/no exogenous insulin requirement, followed by subsequent loss of residual β-cells
- Variable β-cell damage: not complete in certain individuals, with 30-80% of long-term T1DM patients showing insulin micro-secretion detectable via sensitive C-peptide measurements

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Presence of two or more islet cell autoantibodies for Stage 1 diagnosis
- Genetic susceptibility (e.g., HLA DR3-DQ2 or DR4-DQ8 haplotypes)
- Characteristic signs and symptoms of hyperglycemia for clinical diagnosis
- Family history of T1DM increases risk (3% for mother, 5% for father, 8% for sibling)
- Differentiation from other diabetes forms, especially in ages 20-40 with heterogeneous presentations
- Sensitive C-peptide measurements required to detect residual β-cell function in long-term T1DM patients for prognostic assessment
- Autoantibody titer, affinity, and type (e.g., IA-2, ZnT8) assessment required for risk stratification and progression prediction in presymptomatic stages


### diabetic foot examination protocol

Perform a comprehensive foot evaluation at least annually to identify risk factors for ulcers and amputations. diabetic foot examination protocol. Diabetic foot examination protocol. Perform detailed foot exam including palpation of dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses, monofilament and vibration perception testing (VPT). Provide foot care education to all patients with diabetes (e.g., daily foot inspection, avoiding barefoot walking, proper foot hygiene)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic foot examination protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Perform a comprehensive foot evaluation at least annually to identify risk factors for ulcers and amputations.
- Include inspection of skin, assessment of foot deformities, neurological assessment (10-g monofilament or Ipswich touch test with at least one additional assessment: pinprick, temperature, or vibration), and vascular assessment (pulses in legs and feet).
- Inspect feet at every visit for individuals with sensory loss, prior ulceration, or amputation.
- Obtain history of ulceration, amputation, Charcot foot, vascular interventions, smoking, retinopathy, and renal disease, and assess current symptoms of neuropathy and vascular disease.
- Provide general preventive foot self-care education to all people with diabetes, including daily foot inspection techniques.
- Assess capillary refill time, rubor on dependency, pallor on elevation, and venous filling time as part of initial PAD screening.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes diagnosis
- Evidence of sensory loss, prior ulceration, or amputation (for more frequent examinations)
- History of foot complications or high-risk conditions (e.g., PAD, Charcot foot, dialysis)
- Patient willingness to participate in self-care education
- Access to appropriate diagnostic tools (e.g., 10-g monofilament, vascular assessment tools)
- Referral for ankle-brachial index with toe pressures for individuals with history of leg fatigue, claudication, rest pain relieved with dependency, or decreased/absent pedal pulses.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance on 10-g monofilament testing alone for diagnosing early diabetic peripheral neuropathy (DPN).
- Use of nerve conduction studies, electromyography, nerve biopsy, or intraepidermal nerve fiber density biopsies for routine DPN diagnosis.
- Fixed-frequency foot examinations without risk stratification.
- Exclusion of smoking cessation counseling in high-risk patients.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Incorporation of Ipswich touch test as an alternative to 10-g monofilament for LOPS screening.
- Referral to foot care specialists for individuals with high-risk feet (e.g., dialysis, Charcot foot, prior ulcers/amputations, PAD).
- Use of specialized therapeutic footwear for high-risk individuals (e.g., LOPS, deformities, ulcers, poor circulation).
- Consideration of adjunctive treatments (e.g., negative-pressure wound therapy, skin substitutes, topical oxygen therapy) for chronic non-healing foot ulcers.
- Interprofessional approach facilitated by a podiatrist for high-risk foot care.
- Refer individuals who smoke and have high-risk features (prior lower-extremity complications, LOPS, structural abnormalities, or PAD) to smoking cessation counseling.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic foot examination protocol

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Perform detailed foot exam including palpation of dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses, monofilament and vibration perception testing (VPT)
- Provide foot care education to all patients with diabetes (e.g., daily foot inspection, avoiding barefoot walking, proper foot hygiene)
- Refer patients with foot deformity, absent pedal pulses, or ulceration to specialist assessment within 24 hours
- Commence antibiotics immediately for clinical signs of foot infection; admit urgently for severe/limb-threatening infection
- Use Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) or intermittently scanned CGM (iSCGM) for advanced monitoring in cases of recurrent hypoglycemia or severe glucose fluctuations
- Perform retinal check-up (fundus examination for retinopathy) as part of basic clinical exam
- Perform blood urea/serum creatinine tests as part of basic clinical exam

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient with diabetes mellitus
- Presence of foot deformity, absent pedal pulses, or ulceration for specialist referral
- Clinical signs of infection for antibiotic initiation
- Recurrent hypoglycemia or severe glucose fluctuations for CGM use
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, cognitive function) for individualized care
- Presence of recurrent hypoglycemia, severe glucose fluctuations, disparity in measured glucose values and HbA1c, severe anemia, hemoglobinopathy, or hypoglycemia unawareness for advanced monitoring with CGM or iSCGM
- Presence of clinical signs of severe/limb-threatening infection for urgent admission and intravenous antibiotic therapy


### small fiber neuropathy assessment

small fiber neuropathy assessment in T1DM. Positive symptoms predominate, including burning or lancinating pain, hyperalgesia, dysesthesia, and allodynia. Loss of pinprick sensation and abnormal thermal sensation are objective tests for small fiber neuropathy

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** small fiber neuropathy assessment in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Positive symptoms predominate, including burning or lancinating pain, hyperalgesia, dysesthesia, and allodynia
- Loss of pinprick sensation and abnormal thermal sensation are objective tests for small fiber neuropathy
- Sudomotor dysfunction (e.g., decreased sweating, dry skin) is an early and sensitive marker, detectable via QSART or Sudoscan
- Corneal confocal microscopy (CCM) is an emerging non-invasive diagnostic tool for early detection
- Motor functions and deep tendon reflexes are typically preserved in small fiber neuropathy
- Abnormal thermal sensation is an additional manifestation of small fiber neuropathy
- Sudomotor dysfunction in small fiber DSPN includes impaired blood flow and cold feet

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Routine nerve conduction studies (NCV) may not detect small fiber dysfunction due to preserved large fiber conduction
- Skin biopsy for intraepidermal nerve fiber density (IENFD) is not recommended for routine practice
- Sudomotor function tests (QSART or Sudoscan) require specialized equipment and trained personnel
- Corneal confocal microscopy requires access to specialized imaging technology
- Differentiation between small fiber and large fiber neuropathy is essential for appropriate management
- Sudomotor dysfunction assessment requires specialized equipment and trained personnel for QSART and Sudoscan
- Corneal confocal microscopy (CCM) requires access to specialized imaging technology for non-invasive visualization of corneal nerve fibers


### sleep disorders in diabetes

Sleep disorders in diabetes management. Sleep duration, quality, and timing (chronotype) are critical factors linked to diabetes risk and management.. Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) is highly prevalent in diabetes (up to 58% in type 2 diabetes, >50% in type 1 diabetes) and should be evaluated in symptomatic individuals.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Sleep disorders in diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Sleep duration, quality, and timing (chronotype) are critical factors linked to diabetes risk and management.
- Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) is highly prevalent in diabetes (up to 58% in type 2 diabetes, >50% in type 1 diabetes) and should be evaluated in symptomatic individuals.
- Sleep disturbances reduce diabetes self-management engagement and increase hypoglycemia risk in type 1 diabetes.
- Shift work and circadian rhythm disorders are associated with elevated A1C, neuropathy, and psychological distress in diabetes.
- Sleep disorders (OSA, insomnia, restless leg syndrome) are common in diabetes and linked to microvascular/macrovascular complications.
- Sleep disturbances in parents of children/adolescents with diabetes are common, particularly after diagnosis, and may impact family sleep patterns and diabetes management.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Symptoms suggestive of OSA (e.g., excessive daytime sleepiness, snoring, witnessed apnea) warrant evaluation.
- Comprehensive lifestyle assessment including sleep duration, shift work, and work schedules is recommended.
- Diabetes technology (e.g., AID systems) may be beneficial but requires individualized assessment for sleep impact.
- Referral to sleep specialists is advised for significant sleep difficulties, in collaboration with the diabetes care team.
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., obesity, central obesity) increase OSA risk and require targeted screening.
- Screening for sleep-disordered breathing in individuals with obesity and type 2 diabetes should be prioritized, as prevalence may exceed 80% in this subgroup.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets without consideration of sleep disorders or circadian misalignment.
- Routine use of sedative-hypnotics for sleep without addressing underlying sleep disorders in diabetes.
- Ignoring sleep hygiene education as a primary intervention for sleep disturbances in diabetes.
- Assuming sleep duration within normal ranges (7 hours) is universally optimal without individualization.
- Excluding sleep assessment in standard diabetes care protocols.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- CBT for insomnia (including sleep extension) improves sleep outcomes, A1C, fasting glucose, and depressive symptoms in diabetes.
- GLP-1 RAs and dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs (e.g., tirzepatide) show promise in reducing OSA severity and body weight in obesity-related OSA.
- Sleep education (sleep hygiene) improves sleep quality, reduces A1C, and decreases insulin resistance in type 2 diabetes.
- Pharmacologic options (e.g., suvorexant, ramelteon, lemborexant) may be considered for insomnia in diabetes with careful monitoring.
- Standardized screening for sleep disorders (OSA, insomnia, restless leg syndrome) is now part of diabetes care.
- Sleep extension interventions are now recognized as evidence-based strategies to improve sleep outcomes and potentially reduce insulin resistance in diabetes.


### peripheral neuropathy management

Peripheral neuropathy management in exercise and foot care. Assess kinesthetic and proprioceptive sensation during physical activity to prevent skin breakdown and Charcot joint destruction. Use proper footwear during moderate-intensity walking to reduce risk of foot ulcers or reulceration peripheral neuropathy management. peripheral neuropathy management. Refer atypical cases to a neurologist for further investigations like nerve conduction studies. Tight glycemic control is essential for peripheral neuropathy management

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Peripheral neuropathy management in exercise and foot care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Assess kinesthetic and proprioceptive sensation during physical activity to prevent skin breakdown and Charcot joint destruction
- Use proper footwear during moderate-intensity walking to reduce risk of foot ulcers or reulceration
- Engage in 150 minutes/week of moderate exercise to improve outcomes in prediabetic neuropathy
- Wear proper footwear and examine feet daily to detect lesions early
- Restrict to non-weight-bearing activities for individuals with foot injury or open sores
- Perform a thorough assessment of kinesthetic and proprioceptive sensation during physical activity, particularly in individuals with severe neuropathy

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Peripheral neuropathy diagnosis confirmed
- Assessment of neuropathy severity to determine risk of skin breakdown or joint damage
- Proper footwear must be used during exercise
- Daily foot examination for early lesion detection
- Non-weight-bearing restriction for active foot injuries or open sores
- Assessment of pain sensation and pain threshold in extremities to evaluate neuropathy severity

**Updated Recommendations:**
- 150 minutes/week of moderate exercise recommended for prediabetic neuropathy
- Emphasis on daily foot examination and proper footwear as standard care

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** peripheral neuropathy management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Refer atypical cases to a neurologist for further investigations like nerve conduction studies
- Tight glycemic control is essential for peripheral neuropathy management
- First-line medications for painful peripheral neuropathy include antiepileptics (gabapentin, pregabalin), tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) (amitriptyline, imipramine), and SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine)
- Use maximum dose of first-line drugs or combine two drugs for pain relief in painful neuropathy
- Second-line drugs like opioids (tramadol) are advised for resistant painful neuropathies
- Refer males with sexual dysfunction to specialists for appropriate therapy

**Prerequisites:**
- Vitamin B12 assay (if possible) and TSH testing for evaluation
- Paraproteinemia testing (if possible) for atypical cases
- Nerve conduction studies for atypical cases referred to a neurologist
- Vibration perception threshold (VPT) examination using a biothesiometer for assessing sensory loss
- Manual palpation of dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses and ABI assessment if peripheral pulsations are absent or clinical suspicion of ischemia
- Record vibration perception results as absent, reduced, or present when using tuning fork assessment
- Assess VPT using a biothesiometer with the patient in supine position (with or without eyes closed)


### diabetic autonomic neuropathy

Exercise safety in diabetic autonomic neuropathy. Cardiac investigation required before initiating physical activity more intense than accustomed. Monitor for exercise-induced injury due to decreased cardiac responsiveness. Assess postural hypotension risk before and during exercise. Evaluate thermoregulation impairment during physical activity diabetic autonomic neuropathy. Diabetic autonomic neuropathy (DAN) screening and management. Screen for autonomic neuropathy in patients with long-standing diabetes or severe hyperglycemia, especially those with other microvascular complications (e.g., retinopathy, nephropathy).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Exercise safety in diabetic autonomic neuropathy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Cardiac investigation required before initiating physical activity more intense than accustomed
- Monitor for exercise-induced injury due to decreased cardiac responsiveness
- Assess postural hypotension risk before and during exercise
- Evaluate thermoregulation impairment during physical activity
- Screen for hypoglycemia susceptibility during and after exercise
- Assess impaired night vision due to impaired pupillary reaction during physical activity

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diagnosis of diabetic autonomic neuropathy confirmed
- Cardiac autonomic neuropathy assessment completed
- Baseline cardiovascular risk stratification performed
- Individualized exercise prescription developed
- Continuous glucose monitoring recommended during activity
- Assessment of cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy (CAN) as an independent risk factor for silent myocardial ischemia

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Unsupervised high-intensity exercise programs
- Ignoring postural symptoms during physical activity
- Assuming normal cardiac response to exercise
- Routine exercise without prior autonomic screening
- Standardized exercise protocols without risk stratification

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Cardiac autonomic reflex tests (e.g., heart rate response to standing)
- Continuous glucose monitoring during exercise
- Gradual intensity progression with symptom monitoring
- Blood pressure monitoring before and after exercise
- Thermoregulation assessment in hot/cold environments

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic autonomic neuropathy (DAN) screening and management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Screen for autonomic neuropathy in patients with long-standing diabetes or severe hyperglycemia, especially those with other microvascular complications (e.g., retinopathy, nephropathy).
- Assess for autonomic symptoms across organ systems: cardiovascular (e.g., orthostatic hypotension, resting tachycardia), gastrointestinal (e.g., gastroparesis, constipation), and genitourinary (e.g., erectile dysfunction, neurogenic bladder).
- Evaluate risk factors: duration of diabetes, hyperglycemia severity, insulin resistance (e.g., dyslipidemia, hypertension), smoking, alcohol exposure, and genetic predisposition.
- Consider autoimmune contributions (e.g., CIDP, vasculitis) in proximal neuropathies, particularly in T1DM/T2DM patients with atypical presentations.
- Individualize management based on symptom severity and organ system involvement to improve quality of life and reduce complications.
- Assess for heat intolerance and intraoperative cardiovascular instability as part of cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy evaluation
- Evaluate nocturnal diarrhea and fecal incontinence as potential indicators of advanced gastrointestinal autonomic neuropathy

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Documented diagnosis of diabetes mellitus (T1DM or T2DM).
- Presence of risk factors: ≥5–10 years diabetes duration, poor glycemic control, or coexisting microvascular complications.
- Exclusion of non-diabetic causes of autonomic neuropathy (e.g., autoimmune disorders, toxic exposures).
- Baseline assessment of autonomic symptoms (e.g., orthostatic vitals, gastrointestinal motility, sexual function).
- Multidisciplinary collaboration (e.g., endocrinology, cardiology, gastroenterology) for complex cases.
- Document height as a potential independent predictor for diabetic autonomic neuropathy due to its association with length-dependent nerve damage


### type 1 diabetes autoimmune risk assessment

Type 1 diabetes autoimmune risk assessment and screening. Screen for islet autoantibodies (GAD65, IA-2, IA-2b, ZnT8, insulin autoantibodies) in high-risk individuals (e.g., first-degree relatives of type 1 diabetes patients, those with other autoimmune diseases, or genetic risk factors).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Type 1 diabetes autoimmune risk assessment and screening

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen for islet autoantibodies (GAD65, IA-2, IA-2b, ZnT8, insulin autoantibodies) in high-risk individuals (e.g., first-degree relatives of type 1 diabetes patients, those with other autoimmune diseases, or genetic risk factors).
- Monitor metabolic status (random glucose, A1C) at intervals of 6 months to 3 years based on age and risk profile to detect progression to stage 2 or 3 type 1 diabetes.
- Perform HLA typing (DQB1, DRB1 haplotypes) to assess genetic risk, particularly for DR3-DQ2 and DR4-DQ8 alleles (predisposing) or DRB1*1501 and DQA1*0102-DQB1*0602 (protective).
- Screen for autoimmune comorbidities (thyroid dysfunction, celiac disease, pernicious anemia) at diagnosis and periodically, especially in symptomatic individuals or high-risk groups.
- Avoid delays in diagnostic testing for stage 3 type 1 diabetes to prevent DKA, particularly in children and adolescents.
- Screen for celiac disease using serum IgA and tissue transglutaminase (tTG) IgA antibodies (or tTG IgG/IgG deamidated gliadin peptide if IgA deficient) at diagnosis, 2 years later, and 5 years later in type 1 diabetes, or sooner if symptomatic (e.g., poor growth, increased hypoglycemia).
- Repeat thyroid function tests after achieving metabolic stability and glycemic goals if initially performed during acute hyperglycemia, ketosis, or ketoacidosis to avoid misleading results.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Presence of one or more islet autoantibodies for metabolic monitoring and progression risk assessment.
- Family history of type 1 diabetes or other autoimmune diseases for targeted screening.
- Metabolic stability (e.g., post-hyperglycemia resolution) before thyroid function testing to avoid misleading results.
- Symptoms suggestive of autoimmune comorbidities (e.g., gastrointestinal symptoms for celiac disease, fatigue for thyroid dysfunction) for diagnostic workup.
- High-risk genetic profile (e.g., HLA-DR3-DQ2 or DR4-DQ8) for prioritized autoantibody screening.
- Metabolic stability and achievement of glycemic goals before repeating thyroid function tests if initially performed during acute hyperglycemia, ketosis, or ketoacidosis.
- Consider bone mineral density monitoring via dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry in older adults (≥65 years) or younger individuals with multiple risk factors every 2–3 years.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine fixed-interval screening without consideration of age, antibody specificity, or genetic risk.
- Assuming obesity precludes type 1 diabetes diagnosis.
- Relying solely on single autoantibody results without metabolic evaluation.
- Performing thyroid function tests during acute metabolic instability (e.g., DKA) without repeat testing.
- Discontinuing celiac disease screening after 5 years in asymptomatic individuals without risk factors.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Stage-based classification (Stage 1: multiple autoantibodies + normoglycemia; Stage 2: autoantibodies + dysglycemia; Stage 3: overt hyperglycemia) for risk stratification.
- Semiannual OGTT in high-risk family members to reduce DKA risk at diagnosis.
- Genetic screening (HLA-DQB1/DRB1) to identify high-risk populations for early intervention trials.
- Increased vigilance for SARS-CoV-2 as a potential trigger for type 1 diabetes in susceptible individuals, with ongoing research via registries like CoviDIAB.
- Consideration of vitamin B12 deficiency screening in individuals with peripheral neuropathy or unexplained anemia.
- Genetic testing for HLA-DQ2/DQ8 may not be necessary for celiac disease screening in type 1 diabetes due to near-universal presence in affected individuals.
- Consider osteoporosis drug therapy in older adults with diabetes at increased fracture risk (e.g., T-score ≤−2.5, history of fragility fracture, or elevated FRAX score).


### diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation

Diabetic neuropathy is a diagnosis of exclusion; nondiabetic neuropathies may coexist and require evaluation.. Up to 50% of diabetic peripheral neuropathy (DPN) cases may be asymptomatic, necessitating routine screening. diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation. Diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation. Sensory neuropathy presents with paresthesiae (tingling, numbness, pain) or loss of sensation in touch, pain, temperature, and vibration diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation. diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation. DN is defined as the presence of signs and/or symptoms of peripheral neuropathy in a patient with diabetes, after excluding other causes.. Symptoms are often mild and frequently unnoticed in the initial stages of DN.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetic neuropathy is a diagnosis of exclusion; nondiabetic neuropathies may coexist and require evaluation.
- Up to 50% of diabetic peripheral neuropathy (DPN) cases may be asymptomatic, necessitating routine screening.
- Symptoms vary by fiber involvement: small-fiber dysfunction causes pain/dysesthesia; large-fiber dysfunction causes numbness, balance issues, and loss of protective sensation (LOPS).
- Autonomic neuropathy symptoms include orthostatic dizziness, syncope, erectile dysfunction, sweating changes, resting tachycardia, and orthostatic hypotension.
- Annual screening for DPN and autonomic neuropathy is recommended starting at diagnosis for type 2 diabetes and 5 years post-diagnosis for type 1 diabetes.
- Impaired counterregulatory responses to hypoglycemia in type 1 and type 2 diabetes can lead to impaired hypoglycemia awareness, which is not directly linked to autonomic neuropathy but should be considered in autonomic assessment.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diagnosis of type 2 diabetes (screening at diagnosis) or type 1 diabetes (screening 5 years post-diagnosis).
- Annual reassessment for DPN and autonomic neuropathy in all people with diabetes.
- Use of validated tools: 10-g monofilament, 128-Hz tuning fork (vibration), pinprick/temperature testing, and symptom questionnaires.
- Consideration of non-diabetic causes of neuropathy (e.g., vitamin B12 deficiency, toxins, medications) in diagnostic workup.
- Referral to specialist (neurologist) for atypical presentations (acute/subacute, asymmetric, motor involvement).
- Assessment for autonomic neuropathy should be prioritized in individuals with evidence of other microvascular complications, particularly kidney disease and diabetic peripheral neuropathy.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of electrophysiological testing or MRI for typical DPN presentations.
- Fixed reliance on numeric A1C targets for neuropathy risk stratification.
- Exclusive focus on symptomatic DPN without screening asymptomatic cases.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Emphasis on 10-g monofilament testing for identifying feet at risk for ulceration/amputation.
- Inclusion of Ipswich touch test and cardiovascular autonomic testing for early CAN detection.
- Recognition of CAN as an independent mortality risk factor requiring proactive screening.
- Consideration of sudomotor dysfunction and hypoglycemia unawareness in autonomic neuropathy assessment.
- Integration of patient-reported outcomes (e.g., erectile dysfunction, constipation) in autonomic neuropathy screening.
- Advanced CAN may be associated with resting tachycardia (>100 bpm) and orthostatic hypotension (a fall in systolic or diastolic blood pressure by >20 mmHg or >10 mmHg, respectively, upon standing without an appropriate increase in heart rate).
- Early CAN may be completely asymptomatic and detected only by decreased heart rate variability with deep breathing.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation

**Core Management Principles:**
- DN is defined as the presence of signs and/or symptoms of peripheral neuropathy in a patient with diabetes, after excluding other causes.
- Symptoms are often mild and frequently unnoticed in the initial stages of DN.
- DN is mainly classified into diffuse neuropathy (>90%) and focal/multifocal neuropathy (<10%).
- Focal/multifocal neuropathies are characterized by acute/subacute onset, focal deficits, pain, and spontaneous partial/complete recovery.
- DSPN is the classical presentation of neuropathy in diabetes, typically length-dependent, starting in the feet and legs.
- DSPN can present as predominantly small-fiber neuropathy, large-fiber neuropathy, or mixed small and large-fiber neuropathy

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Exclusion of other causes of neuropathy before diagnosing DN.
- Recognition that DSPN is a length-dependent polyneuropathy affecting lower limbs first.
- Exclusion of other causes of neuropathy is mandatory before diagnosing DN

### Source 3: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic neuropathy classification and presentation

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Sensory neuropathy presents with paresthesiae (tingling, numbness, pain) or loss of sensation in touch, pain, temperature, and vibration
- Neuropathy can cause foot deformities, dry plantar skin, and neuropathic foot ulcers prone to infection, cellulitis, and osteomyelitis, often requiring partial foot amputation
- Motor weakness in small foot muscles leads to inability to grip footwear, arch alteration, and pressure on the first metatarsal and heel
- Diabetic amyotrophy is indicated by quadriceps wasting, absent knee jerk, and bilateral hip muscle weakness
- Cranial motor neuropathy may involve 3rd, 6th, and rarely 7th nerves; radiculopathy and cranial neuropathies are reversible, while sensorimotor symmetrical polyneuropathy is progressive and irreversible
- Autonomic neuropathy may present with resting tachycardia, fixed heart rate, or exercise intolerance due to cardiac autonomic dysfunction

**Prerequisites:**
- Assess for autonomic neuropathy symptoms such as orthostatic hypotension, gastroparesis, diarrhea/constipation, impotence, cystopathy, and gustatory sweating
- Exclude other causes of neuropathy (e.g., nutritional deficiency, hypothyroidism, spinal disorders, paraneoplastic syndrome, drug-induced, alcohol abuse, uremic neuropathies)
- Perform foot examination for sensation, motor power assessment, and tendon jerks
- Measure blood pressure in sitting and standing positions and conduct ECG for heart rate variability to diagnose autonomic neuropathy
- Perform 10-g monofilament testing for protective sensation in feet as part of standardized foot examination


### diabetic neuropathy management

Optimize glucose, weight, blood pressure, and lipid management to prevent or slow progression of diabetic neuropathy.. Assess and treat pain related to diabetic peripheral neuropathy and autonomic neuropathy symptoms to improve quality of life. diabetic neuropathy management. Painful diabetic neuropathy management. Treat painful neuropathy with drugs chosen based on efficacy and tolerability for the individual patient. Continue treatment for at least 2-4 weeks before assessing effectiveness. Consider a 30-50% reduction in pain as a marker of effectiveness

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetic neuropathy management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Optimize glucose, weight, blood pressure, and lipid management to prevent or slow progression of diabetic neuropathy.
- Assess and treat pain related to diabetic peripheral neuropathy and autonomic neuropathy symptoms to improve quality of life.
- Use gabapentinoids, SNRIs, tricyclic antidepressants, or sodium channel blockers as initial pharmacologic treatments for neuropathic pain.
- Avoid opioids (including tramadol and tapentadol) for neuropathic pain treatment except in rare circumstances due to adverse events.
- Combine pharmacologic agents for neuropathic pain if monotherapy is ineffective.
- Concurrent treatment of sleep and mood disorders alongside neuropathic pain management due to their increased frequency in individuals with diabetic peripheral neuropathy.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 1 diabetes for prevention/delay of neuropathy development.
- Type 2 diabetes for slowing progression of neuropathy.
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk) for individualized A1C targets.
- Diagnosis of diabetic peripheral neuropathy or autonomic neuropathy symptoms.
- Prior optimization of glycemic control and cardiovascular risk factors.
- Diagnosis of autonomic neuropathy symptoms (e.g., cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy) for targeted management.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Opioids (including tramadol and tapentadol) for routine neuropathic pain treatment due to adverse events and lack of long-term efficacy.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults without consideration of patient-specific factors.
- Conventional lipid-lowering pharmacotherapy (e.g., statins, fibrates) for treating or preventing DPN.
- Routine use of GLP-1 RA agents for DPN management due to controversial and limited evidence.
- Use of lidocaine patches for widespread neuropathic pain.
- Lidocaine patches for widespread neuropathic pain due to limited efficacy.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Intensive glucose and blood pressure management to reduce risk of CAN in type 2 diabetes.
- Combination therapy with gabapentinoids, SNRIs, TCAs, or sodium channel blockers for neuropathic pain.
- Topical capsaicin (8% patch) for localized neuropathic pain in patients contraindicated for oral therapy.
- Early glycemic management in type 1 diabetes to delay or prevent DPN and CAN.
- Lifestyle interventions (e.g., exercise, weight loss) to improve neuropathy symptoms and outcomes.
- Topical capsaicin (8% patch) for localized neuropathic pain in patients contraindicated for or preferring non-oral therapy.
- Metabolic surgery as a potential intervention for improving neuropathy outcomes in obese individuals with type 2 diabetes (evidence from observational studies).

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Painful diabetic neuropathy management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Treat painful neuropathy with drugs chosen based on efficacy and tolerability for the individual patient
- Continue treatment for at least 2-4 weeks before assessing effectiveness
- Consider a 30-50% reduction in pain as a marker of effectiveness
- Use pregabalin or gabapentin as first-line agents due to strong evidence and FDA approval
- Titrate gabapentinoids and TCAs cautiously in elderly patients due to higher side effect risk
- Consider benfotiamine (a lipid-soluble thiamine derivative) as a potential adjunctive therapy for diabetic neuropathy, particularly in patients with poor glycemic control, based on randomized controlled trials showing improvement in neuropathic symptoms

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Diagnosis of painful diabetic neuropathy confirmed by clinical evaluation
- Assessment of patient-specific factors including age, comorbidities, and renal function
- Prioritize non-opioid agents for neuropathic pain management
- Monitor for adverse effects such as sedation, dizziness, and orthostatic hypotension
- Avoid TCAs in patients with glaucoma, symptomatic prostatic disease, or cardiovascular disease
- Assess and document glycemic control (HbA1c) as a prerequisite for initiating neuropathic pain management, as poor glycemic control may exacerbate neuropathy progression


### glucose monitoring strategy in adolescent T1DM

Early and sustained CGM use in adolescent T1DM. Initiate continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) within the first year of T1DM diagnosis to improve glycemic outcomes. Maintain uninterrupted CGM access to sustain HbA1c reductions in youth with public insurance glucose monitoring strategy in adolescent T1DM. glucose monitoring strategy in adolescent T1DM. Monitor capillary blood glucose prior to meals/snacks, at bedtime, postprandial occasionally, before exercise, when hypoglycemia is suspected, after treating hypoglycemia, and before critical tasks (e.g., driving)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Early and sustained CGM use in adolescent T1DM

**Core Recommendations:**
- Initiate continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) within the first year of T1DM diagnosis to improve glycemic outcomes
- Maintain uninterrupted CGM access to sustain HbA1c reductions in youth with public insurance
- Use structured individualized education to enhance CGM adoption and adherence in poorly controlled T1DM
- Leverage team-based care models (e.g., Teamwork, Targets, Technology, and Tight Control) for early CGM initiation
- Prioritize real-time CGM over intermittently scanned CGM in adolescents for tighter glycemic control
- Integrate peer mentoring programs to support CGM adoption and reduce diabetes distress in underserved adolescent T1DM populations
- Utilize professional CGM (pCGM) for data-driven clinical decision-making in adolescents with poorly controlled T1DM

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Newly diagnosed or existing adolescent T1DM patients
- Access to diabetes education and technology training
- Healthcare team with CGM implementation expertise
- Insurance coverage or financial support for CGM devices
- Patient/family willingness to engage in frequent glucose monitoring
- Healthcare team proficiency in interpreting CGM data and providing actionable feedback
- Access to dermatology or allergy specialists for managing skin-related adverse reactions to CGM devices

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine reliance on self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) as the sole monitoring strategy in adolescents
- Delayed CGM initiation beyond the first year post-diagnosis
- Intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) as the primary monitoring method in high-risk adolescents
- Non-structured or ad-hoc diabetes education for CGM users
- Discontinuation of CGM due to transient technical or skin-related issues
- Discontinuation of CGM due to minor technical issues without attempting troubleshooting or device replacement
- Reliance on self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) as the sole monitoring strategy in adolescents with suboptimal glycemic control

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Early CGM initiation (<1 year post-diagnosis) linked to improved long-term glycemic outcomes
- Uninterrupted CGM access associated with sustained HbA1c reductions in underserved youth
- Structured education programs to optimize CGM use in adolescents with T1DM
- Team-based care models integrating CGM for tight glycemic control in newly diagnosed T1DM
- Real-time CGM prioritization over isCGM for adolescents with suboptimal glycemic control
- Peer mentoring programs improve CGM use and reduce diabetes distress in underserved adolescent T1DM populations
- Professional CGM (pCGM) enhances clinical decision-making and glycemic outcomes in adolescents with poorly controlled T1DM

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** glucose monitoring strategy in adolescent T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Monitor capillary blood glucose prior to meals/snacks, at bedtime, postprandial occasionally, before exercise, when hypoglycemia is suspected, after treating hypoglycemia, and before critical tasks (e.g., driving)
- Check blood glucose intermittently (1-2 weeks) at 1-3 AM for nocturnal hypoglycemia and frequently during illness/travel
- Use SMBG values systematically for insulin dose adjustments, meal timing/content, and treatment decisions
- Review documented SMBG logs during each visit, correlating with HbA1c and insulin doses
- Educate patients/caregivers on interpreting SMBG patterns and making minor insulin adjustments
- Cross-check SMBG values with venous plasma glucose when in doubt, especially outside the 60-160 mg/dl range or in postprandial states
- Document remarks in SMBG logs explaining aberrant glucose values (e.g., hyperglycemia due to eating outside, less insulin dose, or hypoglycemia due to delayed meals, excessive exercise)

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- T1DM diagnosis in adolescent patients
- Access to glucometer and test strips (or urine glucose strips as last resort)
- Patient/caregiver training on SMBG technique and interpretation
- Psychosocial support and parental supervision for consistent SMBG adherence
- Financial resources for frequent testing (alternative strategies for cost-constrained settings)
- Patient/caregiver training on recognizing and responding to SMBG patterns, including minor insulin dose adjustments
- Assessment of psychosocial factors (e.g., stress, self-esteem) and parental support as predictors of SMBG adherence in adolescents


### insulin therapy optimization in T1DM

All children and adults with T1DM require insulin as soon as diagnosed and continuously thereafter.. Optimal insulin use depends on a diabetes team (physician, educator, nutritionist) educating and supporting patients/families.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy optimization in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- All children and adults with T1DM require insulin as soon as diagnosed and continuously thereafter.
- Optimal insulin use depends on a diabetes team (physician, educator, nutritionist) educating and supporting patients/families.
- Intensive glycemic control using MDII or CSII improves HbA1c and reduces microvascular complications.
- Insulin regimen selection considers motivation, education, and financial resources of the family.
- Patient cooperation and family support are key to achieving good glycemic control.
- Short-acting bovine insulin was historically the first insulin used in T1DM treatment (1922), marking the beginning of insulin therapy evolution.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Physiological insulin regimens (MDII or CSII) should be used as far as feasible in all age groups.
- Diabetes team support (physician, educator, nutritionist) is essential for education and insulin dose adjustment.
- Insulin regimen selection must consider family motivation, education, and financial resources.
- Target HbA1c < 7.5% in children and < 7% in adults should be strived for.
- Insulin analogs should be individualized to settings where distinct advantages exist (e.g., toddlers, school-going children, nocturnal hypoglycemia).
- Recombinant human insulin (available since 1982) is a foundational requirement for modern insulin therapy in T1DM.


### neuropathy management in T1DM

Neuropathy is a common microvascular complication in T1DM, contributing significantly to foot ulcers and lower extremity amputations.. Management focuses on achieving and maintaining good glycemic control (HbA1C <7%) to prevent and potentially reverse early-stage neuropathy.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** neuropathy management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Neuropathy is a common microvascular complication in T1DM, contributing significantly to foot ulcers and lower extremity amputations.
- Management focuses on achieving and maintaining good glycemic control (HbA1C <7%) to prevent and potentially reverse early-stage neuropathy.
- Risk factors such as obesity, hypertension, dyslipidemia, smoking, and alcohol use should be addressed as part of management.
- Diagnosis involves clinical tests like ankle jerk, 128 Hz tuning fork vibration test, and 10 g monofilament test for large fiber neuropathy.
- Cardiovascular autonomic function tests (e.g., heart rate variability, 30:15 ratio) are used to assess autonomic neuropathy.
- Neuropathy prevalence in T1DM shows a sharp increase in patients aged >16 years (59.1%) compared to those <16 years (9.5%), and in those with diabetes duration >10 years (35.0%) versus <10 years (9.4%).
- Small fiber neuropathy (81%) is more prevalent than large fiber neuropathy (59%) in long-term T1DM (mean duration 40 years).

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- HbA1C levels must be maintained below 7% to reduce onset and progression of diabetic neuropathy.
- Diagnostic tests (e.g., monofilament, tuning fork) must be performed to confirm neuropathy and assess severity.
- Cardiovascular autonomic function tests require specific conditions (e.g., resting supine position, respiratory rate of 6/min).
- Pharmacological agents like epalrestat or benfotiamine may be considered, but require monitoring for adverse effects (e.g., hepatic dysfunction).
- Intensive glycemic control is critical, as hyperglycemia is the primary driver of neuropathy progression.
- Cardiovascular autonomic function tests require specific conditions: resting supine position, respiratory rate of 6/min, and ECG monitoring for heart rate variability (HRV) interpretation.
- Diagnostic tests for large fiber neuropathy include ankle jerk, 128 Hz tuning fork vibration test, and 10 g monofilament test (abnormal if loss of protective sensation at one out of four sites).


### non-diabetic neuropathy differentiation in T1DM

Identify non-diabetic neuropathies in T1DM patients by excluding diabetic neuropathies when atypical features are present.. Use combination of typical symptoms and symmetrical distal sensory loss or signs to suggest diabetic sensorimotor polyneuropathy (DSPN) without additional evaluation.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** non-diabetic neuropathy differentiation in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Identify non-diabetic neuropathies in T1DM patients by excluding diabetic neuropathies when atypical features are present.
- Use combination of typical symptoms and symmetrical distal sensory loss or signs to suggest diabetic sensorimotor polyneuropathy (DSPN) without additional evaluation.
- Evaluate for non-diabetic neuropathies in patients with atypical features such as onset <5 years from diabetes diagnosis, pure motor neuropathy, asymmetrical neuropathy, rapid progression, or family history of non-diabetic neuropathy.
- Consider pressure palsies and chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (CIDP) as more common non-diabetic neuropathies in T1DM patients, given their higher prevalence in this population.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Exclude non-diabetic neuropathies before diagnosing diabetic nephropathy (DN) to enable specific treatment measures.
- Avoid extensive laboratory evaluation for non-diabetic neuropathies in all diabetic patients with neuropathy due to cost-effectiveness concerns.
- Diagnose diabetic nephropathy (DN) only after excluding non-diabetic neuropathies to enable targeted treatment interventions.


### gastrointestinal neuropathy diagnosis

Gastrointestinal neuropathy diagnosis and management. Gastrointestinal neuropathies are diagnosed after excluding reversible/iatrogenic causes (e.g., medications like metformin, GLP-1-based therapies, opioids) and organic causes (e.g., gastric outlet obstruction, peptic ulcer disease). gastrointestinal neuropathy diagnosis. Gastrointestinal autonomic neuropathy diagnosis. Assess for esophageal dysmotility, gastroparesis, enteropathy, colonic hypomotility, and fecal incontinence as major manifestations of gastrointestinal autonomic neuropathy.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Gastrointestinal neuropathy diagnosis and management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Gastrointestinal neuropathies are diagnosed after excluding reversible/iatrogenic causes (e.g., medications like metformin, GLP-1-based therapies, opioids) and organic causes (e.g., gastric outlet obstruction, peptic ulcer disease).
- Gastroparesis should be suspected in individuals with erratic glycemic control or unexplained upper gastrointestinal symptoms.
- Diagnostic gold standard for gastroparesis is scintigraphy of digestible solids at 15-min intervals for 4 hours post-meal intake.
- ¹³C octanoic acid breath test is an approved alternative to scintigraphy for gastric emptying measurement.
- Evaluation of bladder function is recommended for individuals with diabetes and recurrent urinary tract infections, pyelonephritis, incontinence, or palpable bladder.
- Genitourinary disturbances (e.g., erectile dysfunction, retrograde ejaculation in men; decreased sexual desire, pain during intercourse, decreased arousal, and inadequate lubrication in women) are recognized as potential manifestations of diabetic autonomic neuropathy.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Exclusion of reversible causes (e.g., medication-induced symptoms, organic obstruction) prior to diagnosis or specialized testing.
- Endoscopy and/or imaging to rule out organic causes of gastrointestinal symptoms.
- Scintigraphy or ¹³C octanoic acid breath test for confirmed gastroparesis diagnosis.
- Assessment of glycemic control and symptom patterns to identify potential gastroparesis.
- Evaluation of bladder function in patients with recurrent urinary tract infections or incontinence.
- Evaluation of bladder function is specifically recommended for individuals with diabetes who have recurrent urinary tract infections, pyelonephritis, incontinence, or a palpable bladder.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of fixed A1C targets for diagnosing or managing gastrointestinal neuropathies.
- Empiric treatment without prior exclusion of reversible/iatrogenic causes.
- Reliance solely on symptom reporting without objective testing (e.g., scintigraphy) for gastroparesis diagnosis.
- Assumption that glycemic control alone reverses established gastrointestinal neuropathy.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of ¹³C octanoic acid breath test as an approved alternative to scintigraphy for gastric emptying assessment.
- Focus on individualized treatment strategies for symptom relief in autonomic neuropathy (e.g., painful DPN, bladder dysfunction).
- Emphasis on modifiable risk factor management (e.g., obesity, lipids, blood pressure) to slow progression of diabetic neuropathies.
- Incorporation of bladder function evaluation in patients with recurrent urinary tract infections or incontinence.
- Recognition of genitourinary disturbances (e.g., erectile dysfunction, sexual dysfunction) as potential manifestations of diabetic autonomic neuropathy.
- Recognition of biliary dysfunction as a potential manifestation of gastrointestinal autonomic neuropathy.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Gastrointestinal autonomic neuropathy diagnosis

**Core Management Principles:**
- Assess for esophageal dysmotility, gastroparesis, enteropathy, colonic hypomotility, and fecal incontinence as major manifestations of gastrointestinal autonomic neuropathy.
- Differentiate acute diabetic gastroparesis (reversible with severe hyperglycemia) from chronic gastroparesis (due to autonomic neuropathy).
- Evaluate upper abdominal symptoms like postprandial fullness or glycemic fluctuations in chronic gastroparesis.
- Assess for small intestinal bacterial overgrowth (SIBO) in patients with suspected enteropathy or chronic diarrhea, as it is a common complication of diabetic enteropathy.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Presence of diabetes mellitus as the underlying cause.
- Symptoms consistent with gastrointestinal autonomic dysfunction (e.g., nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, or fecal incontinence).
- Exclusion of other causes of gastrointestinal symptoms (e.g., structural or medication-related).
- Documentation of persistent symptoms for at least 3 months despite glycemic control optimization before attributing gastrointestinal symptoms to autonomic neuropathy.


### foot disease risk stratification

Categorize feet into Low risk, At Risk, or Active Foot Disease based on clinical features. Perform annual foot examination for low-risk patients. Increase frequency of foot assessment to every 6 months or more for high-risk patients. Examine footwear and inspect skin, nails, and structural deformities

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** foot disease risk stratification

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Categorize feet into Low risk, At Risk, or Active Foot Disease based on clinical features
- Perform annual foot examination for low-risk patients
- Increase frequency of foot assessment to every 6 months or more for high-risk patients
- Examine footwear and inspect skin, nails, and structural deformities
- Record presence of toe deformities, bunions, high arch foot, or Charcot foot
- Record visual impairment status as part of basic foot screening

**Prerequisites:**
- Assess peripheral sensation using 10g monofilament
- Evaluate arterial supply via pedal pulses
- Review history of diabetes-related foot complications
- Include medical history in risk categorization
- Examine for foot deformities as part of basic screening
- Assess vibration sensation in addition to 10g monofilament for peripheral sensation evaluation


### exercise-induced hypoglycemia management

exercise-induced hypoglycemia management in children/adolescents with type 1 diabetes. Adjust insulin doses (prandial or basal) or carbohydrate intake before/after exercise to prevent hypoglycemia. Use CGM with frequent glucose monitoring before, during, and after exercise for safety

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** exercise-induced hypoglycemia management in children/adolescents with type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Adjust insulin doses (prandial or basal) or carbohydrate intake before/after exercise to prevent hypoglycemia
- Use CGM with frequent glucose monitoring before, during, and after exercise for safety
- Postpone intense activity if marked hyperglycemia (glucose ≥350 mg/dL) with ketones or β-hydroxybutyrate ≥0.6 mmol/L
- For pump users without AID, lower basal rates by ~10–50% or suspend for 1–2 hours during exercise
- Use AID systems with conservative glucose goals or increased targets to prevent hypoglycemia during exercise
- Postpone intense activity if moderate to large urine ketones are present (in addition to hyperglycemia ≥350 mg/dL and β-hydroxybutyrate >1.5 mmol/L)
- Consider additional carbohydrate intake during and/or after exercise based on duration and intensity of physical activity (e.g., 10–15 g for low- to moderate-intensity aerobic activities of 30–60 minutes if fasting)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be on insulin or insulin secretagogues
