- CGM availability for real-time glucose monitoring
- Individualized glucose targets based on insulin plan and exercise type/intensity/duration
- Assessment of β-hydroxybutyrate levels if hyperglycemia is present before exercise
- Healthy eating pattern and physical activity encouraged for children/adolescents with obesity
- Assessment of urine ketones in addition to β-hydroxybutyrate levels when marked hyperglycemia (≥350 mg/dL) is present before exercise
- Monitoring of weight status and cardiovascular risk factors in children and adolescents with type 1 diabetes and obesity

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed carbohydrate intake without adjustment for exercise intensity/duration
- Routine avoidance of exercise due to hypoglycemia risk without individualized strategies
- Non-adjustment of insulin doses for pre- or post-exercise periods
- Ignoring nocturnal hypoglycemia risk in prolonged or high-intensity exercise
- Using hyperglycemia thresholds without considering ketones or β-hydroxybutyrate levels

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of AID systems with device-specific conservative settings for exercise
- Post-exercise basal rate reduction (~20%) to mitigate delayed hypoglycemia
- Carbohydrate intake timing optimization for AID users to prevent rebound hypoglycemia
- Individualized glucose targets (126–180 mg/dL) before activity based on exercise parameters
- Monitoring weight status and promoting healthy eating/physical activity in pediatric T1D with obesity
- Device-specific conservative settings for AID systems to prevent hypoglycemia during exercise


### exercise benefits in T1DM management

exercise benefits and risks in T1DM management. Regular physical activity increases general well-being and helps prevent obesity and cardiovascular risk in T1DM patients.. Exercise may modestly improve glycaemic control and reduce microvascular complications in T1DM.. T1DM should not limit participation in sports or career pursuits in sports.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** exercise benefits and risks in T1DM management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Regular physical activity increases general well-being and helps prevent obesity and cardiovascular risk in T1DM patients.
- Exercise may modestly improve glycaemic control and reduce microvascular complications in T1DM.
- T1DM should not limit participation in sports or career pursuits in sports.
- Exercise facilitates glucose uptake, improves glucose clearance, and sustains insulin sensitivity.
- Exercise regulates counter-regulatory hormones and decreases hepatic glucose output.
- Prior exposure to aerobic exercise or hypoglycemia blunts glucose counter-regulatory responses (e.g., glucagon and catecholamines) in T1DM individuals.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Carbohydrate intake (e.g., 20g glucose) before, during, and after physical activity to avoid hypoglycemia if glucose < 90 mg/dl.
- 10g glucose ingestion before aerobic exercise if starting glycemia is 90–124 mg/dl.
- Aerobic exercise can be started without glucose ingestion if starting glycemia is 126–180 mg/dl.
- Consistency in timing and dose of insulin, carbohydrate intake, duration, and intensity of exercise to minimize glucose variability.
- Women without medical or obstetric contraindications should be encouraged to do at least 30 min/day of physical activity.
- Adjustments in insulin or diet are easier once the individual adapts to a predictable and regular exercise schedule.


### insulin dosing for enteral/parenteral nutrition

Insulin orders must cover basal, prandial, and correctional needs for individuals on enteral/parenteral nutrition.. Basal insulin must continue for type 1 diabetes even if feedings are discontinued. insulin dosing for enteral/parenteral nutrition. insulin dosing for enteral/parenteral nutrition. Intravenous insulin is the preferred approach for patients with T1DM receiving enteral or parenteral feedings. Subcutaneous basal-bolus insulin is an alternative for those receiving intermittent feeds similar to major meals and minor snacks

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin dosing for enteral/parenteral nutrition

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin orders must cover basal, prandial, and correctional needs for individuals on enteral/parenteral nutrition.
- Basal insulin must continue for type 1 diabetes even if feedings are discontinued.
- Insulin dose for nutritional component calculated as 1 unit per 10–15 g carbohydrate in enteral/parenteral formulations.
- Rapid-acting insulin every 4h, regular insulin every 6h, or NPH insulin every 8–12h are options for nutritional coverage during continuous tube feeds.
- For bolus feedings, administer ~1 unit rapid-acting insulin per 10–15g carbohydrate subcutaneously before each feeding.
- For continuous peripheral or central parenteral nutrition, human regular insulin may be added to the total parenteral nutrition solution at a starting dose of 1 unit per 10 g dextrose, adjusted daily based on glycemic response.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must be receiving enteral or parenteral nutrition requiring insulin.
- Commercially available enteral nutrition preparations contain variable carbohydrates and infusion rates.
- Continuous enteral/parenteral nutrition results in a continuous postprandial state.
- Correctional insulin should be administered subcutaneously every 6h (regular) or every 4h (rapid-acting).
- If enteral nutrition is interrupted, intravenous dextrose may be given for the duration of active insulin time.
- Automated self-adjusting insulin algorithms require continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) or frequent point-of-care glucose checks to ensure safety and efficacy in hospitalized individuals on enteral/parenteral nutrition.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Covering all insulin needs using basal insulin only (risk of hypoglycemia).
- Using only basal insulin for type 1 diabetes without prandial/correctional coverage.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Automated self-adjusting insulin algorithms are safe and effective for hyperglycemia control in hospitalized individuals on enteral/parenteral nutrition.
- Adding human regular insulin to parenteral nutrition solution (1 unit per 10g dextrose) if >20 units correctional insulin required in 24h.
- Using NPH insulin for nocturnal tube feeding coverage.
- Subcutaneous insulin glargine achieves equivalent glycemic management to adding insulin to parenteral nutrition bags.
- Target blood glucose <140 mg/dL (7.8 mmol/L) increases hypoglycemia risk in continuous enteral/parenteral nutrition.
- Adding human regular insulin to parenteral nutrition solution is preferred over subcutaneous correctional insulin when >20 units of correctional insulin are required in 24 hours to reduce injection burden and hypoglycemia risk if nutrition is interrupted.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin dosing for enteral/parenteral nutrition

**Core Management Principles:**
- Intravenous insulin is the preferred approach for patients with T1DM receiving enteral or parenteral feedings
- Subcutaneous basal-bolus insulin is an alternative for those receiving intermittent feeds similar to major meals and minor snacks
- Initial doses transitioning from intravenous to subcutaneous insulin should use long/intermediate-acting insulin comprising 50% of total daily dose (TDD) with rapid-acting analogs and 25% of TDD with regular insulin
- Correctional doses should be given based on glucose levels and urgency of correction
- Preliminary dose escalation assumes 1 unit of regular human insulin or rapid-acting insulin subcutaneously per 10-15 g carbohydrate before each meal
- For patients on continuous enteral nutrition, intravenous insulin should be titrated to maintain glucose levels between 140-180 mg/dL (7.8-10.0 mmol/L) unless otherwise specified by individual patient goals
- Subcutaneous basal insulin should be initiated at least 2-4 hours before discontinuing intravenous insulin to ensure overlap and prevent rebound hyperglycemia

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patients must be receiving enteral or parenteral nutrition
- For subcutaneous basal-bolus use, feeds must be intermittent and similar to major meals and minor snacks
- Transition from intravenous to subcutaneous insulin requires prior intravenous insulin use
- Dose calculations must account for carbohydrate content of meals
- Correctional dosing requires monitoring of glucose levels and clinical urgency
- Intravenous insulin transition to subcutaneous requires stable enteral/parenteral nutrition with consistent carbohydrate delivery and no planned changes in feeding rate within 24 hours
- Correctional dosing should prioritize rapid-acting insulin analogs over regular insulin for patients with T1DM due to faster onset and shorter duration of action


### hypoglycemia counterregulation failure in T1DM

Loss of first-line defense: insulin secretion fails to decline as plasma glucose falls below 80-85 mg/dl. Loss of second-line defense: glucagon secretion fails to increase despite falling glucose (60-65 mg/dl threshold)

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hypoglycemia counterregulation failure in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Loss of first-line defense: insulin secretion fails to decline as plasma glucose falls below 80-85 mg/dl
- Loss of second-line defense: glucagon secretion fails to increase despite falling glucose (60-65 mg/dl threshold)
- Attenuated epinephrine response (third-line defense) in long-standing T1DM or recurrent hypoglycemia
- Blunted neurogenic symptoms (sympathoadrenal discharge) leading to hypoglycemia unawareness
- Neuroglycopenic symptoms (<50 mg/dl) manifest as cognitive impairment, seizures, or coma requiring external assistance
- Unpredictable activity patterns and fussy eating behavior in children increase hypoglycemia risk
- Permanent neurological damage may occur in prolonged neuroglycopenic hypoglycemia despite neurological recovery post-correction

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Monitor plasma glucose at ≤70 mg/dl to trigger hypoglycemia concern
- Avoid aggressive glycemic therapy to prevent recurrent hypoglycemia
- Scrupulously avoid hypoglycemia for 2-3 weeks to reverse hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF)
- Educate patients (especially children) on recognizing non-specific neurogenic symptoms (e.g., hunger, sweating, tremor)
- Adjust insulin dosing to prevent iatrogenic hyperinsulinemia
- Educate young children (especially) on hypoglycemia symptoms due to their inability to communicate symptoms or initiate behavioral eating responses


### atypical type 1 diabetes variants

Atypical type 1 diabetes variants (autoantibody-negative insulinopenic diabetes). Individuals have permanent insulinopenia and are prone to DKA but lack evidence of β-cell autoimmunity.. Only a minority of people with type 1 diabetes fall into this autoantibody-negative category.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Atypical type 1 diabetes variants (autoantibody-negative insulinopenic diabetes)

**Core Recommendations:**
- Individuals have permanent insulinopenia and are prone to DKA but lack evidence of β-cell autoimmunity.
- Only a minority of people with type 1 diabetes fall into this autoantibody-negative category.
- DKA can occur spontaneously in these variants despite absence of autoimmune markers.
- DKA can occur in atypical type 1 diabetes variants despite the absence of precipitating factors (e.g., illness, medications) that typically trigger DKA in type 2 diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Documentation of permanent insulinopenia (absolute insulin deficiency).
- Exclusion of β-cell autoimmunity via islet autoantibody testing.
- Absence of type 2 diabetes features (e.g., insulin resistance, obesity).
- Documentation of absolute insulinopenia via fasting C-peptide levels or insulin levels in atypical type 1 diabetes variants to confirm insulin deficiency

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on clinical phenotype (e.g., age, BMI) to classify diabetes without autoantibody testing.
- Assuming all insulinopenic diabetes cases are autoimmune type 1 diabetes.
- Assuming DKA in adults is exclusively associated with type 2 diabetes without further evaluation for atypical type 1 diabetes variants

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consider islet autoantibody testing in adults without traditional type 2 diabetes risk factors or of younger age to exclude type 1 diabetes.
- Recognition of atypical type 1 diabetes variants as a distinct subgroup within type 1 diabetes.
- Consider islet autoantibody testing in adults without traditional type 2 diabetes risk factors (e.g., obesity, older age) or of younger age to exclude type 1 diabetes, including atypical variants


### cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy management

cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy (CAN) management in T1DM. CAN increases cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk, with early stages often asymptomatic (reduced heart rate variability during deep breathing may be the only abnormality).

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy (CAN) management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- CAN increases cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk, with early stages often asymptomatic (reduced heart rate variability during deep breathing may be the only abnormality).
- Advanced CAN may present with resting tachycardia, orthostatic hypotension, exercise intolerance, and loss of nocturnal blood pressure dip.
- CAN is associated with increased mortality, sudden death (malignant arrhythmias), silent myocardial ischemia, and delayed CVD presentation.
- Intensification of blood glucose and blood pressure control in CAN patients may paradoxically increase cardiovascular event risk.
- Smoking cessation is strongly recommended to reduce vascular complications and CVD risk in T1DM.
- CAN may be associated with impaired coronary flow reserve in T1DM patients without CAD, predicting future diastolic dysfunction
- Diabetic dyslipidemia in T1DM is characterized by elevated triglycerides, low HDL-cholesterol, and predominance of small dense LDL particles

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Routine screening for CAN in T1DM patients, particularly those with poor glycemic control, hypertension, dyslipidemia, or obesity.
- Assessment of lipid profile (total cholesterol, LDL-C, HDL-C, triglycerides) in T1DM patients with poor glycemic control or obesity.
- Evaluation for silent myocardial ischemia in T1DM patients with CAN due to delayed presentation risk.
- Monitoring for orthostatic hypotension and resting tachycardia in advanced CAN cases.
- Lifestyle modification (diet, physical activity) to address obesity and insulin resistance in T1DM.
- Assessment of lipid profile (total cholesterol, LDL-C, HDL-C, triglycerides) in T1DM patients with poor glycemic control (HbA1c ≥9.5%) or obesity


### physical activity contraindications

Vigorous exercise contraindications in diabetic retinopathy. Vigorous-intensity aerobic or resistance exercise may be contraindicated in patients with proliferative diabetic retinopathy or severe nonproliferative diabetic retinopathy due to risk of vitreous hemorrhage or retinal detachment. physical activity contraindications. Physical activity contraindications in pregnancy with type 1 diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Vigorous exercise contraindications in diabetic retinopathy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Vigorous-intensity aerobic or resistance exercise may be contraindicated in patients with proliferative diabetic retinopathy or severe nonproliferative diabetic retinopathy due to risk of vitreous hemorrhage or retinal detachment.
- High-intensity exercise may also be contraindicated in patients with active diabetic macular edema due to risk of further retinal damage or hemorrhage.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Presence of proliferative diabetic retinopathy or severe nonproliferative diabetic retinopathy must be confirmed before applying contraindication.
- Retinal imaging (e.g., fluorescein angiography or optical coherence tomography) must confirm the absence of active neovascularization or macular edema before allowing moderate-intensity exercise in patients with diabetic retinopathy.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consultation with an ophthalmologist prior to initiating an intense exercise plan is recommended for patients with high-risk diabetic retinopathy.
- For patients with stable diabetic retinopathy without high-risk features, moderate-intensity exercise is generally safe and should be encouraged as part of diabetes management.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Physical activity contraindications in pregnancy with type 1 diabetes

**Core Management Principles:**
- Identify absolute contraindications to physical activity during pregnancy including hemodynamically significant heart disease, restrictive lung disease, incompetent cervix, multiple gestation at risk for premature labor, persistent second or third trimester bleeding, placenta previa after 26 weeks, premature labor, ruptured membranes, and pre-eclampsia.
- Assess relative contraindications such as severe anemia, unevaluated maternal cardiac arrhythmias, chronic bronchitis, uncontrolled diabetes, extreme morbid obesity, extreme underweight (BMI <12 kg/m²), history of extremely sedentary lifestyle, poorly controlled hypertension, hyperthyroidism, seizures, orthopedic limitations, and heavy smoking.
- Implement hypoglycemia prevention measures in high-risk patients with type 1 diabetes during pregnancy, including frequent blood glucose monitoring between 02:00 and 04:00 am, avoiding pre-bedtime plasma glucose below 108 mg/dl, and prescribing glucagon for home use by a partner.
- Screen for intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR) in the current pregnancy as a relative obstetric contraindication to physical activity

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Pregnancy with type 1 diabetes must be evaluated for absolute and relative contraindications to physical activity before recommending any exercise regimen.
- High-risk patients for hypoglycemia should be identified early, particularly those with impaired hypoglycemia awareness or a history of severe hypoglycemia in the year preceding pregnancy.
- Reduce insulin dose by approximately 10% at 8-16 weeks gestation as a hypoglycemia prevention measure in high-risk patients


### perioperative insulin management in T1DM

Give basal insulin as usual for minor surgeries (<2 hours) with resumption of regular diet within 2-4 hours.. Administer subcutaneous rapid-acting insulin analog for blood glucose (BG) ≥ 180 mg/dl or 50% of regular insulin before surgery if meals are allowed.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** perioperative insulin management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Give basal insulin as usual for minor surgeries (<2 hours) with resumption of regular diet within 2-4 hours.
- Administer subcutaneous rapid-acting insulin analog for blood glucose (BG) ≥ 180 mg/dl or 50% of regular insulin before surgery if meals are allowed.
- Prefer rapid-acting insulin analogs in daycare surgeries due to faster onset (5-15 min) and peak (1-1.5 hours).
- Aim for capillary BG between 108-180 mg/dl (acceptable range 72-216 mg/dl) before surgery.
- Prefer intravenous insulin for surgeries lasting >2 hours, with transition to subcutaneous insulin only after stable BG and oral intake for 4-6 hours.
- Check blood gases if ketone or BG levels are high before surgery.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Check BG, blood beta-hydroxybutyrate (if available), urinary ketones, and serum electrolytes before surgery.
- Delay surgery until correction of diabetic ketoacidosis, circulating volume, and electrolyte deficits.
- Ensure surgery is performed at centers with appropriate expertise and facilities for T1DM management.
- Evaluate patients planned for elective surgery well in advance.
- Monitor BG, ketones, and electrolytes before emergency surgery.
- Ensure oral intake has improved or patients are receiving stable enteral/parenteral feeds before transitioning from intravenous to subcutaneous insulin.


### CVD risk stratification in T1DM

CVD prevalence in T1DM increases with age (6% in 15-29 years to 25% in 45-59 years) and diabetes duration (6-9% in 1-7 years to 13-15% in >15 years).. T1DM attenuates the protective effect of female sex on CVD risk, with higher relative risk in females compared to males.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** CVD risk stratification in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- CVD prevalence in T1DM increases with age (6% in 15-29 years to 25% in 45-59 years) and diabetes duration (6-9% in 1-7 years to 13-15% in >15 years).
- T1DM attenuates the protective effect of female sex on CVD risk, with higher relative risk in females compared to males.
- Patients with T1DM experience CV events 10-15 years earlier than the general population.
- PAD is underreported in T1DM due to asymptomatic course, neuropathy masking symptoms, and lack of universal screening.
- Presence of PAD in T1DM is a strong predictor of atherosclerotic burden and lower-extremity amputations.
- In the Eurodiab IDDM Complications Study, CVD prevalence in T1DM was slightly higher in females (10%) than males (9%) despite the general population's protective effect of female sex.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Screening for silent myocardial ischemia and inducible ischemia should be actively considered in T1DM patients.
- Regular assessment of CVD risk factors (e.g., age, diabetes duration, sex) is mandatory for risk stratification.
- Monitoring for PAD should include clinical examination and targeted investigations despite asymptomatic presentation.
- Longitudinal follow-up of T1DM patients for CV events and mortality is essential due to elevated risk.
- Diabetic kidney disease progression should be evaluated as a predictor of worse post-stroke outcomes.
- Incorporate standardized mortality ratio (SMR) assessment for total and CV mortality in T1DM risk stratification, with higher priority in females (SMR 13.0 vs. 5.0 for total mortality; 24.7 vs. 8.8 for CV mortality).


### high-intensity interval training protocol

High-intensity interval training (HIIT) protocol for diabetes care. Perform short bursts of aerobic training at 65-90% VO₂peak or 75-95% heart rate peak for 10s to 4 minutes with 12s to 5 minutes of active or passive recovery. Monitor blood glucose during and after HIIT due to risk of transient hyperglycemia (type 1) or delayed hypoglycemia (type 2)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** High-intensity interval training (HIIT) protocol for diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Perform short bursts of aerobic training at 65-90% VO₂peak or 75-95% heart rate peak for 10s to 4 minutes with 12s to 5 minutes of active or passive recovery
- Monitor blood glucose during and after HIIT due to risk of transient hyperglycemia (type 1) or delayed hypoglycemia (type 2)
- Use bolus correction in type 1 diabetes to mitigate postexercise hyperglycemia
- Incorporate HIIT within a broader activity plan with adequate recovery to prevent injury
- Individualize HIIT participation based on patient willingness and ability to engage in high-intensity activity

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals with type 1 or type 2 diabetes without contraindications to high-intensity exercise
- Baseline assessment of VO₂peak or heart rate peak to determine appropriate intensity zones
- Glucose monitoring capability during and after exercise
- Supervision or structured program for initial HIIT sessions to ensure safety and proper technique
- Gradual progression of HIIT intensity and duration based on patient tolerance and response

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Continuous moderate-intensity exercise as the sole recommended modality for glycemic control in diabetes
- Unsupervised high-intensity exercise without glucose monitoring in type 1 diabetes
- Fixed HIIT protocols without consideration for individual fitness levels or recovery needs

**Updated Recommendations:**
- HIIT as a time-efficient modality for significant physiologic and metabolic adaptations in diabetes
- Superior improvements in glycemia and body composition with HIIT-style walking compared to continuous walking in type 2 diabetes
- Need for careful glucose monitoring and potential bolus correction in type 1 diabetes during HIIT
- Integration of HIIT within broader activity plans with attention to recovery to prevent injury
- Evidence of reduced A1C and insulin requirements with weeks of HIIT in type 1 diabetes
- HIIT may improve β-cell function and cardiac function in addition to glycemia and body composition
- HIIT cycling may not improve cardiovascular response or visceral adiposity compared to continuous cycling despite similar A1C improvements


### iatrogenic hypoglycemia pathogenesis in T1DM

pathogenesis of iatrogenic hypoglycemia in T1DM. Hypoglycemia associated autonomic failure (HAAF) develops as a consequence of recurrent hypoglycemic episodes.. HAAF leads to the loss of autonomic warning symptoms such as palpitations, tremors, and sweating.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pathogenesis of iatrogenic hypoglycemia in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Hypoglycemia associated autonomic failure (HAAF) develops as a consequence of recurrent hypoglycemic episodes.
- HAAF leads to the loss of autonomic warning symptoms such as palpitations, tremors, and sweating.
- The absence of warning symptoms increases the risk of severe hypoglycemia and hypoglycemia unawareness.
- Pathogenesis involves the failure of counter-regulatory hormone responses (glucagon and epinephrine) during low glucose states.
- Clinical progression moves from frequent mild hypoglycemia to severe neuroglycopenic events due to autonomic exhaustion.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Implementation of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) to detect trends before critical lows.
- Strict avoidance of hypoglycemia through individualized insulin dosing and carbohydrate intake.
- Patient education on recognizing early warning signs before HAAF sets in.
- Regular assessment of hypoglycemia awareness status during clinical visits.
- Adjustment of insulin regimens to prevent nocturnal and fasting hypoglycemia.


### sleep duration guidelines

Sleep duration and quality management in diabetes. Aim for consistent, uninterrupted sleep, even on weekends.. Avoid long (>8 h) and short (<6 h) sleep durations as they negatively impact A1C.. Address irregular sleep patterns influenced by insomnia, obstructive sleep apnea, and restless leg syndrome.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Sleep duration and quality management in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Aim for consistent, uninterrupted sleep, even on weekends.
- Avoid long (>8 h) and short (<6 h) sleep durations as they negatively impact A1C.
- Address irregular sleep patterns influenced by insomnia, obstructive sleep apnea, and restless leg syndrome.
- Consider chronotype differences, noting evening chronotypes may face higher risks of inactivity and poorer glycemic levels.
- Prioritize early bedtimes and wake times for evening chronotypes to mitigate glycemic risks.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient has type 2 diabetes.
- Patient exhibits irregular sleep patterns or specific sleep disorders (insomnia, OSA, RLS).
- Patient is an evening chronotype (night owl) requiring specific behavioral adjustments.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consistent sleep schedule enforcement across weekends.
- Specific monitoring of sleep duration thresholds (>8h and <6h) for A1C impact.
- Chronotype-aware glycemic management strategies.


### type 1 diabetes prediction monitoring

Monitor individuals with islet autoantibody-positive pre-stage 3 type 1 diabetes using consensus guidance.. Utilize OGTT metrics as they surpass continuous glucose monitoring data for predicting progression in multiple-autoantibody-positive individuals.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Type 1 Diabetes Prediction Monitoring

**Core Recommendations:**
- Monitor individuals with islet autoantibody-positive pre-stage 3 type 1 diabetes using consensus guidance.
- Utilize OGTT metrics as they surpass continuous glucose monitoring data for predicting progression in multiple-autoantibody-positive individuals.
- Implement in-home blood spot sampling for islet autoantibody screening throughout the general population.
- Consider ethical, equity, and health system perspectives when planning type 1 diabetes screening programs.
- Evaluate dietary patterns and sugar intake as factors associated with progression from islet autoimmunity to type 1 diabetes.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be islet autoantibody-positive to be considered for pre-stage 3 type 1 diabetes monitoring.
- Screening should target children and young adults, particularly those with family history or in high-risk populations.
- Clinical assessment must include evaluation of multiple islet autoantibodies to assess risk of progression.
- Health systems must have capacity for ethical and equitable implementation of population-wide screening.
- Monitoring protocols should account for patient-specific factors including age and family history.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Relying solely on continuous glucose monitoring data for T1D prediction in multiple-autoantibody-positive individuals.
- Using lifestyle intervention or metformin for the prevention of type 1 diabetes.
- Screening the general adult population for type 1 diabetes without specific risk stratification.
- Using vitamin D supplementation for the prevention of type 1 diabetes progression.
- Relying on statin therapy for the prevention of type 1 diabetes.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consensus guidance for monitoring individuals with islet autoantibody-positive pre-stage 3 type 1 diabetes.
- In-home blood spot sampling for islet autoantibody screening in the general population.
- Use of OGTT metrics for improved prediction of T1D progression in autoantibody-positive individuals.
- Consideration of ethical and equity frameworks in T1D screening program design.
- Integration of dietary and sugar intake monitoring in T1D risk assessment protocols.


### type 1 diabetes diagnostic flowchart

Type 1 Diabetes Diagnostic Flowchart & Staging. GAD65 is the primary antibody to measure, followed by IA-2 and/or ZnT8 if negative.. A negative antibody result does not exclude type 1 diabetes in individuals <35 years old.. C-peptide testing is indicated only in insulin-treated individuals and should be measured prior to insulin discontinuation.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Type 1 Diabetes Diagnostic Flowchart & Staging

**Core Recommendations:**
- GAD65 is the primary antibody to measure, followed by IA-2 and/or ZnT8 if negative.
- A negative antibody result does not exclude type 1 diabetes in individuals <35 years old.
- C-peptide testing is indicated only in insulin-treated individuals and should be measured prior to insulin discontinuation.
- Monogenic diabetes is suggested by A1C <58 mmol/mol, family history, specific features, or prediction model probability >5%.
- Type 1 diabetes staging (Stage 1-3) is based on autoimmunity presence and glycemic status (normoglycemia to overt hyperglycemia).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be newly diagnosed or suspected of having type 1 diabetes.
- C-peptide testing requires the patient to be receiving insulin treatment.
- C-peptide testing should not be performed within 2 weeks of a hyperglycemic emergency.
- Classification of monogenic diabetes requires genetic testing confirmation or specific clinical features.
- Autoantibody screening is recommended for those with family history or elevated genetic risk.
- C-peptide testing should be performed within 5 hours of eating if using a random sample to replace formal stimulation tests.
- C-peptide testing is indicated for individuals not yet treated with insulin only if they require careful monitoring for rapid insulin initiation.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Relying on nonvalidated autoantibody tests for diagnosing latent autoimmune diabetes in adults (LADA).
- Using a single clinical feature to confirm type 1 diabetes in isolation.
- Testing C-peptide within 2 weeks of a hyperglycemic emergency.
- Using the term LADA as a distinct disease entity separate from type 1 diabetes (now included under type 1).
- Repeating C-peptide tests for very low levels (<80 pmol/L) without clinical indication.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of diabetes prediction models (e.g., diabetesgenes.org/exeter-diabetes-app) to suggest monogenic diabetes.
- Incorporation of teplizumab as an approved treatment for high-risk individuals with multiple autoantibodies.
- Recognition that a diagnosis of type 1 diabetes does not preclude having features of type 2 diabetes (e.g., obesity, insulin resistance).
- Standardized islet autoantibody tests recommended for adults with overlapping phenotypic risk factors.
- Use of random C-peptide samples (with concurrent glucose) to replace formal stimulation tests in classification.
- Use of teplizumab as an approved treatment for high-risk individuals with multiple autoantibodies.


### exercise management in pediatric T1DM

exercise management and hypoglycemia treatment in pediatric T1DM. Exercise must always be encouraged for the child and family members to improve diabetes control and quality of life.. Blood glucose (BG) must be checked frequently before, after, and during prolonged sports or unexpected activity.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** exercise management and hypoglycemia treatment in pediatric T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Exercise must always be encouraged for the child and family members to improve diabetes control and quality of life.
- Blood glucose (BG) must be checked frequently before, after, and during prolonged sports or unexpected activity.
- Insulin doses should be decreased in anticipation of planned physical activity.
- For prolonged sports, consume slowly absorbed snacks like nuts, seeds, peanut chikki, coconut barfi, or chocolate before starting.
- If hypoglycemia occurs during activity, administer simple sugars to correct it immediately.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Confirm hypoglycemia with a glucometer whenever possible before initiating treatment.
- Ensure the patient is conscious and able to take oral glucose for standard treatment.
- For unconscious patients or those having fits, place in recovery position and do not force food/drink into the mouth.
- Administer Inj Glucagon (0.5 mg for children <12 years; 1 mg or 10-30 µg/kg for children >12 years) if unconscious.
- If glucagon is unavailable, shift to a nearby medical facility to administer IV glucose.

Individuals with T1DM must proactively plan for multiple aspects of travel including work, leisure, and family functions.. Patients should carry vital information and supplies to manage difficulties encountered while traveling.. Travel planning must address specific challenges faced by T1DM patients in various environments.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** travel management for T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Individuals with T1DM must proactively plan for multiple aspects of travel including work, leisure, and family functions.
- Patients should carry vital information and supplies to manage difficulties encountered while traveling.
- Travel planning must address specific challenges faced by T1DM patients in various environments.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Access to essential diabetes management information tailored for travelers.
- Availability of necessary supplies to handle potential difficulties during travel.


### genitourinary autonomic neuropathy in T1DM

Urinary incontinence and diabetic cystopathy (neurogenic bladder) manifesting as nocturia, frequent urination, urgency, and weak urinary stream.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** genitourinary autonomic neuropathy in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Urinary incontinence and diabetic cystopathy (neurogenic bladder) manifesting as nocturia, frequent urination, urgency, and weak urinary stream.
- Genital dysfunction in men includes erectile dysfunction and retrograde ejaculation; in women, decreased sexual desire, pain during intercourse, decreased arousal, and inadequate lubrication.
- Sudomotor dysfunction may present as hypohidrosis/anhidrosis of the lower body (increasing foot ulcer risk) or hyperhidrosis of the upper body, including gustatory sweating.
- Autonomic neuropathy contributes to Charcot neuroarthropathy via increased peripheral blood flow and bone resorption.
- Diabetic ophthalmoplegia (cranial nerve III, VI, VII involvement) presents with ptosis, diplopia, and orbital pain, with pupillary sparing in most cases.
- Carpal tunnel syndrome manifesting as median nerve compression symptoms due to pressure damage under the flexor retinaculum.
- Abdominal pain in dermatomal distribution with asymmetric abdominal bulge due to muscle weakness.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Exclusion of surgical causes (e.g., aneurysm, neoplasms) in cases of diabetic ophthalmoplegia with pupillary involvement.
- Differential diagnosis of genitourinary autonomic symptoms to rule out conditions like herpes zoster or spinal nerve root compression.
- Evaluation of proximal lower limb weakness and pain to distinguish diabetic amyotrophy from CIDP, MGUS, or inflammatory vasculitis.
- Exclusion of surgical causes (aneurysm, neoplasms) specifically for diabetic ophthalmoplegia cases with pupillary involvement.
- Differential diagnosis of abdominal pain to rule out herpes zoster and spinal nerve root compression.
- Differential diagnosis of diabetic amyotrophy to distinguish from CIDP, MGUS, and inflammatory vasculitis.


### substance use and mortality risk in T1DM

Adolescents with T1DM may engage in experimental high-risk behaviors due to chronic illness stress.. Smoking worsens glycemic control, increases cardiovascular risk, and accelerates microvascular complications in T1DM.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** substance use and mortality risk in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Adolescents with T1DM may engage in experimental high-risk behaviors due to chronic illness stress.
- Smoking worsens glycemic control, increases cardiovascular risk, and accelerates microvascular complications in T1DM.
- Alcohol consumption impairs gluconeogenesis, increases risk of DKA and hypoglycemia, and reduces diabetes self-care behaviors.
- Street drug use is associated with poor glycemic control, acute events, and higher mortality in T1DM.
- Physicians should actively counsel patients against smoking initiation and provide cessation strategies.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Smoking cessation counseling should be a routine component of diabetes care.
- Patients should be educated on 'responsible drinking' including carbohydrate intake and frequent glucose monitoring.
- Peers should be informed about diabetes diagnosis and hypoglycemia risk management.
- Physicians should frequently assess substance use tacitly and refer for psychotherapy as needed.
- ADA recommends against the use of e-cigarettes for smoking cessation.
- Patients should never drink alcohol alone.
- Peers must be informed about the patient's diabetes diagnosis and the specific risks of hypoglycemia and DKA.


### genetic and modifiable risk factors for T1DM nephropathy

Genetic factors (e.g., FRMD3, CARD, AFF3 genes, chromosome 15q26 intergenic SNP, ERBB4 intronic SNP) contribute to susceptibility or protection from nephropathy in T1DM.. Hyperglycemia is a critical modifiable risk factor for both initiation and progression of diabetic nephropathy.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** genetic and modifiable risk factors for T1DM nephropathy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Genetic factors (e.g., FRMD3, CARD, AFF3 genes, chromosome 15q26 intergenic SNP, ERBB4 intronic SNP) contribute to susceptibility or protection from nephropathy in T1DM.
- Hyperglycemia is a critical modifiable risk factor for both initiation and progression of diabetic nephropathy.
- Hypertension, dyslipidemia, and proteinuria are key modifiable risk factors for progression of nephropathy.
- Poor glycemic control early in T1DM has a 'legacy effect' or 'metabolic memory,' increasing long-term risk of complications even after later glycemic improvement.
- Higher BMI, smoking, and oral contraceptive pill use are modifiable risk factors for progression of diabetic nephropathy.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Early and sustained glycemic control is essential to prevent initiation and progression of nephropathy.
- Regular monitoring of blood pressure, lipid levels, and proteinuria is required for risk assessment and management.
- Genetic screening for susceptibility loci may be considered in high-risk families with clustering of T1DM and nephropathy.


### neuropathy grading criteria

Grade 2a: Abnormal nerve conduction (NC) plus symptoms of diabetic sensorimotor polyneuropathy (DSPN) with or without signs. Grade 2b: Abnormal NC plus moderate weakness (50%) of ankle dorsiflexion with or without DSPN symptoms

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** neuropathy grading criteria

**Core Management Principles:**
- Grade 2a: Abnormal nerve conduction (NC) plus symptoms of diabetic sensorimotor polyneuropathy (DSPN) with or without signs
- Grade 2b: Abnormal NC plus moderate weakness (50%) of ankle dorsiflexion with or without DSPN symptoms


### nocturnal hypoglycemia in adolescents

nocturnal hypoglycemia in adolescents with T1DM. Nocturnal hypoglycemia is a significant concern in adolescents with T1DM due to the longest inter-prandial interval and reduced sympathoadrenal responses during sleep.. Adolescents are less likely to be awakened by hypoglycemia compared to non-diabetic individuals, increasing the risk of undetected events.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** nocturnal hypoglycemia in adolescents with T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Nocturnal hypoglycemia is a significant concern in adolescents with T1DM due to the longest inter-prandial interval and reduced sympathoadrenal responses during sleep.
- Adolescents are less likely to be awakened by hypoglycemia compared to non-diabetic individuals, increasing the risk of undetected events.
- Fear of undetected nocturnal hypoglycemia leading to severe outcomes (e.g., brain damage or death) is a major psychological burden.
- Bedtime snacks are a traditional approach to prevent nocturnal hypoglycemia.
- The Somogyi phenomenon (midnight hypoglycemia causing high morning fasting blood glucose) should be considered before increasing nighttime insulin doses.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Use of insulin analogs in multiple daily injection (MDI) regimens or continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) regimens to reduce nocturnal hypoglycemia risk.
- Consider bedtime administration of slowly digested complex carbohydrates (e.g., uncooked cornstarch) for sustained glucose delivery.
- Evaluate the possibility of the Somogyi phenomenon before adjusting nighttime intermediate or long-acting insulin doses.


### pediatric diabetes phenotype differentiation

Pediatric diabetes phenotype differentiation and type 2 diabetes management. Distinguish between type 1 and type 2 diabetes in children despite overlapping features such as obesity, autoantibodies, and ketosis.. Treat both insulin resistance and absolute insulinopenia phenotypes appropriately, acknowledging potential overlap.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetes phenotype differentiation and type 2 diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Distinguish between type 1 and type 2 diabetes in children despite overlapping features such as obesity, autoantibodies, and ketosis.
- Treat both insulin resistance and absolute insulinopenia phenotypes appropriately, acknowledging potential overlap.
- Provide comprehensive lifestyle programs integrated with diabetes management for children and adolescents with overweight/obesity and type 2 diabetes.
- Assess glycemic status at least every 3 months or as clinically indicated in type 2 diabetes.
- Set individualized A1C goals (<6.5% for low hypoglycemia risk; higher for those at risk) and monitor blood glucose or CGM as needed.
- Initiate subcutaneous or intravenous insulin treatment in individuals with ketoacidosis to rapidly correct hyperglycemia and metabolic derangement.
- Consider assessment and treatment for hyperglycemic hyperosmolar state in individuals presenting with severe hyperglycemia (blood glucose ≥600 mg/dL).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Overweight or obesity with type 2 diabetes diagnosis for lifestyle intervention eligibility.
- A1C <8.5% and asymptomatic status for metformin monotherapy initiation.
- Marked hyperglycemia (A1C ≥8.5% or blood glucose ≥600 mg/dL) requires initial insulin therapy.
- Presence of ketoacidosis necessitates rapid insulin treatment until acidosis resolves.
- Class 2 obesity or higher (BMI 35 to <40 kg/m² or 120% to <140% percentile) with comorbidities for metabolic surgery consideration.
- Resolution of ketosis or ketoacidosis before initiating metformin therapy.
- Random blood glucose concentrations ≥250 mg/dL and/or A1C ≥8.5% as criteria for initial insulin treatment when distinction between types is unclear.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized risk assessment.
- Reliance solely on metformin for durable glycemic control in all pediatric type 2 diabetes cases.
- Use of linagliptin or saxagliptin as effective A1C-lowering agents in pediatric type 2 diabetes.
- Exclusive use of insulin for long-term management without considering noninsulin therapies.
- Assumption that type 1 and type 2 diabetes phenotypes are mutually exclusive in pediatric cases.
- Reliance solely on metformin for durable glycemic control in all pediatric type 2 diabetes cases (evidenced by TODAY study showing ~50% efficacy).
- Use of linagliptin as an effective A1C-lowering agent in pediatric type 2 diabetes (demonstrated no benefit versus placebo in trials).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Early consideration of multiple add-on therapies (metformin, GLP-1 RA, SGLT2i) at type 2 diabetes diagnosis.
- Use of GLP-1 receptor agonists (e.g., liraglutide, semaglutide) and SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin, canagliflozin) as approved pharmacologic options.
- Recommendation for metabolic surgery in adolescents with class 2 obesity or higher and uncontrolled type 2 diabetes despite lifestyle and pharmacologic therapy.
- Integration of family-centered, culturally appropriate lifestyle interventions for physical activity and nutrition.
- Consideration of CGM in pediatric type 2 diabetes for frequent monitoring needs or insulin use.


### pediatric hypoglycemia treatment

Treat both symptomatic and asymptomatic hypoglycemia detected by SMBG or CGMS in children.. Administer 0.3 g/kg body weight oral glucose (e.g., ~5 g for a 15 kg child) for correction.. Follow oral glucose with a mixed snack/meal after 15 minutes to sustain glucose levels.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pediatric hypoglycemia treatment

**Core Management Principles:**
- Treat both symptomatic and asymptomatic hypoglycemia detected by SMBG or CGMS in children.
- Administer 0.3 g/kg body weight oral glucose (e.g., ~5 g for a 15 kg child) for correction.
- Follow oral glucose with a mixed snack/meal after 15 minutes to sustain glucose levels.
- Recheck plasma glucose 15 minutes post-treatment to confirm normalization.
- Administer glucagon subcutaneously or intramuscularly (0.5 mg ≤20 kg, 1 mg >20 kg) for severe neuroglycopenic symptoms or inadequate oral intake.
- Repeating the oral glucose treatment if the recheck blood glucose remains below 70 mg/dl

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Glucose or sucrose is preferred over fructose for hypoglycemia correction.
- IV dextrose (0.25 g/kg, max 25 g) is required for severe cases with IV access and qualified personnel.
- Glucagon kit must be available at home, school/daycare, and during travel.
- Glucagon prescriptions must be renewed before expiration.
- Patients should wear a recognition band for emergency intervention.
- Presence of qualified medical personnel is required for IV dextrose administration


### pediatric insulin dosing strategy

An optimal insulin dose achieves good glycemic control without frequent hypoglycemic episodes.. Insulin dose may be high initially due to counterregulatory hormones and glucotoxicity.. Dose typically decreases during the 'honeymoon phase' to < 0.5 IU/kg/day.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pediatric insulin dosing strategy

**Core Management Principles:**
- An optimal insulin dose achieves good glycemic control without frequent hypoglycemic episodes.
- Insulin dose may be high initially due to counterregulatory hormones and glucotoxicity.
- Dose typically decreases during the 'honeymoon phase' to < 0.5 IU/kg/day.
- Dose adjustments should support appropriate growth and development in children.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Initial dose must account for high counterregulatory hormone levels and glucotoxicity.
- Dose titration should be individualized to avoid hypoglycemia.
- Monitoring for the 'honeymoon phase' to adjust dose downward as needed.


### pediatric diabetic neuropathy screening

Pediatric diabetic neuropathy screening protocol. Annual comprehensive foot exam including inspection, pulse palpation, proprioception, vibration, and monofilament sensation testing. Assessment of neuropathic pain symptoms during annual visits

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pediatric diabetic neuropathy screening protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Annual comprehensive foot exam including inspection, pulse palpation, proprioception, vibration, and monofilament sensation testing
- Assessment of neuropathic pain symptoms during annual visits
- Foot inspection at every visit to educate youth on foot care and detect concurrent issues like tinea pedis or paronychia

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 1 diabetes diagnosis
- Adolescent age group (post-pubertal)
- Diabetes duration of at least 1–2 years
- Presence of CVD risk factors (for neuropathy prevalence association)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Screening for diabetic neuropathy in prepubertal children
- Screening for diabetic neuropathy after only 1–2 years of diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Annual comprehensive foot exam including monofilament testing for adolescents with type 1 diabetes
- Integration of foot care education during routine visits


### insulin injection technique for pediatric T1DM

Select an appropriate injection site and always rotate sites. Clean the skin with soap and water if visibly dirty, then allow the area to dry before injecting. Pinch 1-2-inch portion of skin and fat between thumb and first finger

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin injection technique for pediatric T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Select an appropriate injection site and always rotate sites
- Clean the skin with soap and water if visibly dirty, then allow the area to dry before injecting
- Pinch 1-2-inch portion of skin and fat between thumb and first finger
- Insert needle at 45° angle for children/adolescents or perpendicularly for most adults, inject steadily, and hold needle in skin for 10-15 seconds
- Press injection site for 5-10 seconds after removal to prevent insulin leakage

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Use appropriate needle length and angle based on pediatric patient's body habitus
- Ensure proper site rotation to prevent lipohypertrophy or lipoatrophy
- Maintain clean injection sites to reduce infection risk
- Hold needle in place for 10-15 seconds to ensure complete insulin delivery
- Dispose of needles safely in a tin or plastic box
- Monitor and adjust insulin requirements based on dynamic changes during puberty, growth hormone levels, and sex hormones
- Account for individual variability in insulin needs related to time of day, meals, and exercise


### psychological support in T1DM pregnancy

Proactively support women with T1DM in informed decision-making during pregnancy transition. Facilitate communication within the healthcare team for coordinated care. Conduct psychological assessment as part of preconception to postpartum care

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** psychological support in T1DM pregnancy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Proactively support women with T1DM in informed decision-making during pregnancy transition
- Facilitate communication within the healthcare team for coordinated care
- Conduct psychological assessment as part of preconception to postpartum care
- Refer to psychiatrist/psychologist if psychological issues (e.g., depression, anxiety) are identified

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Psychological assessment must be integrated into preconception to postpartum care pathways
- Healthcare team coordination is mandatory for managing psychological support in T1DM pregnancy


### insulin therapy and glycemic variability factors

Incorrect insulin therapy due to syringe-vial mismatch, incorrect technique, incorrect timing, insulin stacking, or higher insulin dose. Decreased exogenous glucose delivery into blood from missed meals, delayed absorption, or malabsorption. Increased glucose utilization during or after exercise

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy and glycemic variability factors

**Core Management Principles:**
- Incorrect insulin therapy due to syringe-vial mismatch, incorrect technique, incorrect timing, insulin stacking, or higher insulin dose
- Decreased exogenous glucose delivery into blood from missed meals, delayed absorption, or malabsorption
- Increased glucose utilization during or after exercise
- Impaired endogenous glucose production due to alcohol ingestion, liver disease, or renal disease
- Improved insulin sensitivity late after exercise, in the middle of the night, or following improved fitness
- Concomitant adrenal insufficiency leading to unpredictable glycemic variability due to altered counterregulatory hormone responses

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Accurate insulin administration technique and timing
- Regular self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG)
- Monitoring for conditions affecting glucose metabolism (e.g., liver/renal disease, hypothyroidism)
- Adjustment of insulin doses during exercise or illness
- Avoidance of alcohol ingestion to prevent impaired glucose production
- Monitoring for adrenal insufficiency in patients with unexplained glycemic variability or recurrent hypoglycemia


### insulin therapy education protocol

Educate patients about the action profile of different insulin preparations. Emphasize the importance of regular meal timings. Avoid intramuscular administration of insulin. Prevent syringe-vial mismatch. Teach symptoms of hypoglycemia, its recognition and appropriate management

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy education protocol

**Core Management Principles:**
- Educate patients about the action profile of different insulin preparations
- Emphasize the importance of regular meal timings
- Avoid intramuscular administration of insulin
- Prevent syringe-vial mismatch
- Teach symptoms of hypoglycemia, its recognition and appropriate management
- Teach proper insulin storage conditions (e.g., refrigeration for unopened vials, room temperature for in-use vials) and handling to maintain potency
- Demonstrate and practice correct injection site rotation to prevent lipodystrophy and ensure consistent absorption

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Ensure patients understand the necessity of matching insulin type to meal carbohydrate content for prandial dosing
- Mandate verification of insulin type and dose before each administration to prevent errors
- Require documentation of insulin doses, timing, and glucose levels in a logbook or digital app for review during follow-ups


### lifestyle management in T1DM

Maintain glycemia in the normal to near-normal range with minimal/no hypoglycemia. Ensure adequate nutrition to facilitate healthy growth and development in children and adolescents. Address individual nutrition needs, incorporating personal, social, and cultural preferences

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** lifestyle management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Maintain glycemia in the normal to near-normal range with minimal/no hypoglycemia
- Ensure adequate nutrition to facilitate healthy growth and development in children and adolescents
- Address individual nutrition needs, incorporating personal, social, and cultural preferences
- Improve overall health through appropriate food choices and individualized diet plans
- Encourage consumption of complex carbohydrates (≥70% of total carbohydrates) and limit sucrose to <10% of total calories
- Maintain optimal blood pressure, weight, and lipid levels
- Prevent the development or progression of diabetes-related microvascular and macrovascular complications

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Individualize diet plans considering socio-cultural environment, food habits, preferences, and work schedule
- Ensure minimum carbohydrate intake of 130 g/day in adults and 175 g/day in pregnancy
- Limit saturated fat to <10% of total calories and dietary cholesterol to <300 mg/day
- Encourage fiber intake (≥14 g per 1000 kcal in children ≥1 year or 'Age in years + 5 g')
- Promote balanced protein intake (15-20% of calories) with preference for low-fat and vegetarian sources
- In patients with raised LDL cholesterol, restrict saturated fat intake to <7% of total calories and dietary cholesterol to <200 mg/day
- In patients with diabetic nephropathy, daily protein intake should not be restricted to <0.8 g/kg body weight


### long-acting insulin analogue use in T1DM

Use relatively peakless long-acting insulin analogues (e.g., glargine, detemir, degludec) instead of intermediate-acting NPH insulin to reduce nocturnal hypoglycemia. Prefer rapid-acting insulin analogues (e.g., aspart, lispro, glulisine) over regular insulin in patients with delayed pre-meal hypoglycemia

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** long-acting insulin analogue use in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use relatively peakless long-acting insulin analogues (e.g., glargine, detemir, degludec) instead of intermediate-acting NPH insulin to reduce nocturnal hypoglycemia
- Prefer rapid-acting insulin analogues (e.g., aspart, lispro, glulisine) over regular insulin in patients with delayed pre-meal hypoglycemia
- Consider extra cost of insulin analogues and restrict substitution to cases where hypoglycemia persists despite best measures
- Consider switching to long-acting insulin analogues (glargine, detemir, degludec) if nocturnal hypoglycemia persists despite optimizing NPH dosing and lifestyle adjustments

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Hypoglycemia must persist despite best measures (e.g., ensuring mid-meal snack) before switching to insulin analogues
- Ensure mid-meal snacks are provided as a first-line measure before considering insulin analogue substitution


### ketone monitoring in T1DM management

Use urine ketone strips or nitroprusside test for quick bedside monitoring of ketonuria during illness with fever/vomiting or persistent polyuria with elevated blood glucose (>250 mg/dl).

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ketone monitoring in T1DM management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use urine ketone strips or nitroprusside test for quick bedside monitoring of ketonuria during illness with fever/vomiting or persistent polyuria with elevated blood glucose (>250 mg/dl).
- Measure blood beta-hydroxybutyrate directly using point-of-care meters or laboratory tests when feasible, as the ratio of beta-hydroxybutyrate to acetoacetate increases significantly in DKA.
- Interpret blood ketone levels alongside concurrent blood glucose values for accurate assessment.
- Advise increased fluid and carbohydrate intake if blood glucose is <180 mg/dl and ketone levels are 0.6–1.5 mmol/L; administer additional rapid-acting insulin if blood glucose is >180 mg/dl.
- Emergency admission is warranted for blood ketone levels >3 mmol/L due to high risk of acidosis.
- False positive ketonuria may occur in patients using drugs like penicillamine, captopril, and mesna, requiring clinical correlation for accurate interpretation.
- False negative ketonuria is rarely encountered in patients with DKA and shock due to the liver's conversion of ketones to beta-hydroxybutyrate.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Urine ketone test strips should be available in limited care settings and used during illness with fever/vomiting or persistent polyuria with elevated blood glucose (>250 mg/dl).
- Blood ketone monitoring should be performed using point-of-care meters or laboratory tests when feasible.
- Patients should keep blood or urine ketone strips at home for sick-day use and check expiry dates periodically.
- Urine ketone testing should be performed during illness with fever and/or vomiting, persistent polyuria with elevated blood glucose (>250 mg/dl), especially in the presence of abdominal pain or rapid breathing.


### HbA1c monitoring and target guidelines in T1DM

HbA1c measurement should be performed at least every 3 months in patients with T1DM using NGSP-certified assays standardized to the DCCT reference assay.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** HbA1c monitoring and target guidelines in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- HbA1c measurement should be performed at least every 3 months in patients with T1DM using NGSP-certified assays standardized to the DCCT reference assay.
- HbA1c targets should be individualized for each patient, aiming for values as close to normal as possible while avoiding severe hypoglycemia and frequent mild to moderate hypoglycemia.
- For children and adolescents, HbA1c targets vary by guidelines (ISPAD: <7%, NICE: <6.5%, ADA: <7.5%), with special caution in children below six years due to high risk of adverse neurologic outcomes from severe hypoglycemia.
- Adolescents often struggle to achieve HbA1c targets due to hormonal changes, psychological adjustments, and increased independence in diabetes care.
- Hemoglobinopathies (e.g., HbS, HbE, β-thalassemia) may interfere with HbA1c measurement, requiring alternative methods like immunoassays, boronate affinity HPLC, or non-HbA1c biomarkers (fructosamine, glycated albumin, 1-5 anhydroglucitol).
- HbA1c targets in children below six years should prioritize avoidance of severe hypoglycemia due to high risk of adverse neurologic outcomes, even if it means accepting higher HbA1c values than in older children or adults.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- HbA1c assays must be NGSP-certified and standardized to the DCCT reference assay for accurate measurement.
- Alternative glycemic biomarkers (e.g., fructosamine) should be used in patients with hemoglobinopathies or accelerated red cell turnover.
- Caregiver involvement is critical for achieving HbA1c targets in children below six years.
- HbA1c targets should be individualized based on age, hypoglycemia risk, and patient-specific factors.
- Healthcare providers should recognize the challenges in adolescents (hormonal, psychological) when setting HbA1c targets.
- For patients with hemoglobinopathies (e.g., HbS, HbE, β-thalassemia), alternative glycemic biomarkers (fructosamine, glycated albumin, 1-5 anhydroglucitol) must be used instead of HbA1c for accurate glycemic monitoring.


### Charcot foot progression and complications

Charcot foot progression and complications in T1DM. Acute Charcot foot presents with redness, swelling, and elevated local temperature in the foot.. Differential diagnoses of acute Charcot foot include cellulitis, osteomyelitis, gout, and deep venous thrombosis.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Charcot foot progression and complications in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Acute Charcot foot presents with redness, swelling, and elevated local temperature in the foot.
- Differential diagnoses of acute Charcot foot include cellulitis, osteomyelitis, gout, and deep venous thrombosis.
- Untreated Charcot foot leads to recurrent microfractures, bone resorption, and deformity, increasing foot ulceration risk.
- Sensorimotor neuropathy, autonomic neuropathy, and intact peripheral circulation are prerequisites for Charcot neuroarthropathy.
- Osteoporotic changes in foot bones due to long-standing diabetic neuropathy (DN) may precede Charcot foot deformity

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Offloading with a total contact cast (TCC) is required in the acute phase of Charcot foot.
- Use of protective braces (e.g., Charcot restraint orthotic walker, patellar tendon-bearing brace) after acute phase.
- Presence of sensorimotor neuropathy, autonomic neuropathy, and intact peripheral circulation for Charcot neuroarthropathy development.
- Intact peripheral circulation is a prerequisite for Charcot neuroarthropathy development
