Grade 0: Normal nerve conduction (NC). Grade 1a: Abnormal NC without symptoms or signs. Grade 1b: Abnormal NC plus signs of DSPN but no symptoms

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DSPN severity staging system

**Core Management Principles:**
- Grade 0: Normal nerve conduction (NC)
- Grade 1a: Abnormal NC without symptoms or signs
- Grade 1b: Abnormal NC plus signs of DSPN but no symptoms


### biphasic insulin regimen optimization

biphasic insulin regimen optimization in T1DM. Mimic physiological insulin action relative to meals, snacks, and exercise while avoiding hypoglycemia. Use premixed insulin (fixed ratio of short/intermediate-acting insulin) twice daily (before breakfast and dinner) with a short-acting insulin before lunch as a practical option in resource-limited settings

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** biphasic insulin regimen optimization in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Mimic physiological insulin action relative to meals, snacks, and exercise while avoiding hypoglycemia
- Use premixed insulin (fixed ratio of short/intermediate-acting insulin) twice daily (before breakfast and dinner) with a short-acting insulin before lunch as a practical option in resource-limited settings
- Resuspend premixed insulin by gently shaking or rolling the pen/vial before injection
- Adjust basal insulin based on fasting glucose and bolus doses based on post-meal glucose levels in basal-bolus regimens
- Provide patient/family education and motivation for successful regimen implementation
- Use 'meal bolus' calculators (incorporated into pumps or separate devices) to determine prandial insulin doses based on pre-meal glucose, carbohydrate content, and exercise in basal-bolus regimens
- Adjust basal insulin rates in CSII based on circadian variations (e.g., higher rates after 4 AM due to dawn phenomenon and counter-regulatory hormone release)

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Total daily dose (TDD) should be split approximately 2/3 in the morning and 1/3 at night for biphasic regimens
- Meals and snacks should not vary significantly in timing or quantity to avoid hypoglycemia in fixed-ratio regimens
- Basal-bolus regimens require 40-60% of TDD as basal insulin (preferably long-acting analogs if affordable)
- CSII requires motivated patients/families willing to monitor glucose 4-6 times/day (finger-prick or CGMS) and adjust doses
- CSII candidates must have backup insulin for subcutaneous injection in case of pump malfunction
- For CSII, patients must have access to CGMS for trend-based insulin suspension or hybrid closed-loop functionality to reduce hypoglycemia risk
- Hybrid closed-loop insulin pumps require patient/caregiver willingness to monitor glucose 4-6 times/day and adjust bolus doses manually


### carbohydrate restriction risks in pediatric T1DM

Restrictive carbohydrate diets in children with T1DM may lead to endocrine and metabolic consequences, as highlighted in case series evidence.. Dietary advice on carbohydrate intake for pregnant women with T1DM should be carefully managed to avoid adverse outcomes.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** carbohydrate restriction risks in pediatric T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Restrictive carbohydrate diets in children with T1DM may lead to endocrine and metabolic consequences, as highlighted in case series evidence.
- Dietary advice on carbohydrate intake for pregnant women with T1DM should be carefully managed to avoid adverse outcomes.
- Physical activity interventions in children with T1DM require careful consideration of carbohydrate availability to prevent hypoglycemia.
- Pregnant women with T1DM require tailored carbohydrate intake to balance glycemic control and fetal growth, with risks of excessive restriction including preterm birth or macrosomia if not properly managed.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Carbohydrate intake must be sufficient to support growth, development, and metabolic needs in pediatric T1DM.
- Dietary recommendations should be individualized based on age, activity level, and glycemic control.
- Close monitoring of blood glucose levels is essential when adjusting carbohydrate intake or physical activity.
- Carbohydrate intake should be adjusted based on the child's growth velocity and pubertal status to support optimal development without compromising glycemic stability.


### cardiovascular risk clustering in adolescent T1DM

Clustering of cardiovascular risk factors is common in adolescents with T1DM, placing them at high risk for future cardiovascular events.. 14-45% of children with T1DM have two or more cardiovascular risk factors.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** cardiovascular risk clustering in adolescent T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Clustering of cardiovascular risk factors is common in adolescents with T1DM, placing them at high risk for future cardiovascular events.
- 14-45% of children with T1DM have two or more cardiovascular risk factors.
- Overweight/obesity is prevalent in ~40% of children with T1DM and is associated with higher rates of hypertension, metabolic syndrome, and transaminitis.
- Clustering of cardiovascular risk factors in adolescent T1DM includes dyslipidemia (elevated LDL, triglycerides, and low HDL) as a key component, in addition to hypertension and metabolic syndrome.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Screening for cardiovascular risk factors (e.g., hypertension, metabolic syndrome) in adolescents with T1DM.
- Regular monitoring of body weight and metabolic parameters in this population.
- Mandatory annual screening for dyslipidemia (lipid profile) in adolescents with T1DM, regardless of symptom presence.


### cardiovascular stress testing in T1DM

Exercise treadmill test (TMT) is the first-line test for patients without peripheral neuropathy, foot deformity, previous amputation, or baseline significant ST-T abnormalities due to cost-effectiveness and availability.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** cardiovascular stress testing in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Exercise treadmill test (TMT) is the first-line test for patients without peripheral neuropathy, foot deformity, previous amputation, or baseline significant ST-T abnormalities due to cost-effectiveness and availability.
- Pharmacological stress testing (dobutamine stress echocardiography or dobutamine stress myocardial perfusion imaging) is used when TMT is contraindicated, though limited by cost and availability.
- Coronary artery calcification (CAC) scoring via electron beam computerized tomography (EBCT) quantifies atherosclerotic plaque burden in T1DM patients.
- CAC scoring is recommended for quantifying atherosclerotic plaque burden in T1DM patients, with specific score ranges (0, 1-10, 11-100, 101-400, >400) corresponding to no, minimal, mild, moderate, and severe plaque burden respectively

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patients must be evaluated for peripheral neuropathy, foot deformity, previous amputation, or baseline significant ST-T abnormalities before selecting stress testing modality.
- CAC scoring requires electron beam computerized tomography (EBCT) for accurate quantification of plaque burden.
- Patients with peripheral neuropathy, foot deformity, previous amputation, or baseline significant ST-T abnormalities are ineligible for TMT and require pharmacological stress testing or CAC scoring


### carbohydrate management during exercise in T1DM

Adjust carbohydrate intake based on pre-exercise blood glucose (BG) levels: 0.3-0.5 g/kg/h for BG < 90 mg/dl, 0.8-1.0 g/kg/h for BG 90-150 mg/dl, and avoid carbohydrates for BG 150-250 mg/dl until BG < 150 mg/dl.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** carbohydrate management during exercise in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Adjust carbohydrate intake based on pre-exercise blood glucose (BG) levels: 0.3-0.5 g/kg/h for BG < 90 mg/dl, 0.8-1.0 g/kg/h for BG 90-150 mg/dl, and avoid carbohydrates for BG 150-250 mg/dl until BG < 150 mg/dl.
- For prolonged activities, consume additional carbohydrates (0.5-1.0 g/kg/h) based on BG testing results.
- Children and adolescents with T1DM should engage in 60+ minutes/day of moderate or vigorous-intensity aerobic activity, plus vigorous, muscle-strengthening, and bone-strengthening activities at least three days a week.
- Patients with cardiovascular disease (CVD) signs, diabetes-related complications, or sedentary individuals >30 years with diabetes ≥10 years should undergo CVD evaluation before starting new exercise programs.
- Vigorous exercise is contraindicated in patients with severe non-proliferative diabetic retinopathy (NPDR) or proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR) to reduce vitreous hemorrhage risk.
- For prolonged activities (>60 minutes), monitor blood glucose every 30 minutes and adjust carbohydrate intake (0.5-1.0 g/kg/h) based on trends to prevent hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Pre-exercise BG testing to determine carbohydrate intake adjustments.
- CVD evaluation for high-risk patients before initiating new exercise programs.
- Avoidance of vigorous exercise in patients with severe NPDR or PDR.
- Use quick-acting carbohydrates (e.g., glucose tablets, fruit juice) for hypoglycemia correction during exercise to ensure rapid glucose absorption.


### autonomic dysfunction management table

autonomic dysfunction management in T1DM. Use postural maneuvers and compression stockings for orthostatic hypotension; avoid hot water baths.. Administer Fludrocortisone (0.5-2 mg OD) or Midodrine (2.5-10 mg TDS) for orthostatic hypotension.. Follow a low-fat, small frequent meal diet for gastroparesis; use prokinetics like Metoclopramide or Domperidone.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** autonomic dysfunction management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use postural maneuvers and compression stockings for orthostatic hypotension; avoid hot water baths.
- Administer Fludrocortisone (0.5-2 mg OD) or Midodrine (2.5-10 mg TDS) for orthostatic hypotension.
- Follow a low-fat, small frequent meal diet for gastroparesis; use prokinetics like Metoclopramide or Domperidone.
- Use soluble fiber, antimotility agents (Loperamide), and gut antibiotics (Metronidazole/Rifaximin) for diarrhea.
- Rule out infective, metabolic, or intrinsic GI disorders before treating autonomic diarrhea.
- Check for warmth of water with hands/elbow before rinsing feet to avoid thermal injury in patients with sensory deficits
- Wear clean, cotton socks (preferably white) with seams facing outwards and change daily

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Confirm orthostatic hypotension by observing a fall in systolic BP without an increase in heart rate.
- Perform upper GI endoscopy to rule out obstruction in suspected gastroparesis.
- Conduct stool routine microscopy, culture, and HIV serology to exclude infective causes of diarrhea.
- Evaluate for exocrine pancreatic dysfunction, gluten-sensitive enteropathy, and bacterial overgrowth in diarrhea.
- Consider gastroenterology consultation for complex or refractory autonomic dysfunction cases.
- Rule out obstruction via upper GI endoscopy for suspected gastroparesis
- Evaluate for thyrotoxicosis in diarrhea management


### barriers to T1DM management in poverty

cost-driven barriers to T1DM management in poverty. Families may ration insulin or blood glucose (BG) testing due to financial constraints and shame in disclosing this to healthcare teams.. Parents prioritize cost-effectiveness and affordability in all aspects of diabetes care, including insulin type and delivery methods.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** cost-driven barriers to T1DM management in poverty

**Core Management Principles:**
- Families may ration insulin or blood glucose (BG) testing due to financial constraints and shame in disclosing this to healthcare teams.
- Parents prioritize cost-effectiveness and affordability in all aspects of diabetes care, including insulin type and delivery methods.
- Reusing lancets, needles, and other supplies with proper care is practiced to reduce costs.
- Families explore bulk purchasing of supplies and income generation to offset expenses.
- Peer support, charitable options, and subsidized programs are leveraged to improve access to care.
- Families may face food insecurity, which directly impacts glycemic control and complicates T1DM management due to inconsistent access to adequate nutrition.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Insulin in vials is preferred over cartridges for cost savings.
- Reusable insulin pens are prioritized over disposable ones to reduce expenses.
- Multiple-dose insulin regimens are used to achieve metabolic control without relying on insulin pumps.
- Proper care and hygiene practices are maintained when reusing supplies like lancets and needles.
- Diabetes care education is emphasized as a mandatory requirement to overcome barriers posed by poverty and illiteracy, enabling families to implement cost-effective strategies.


### diabetic foot ulcer prevention protocol

Foot examination should be done at each visit in patients with diabetic neuropathy (DN) and at least annually in those without DN.. Identification of foot at risk is the most important step in the prevention of diabetic foot ulcer.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetic foot ulcer prevention protocol

**Core Management Principles:**
- Foot examination should be done at each visit in patients with diabetic neuropathy (DN) and at least annually in those without DN.
- Identification of foot at risk is the most important step in the prevention of diabetic foot ulcer.
- Inspect feet daily yourself (with the help of a mirror to inspect soles) or with the help of someone, especially when vision is compromised.
- Wash feet daily with a non-medicated soap; pat feet dry with a soft absorbent cloth, especially between toes.
- Apply lubricants containing urea or salicylates to the feet, sparing the interdigital spaces.
- Avoid walking barefoot, even indoors, to prevent trauma to the feet.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Foot examination at each visit for patients with DN.
- Annual foot examination for patients without DN.
- Patients with foot at risk should be educated on foot care practices by a trained healthcare professional during each visit.


### comprehensive pediatric monitoring

comprehensive pediatric monitoring in T1DM. Track glycemic control using HbA1c every 3 months or Time in Range (TIR) if using CGMS from the beginning.. Monitor growth, development, blood pressure, puberty, and retina regularly.. Perform periodic tests for comorbidities: TSH, tTG-IgA, lipids, hemoglobin, and urine albumin-creatinine ratio.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** comprehensive pediatric monitoring in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Track glycemic control using HbA1c every 3 months or Time in Range (TIR) if using CGMS from the beginning.
- Monitor growth, development, blood pressure, puberty, and retina regularly.
- Perform periodic tests for comorbidities: TSH, tTG-IgA, lipids, hemoglobin, and urine albumin-creatinine ratio.
- Educate families on sick day management: frequent BG checks, ketone monitoring, insulin dose adjustments, and hydration.
- Teach families to recognize emergency signs (e.g., rapid breathing, altered behavior) and know whom to contact.
- Calculate total daily dose (TDD) of insulin by summing all units (long and short-acting) for sick day management adjustments.
- Administer extra rapid/short-acting insulin as a percentage of TDD (e.g., 5-10%) based on blood ketone levels and glucose ranges during sick days.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Regular daily SMBG, multiple dose basal-bolus insulin regimen, and ongoing self-adjustments for glycemic control.
- Blood ketone meters (e.g., Freestyle Optium) should be used for monitoring during sick days.
- Pump users must monitor BG frequently and keep extra rapid/short-acting insulin supplies.
- Sick children should be reviewed by an endocrinologist or pediatrician at illness onset.
- Avoid skipping insulin doses during illness, even if the child is not eating.
- Ensure families have access to blood ketone meters (e.g., Freestyle Optium) and test strips for reliable sick day monitoring.
- Pump users must maintain frequent BG monitoring and keep backup rapid/short-acting insulin supplies to prevent DKA in case of pump malfunction.


### T1DM mental health management guidelines

Screen all T1DM patients for depression, anxiety, and diabetes distress at diagnosis and annually using validated tools like PHQ-9, GAD-7, and PAID.. Integrate mental health professionals into the diabetes care team for collaborative management.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** T1DM mental health management guidelines

**Core Management Principles:**
- Screen all T1DM patients for depression, anxiety, and diabetes distress at diagnosis and annually using validated tools like PHQ-9, GAD-7, and PAID.
- Integrate mental health professionals into the diabetes care team for collaborative management.
- Provide structured psychological support, including cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) and diabetes self-management education (DSME) to address emotional and behavioral challenges.
- Encourage peer support groups and family involvement to improve coping and adherence to treatment.
- Monitor for burnout and eating disorders, particularly in adolescents and young adults with T1DM.
- Screen for fear of hypoglycemia using validated tools like the Hypoglycemia Fear Survey (HFS) in patients with recurrent hypoglycemia or poor glycemic control.
- Assess sleep disturbances and insomnia in T1DM patients, particularly those with nocturnal hypoglycemia or poor glycemic control.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Mandatory mental health screening at diagnosis and annual follow-ups for all T1DM patients.
- Training of diabetes care teams in recognizing and managing mental health comorbidities.
- Availability of mental health professionals with expertise in diabetes-related psychological issues.
- Access to culturally appropriate and age-specific mental health interventions.
- Integration of mental health assessments into routine diabetes care visits.
- Mandatory training of mental health professionals in diabetes-specific psychological issues and cultural competencies for Indian patients.
- Establish referral pathways for mental health services within diabetes care centers, including tele-mental health options for remote areas.


### T1DM diagnosis and management framework

holistic T1DM diagnosis and management framework. Start a long-term, close relationship between patient, family, and diabetes care team for effective management.. Provide age-appropriate, culturally sensitive education tailored to socio-economic status, motivation, and medical status.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** holistic T1DM diagnosis and management framework

**Core Management Principles:**
- Start a long-term, close relationship between patient, family, and diabetes care team for effective management.
- Provide age-appropriate, culturally sensitive education tailored to socio-economic status, motivation, and medical status.
- Emphasize lifelong self-care with insulin, pathophysiology education, and differentiation from T2DM.
- Focus on comprehensive self-management skills including blood glucose/ketone testing, insulin handling, hypoglycemia management, diet, and exercise.
- Address psychological responses, family dynamics, and coping mechanisms to prevent burnout.
- Incorporate cognitive behavior therapy (CBT) explicitly into routine psychological support for patients and families to address burnout and coping mechanisms.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Lifelong insulin therapy for T1DM management.
- Regular blood glucose and ketone monitoring with pattern discernment.
- Standardized education on insulin types, action profiles, storage, and injection techniques.
- Access to diabetes technology and peer support groups.
- Psychological support and cognitive behavior therapy for patients and families.
- Mandatory inclusion of ketone testing in blood glucose monitoring protocols to detect and prevent diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA).



## Diabetes Lifestyle and Weight Management

This collection of guidelines integrates lifestyle, nutritional, and therapeutic strategies to optimize diabetes care and weight management, emphasizing individualized, evidence-based approaches that address glycemic control, metabolic health, and patient-centered outcomes across diverse populations and settings.

**Key Features:**
- Nutritional management in diabetes: Focus on nutrient-dense eating patterns and individualized meal plans, including adjustments during illness.
- Obesity treatment: Annual screening using BMI with body fat measurement, targeting 5–7% weight loss to improve glycemia and cardiovascular risk.
- Weight management in diabetes care: Counseling for weight reduction in individuals with dyslipidemia, emphasizing individualized energy deficits and behavior modification.
- Obesity pharmacotherapy: Gradual initiation of weight-loss medications with adjustments to diabetes medications to reduce hypoglycemia risk.
- Lifestyle modification components: Comprehensive approach including nutrition, physical activity, behavioral changes, and stress management tailored to patient needs.
- Exercise-based glycemic management: Structured exercise interventions lower A1C by 0.66% in type 2 diabetes, with insulin dose adjustments for anticipated exercise.
- Physical activity counseling: Baseline evaluation of activity and sedentary time, with encouragement to meet activity guidelines.
- Resistance exercise benefits: 2–3 sessions weekly to improve strength, balance, and daily living activities, suitable for all intensities.
- Hospital medical nutrition therapy: Adequate caloric provision, glycemic optimization, and coordination of meal delivery and insulin coverage.
- Exercise and muscle preservation: Emphasis on muscle-strengthening activities alongside physical activity, especially during pharmacotherapy or metabolic surgery.
- Non-pharmacological stress interventions: Tailored techniques such as meditation, yoga, and hobbies to manage stress and improve mental health.
- Nutrition and exercise for older adults: Healthful eating with adequate protein intake and regular physical activity to maintain lean body mass and function.
- Medication-induced weight gain management: Review of concomitant medications to minimize weight-promoting effects and explore alternatives.
- Metabolic surgery guidelines: Consideration for individuals with type 2 diabetes and BMI ≥30 kg/m² (or ≥27.5 kg/m² in Asian Americans) who are surgical candidates.

**Notable Details:**
- Weight loss of 5–7% of baseline body weight is recommended to improve glycemia and cardiovascular risk factors.
- Structured exercise interventions of at least 8 weeks lower A1C by 0.66% in type 2 diabetes, even without BMI change.
- Metabolic surgery is considered for individuals with type 2 diabetes and BMI ≥30 kg/m² (or ≥27.5 kg/m² in Asian Americans).
- Adults with diabetes are encouraged to participate in 2–3 resistance exercise sessions per week on non-consecutive days.
- Adequate protein intake of at least 0.8 g/kg body weight/day is recommended for older adults with diabetes to maintain lean body mass.

**Topics in this group:**

### nutritional management in diabetes

Shift focus from macronutrients/micronutrients to overall eating patterns and nutrient-dense foods (e.g., vegetables, fruits, legumes). Encourage individualized meal plans based on cultural preferences, health literacy, and food access nutritional management in diabetes. Nutritional management during illness in diabetes. Modify diabetic diet by adjusting food consistency and texture during fever or illness. Encourage semi-solid foods and fluids to maintain adequate calorie intake. Recommend thin soups, milk, buttermilk, or fresh lime juice as suitable options

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Nutritional management in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Shift focus from macronutrients/micronutrients to overall eating patterns and nutrient-dense foods (e.g., vegetables, fruits, legumes)
- Encourage individualized meal plans based on cultural preferences, health literacy, and food access
- Promote collaborative development of eating plans with RDNs, emphasizing shared decision-making
- Limit processed foods, added sugars, salt, and saturated fats; prioritize whole foods and plant-based proteins
- Use practical tools like the diabetes plate method or simplified carbohydrate counting for meal planning

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Referral to a registered dietitian nutritionist (RDN) experienced in diabetes care at diagnosis and as needed
- Assessment of patient-specific factors (e.g., comorbidities like CKD, heart failure, or pregnancy-related nutrition concerns)
- Evaluation of food literacy, numeracy, and willingness to make behavioral changes
- Monitoring of medication adjustments in relation to nutrition care plans (e.g., insulin dose modifications)
- Consideration of cultural appropriateness and socioeconomic barriers to healthy eating

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Emphasizing fixed macronutrient ratios (e.g., low-carbohydrate diets as universally optimal)
- Routine use of dietary supplements (e.g., vitamins, minerals, herbal supplements) without deficiency
- One-size-fits-all eating patterns or rigid meal plans
- Low-protein diets for diabetes-related CKD (albuminuria/eGFR reduction)
- Reliance on glycemic index (GI) alone for glycemic management without considering overall eating patterns

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of adaptive closed-loop AID systems that reduce reliance on precise carbohydrate counting
- DASH4D eating pattern (moderate carbs, rich in fruits/vegetables/whole grains, low in saturated fat/sodium) for T2D with hypertension
- Avoidance of very-low-carbohydrate diets in patients on SGLT2 inhibitors due to ketoacidosis risk
- Recommendation to limit sodium to <2,300 mg/day, with stronger emphasis on whole foods to reduce processed food intake
- Focus on plant-based proteins and replacement of animal proteins to improve cardiometabolic outcomes

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Nutritional management during illness in diabetes

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Modify diabetic diet by adjusting food consistency and texture during fever or illness
- Encourage semi-solid foods and fluids to maintain adequate calorie intake
- Recommend thin soups, milk, buttermilk, or fresh lime juice as suitable options

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient must have diabetes and be experiencing fever or other acute illness
- Patient must be experiencing acute illness (e.g., fever) with associated reduced oral intake or gastrointestinal symptoms


### obesity treatment guidelines

Obesity treatment guidelines updates. Screening for overweight and obesity should occur annually using BMI with additional body fat measurement (e.g., anthropometric assessment) where feasible.. Weight loss of 5–7% of baseline body weight is recommended to improve glycemia and cardiovascular risk factors. obesity treatment guidelines. Obesity treatment approach. Weight reduction should be attempted in obese individuals. Lifestyle modification is the mainstay of treatment. Drugs may be used judiciously as an adjuvant therapy. Bariatric surgery may be an option in morbid obesity. Weight loss goals should be individualized based on patient characteristics, comorbidities, and risk-benefit assessment

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Obesity treatment guidelines updates

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screening for overweight and obesity should occur annually using BMI with additional body fat measurement (e.g., anthropometric assessment) where feasible.
- Weight loss of 5–7% of baseline body weight is recommended to improve glycemia and cardiovascular risk factors.
- Counseling and regular monitoring for adequate nutrition intake are advised for individuals pursuing intentional weight loss.
- Engage care team members to minimize use of weight-promoting medications when clinically appropriate.
- Individualized dose and dose titration for obesity pharmacotherapy should balance efficacy, benefits, and tolerability.
- Screening for overweight and obesity should incorporate additional body fat measurement (e.g., anthropometric assessment or direct measurement) where available and feasible to confirm excess adiposity.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening for overweight and obesity must include BMI and, where feasible, additional body fat measurement.
- Weight loss targets (5–7% of baseline body weight) are recommended to improve glycemia and cardiovascular risk factors.
- Intentional weight loss requires counseling and regular monitoring for adequate nutrition intake.
- Minimizing weight-promoting medications requires engagement of other care team members.
- Obesity pharmacotherapy dosing must balance efficacy, benefits, and tolerability.
- Alternative structured lifestyle programs must include clearly defined components and modes of delivery (e.g., in-person, telehealth, digital platforms) as per updated Recommendation 8.8b.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of GLP-1 RA-based therapy and/or metabolic surgery as treatment options for obesity in people with type 1 diabetes.
- Updated obesity pharmacotherapy costs as of 15 July 2025.
- Recommendation to consider alternative pharmacologic agents for treatment modification and intensification.
- Clarification of components of alternative structured lifestyle programs and modes of delivery.
- Recommendation for individualized obesity pharmacotherapy dosing and titration.
- Recommendation 8.21 now includes considering alternative pharmacologic agents for treatment modification and intensification in obesity management.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Obesity treatment approach

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Weight reduction should be attempted in obese individuals
- Lifestyle modification is the mainstay of treatment
- Drugs may be used judiciously as an adjuvant therapy
- Bariatric surgery may be an option in morbid obesity
- Weight loss goals should be individualized based on patient characteristics, comorbidities, and risk-benefit assessment

**Prerequisites:**
- Obesity diagnosis (BMI ≥30 kg/m² or higher)
- Patient readiness for lifestyle modification
- Assessment of comorbidities and risk factors
- Prior trial of non-pharmacological interventions (if applicable)
- Documentation of failed attempts at lifestyle modification prior to considering pharmacotherapy (unless contraindicated)


### weight management in diabetes care

Individualized eating plan with energy deficit + enhanced physical activity for weight loss. Behavior modification goals addressing physical activity, calorie restriction, and motivation. Minimum 5% weight loss required for metabolic improvements; 7–10% for high-risk individuals

### Source 1: 2026 ACC-AHA-AACVPR-ABC-ACPM-ADA AGS-APhA-ASPC-NLA-PCNA Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Weight Management in Diabetes Care for Dyslipidemia

**Key Clinical Actions:**
- Counseling and treatment to achieve weight reduction are recommended for individuals with overweight or obesity and dyslipidemia.
- Maintenance of a healthy weight is emphasized to improve dyslipidemia outcomes.
- Intensive lifestyle interventions (e.g., structured diet, physical activity, and behavioral therapy) should be employed to achieve weight reduction in individuals with overweight or obesity and dyslipidemia.

**Patient Preparation / Diagnostic Criteria:**
- Individuals must have overweight or obesity with concurrent dyslipidemia.
- Weight management strategies should be tailored to the individual's diabetes type (Type 1 or Type 2), with consideration of glycemic control and hypoglycemia risk.

### Source 2: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Weight Management in Diabetes Care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Individualized eating plan with energy deficit + enhanced physical activity for weight loss
- Behavior modification goals addressing physical activity, calorie restriction, and motivation
- Minimum 5% weight loss required for metabolic improvements; 7–10% for high-risk individuals
- Use of GLP-1 RAs or metabolic surgery for weight loss in type 1 diabetes with obesity
- Person-centered approaches with motivational interviewing for weight discussions
- Counseling on anticipating increased risk of hypoglycemia and reduction in insulin requirements when initiating GLP-1 RA therapy in type 1 diabetes
- Implementation of sick-day rules and ketone monitoring for patients on GLP-1 RA therapy in type 1 diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Intensive lifestyle intervention programs with frequent follow-up
- Screening for disordered eating behaviors during DSMES/MNT encounters
- Shared decision-making for GLP-1 RA therapy in type 1 diabetes
- Close monitoring of insulin requirements and ketone production with GLP-1 RA use
- Multidisciplinary evaluation for metabolic surgery in type 1 diabetes
- Detailed review of drug side effect profiles before initiating GLP-1 RA or dual GIP/GLP-1 RA therapy in type 1 diabetes
- Exclusion criteria assessment for preexisting gastroparesis, hypoglycemia unawareness, or recent episodes of diabetic/euglycemic ketoacidosis before starting GLP-1 RA therapy

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Rigid meal plans and strict tracking of food intake/body weight in high-risk individuals
- Extrapolating GLP-1 RA dose escalation protocols from type 2 to type 1 diabetes
- Assuming all patients with overweight/obesity want to discuss weight management

**Updated Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs for weight loss in type 1 diabetes with obesity
- Minimum 5% weight loss threshold for glycemic/lipid/blood pressure benefits
- Culturally tailored interventions for populations with lower traditional intervention efficacy
- Long-term weight loss maintenance interventions with real-world clinic-based follow-up
- Modification of AID settings and mealtime insulin dosing with GLP-1 RA therapy
- Use of dual GIP and GLP-1 receptor agonists for weight loss in type 1 diabetes with obesity
- Consideration of comorbidities such as obstructive sleep apnea, MASH, or coronary artery disease to guide GLP-1 RA selection in type 1 diabetes


### obesity pharmacotherapy guidelines

Obesity pharmacotherapy in diabetes care. Initiate obesity pharmacotherapy at the lowest dose with gradual stepwise uptitration based on tolerability and response.. Evaluate and adjust diabetes medications (e.g., reduce sulfonylureas or insulin) to mitigate hypoglycemia risk when adding GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Obesity pharmacotherapy in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Initiate obesity pharmacotherapy at the lowest dose with gradual stepwise uptitration based on tolerability and response.
- Evaluate and adjust diabetes medications (e.g., reduce sulfonylureas or insulin) to mitigate hypoglycemia risk when adding GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs.
- Assess effectiveness and safety of obesity medications monthly for the first 3 months and quarterly thereafter.
- Continue long-term pharmacotherapy for individuals achieving >5% weight loss after 3 months, unless contraindicated or poorly tolerated.
- Use shared decision-making to determine long-term weight management strategies, including dose adjustments or intermittent therapy.
- Discontinue sulfonylureas or reduce insulin dosing (e.g., reduce bolus by 10–20%, basal ~10% if A1C <7.5%) when initiating GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs to mitigate hypoglycemia risk.
- Evaluate and adjust diabetes medications to balance achievement of weight loss goals, health benefits, and tolerability; treatment dose may be less than the maximum approved dose.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- BMI ≥30 kg/m² or ≥27 kg/m² with one or more obesity-associated comorbid conditions (e.g., type 2 diabetes, hypertension, dyslipidemia).
- Prior to initiation, evaluate patient comorbidities, treatment burden, cost, and accessibility.
- Contraindicated in pregnancy, active conception attempts, or nursing; counsel individuals of childbearing potential on contraception.
- Monitor for nutritional deficiencies, particularly vitamin D, in individuals receiving GLP-1 RAs.
- Adjust diabetes medications (e.g., sulfonylureas, insulin) to avoid hypoglycemia when initiating GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs.
- Evaluate for gallbladder disease if cholelithiasis or cholecystitis is suspected; avoid use in at-risk individuals when prescribing GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs.
- Monitor for diabetic retinopathy in individuals at high risk (older individuals and those with longer duration of type 2 diabetes [≥10 years]) when using GLP-1 RAs.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Phentermine and other older adrenergic agents approved only for short-term treatment.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized consideration of patient-specific factors.
- Discontinuation of obesity pharmacotherapy without a structured plan for weight maintenance or alternative strategies.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- GLP-1 RAs (e.g., liraglutide, semaglutide) and dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs (e.g., tirzepatide) preferred for weight loss and glycemic improvement in type 2 diabetes.
- Setmelanotide approved for rare genetic mutations causing severe hyperphagia and extreme obesity (e.g., leptin receptor deficiency).
- Semaglutide 2.4 mg and tirzepatide demonstrated significant weight loss and A1C reduction in clinical trials (STEP 2, SURMOUNT-2).
- Tirzepatide may reduce efficacy of oral hormonal contraceptives; switch to nonoral or barrier methods for 4 weeks post-initiation and post-dose escalation.
- Gradual, flexible titration of semaglutide improves medication-taking behavior and reduces adverse events without compromising efficacy.
- Setmelanotide is approved for rare genetic mutations causing severe hyperphagia and extreme obesity, such as leptin receptor deficiency and proopiomelanocortin deficiency.
- Orlistat may cause malabsorption of fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) and certain medications (e.g., cyclosporine, thyroid hormone, anticonvulsants); monitor renal function and discontinue if oxalate nephropathy occurs.


### lifestyle modification components

Improve health through optimum nutrition. Maintain glycemic control and optimum blood lipid levels. Achieve optimal physical activity and individualized meal plans. Advise behavioral changes for smoking, tobacco, and alcohol cessation. Advocate stress management and individualized diet based on complications

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** lifestyle modification components

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Improve health through optimum nutrition
- Maintain glycemic control and optimum blood lipid levels
- Achieve optimal physical activity and individualized meal plans
- Advise behavioral changes for smoking, tobacco, and alcohol cessation
- Advocate stress management and individualized diet based on complications
- Encourage consumption of high-fiber carbohydrates (e.g., whole grains, legumes, oats, barley) and low-GI fruits to manage glycemic response

**Prerequisites:**
- Nutritional assessment including metabolic, nutritional, and lifestyle parameters
- Setting practical, achievable, and individualized goals
- Nutritional intervention with education and individualized meal plans
- Evaluation of goal achievement and necessary adjustments
- Diet planning based on age, sex, physical activity, height, weight, BMI, and cultural factors
- Consider increased caloric needs during pregnancy, lactation, and recovery from illness for individualized meal planning


### exercise-based glycemic management

Exercise-based glycemic management in diabetes. Structured exercise interventions of at least 8 weeks lower A1C by 0.66% in type 2 diabetes, even without BMI change. Combining aerobic and resistance exercise may provide greater glycemic benefits than either mode alone in type 2 diabetes exercise-based glycemic management. exercise-based glycemic management in diabetes. Reduce pre-meal bolus insulin dose if exercise is anticipated within 1 hour of rapid-acting insulin or within 3 hours of regular insulin. Prefer reduced insulin dosage over carbohydrate supplementation for regular physical activities in children with obesity

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Exercise-based glycemic management in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Structured exercise interventions of at least 8 weeks lower A1C by 0.66% in type 2 diabetes, even without BMI change
- Combining aerobic and resistance exercise may provide greater glycemic benefits than either mode alone in type 2 diabetes
- Aerobic exercise reduces glycemia acutely in type 1 diabetes compared to high-intensity interval or resistance exercise
- All exercise modes (aerobic, high-intensity interval, resistance) improve 24-hour time in range in type 1 diabetes
- Daily step counts of ~7,000–10,000 steps/day may improve time in range and reduce insulin requirements in type 1 diabetes
- Regular moderate-intensity physical activity prior to and during pregnancy reduces the odds of developing gestational diabetes mellitus by nearly 40% in individuals at risk

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 2 diabetes diagnosis for structured exercise intervention recommendations
- Type 1 diabetes diagnosis with consideration of exercise type, intensity, and duration to prevent glycemic variability
- Childbearing potential or pregnancy for preconception and prenatal exercise recommendations
- Assessment of hypoglycemia risk in type 1 diabetes prior to exercise recommendations
- Tolerance assessment for moderate-intensity physical activity in pregnancy
- Assessment of tolerance for moderate-intensity physical activity in individuals of childbearing potential or during pregnancy

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Recommendations for fixed exercise prescriptions without individualized glycemic response consideration in type 1 diabetes
- Exclusive reliance on aerobic or resistance exercise alone for optimal glycemic control in type 2 diabetes
- Prescribing exercise without accounting for step count or daily activity levels in type 1 diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of combined aerobic and resistance training for enhanced glycemic benefits in type 2 diabetes
- Aerobic exercise prioritization for acute glycemic reduction in type 1 diabetes
- Step count targets (~7,000–10,000 steps/day) as adjunctive glycemic management in type 1 diabetes
- Preconception and prenatal moderate-intensity physical activity recommendations for individuals with preexisting diabetes or at risk for gestational diabetes
- Individualized exercise planning in type 1 diabetes to mitigate hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia risks
- Acute and long-term prenatal exercise positively impacts glycemia, with a larger effect noted in individuals with preexisting diabetes

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** exercise-based glycemic management in diabetes

**Core Management Principles:**
- Reduce pre-meal bolus insulin dose if exercise is anticipated within 1 hour of rapid-acting insulin or within 3 hours of regular insulin
- Prefer reduced insulin dosage over carbohydrate supplementation for regular physical activities in children with obesity
- Use carbohydrate consumption before activity to maintain blood glucose in cases of spontaneous exercise or postprandial hyperglycemia
- Set a temporary lower basal rate (50-80% reduction) in CSII users at least 90 minutes before exercise, lasting until activity stops
- Monitor blood glucose at regular intervals during unaccustomed activities and supplement carbohydrates adequately
- Consider a 30-50% reduction in basal insulin the previous day and on the day of activity for all-day or prolonged activities
- Prefer administration of foods containing fat or protein with carbohydrates for activities lasting more than 30-60 minutes

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patients must avoid exercise if hyperglycemia (random BG >250 mg/dl) with any ketonuria or ketosis (blood β-hydroxybutyrate >0.5 mmol/L) is present
- Blood ketone measurement is preferred over urine ketones for rapid detection and monitoring of ketosis
- Patients using CSII should have access to continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) with low glucose suspension for hypoglycemia prevention
- Patients must maintain adequate hydration before, during, and after exercise
- Patients should engage in 150+ minutes of moderate-to-vigorous activity weekly, spread over at least 3 days, with 2-3 resistance sessions weekly
- Adults with T1DM should engage in 150+ minutes of moderate-to-vigorous intensity activity per week spread over at least three days/week
- Adults with T1DM should engage in 2-3 sessions per week of resistance exercise on non-consecutive days


### physical activity counseling guideline

Physical activity counseling in diabetes care. Evaluate baseline physical activity and sedentary time for all people with diabetes and those at risk.. Encourage increase in physical activities above baseline to meet activity guidelines for those not meeting targets.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Physical activity counseling in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Evaluate baseline physical activity and sedentary time for all people with diabetes and those at risk.
- Encourage increase in physical activities above baseline to meet activity guidelines for those not meeting targets.
- Interrupt prolonged sitting at least every 30 minutes for blood glucose and other health benefits.
- Counsel children/adolescents with type 1 or type 2 diabetes to engage in 60+ min/day of moderate/vigorous aerobic activity, with muscle/bone strengthening at least 3 days/week.
- Counsel adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes to engage in 150+ min/week of moderate-to-vigorous aerobic activity, spread over at least 3 days, with no more than 2 consecutive inactive days.
- Counsel adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes to include flexibility and balance training 2–3 days/week, particularly for older adults or those with limited mobility.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- People with diabetes or at risk for diabetes must be evaluated for baseline physical activity and sedentary time.
- Children/adolescents with type 1 or type 2 diabetes require counseling for 60+ min/day aerobic activity.
- Adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes require counseling for 150+ min/week aerobic activity.
- Adults treated with obesity pharmacotherapy or metabolic surgery should be counseled on meeting activity recommendations, particularly muscle-strengthening exercises.
- For adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes, assess readiness to change and barriers to physical activity before counseling to tailor recommendations effectively.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Interrupting prolonged sitting at least every 30 minutes for blood glucose and other health benefits.
- Recommendation for 2–3 sessions/week of resistance exercise on nonconsecutive days for adults with type 1 or type 2 diabetes.
- Emphasis on muscle-strengthening exercises for individuals treated with obesity pharmacotherapy or metabolic surgery to maintain lean body mass.
- Recommend structured warm-up and cool-down periods for adults engaging in moderate-to-vigorous aerobic activity to reduce injury risk and improve adherence.


### resistance exercise benefits

Resistance exercise benefits in diabetes care. Adults with diabetes are encouraged to participate in 2–3 sessions/week of resistance exercise on non-consecutive days.. Resistance training of any intensity is recommended to improve strength, balance, and activities of daily living.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Resistance exercise benefits in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Adults with diabetes are encouraged to participate in 2–3 sessions/week of resistance exercise on non-consecutive days.
- Resistance training of any intensity is recommended to improve strength, balance, and activities of daily living.
- Older adults with diabetes are encouraged to participate in flexibility and balance training 2–3 times/week.
- Resistance exercise improves insulin sensitivity, glucose levels, and cardiometabolic health.
- People with diabetes should monitor blood glucose levels during and after exercise, including understanding prolonged effects based on intensity and duration.
- Resistance training can improve bone mineral density alongside glycemia, strength, and cardiometabolic health.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Pre-exercise glucose levels should be considered (<90 mg/dL may require adjustments in insulin or carbohydrate intake).
- Medication adjustments (e.g., insulin dose reduction) should be individualized based on exercise intensity, duration, and timing.
- Patients should be educated on using CGM or blood glucose monitoring during and after exercise.
- Exercise plans should account for diabetes-related complications (e.g., retinopathy, neuropathy) as outlined in relevant sections.
- Carbohydrate intake and medication adjustments around exercise should be tailored to individual glycemic responses.
- Medication adjustments around exercise should consider the use of insulin pumps or reduced pre-exercise insulin dosage as part of individualized glycemic management.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine preventive measures for hypoglycemia are not usually advised in people with diabetes not treated with insulin or insulin secretagogues.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- High-intensity resistance exercise may raise glucose levels in some individuals, especially if pre-exercise glucose is elevated.
- Use of AID (Automated Insulin Delivery) technology around exercise is recommended for better glycemic management.
- Flexibility and balance activities (e.g., yoga, tai chi) are now emphasized for older adults with diabetes to improve A1C, strength, and balance.
- Physical activity is associated with lower risk of diabetes-related complications, even at lower activity levels.
- High-intensity interval training (HIIT) may raise glucose levels in some individuals, particularly if pre-exercise glucose is elevated, and requires tailored management.


### hospital medical nutrition therapy

Provide adequate calories to meet metabolic demands. Optimize glycemic outcomes. Address personal food preferences. Facilitate creation of a discharge plan. Coordinate glucose checks, meal delivery, and nutritional insulin coverage (meal triad)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hospital medical nutrition therapy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Provide adequate calories to meet metabolic demands
- Optimize glycemic outcomes
- Address personal food preferences
- Facilitate creation of a discharge plan
- Coordinate glucose checks, meal delivery, and nutritional insulin coverage (meal triad)
- Offer carbohydrate counting support for patients using insulin pumps or multiple daily injections
- Provide education on insulin stacking risks with 'meals on demand' systems

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individualization based on treatment goals, physiological parameters, and medication use
- Controlled carbohydrate meal plans preferred for prandial insulin matching
- Carbohydrate counting available for patients who count carbohydrates at home
- Meal triad coordination to prevent hyperglycemia or hypoglycemia
- Hospital food service must support carbohydrate counting for implementation
- Real-time glucose monitoring required for patients using 'meals on demand' with insulin therapy

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed meal plans or specified macronutrient percentages
- Uncoordinated glucose monitoring, meal delivery, and insulin dosing

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Meal triad coordination concept
- Meals on demand option (with caution for insulin stacking)
- Carbohydrate counting availability for insulin pump users
- Carbohydrate counting support for insulin pump users as a standard option


### exercise and muscle preservation

Exercise and muscle preservation in diabetes/obesity management. Emphasize muscle-strengthening activities alongside physical activity guidelines for people with diabetes and obesity, especially those undergoing obesity pharmacotherapy or metabolic surgery

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Exercise and muscle preservation in diabetes/obesity management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Emphasize muscle-strengthening activities alongside physical activity guidelines for people with diabetes and obesity, especially those undergoing obesity pharmacotherapy or metabolic surgery
- Prioritize combined aerobic and resistance training over aerobic exercise alone for greater improvements in weight loss, functional capacity, muscle mass, and strength
- Engage in exercise programs before and after metabolic surgery to support muscle mass, reduce weight recurrence, and improve insulin sensitivity
- Monitor for potential inhibitory effects on cardiac function when combining exercise with GLP-1 RA therapy, though evidence is limited and emerging
- Monitor for potential inhibitory effects on left ventricular diastolic function when combining structured exercise with GLP-1 RA therapy, particularly in people with type 2 diabetes, though evidence is limited and emerging

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients undergoing obesity pharmacotherapy or metabolic surgery should be assessed for muscle mass and strength prior to initiating exercise programs
- Exercise programs should be tailored to individual functional capacity and comorbidities
- Combination therapy (e.g., GLP-1 RA + exercise) should be individualized based on patient-specific risks and benefits
- Assess cardiac function (e.g., left ventricular diastolic function) in patients with type 2 diabetes prior to initiating combined exercise and GLP-1 RA therapy, especially those with pre-existing cardiovascular conditions

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on aerobic exercise without resistance training for muscle preservation in diabetes/obesity management
- Assuming that weight loss alone (without exercise) preserves muscle mass and strength

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Combined aerobic and resistance training as a preferred strategy for muscle preservation and functional improvement in diabetes/obesity
- Pre- and post-operative exercise programs to mitigate muscle loss in metabolic surgery patients
- Synergistic benefits of structured exercise with GLP-1 RA therapy on body composition and metabolic outcomes
- Need for longer-duration studies on exercise combined with newer-generation obesity pharmacotherapies
- Need for longer-duration studies specifically evaluating the interaction between newer-generation GLP-1 RAs and structured exercise on cardiac function and body composition in diabetes/obesity management


### non-pharmacological stress interventions

Practice stress management techniques such as meditation, yoga, outdoor walks, exercise, and hobbies like reading, gardening, or painting. Engage in yoga under expert guidance for therapeutic benefits in physical and mental health. Stress management should be tailored to individual preferences and capabilities

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** non-pharmacological stress interventions

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Practice stress management techniques such as meditation, yoga, outdoor walks, exercise, and hobbies like reading, gardening, or painting
- Engage in yoga under expert guidance for therapeutic benefits in physical and mental health

**Prerequisites:**
- Stress management should be tailored to individual preferences and capabilities
- Expert guidance is required for yoga practice
- Stress management should be culturally appropriate and contextually relevant to the Indian setting


### nutrition and exercise for older adults

Nutrition and exercise for older adults with diabetes. Recommend healthful eating with adequate protein intake (at least 0.8 g/kg body weight/day) to maintain lean body mass and function. Recommend regular physical activity including aerobic, weight-bearing, and resistance training as tolerated

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Nutrition and exercise for older adults with diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Recommend healthful eating with adequate protein intake (at least 0.8 g/kg body weight/day) to maintain lean body mass and function
- Recommend regular physical activity including aerobic, weight-bearing, and resistance training as tolerated
- Tailor lifestyle management to frailty status, addressing sarcopenia, dynapenia, and osteopenia risks
- Consider intensive lifestyle interventions (dietary changes, physical activity, 5–7% weight loss) for older adults with type 2 diabetes and overweight/obesity who can exercise safely
- Focus on functional status and quality of life rather than weight loss in frail older adults
- Consider malnutrition screening (e.g., MNA-SF) and interventions for older adults with diabetes, given its association with sarcopenia, frailty, and functional decline
- Match complexity of treatment plans to self-management ability and available social/medical support (e.g., independent living, caregiver availability, facility-based care)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Older adults with diabetes and the capacity to exercise safely
- Assessment of frailty status, malnutrition risk, and functional limitations
- Individualized protein intake goals (0.8–1.5 g/kg body weight/day or 15–20% of calories)
- Structured physical activity programs (e.g., LIFE study) for frail older adults
- Shared decision-making to align goals with patient-specific factors (life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk)
- Assessment of hypoglycemia risk factors (e.g., cognitive impairment, renal impairment, polypharmacy) before selecting or deintensifying medications
- Evaluation of self-management capacity (e.g., ability to perform glucose monitoring, medication administration) and social support systems (e.g., caregiver availability, living arrangement)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults
- High-protein diets beyond general recommendations (0.8–1.5 g/kg/day) for glycemic or cardiovascular outcomes
- Intensive weight-loss interventions in frail older adults without functional capacity
- Complex treatment plans with high hypoglycemia risk (e.g., insulin, sulfonylureas) without deintensification
- One-size-fits-all dietary or exercise recommendations without tailoring to frailty or sarcopenia
- Fixed A1C targets without individualized consideration of hypoglycemia risk, life expectancy, or functional status
- Intensive glycemic management with high hypoglycemia risk (e.g., insulin, sulfonylureas) in older adults with complex health status or limited life expectancy

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Moderately higher protein intake (20–30%) for satiety and diabetes management in select older adults
- Combined protein supplementation and resistance exercise for sarcopenia management
- Deintensification of hypoglycemia-causing medications (e.g., insulin, sulfonylureas) in high-risk older adults
- Cost-of-care considerations to reduce barriers to medication adherence and self-management
- Use of the 4S pathway for assessing treatment difficulty and deintensifying therapy
- Use the 4S pathway (Fig. 13.2) for assessing treatment difficulty, reevaluating glycemic goals via shared decision-making, and deintensifying/simplifying treatments in older adults
- Consider cost-of-care and insurance coverage when selecting glucose-lowering agents to reduce barriers to medication adherence and self-management


### medication-induced weight gain management

Medication-induced weight gain management in diabetes care. Review individual’s concomitant medications to identify those that promote weight gain. Engage other care team members to minimize or provide alternatives for weight-promoting medications when clinically appropriate

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Medication-induced weight gain management in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Review individual’s concomitant medications to identify those that promote weight gain
- Engage other care team members to minimize or provide alternatives for weight-promoting medications when clinically appropriate
- Avoid or substitute medications with high weight gain potential (e.g., antipsychotics, tricyclic antidepressants, glucocorticoids, β-blockers) when possible
- Monitor weight changes closely in patients on obesogenic medications
- Consider dose-dependent impact of multiple obesogenic medications on weight loss outcomes
- Assess the patient’s baseline weight and metabolic status prior to initiating obesogenic medications to establish a reference for future monitoring

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be on medications with known weight-promoting effects
- Clinical stability and safety must allow for medication review or substitution
- Multidisciplinary care team involvement is feasible
- Patient must be monitored for weight changes and metabolic outcomes
- Prioritize alternatives that do not compromise primary treatment goals
- Documentation of the rationale for selecting or continuing weight-promoting medications when alternatives are not feasible, including patient preferences and clinical necessity

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of weight-promoting medications without consideration of alternatives
- Assuming lifestyle interventions alone can overcome medication-induced weight gain
- Ignoring the cumulative effect of multiple obesogenic medications on weight loss
- Assuming that switching to a single obesogenic medication will have negligible impact on weight management without considering cumulative effects

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Systematic review of medication lists for weight-promoting effects as part of diabetes care
- Integration of pharmacologic alternatives with lower weight gain liability
- Dose optimization or deprescribing of obesogenic medications when clinically feasible
- Patient education on medication-related weight gain risks and management strategies
- Use of weight loss benchmarks (e.g., 5% or 10%) as outcome measures in patients on obesogenic medications
- Incorporate shared decision-making tools to discuss medication-related weight gain risks and alternatives with patients before prescribing obesogenic drugs


### metabolic surgery guidelines

Metabolic surgery for type 2 diabetes management. Consider metabolic surgery for weight and glycemic management in people with type 2 diabetes with BMI ≥30.0 kg/m² (or ≥27.5 kg/m² in Asian American individuals) who are good surgical candidates.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Metabolic surgery for type 2 diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Consider metabolic surgery for weight and glycemic management in people with type 2 diabetes with BMI ≥30.0 kg/m² (or ≥27.5 kg/m² in Asian American individuals) who are good surgical candidates.
- Perform metabolic surgery in high-volume centers with interprofessional teams experienced in managing obesity, diabetes, and gastrointestinal surgery.
- Evaluate candidates for comorbid psychological conditions and social circumstances that may interfere with surgery outcomes.
- Monitor individuals post-surgery for insufficient weight loss or weight recurrence at least every 6–12 months and consider additional interventions if needed.
- Use continuous glucose monitoring to manage post-metabolic surgery hypoglycemia and adjust medications as required.
- Assess candidates for substance use disorders, severe depression, suicidal ideation, or other significant behavioral health conditions prior to metabolic surgery and postpone surgery until these are appropriately addressed.
- Provide medical nutrition therapy with a registered dietitian nutritionist experienced in post-metabolic surgery hypoglycemia for individuals with post-metabolic surgery hypoglycemia.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- BMI ≥30.0 kg/m² (or ≥27.5 kg/m² for Asian American individuals) with type 2 diabetes.
- Good surgical candidacy (e.g., absence of severe uncontrolled psychiatric conditions or substance use disorders).
- Evaluation for comorbid psychological conditions and social circumstances prior to surgery.
- Post-surgical monitoring for weight loss outcomes and glycemic control.
- High-volume surgical centers with experienced interprofessional teams.
- Assessment by a behavioral health professional with expertise in obesity management prior to consideration for metabolic surgery.
- Evaluation for substance use disorders, severe depression, suicidal ideation, or other significant behavioral health conditions as part of pre-surgical assessment.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of nutrient-stimulated hormone-based therapeutics pre- and post-surgery for chronic obesity without evidence of benefit.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets post-surgery without individualized assessment.
- Reliance solely on medical therapy for type 2 diabetes in eligible surgical candidates.
- Performing metabolic surgery in low-volume centers without specialized teams.
- Ignoring psychological and social factors in surgical candidacy assessment.
- Pre- and post-metabolic surgery use of nutrient-stimulated hormone-based therapeutics for chronic obesity without evidence of benefit (except in individuals with BMI >50 kg/m² where GLP-1 RAs may be considered for significant weight loss prior to surgery).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recommendation for metabolic surgery as a weight and glycemic management approach in eligible individuals with type 2 diabetes.
- Post-metabolic surgery hypoglycemia management with continuous glucose monitoring and medical nutrition therapy.
- Consideration of GLP-1 receptor agonists as adjuvants for weight loss post-surgery or to treat weight recurrence.
- Routine assessment of psychological conditions and social circumstances pre-surgery to optimize outcomes.
- Long-term monitoring for vitamin/mineral deficiencies, osteoporosis, and other metabolic complications post-surgery.
- Consider GLP-1 receptor agonists as adjuvants for weight loss shortly after metabolic surgery or when weight loss has plateaued.
- Consider GLP-1 receptor agonists to treat weight recurrence after metabolic surgery as an alternative to revisional surgery.


### meal replacement therapy

meal replacement therapy for diabetes care. Use of partial or total meal replacements as an additional strategy for energy restriction. Meal replacements are prepackaged foods (bars, shakes, and soups) with fixed macronutrient and micronutrient content. Improves nutrient quality, glycemic management, and reduces portion size and energy intake

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** meal replacement therapy for diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use of partial or total meal replacements as an additional strategy for energy restriction
- Meal replacements are prepackaged foods (bars, shakes, and soups) with fixed macronutrient and micronutrient content
- Improves nutrient quality, glycemic management, and reduces portion size and energy intake
- Short-term strategy (<6 months) for weight loss in landmark clinical trials (Look AHEAD, DiRECT, PREVIEW)
- Long-term follow-up and support from the diabetes care team are required to optimize self-efficacy and maintain behavioral changes
- Meal replacements may be used as part of a structured very-low-calorie diet (VLCD) or low-calorie diet (LCD) protocol for diabetes management

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient eligibility for energy restriction strategies
- Supervision or guidance from a diabetes care team member
- Consideration of patient-specific factors (e.g., comorbidities, lifestyle) for suitability
- Patient must have access to or be prescribed medically appropriate meal replacements by a healthcare provider
- Meal replacements should be integrated into a comprehensive diabetes care plan with regular monitoring of glycemic outcomes and nutritional status

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of meal replacements as a recommended strategy in diabetes care guidelines
- Evidence-based support from meta-analyses and landmark trials for weight loss and glycemic improvements
- Inclusion of meal replacements in structured weight management programs for patients with type 2 diabetes, particularly those with obesity


### prolonged sitting intervention

Break up prolonged sitting with short regular bouts of slow walking or simple resistance exercises at least every 30 minutes. Consider patient mobility limitations or contraindications before recommending resistance exercises

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Prolonged sitting intervention

**Core Recommendations:**
- Break up prolonged sitting with short regular bouts of slow walking or simple resistance exercises at least every 30 minutes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Consider patient mobility limitations or contraindications before recommending resistance exercises


### individualized exercise regimen

Regular physical activity and regulated exercise are essential components of type 2 diabetes management. Exercise program must be individualized based on patient's ability and capacity. Complete evaluation of patients with diabetes is required before recommending an exercise program

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** individualized exercise regimen

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Regular physical activity and regulated exercise are essential components of type 2 diabetes management
- Exercise program must be individualized based on patient's ability and capacity
- Complete evaluation of patients with diabetes is required before recommending an exercise program
- Consume extra quick-acting carbohydrate snack before and during prolonged exercise sessions
- Follow 150 minutes of aerobic activity or equivalent per week with resistance training at least twice weekly and flexibility exercises
- Exercise program should include flexibility exercises to maintain agility and prevent musculoskeletal complications

**Prerequisites:**
- Complete evaluation of patients with diabetes before exercise program initiation
- Individualization of exercise program based on patient's ability and capacity
- Complete evaluation of patients with diabetes must assess cardiovascular risk, musculoskeletal status, and presence of complications (e.g., neuropathy, retinopathy) before exercise program initiation


### individualized type 2 diabetes management

Treatment should be individualized due to the heterogeneous nature of type 2 diabetes. Encourage healthy lifestyle choices such as increasing physical activity and diet modification. Lifestyle changes alone may suffice for glycemic target attainment in some cases

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** individualized type 2 diabetes management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Treatment should be individualized due to the heterogeneous nature of type 2 diabetes
- Encourage healthy lifestyle choices such as increasing physical activity and diet modification
- Lifestyle changes alone may suffice for glycemic target attainment in some cases
- Most patients will require pharmacotherapy to achieve glycemic goals
- Use a suggested algorithm for selecting antihyperglycaemic agents based on class advantages and disadvantages

**Prerequisites:**
- Assess patient-specific factors to tailor treatment approach
- Evaluate patient readiness and ability to adopt lifestyle modifications
- Refer to a table of class-specific advantages and disadvantages of antihyperglycaemic agents (Figure 7.2)


### lifestyle intervention for diabetes remission

Create a 500–750 kcal/day energy deficit via reduced energy intake and increased physical activity. Achieve and maintain ≥5% weight loss to improve glycemia, lipids, and blood pressure. Engage in ≥16 intensive behavioral sessions over 6 months focusing on durable nutritional changes and physical activity

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Lifestyle intervention for diabetes remission

**Core Recommendations:**
- Create a 500–750 kcal/day energy deficit via reduced energy intake and increased physical activity
- Achieve and maintain ≥5% weight loss to improve glycemia, lipids, and blood pressure
- Engage in ≥16 intensive behavioral sessions over 6 months focusing on durable nutritional changes and physical activity
- Monitor and adjust nutrition intake to prevent micronutrient deficiencies, especially with very-low-calorie diets
- Encourage 150–300 minutes/week of physical activity for overall health and glycemic management
- Replace ultraprocessed foods with less processed alternatives to improve weight loss outcomes (e.g., trunk fat reduction)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients with type 2 diabetes and overweight/obesity eligible for intensive lifestyle intervention
- Trained professionals to deliver interventions (face-to-face or remote, individual or group)
- Medical supervision for very-low-calorie interventions (800–1,000 kcal/day)
- Assessment of motivation, life circumstances, cultural considerations, and socioeconomic factors
- Long-term (≥1 year) weight maintenance programs with monthly contact and self-monitoring
- Assess systemic, structural, and socioeconomic factors impacting food access, physical activity opportunities, and diabetes risk before initiating lifestyle interventions

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized consideration
- Reliance on nutrition supplements (herbs, vitamins, minerals, etc.) for weight loss
- Low-carbohydrate diets (>50g–150g/day) as superior to balanced carbohydrate diets for glycemic control
- Unsupervised very-low-calorie interventions without medical monitoring
- Commercial weight loss programs lacking evidence-based practices
- Reliance on nutrition supplements (e.g., herbs, vitamins, minerals, amino acids) for obesity management or weight loss due to lack of clear evidence and high risk of bias in trials

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Culturally tailored behavioral interventions to improve intervention impact
- Very-low-calorie structured eating patterns (800–1,000 kcal/day) for rapid initial weight loss under medical supervision
- Long-term comprehensive weight maintenance programs (≥1 year) with interprofessional teams
- Micronutrient screening and supplementation for individuals consuming <1,200 kcal/day or with rapid weight loss
- Emphasis on protein optimization and resistance training to preserve lean mass during weight loss
- Screen for disordered eating behaviors during diabetes self-management education, medical nutrition therapy, and routine medical visits
- Prophylactic or corrective micronutrient supplementation for individuals undergoing metabolic surgery with documented deficiencies


### Hypertriglyceridemia Medical Nutrition Therapy

Tailored medical nutrition therapy is critical for TG ≥1000 mg/dL, especially with FCS or MCS.. Eliminate alcohol, added sugars, and limit total fat to 10%-15% of calories until chylomicrons clear and TG improve.

### Source 1: 2026 ACC-AHA-AACVPR-ABC-ACPM-ADA AGS-APhA-ASPC-NLA-PCNA Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypertriglyceridemia Medical Nutrition Therapy

**Key Clinical Actions:**
- Tailored medical nutrition therapy is critical for TG ≥1000 mg/dL, especially with FCS or MCS.
- Eliminate alcohol, added sugars, and limit total fat to 10%-15% of calories until chylomicrons clear and TG improve.
- Medical nutrition therapy by an RDN is a core component for dyslipidemia and CKM syndrome management.
- Dietary advice should be individualized based on TG levels, clinical syndrome, and patient preferences.
- Greater number of RDN sessions correlates with improved risk factor optimization and long-term adherence.
- Very limited total fat intake (10%-15% of calories) should be maintained until chylomicrons are cleared and TG levels significantly improve, not just until initial improvement.

**Patient Preparation / Diagnostic Criteria:**
- TG ≥500 to 700 mg/dL saturates lipoprotein lipase, necessitating intervention to prevent chylomicronemia and pancreatitis.
- RDN assessment and counseling are required for designing specific eating plans, including potential use of medium-chain TG oil.
- Assessment of potential secondary causes of hypertriglyceridemia is required before initiating medical nutrition therapy.


### yoga-based adjunct therapy

yoga-based adjunct therapy for type 2 diabetes. Incorporate yoga as a complementary therapy in holistic diabetes management. Use asanas (postures), pranayama (breath control), and dhyana (meditation) as core components. Aim to improve glycemic control, lipid profiles, and body fat content. Target reduction in oxidative stress, blood pressure, and medication doses

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** yoga-based adjunct therapy for type 2 diabetes

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Incorporate yoga as a complementary therapy in holistic diabetes management
- Use asanas (postures), pranayama (breath control), and dhyana (meditation) as core components
- Aim to improve glycemic control, lipid profiles, and body fat content
- Target reduction in oxidative stress, blood pressure, and medication doses
- Enhance pulmonary and autonomic function, mood, sleep, and quality of life

**Prerequisites:**
- Integrate yoga as an adjunct to standard diabetes care, not as a replacement
- Ensure patient suitability and safety before initiating yoga-based interventions
- Clinicians should assess patient suitability and safety before initiating yoga-based interventions, considering individual health status and comorbidities



## Diabetes Care in Pregnancy and Preconception
