This collection of guidelines and frameworks focuses on optimizing diabetes care before, during, and after pregnancy to minimize maternal and fetal complications. It emphasizes preconception planning, strict glycemic control, risk stratification, and tailored postpartum management.

**Key Features:**
- Preconception counseling and glycemic optimization for individuals with diabetes of childbearing potential
- Strict glycemic targets in pregnancy (e.g., HbA1c < 6.5% for pre-gestational diabetes)
- Universal gestational diabetes screening (IADPSG criteria) at first antenatal visit and 24–28 weeks
- Insulin therapy as standard of care for T1DM, T2DM, and GDM with frequent titration
- Hypoglycemia risk management and education, especially high in early pregnancy for T1DM
- Postpartum diabetes screening protocol (75-g OGTT at 4–12 weeks) to detect persistent diabetes or prediabetes
- Telehealth integration to support remote monitoring and education in underserved regions
- Postpartum insulin sensitivity changes requiring rapid dose adjustments (30–40% reduction)

**Notable Details:**
- Pre-pregnancy HbA1c target: <7% (or <6.5% if safely achievable)
- Blood glucose targets: fasting 80–110 mg/dL, pre-meals 72–126 mg/dL, 1-hr post-meals 100–155 mg/dL
- Severe hypoglycemia occurs 3–5 times more frequently in the first half of pregnancy in T1DM
- Low-dose aspirin prophylaxis recommended for preeclampsia in high-risk patients with pre-gestational diabetes
- Insulin requirements drop by ~34% immediately postpartum due to placental delivery
- GDM diagnosis uses either one-step 75-g OGTT (IADPSG) or two-step approach (50-g screen + 100-g OGTT)
- Postpartum OGTT preferred over A1C at 4–12 weeks due to pregnancy-related interference
- Universal GDM screening recommended in India at first antenatal visit and 24–28 weeks

**Topics in this group:**

### pregnancy management in T1DM

Achieve near normoglycemia before and during pregnancy with pre-pregnancy HbA1c < 7% (or < 6.5% if safely achievable) to reduce adverse outcomes.. Monitor blood glucose 6-8 times daily with individualized targets: fasting 80-110 mg/dL, pre-meals 72-126 mg/dL, and 1-hr post-meals 100-155 mg/dL.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pregnancy management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Achieve near normoglycemia before and during pregnancy with pre-pregnancy HbA1c < 7% (or < 6.5% if safely achievable) to reduce adverse outcomes.
- Monitor blood glucose 6-8 times daily with individualized targets: fasting 80-110 mg/dL, pre-meals 72-126 mg/dL, and 1-hr post-meals 100-155 mg/dL.
- Educate patients on insulin storage/technique, self-adjustment, sick day rules, ketone testing, and hypoglycemia management.
- Conduct monthly HbA1c monitoring (not as a replacement for self-monitoring) and assess fetal growth via scans at 28 weeks and 4-weekly intervals.
- Review teratogenic drugs (e.g., ACE inhibitors, statins), optimize thyroid status, and manage comorbidities (nephropathy, retinopathy, hypertension).
- Review and adjust insulin requirements throughout pregnancy, as insulin needs typically increase in the first trimester and may double or triple by the third trimester due to hormonal changes and insulin resistance.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Planned pregnancy with 6 months of preconception glycemic optimization to achieve near-normal HbA1c.
- Preconception counseling to inform women about risks and the importance of glycemic control.
- Monthly HbA1c monitoring during preconception and pregnancy.
- Fetal surveillance via non-stress tests and amniotic fluid volume assessment based on institutional protocols.
- Psychological assessment and referral to mental health professionals if needed during preconception to postpartum care.
- Assess and manage thyroid status (TSH levels) as part of preconception care, given the higher prevalence of thyroid dysfunction in women with T1DM.


### preconception diabetes care framework

Incorporate preconception counseling into routine diabetes care starting at puberty for individuals with diabetes and childbearing potential.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Preconception diabetes care framework

**Core Recommendations:**
- Incorporate preconception counseling into routine diabetes care starting at puberty for individuals with diabetes and childbearing potential.
- Discuss family planning and prescribe effective contraception (including long-acting reversible contraception) until glycemic goals (A1C <6.5%) and treatment plans are optimized for pregnancy.
- Achieve preconception glycemic goals (A1C <6.5%, fasting glucose 80–110 mg/dL, 2-hour postprandial <155 mg/dL) without excessive hypoglycemia, using therapies approved for pregnancy.
- Discontinue GLP-1 receptor agonists and dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists prior to pregnancy and achieve glycemic goals with insulin therapy before attempting conception.
- Provide interprofessional preconception care including endocrinology, maternal-fetal medicine, dietitians, and diabetes educators, with comprehensive screening for comorbidities and complications.
- Incorporate weight management counseling and lifestyle interventions (including referral to a registered dietitian nutritionist) into preconception care, with specific guidance on achieving ≥5% weight loss prior to pregnancy for individuals with overweight/obesity and a history of GDM.
- Include comprehensive nutrition assessment and recommendations (e.g., meal planning, correction of nutritional deficiencies, caffeine intake, safe food preparation) as part of preconception education.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must have diabetes (type 1, type 2, or history of GDM) and childbearing potential.
- Glycemic goals (A1C <6.5%) must be achieved prior to conception using pregnancy-approved therapies.
- Contraception must be used until glycemic goals are met and pregnancy is planned.
- Medication review and adjustments are required to avoid teratogenic drugs (e.g., ACE inhibitors, statins) before pregnancy.
- Comprehensive preconception evaluation including eye exams, renal function, lipid panel, and infectious disease screening must be completed.
- Individuals with prediabetes or a history of GDM must undergo preconception screening for diabetes or prediabetes prior to conception; if not screened preconception, testing should occur before 15 weeks of gestation.
- If type 2 diabetes is diagnosed preconception, initiate treatment with a goal to achieve and maintain an A1C of <6.5% (<48 mmol/mol) prior to conception using therapies approved for use in pregnancy.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of GLP-1 receptor agonists or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists during pregnancy.
- Reliance on estimated A1C or glucose management indicator calculations during pregnancy.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of hypoglycemia risk in older adults or pregnancy.
- Postpartum OGTT replacement with A1C alone in all cases without clinical justification.
- Unplanned pregnancy management without prior preconception optimization.
- Use of estimated A1C or glucose management indicator calculations during pregnancy (explicitly stated as not to be used in pregnancy).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Updated preconception glucose goals (fasting 80–110 mg/dL, 2-hour postprandial <155 mg/dL) to guide therapeutic adjustments.
- Recommendation to discontinue GLP-1 RA and dual GIP/GLP-1 RA therapy prior to pregnancy with specific timing guidance (e.g., semaglutide 2 months prior).
- Consolidation of CGM and AID use guidance in pregnancy into a single section for clarity.
- Blood pressure threshold of 140/90 mmHg for initiation or titration of antihypertensive therapy in pregnancy.
- Consideration of A1C at 6–12 months postpartum as supplementary diagnostic information only when OGTT is declined or intolerable.
- Recommendation to achieve ≥5% weight loss prior to pregnancy for individuals with overweight/obesity and a history of GDM, as it is associated with lower risk of GDM recurrence (OR 0.18, 95% CI 0.04–0.88).


### pregnancy diabetes management

Implement preconception counseling and glycemic optimization before pregnancy. Maintain strict glycemic control throughout pregnancy to reduce maternal and fetal complications. Screen for and manage preeclampsia with low-dose aspirin prophylaxis in high-risk patients pregnancy diabetes management. Pre-gestational diabetes management in pregnancy. Achieve tight glycemic control (preferably HbA1C < 6.5%) before planned pregnancy. Provide folic acid supplementation. Change antihypertensive agents to calcium channel blockers or alpha methyldopa. Discontinue ACEIs, ARBs, diuretics, and statins. Conduct pre-pregnancy baseline evaluation of eyes and kidney function

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pregnancy diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Implement preconception counseling and glycemic optimization before pregnancy
- Maintain strict glycemic control throughout pregnancy to reduce maternal and fetal complications
- Screen for and manage preeclampsia with low-dose aspirin prophylaxis in high-risk patients
- Monitor and adjust non-glucose-lowering medications for safety during pregnancy
- Provide structured postpartum care including glucose tolerance testing and contraception counseling
- Initiate preconception folic acid supplementation (400–800 mcg/day) at least 1–3 months before conception in patients with pregestational diabetes to reduce neural tube defect risk
- Provide patient education on hypoglycemia recognition and management during pregnancy, including glucagon administration training for caregivers

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Preconception glycemic control (A1C <7% ideally, <6.5% if safely achievable)
- Diagnosis of pregestational or gestational diabetes based on standard criteria
- Baseline assessment including retinal and renal evaluations
- Multidisciplinary care team including obstetrics, endocrinology, and nutrition
- Regular fetal monitoring with growth ultrasounds and non-stress tests
- Preconception thyroid function testing (TSH) in patients with type 1 diabetes due to increased risk of thyroid dysfunction
- Baseline electrocardiogram (ECG) in patients with pregestational diabetes and cardiovascular risk factors before considering metformin use

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of ACE inhibitors or ARBs for hypertension in pregnancy
- Routine use of oral hypoglycemic agents (e.g., sulfonylureas) in first trimester
- Postpartum glucose screening without prior risk stratification
- Universal bed rest for gestational diabetes without medical indication
- Routine use of high-dose vitamin supplementation beyond standard prenatal recommendations
- Routine use of thiazolidinediones (e.g., pioglitazone) for glycemic control in pregnancy due to limited safety data and potential adverse fetal effects
- Use of statins for primary prevention of cardiovascular disease in pregnant patients with diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Early initiation of low-dose aspirin (81 mg/day) from 12 to 36 weeks for preeclampsia prevention in high-risk patients
- Use of metformin as an adjunct to insulin in gestational diabetes if glycemic targets are not met with lifestyle alone
- Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) for real-time glycemic trend monitoring in pregnancy
- Postpartum 75g OGTT at 4-12 weeks for all gestational diabetes patients
- Immediate postpartum contraception counseling and initiation for patients with diabetes
- Consider SGLT2 inhibitors (e.g., empagliflozin) in select patients with type 2 diabetes preconception or early pregnancy if benefits outweigh risks, under specialist supervision
- Use of fetal echocardiography at 18–22 weeks in patients with pregestational diabetes and poor glycemic control (A1C ≥8%) to assess for congenital heart defects

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pre-gestational diabetes management in pregnancy

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Achieve tight glycemic control (preferably HbA1C < 6.5%) before planned pregnancy
- Provide folic acid supplementation
- Change antihypertensive agents to calcium channel blockers or alpha methyldopa
- Discontinue ACEIs, ARBs, diuretics, and statins
- Conduct pre-pregnancy baseline evaluation of eyes and kidney function

**Prerequisites:**
- Planned pregnancy
- Preconception checkup completed
- Patient has pre-existing type 1 or type 2 diabetes


### gestational diabetes screening

Gestational diabetes screening and early pregnancy hyperglycemia management. Screen individuals planning pregnancy with risk factors for undiagnosed prediabetes or diabetes.. Test all individuals of childbearing potential for undiagnosed prediabetes or diabetes before 15 weeks of gestation if not screened preconception.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Gestational diabetes screening and early pregnancy hyperglycemia management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen individuals planning pregnancy with risk factors for undiagnosed prediabetes or diabetes.
- Test all individuals of childbearing potential for undiagnosed prediabetes or diabetes before 15 weeks of gestation if not screened preconception.
- Screen for abnormal glucose metabolism (A1C 5.9–6.4% or FPG 110–125 mg/dL) before 15 weeks of gestation to identify high-risk individuals.
- Screen for GDM at 24–28 weeks of gestation in pregnant individuals not previously diagnosed with diabetes or high-risk abnormal glucose metabolism.
- Screen individuals with GDM for prediabetes or diabetes at 4–12 weeks postpartum using 75-g OGTT.
- Monitor fasting and postprandial glucose levels 3–4 times per week in early pregnancy if treatment is initiated for abnormal glucose metabolism (fasting glucose ≥110 mg/dL before 15 weeks).
- Use individualized glycemic goals for pregnant individuals with type 2 diabetes treated with nutrition alone (less stringent goals may apply if hypoglycemia risk is significant).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Risk factors for screening include obesity, dyslipidemia, hypertension, or family history of diabetes.
- Standard diagnostic criteria must be used for testing before 15 weeks of gestation.
- GDM screening at 24–28 weeks applies to pregnant individuals without prior diabetes diagnosis.
- Postpartum screening requires 75-g OGTT and non-pregnancy diagnostic criteria.
- Shared decision-making is recommended for treatment of early abnormal glucose metabolism (fasting glucose ≥110 mg/dL before 15 weeks).
- Exclude individuals with fasting glucose ≥110 mg/dL (≥6.1 mmol/L) from early treatment RCTs due to safety concerns, limiting evidence for this subgroup.
- Consider regression of GDM when retested at 24–28 weeks' gestation; outcomes among those with regression are similar to individuals without GDM, reducing potential benefit of early treatment.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Universal treatment of early pregnancy hyperglycemia less than overt diabetes without risk stratification.
- Routine use of less stringent glucose goals without consideration of hypoglycemia risk.
- Assumption that all early abnormal glucose metabolism will persist or require treatment.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consider testing all individuals of childbearing potential for undiagnosed prediabetes or diabetes.
- Screen for abnormal glucose metabolism (A1C 5.9–6.4% or FPG 110–125 mg/dL) before 15 weeks of gestation to assess risk.
- Use shared decision-making for treatment of early abnormal glucose metabolism (fasting glucose ≥110 mg/dL before 15 weeks).
- Monitor fasting and postprandial glucose levels in early pregnancy if treatment is initiated.
- Postpartum screening for prediabetes or diabetes at 4–12 weeks using 75-g OGTT.
- Aim for less stringent glycemic goals in pregnancy if standard goals cannot be achieved without significant hypoglycemia, based on clinical experience and individualization of care.
- Postprandial glucose monitoring is associated with better glycemic outcomes and lower risk of preeclampsia in pregnant individuals with diabetes.


### glycemic control targets

Glycemic control targets + Time in Range + Hypoglycemia risk management. Glycemic targets are individualized based on patient-specific factors including life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk, and comorbidities. glycemic control targets. Glycemic control targets. Glycemic targets should be individualized based on patient-specific factors such as age, comorbidities, and hypoglycemia risk.. A1C targets are typically <7% for most adults with diabetes, but may be adjusted for specific populations.. Glycemic targets during pregnancy aim for tighter control to reduce maternal and fetal risks.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glycemic control targets + Time in Range + Hypoglycemia risk management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Glycemic targets are individualized based on patient-specific factors including life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk, and comorbidities.
- Time in Range (TIR) is emphasized as a complementary metric to HbA1c for assessing glycemic control, with targets of ≥70% in 70–180 mg/dL range for most adults.
- Severe hypoglycemia prevention is prioritized, particularly in older adults, those with impaired awareness, and patients with CKD or cardiovascular disease.
- Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) is recommended for real-time glycemic monitoring, hypoglycemia detection, and treatment adjustments.
- Structured education (e.g., DAFNE, BGAT) and hypoglycemia awareness training are integrated into care plans for high-risk patients.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., age, comorbidities, hypoglycemia risk) must be assessed before setting glycemic targets.
- HbA1c should be measured using NGSP-certified assays, with consideration of hemoglobinopathies or conditions affecting HbA1c accuracy.
- CGM or frequent self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) is required for patients on insulin or with hypoglycemia unawareness.
- Multidisciplinary care (endocrinology, nephrology, cardiology) is recommended for patients with CKD, cardiovascular disease, or complex diabetes.
- Sick-day management protocols must be in place for patients at risk of hyperglycemic crises (e.g., DKA, HHS).

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric HbA1c targets (e.g., <7% for all patients) without individualized adjustments.
- Reliance solely on HbA1c for glycemic assessment without considering TIR or glycemic variability.
- Use of sulfonylureas in older adults or patients with CKD due to high hypoglycemia risk.
- Discontinuation of insulin therapy in type 1 diabetes based on HbA1c alone.
- Routine use of urine ketone testing without blood ketone monitoring in sick-day management.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adoption of TIR (70–180 mg/dL) as a primary glycemic metric alongside HbA1c, with targets of ≥70% for most adults.
- Integration of CGM data into clinical decision-making for insulin dosing and hypoglycemia prevention.
- Risk stratification tools (e.g., hypoglycemia prediction models) for identifying high-risk patients.
- Nasal glucagon and ready-to-use liquid glucagon formulations for emergency hypoglycemia treatment.
- SGLT2 inhibitor protocols with ketone monitoring to mitigate DKA risk in type 1 diabetes.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glycemic control targets

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Glycemic targets should be individualized based on patient-specific factors such as age, comorbidities, and hypoglycemia risk.
- A1C targets are typically <7% for most adults with diabetes, but may be adjusted for specific populations.
- Glycemic targets during pregnancy aim for tighter control to reduce maternal and fetal risks.

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk) must be assessed before setting targets.
- Pregnancy requires preconception counseling and stricter glycemic targets to minimize complications.


### gestational diabetes mellitus definition

GDM definition and diagnostic strategy. GDM is defined as any degree of glucose intolerance first recognized during pregnancy, though this includes preexisting undiagnosed diabetes.. Diagnosis can be accomplished using either the one-step 75-g OGTT (IADPSG) or the two-step approach (50-g screen followed by 100-g OGTT).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GDM definition and diagnostic strategy

**Core Recommendations:**
- GDM is defined as any degree of glucose intolerance first recognized during pregnancy, though this includes preexisting undiagnosed diabetes.
- Diagnosis can be accomplished using either the one-step 75-g OGTT (IADPSG) or the two-step approach (50-g screen followed by 100-g OGTT).
- The one-step strategy identifies more cases of GDM and is based on pregnancy outcomes, while the two-step approach is supported by ACOG and the NIH consensus panel.
- Universal early screening at <15 weeks of gestation for undiagnosed diabetes is considered over selective screening in high-risk populations.
- Individuals diagnosed with GDM should receive lifelong screening for prediabetes and type 2 diabetes after delivery.
- Early abnormal glucose metabolism in pregnancy is defined as fasting glucose ≥110 mg/dL (6.1 mmol/L) or A1C ≥5.9% (41 mmol/mol), which identifies individuals at higher risk of adverse pregnancy outcomes and later GDM diagnosis.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must not have been previously diagnosed with diabetes prior to the screening period.
- For the one-step strategy, the 75-g OGTT must be performed at 24–28 weeks of gestation after an overnight fast of at least 8 hours.
- For the two-step strategy, the initial 50-g GLT is nonfasting and performed at 24–28 weeks of gestation.
- The 100-g OGTT in the two-step approach must be performed when the individual is fasting.
- Diagnostic criteria for undiagnosed diabetes in early pregnancy are the same as those used in nonpregnant individuals.
- The 75-g OGTT for the one-step strategy must be performed in the morning after an overnight fast of at least 8 hours.
- The 100-g OGTT for the two-step strategy must be performed when the individual is fasting.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- The definition of GDM as any degree of glucose intolerance regardless of hyperglycemia severity without distinguishing preexisting diabetes.
- Selective screening approaches in favor of universal early screening for undiagnosed diabetes in high-risk populations.
- Use of A1C at 24–28 weeks of gestation as a primary screening test for GDM due to inferior performance compared to GLT.
- The older O'Sullivan and Mahan criteria without conversion to Carpenter-Coustan or National Diabetes Data Group thresholds.
- Relying solely on two-step diagnostic criteria without considering the potential benefits of the one-step IADPSG criteria for identifying long-term risks.
- Use of A1C thresholds of 5.7% (39 mmol/mol) for early pregnancy risk stratification due to inconsistent association with adverse perinatal outcomes.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adoption of the IADPSG one-step 75-g OGTT criteria internationally as the preferred approach for GDM diagnosis.
- Recommendation for universal early screening at <15 weeks of gestation to identify undiagnosed diabetes in high-risk populations.
- Use of A1C thresholds of 5.9% (41 mmol/mol) or fasting glucose of 110 mg/dL (6.1 mmol/L) in early pregnancy to identify individuals at higher risk of adverse outcomes.
- Implementation of lifelong screening for prediabetes and type 2 diabetes for individuals diagnosed with GDM postpartum.
- Recognition that the one-step strategy identifies long-term risks of maternal prediabetes, diabetes, and offspring glucose intolerance and adiposity.
- Adoption of the IADPSG one-step 75-g OGTT criteria as the preferred international approach for GDM diagnosis based on pregnancy outcome data from the HAPO study.
- Recommendation for universal early screening at <15 weeks of gestation to identify undiagnosed diabetes in high-risk populations, addressing racial and ethnic disparities in undiagnosed diabetes prevalence.


### insulin therapy in pregnancy

Insulin is recommended for type 1 diabetes in pregnancy and preferred for type 2 diabetes and GDM management.. Frequent titration of insulin is required due to changing physiological demands during pregnancy.. Daily and frequent glucose monitoring is emphasized to adjust insulin doses. insulin therapy in pregnancy. insulin therapy in pregnancy. Multiple subcutaneous insulin injections (MSII) are standard of care to achieve glycemic control in pregnancy. Insulin pump therapy can be offered if glycemic control is difficult with MSII due to significant hypoglycemia

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy in pregnancy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin is recommended for type 1 diabetes in pregnancy and preferred for type 2 diabetes and GDM management.
- Frequent titration of insulin is required due to changing physiological demands during pregnancy.
- Daily and frequent glucose monitoring is emphasized to adjust insulin doses.
- Insulin requirements typically increase by ~5% per week from 16 weeks to 36 weeks, often doubling prepregnancy doses.
- Bolus insulin requirements increase proportionally more than basal insulin as pregnancy progresses.
- Insulin requirements may decrease in early pregnancy for individuals with type 1 diabetes, increasing hypoglycemia risk.
- Insulin requirement levels off toward the end of the third trimester.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 1 diabetes in pregnancy requires insulin therapy.
- Type 2 diabetes or GDM management may require insulin if glycemic goals are not met with lifestyle modifications.
- Interprofessional team management with relevant expertise is recommended for insulin therapy.
- Concentrated insulin formulations may be necessary for achieving glycemic goals in type 2 diabetes.
- Assessment for automated insulin delivery (AID) systems should include glycemic levels, hypoglycemia risk, technology comfort, and psychosocial factors.
- Insulin management should be performed with an interprofessional team with relevant expertise.
- Basal insulin options include insulin glargine, NPH insulin, and insulin degludec; insulin detemir is no longer available.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Insulin detemir (removed from market by manufacturer).
- Glyburide and metformin as first-line treatments for GDM due to placental crossing and safety concerns.
- Use of insulin preparations without demonstrated safety in RCTs (prefer those with RCT evidence).
- Fixed AID systems without pregnancy-specific glucose targets or algorithms (unless used with assistive techniques and expert guidance).
- Insulin detemir (removed from market by manufacturer and no longer available).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Insulin glargine (long-acting) and insulin degludec (ultralong-acting) are acceptable basal insulin options in pregnancy.
- Insulin aspart and insulin lispro (rapid-acting) are widely considered safe and effective for use during pregnancy.
- Faster-acting insulin aspart (faster aspart) may improve time-in-range and reduce hypoglycemia in pregnancy.
- Automated insulin delivery (AID) systems with pregnancy-specific glucose targets (e.g., Ai-DAPT trial) show improved TIR and lower A1C.
- AID systems without pregnancy-specific targets may be used in selected individuals with expert guidance and assistive techniques.
- Insulin degludec was shown in an RCT to be noninferior to detemir, though its prolonged duration of action (at least 42 hours) may present challenges in pregnancy due to slower dose titration requirements (3–4 days between changes).
- Faster-acting insulin aspart (faster aspart) may improve time-in-range and reduce hypoglycemia in pregnancy, with secondary analysis showing higher TIR and less severe hypoglycemia compared to insulin aspart in type 1 diabetes.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy in pregnancy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Multiple subcutaneous insulin injections (MSII) are standard of care to achieve glycemic control in pregnancy
- Insulin pump therapy can be offered if glycemic control is difficult with MSII due to significant hypoglycemia
- Rapid-acting insulin analogs (aspart, lispro) and long-acting insulin analogs (detemir) are preferred over conventional insulins
- Insulin requirements vary throughout pregnancy, increasing in the first 9 weeks, decreasing from 9-16 weeks, then increasing until the 37th week
- Insulin requirements may increase by 2-3 times with progression in pregnancy, shifting towards more prandial insulin

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Glycemic control is difficult to achieve with multiple subcutaneous insulin injections (MSII)
- Significant hypoglycemia risk is present
- Pregnancy progression (gestational age) must be considered for insulin dosing adjustments
- Glucocorticoid treatment for premature fetal lung maturation may require 40-50% higher insulin doses for up to 1 week


### hypoglycemia risk in pregnancy

Hypoglycemia risk in pregnancy + DKA risk and retinopathy management. Educate pregnant individuals with diabetes and family members on hypoglycemia prevention, recognition, and treatment before, during, and after pregnancy. hypoglycemia risk in pregnancy. hypoglycemia risk in pregnancy. Severe hypoglycemia is 3 to 5 times more frequent in the first half of pregnancy than before conception.. Up to 45% of women with T1DM experience severe hypoglycemia during pregnancy.. A history of severe hypoglycemia in the year preceding pregnancy and impaired hypoglycemia awareness are significant risk factors.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypoglycemia risk in pregnancy + DKA risk and retinopathy management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Educate pregnant individuals with diabetes and family members on hypoglycemia prevention, recognition, and treatment before, during, and after pregnancy.
- Advise pregnant individuals with type 1 diabetes to obtain ketone test strips and receive education on DKA prevention and detection due to increased ketogenic state and lower blood glucose thresholds for DKA.
- Implement dilated eye examinations before pregnancy, each trimester, and for 1 year postpartum in individuals with preexisting diabetes to monitor and manage retinopathy risk.
- Stop potentially harmful medications (e.g., ACE inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers, mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists) prior to conception or as soon as possible in the first trimester in individuals of childbearing potential.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Pregnant individuals with type 1 or type 2 diabetes must be assessed for hypoglycemia risk, particularly in the first trimester and postpartum.
- Individuals with preexisting diabetes require baseline dilated eye examination prior to pregnancy.
- Pregnant individuals with diabetes and chronic hypertension require blood pressure monitoring and management with thresholds <140/90 mmHg for therapy initiation.
- Individuals with diabetes and chronic hypertension require blood pressure monitoring throughout gestation to screen for hypertensive disorders of pregnancy.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets during pregnancy without consideration of hypoglycemia risk.
- Use of ACE inhibitors or angiotensin receptor blockers during pregnancy due to fetal risks.
- Lipid-lowering medications (e.g., statins) routinely continued during pregnancy without shared decision-making.
- Use of atenolol for hypertension management during pregnancy due to safety concerns.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Prescribe low-dose aspirin (100–150 mg/day) starting at 12–16 weeks of gestation in pregnant individuals with type 1 or type 2 diabetes to reduce preeclampsia risk.
- Recommend intensive lifestyle interventions and/or metformin for individuals with a history of GDM, overweight/obesity, and prediabetes to prevent type 2 diabetes.
- Screen individuals with recent GDM at 4–12 weeks postpartum using a 75-g oral glucose tolerance test.
- Encourage breastfeeding for all individuals with diabetes, including those with a history of GDM, for maternal and infant health benefits.
- Stop lipid-lowering medications (e.g., statins, bempedoic acid, PCSK9 therapies, fibrates) prior to pregnancy or at the first pregnancy visit, except in high-risk cases (e.g., familial hypercholesterolemia, severe hypertriglyceridemia, prior atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease) where shared decision-making is required.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hypoglycemia risk in pregnancy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Severe hypoglycemia is 3 to 5 times more frequent in the first half of pregnancy than before conception.
- Up to 45% of women with T1DM experience severe hypoglycemia during pregnancy.
- A history of severe hypoglycemia in the year preceding pregnancy and impaired hypoglycemia awareness are significant risk factors.
- Impaired hypoglycemia awareness triples the risk of severe hypoglycemia.
- Intensive insulin treatment, lower HbA1c in early pregnancy, and fluctuating plasma glucose increase hypoglycemia risk.
- 60% of severe hypoglycemic episodes are accounted for by only 10% of pregnant women with T1DM
- Severe hypoglycemia incidence decreases after the first trimester

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Assess hypoglycemia awareness using a simple question like 'Do you recognize symptoms when you have hypoglycemia?'
- Evaluate history of severe hypoglycemia in the year preceding pregnancy.
- Monitor for fluctuating plasma glucose values (<70 to >180 mg/dl).
- Consider longer duration of diabetes as a risk factor.
- Individualize glycemic targets to balance near normoglycemia with hypoglycemia risk.


### telehealth integration in diabetes care

Telehealth should complement, not replace, in-person visits for optimal glycemic management.. Telehealth uses digital tools (video conferencing, mobile apps, remote monitoring) to deliver clinical care, education, and administrative support. telehealth integration in diabetes care. Telehealth integration in diabetes care for remote and underserved regions. Telehealth enables access to diabetes care experts in remote areas where healthcare providers are scarce.. Mobile phone penetration and low-cost digital tools facilitate remote patient-provider interactions.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Telehealth integration in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Telehealth should complement, not replace, in-person visits for optimal glycemic management.
- Telehealth uses digital tools (video conferencing, mobile apps, remote monitoring) to deliver clinical care, education, and administrative support.
- Interactive strategies (web portals, text messaging, medication adjustment) improve glycemic outcomes.
- Telehealth reduces geographic and transportation barriers for underresourced populations and individuals with disabilities.
- DSMES delivered via mHealth shows modest A1C benefits compared to usual care.
- Telemedicine (subset of telehealth) focuses specifically on remote clinical care, supporting diagnosis, treatment, and consultations through real-time communication.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Infrastructure investments (laptops, mobile access, internet) and targeted support (digital literacy education, community health workers) are necessary for success.
- Telehealth design must consider accessibility, affordability, connectivity, trust, and contextual tailoring.
- Curricula should be tailored to demographic characteristics, diabetes knowledge, emotional support, and cultural beliefs.
- DSMES must integrate clinical content, behavioral strategies (goal setting, problem-solving), and engagement with psychosocial concerns.
- Patient populations with limited physical access to healthcare (e.g., rural, disabled) benefit most from telehealth.
- High-quality DSMES must integrate clinical content, behavioral strategies (goal setting, problem-solving), and engagement with psychosocial concerns per national standards.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on in-person visits without telehealth integration for glycemic management.
- One-size-fits-all DSMES without addressing digital divide barriers.
- Telehealth interventions that do not incorporate medication adjustment or interactive communication.
- DSMES programs that fail to tailor content to cultural beliefs or emotional support needs.
- Telehealth strategies that ignore infrastructure and digital literacy requirements.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Telehealth as a complementary tool to in-person care for glycemic management.
- mHealth-based DSMES with modest A1C benefits (DSME: –0.3%, DSMS: –0.4%, DSMES: –0.2%).
- Targeted telehealth support for marginalized populations (e.g., Black and Hispanic adults with type 2 diabetes: A1C reduction of –0.47%).
- Telehealth for pediatric populations (type 1 diabetes) and young adults addressing SDOH.
- Systematic integration of digital literacy education and community health workers in telehealth programs.
- Telehealth interventions for type 1 diabetes in children and adolescents show effectiveness in glycemic management.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Telehealth integration in diabetes care for remote and underserved regions

**Core Management Principles:**
- Telehealth enables access to diabetes care experts in remote areas where healthcare providers are scarce.
- Mobile phone penetration and low-cost digital tools facilitate remote patient-provider interactions.
- Government legalization of telemedicine (e.g., in India) supports broader adoption during crises like COVID-19.
- Digital tools improve care delivery in regions with limited access to specialized diabetes management.
- Cold chain breaches for insulin in remote areas lead to variability in glycemic control, requiring urgent attention.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Availability of mobile phone infrastructure in remote areas.
- Legal and regulatory frameworks supporting telemedicine (e.g., government approval).
- Access to digital tools (e.g., smartphones, internet) for patients and providers.
- Training for healthcare providers in telehealth-based diabetes management.
- Ensuring insulin cold chain integrity in remote storage and transport.


### GDM universal screening

GDM universal screening and management. Ideally all pregnant women in India should be screened for GDM via universal screening. Screening is ideally done at the first antenatal visit to rule out pre-existing diabetes. GDM screening at second trimester (24-28 weeks) uses OGTT with IADPSG criteria

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GDM universal screening and management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Ideally all pregnant women in India should be screened for GDM via universal screening
- Screening is ideally done at the first antenatal visit to rule out pre-existing diabetes
- GDM screening at second trimester (24-28 weeks) uses OGTT with IADPSG criteria
- Lifestyle modification is the initial management option for women with gestational diabetes
- All women with GDM should undergo an OGTT 6 weeks after delivery to reclassify glycemic status
- Screening at the first trimester uses single-point tests (fasting glucose, random glucose, or HbA1C) specifically to rule out pre-existing diabetes, with thresholds indicating pre-existing diabetes (≥126 mg/dl fasting, ≥200 mg/dl random, or ≥6.5% HbA1C)

**Prerequisites:**
- Use OGTT with 75 gm glucose load using IADPSG criteria (Fasting ≥92, 1hr ≥180, 2hr ≥153 mg/dl)
- If fasting state is not possible, use DIPSI guidelines with non-fasting 2hr post glucose ≥140 mg/dl
- Oral antihyperglycemic agents are best avoided in management
- Most patients will need insulin therapy
- Post-delivery reclassification requires OGTT at 6 weeks
- A fasting plasma glucose between 92-125 mg/dl at the first trimester screening is considered as GDM according to IADPSG criteria


### postpartum diabetes screening protocol

Postpartum diabetes screening protocol for individuals with GDM. Test for persistent diabetes or prediabetes at 4–12 weeks postpartum using a fasting 75-g OGTT with nonpregnancy criteria.. Use OGTT over A1C at 4–12 weeks postpartum due to potential interference from pregnancy-related factors. postpartum diabetes screening protocol. Postpartum diabetes screening protocol for GDM. Perform a 2-hour OGTT with 75g glucose 6-8 weeks postpartum for reclassification of diabetes status. Repeat annual screening if initial postpartum OGTT is normal. Annual postpartum screening if initial OGTT is normal. History of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM). Postpartum period (6-8 weeks after delivery)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Postpartum diabetes screening protocol for individuals with GDM

**Core Recommendations:**
- Test for persistent diabetes or prediabetes at 4–12 weeks postpartum using a fasting 75-g OGTT with nonpregnancy criteria.
- Use OGTT over A1C at 4–12 weeks postpartum due to potential interference from pregnancy-related factors.
- Consider A1C at 6–12 months postpartum only if OGTT is declined or intolerable.
- Conduct ongoing screening for prediabetes or type 2 diabetes every 1–3 years after initial postpartum testing.
- Monitor individuals with a history of GDM for lifetime increased risk of diabetes (50–60%) and higher type 2 diabetes risk.
- Consider A1C at 6–12 months postpartum only if OGTT is declined or intolerable, with sensitivity of 80% and specificity of 80.8% for detecting 2-h plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL (≥11.1 mmol/L)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- History of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM).
- Nonpregnancy criteria for OGTT interpretation.
- Patient tolerance and willingness to undergo OGTT.
- Consideration of A1C only if OGTT is declined or intolerable.
- Baseline assessment of BMI and lifestyle factors for risk stratification.
- Baseline assessment of prepregnancy BMI and inter-pregnancy weight changes for risk stratification
- Evaluation of adherence to healthy eating patterns as a modifier of diabetes risk

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of A1C at 4–12 weeks postpartum due to reduced sensitivity from pregnancy-related factors.
- OGTT testing immediately postpartum (during hospitalization) due to variably reduced sensitivity.
- Fixed numeric A1C thresholds without consideration of pregnancy-related confounders.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Emphasis on OGTT at 4–12 weeks postpartum as the preferred screening method.
- Recommendation for ongoing screening every 1–3 years post-delivery.
- Integration of lifestyle interventions and metformin for diabetes prevention in high-risk individuals.
- Recognition of inter-pregnancy weight management as a critical risk modifier.
- Lifetime risk assessment for diabetes in individuals with a history of GDM.
- Recognition that inter-pregnancy weight gain increases risk of adverse pregnancy outcomes and subsequent type 2 diabetes
- Emphasis on postdelivery lifestyle interventions and metformin for diabetes prevention in individuals with prediabetes and a history of GDM

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Postpartum diabetes screening protocol for GDM

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Perform a 2-hour OGTT with 75g glucose 6-8 weeks postpartum for reclassification of diabetes status
- Repeat annual screening if initial postpartum OGTT is normal

**Prerequisites:**
- History of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM)
- Postpartum period (6-8 weeks after delivery)


### postpartum insulin sensitivity changes

Insulin sensitivity increases dramatically immediately postpartum with placental delivery. Insulin requirements in the immediate postpartum period are roughly 34% lower than prepregnancy levels. Insulin sensitivity returns to prepregnancy levels over 1–2 weeks postpartum postpartum insulin sensitivity changes. Postpartum Insulin Sensitivity & Breastfeeding Management. Insulin requirement declines by 30-40% immediately after delivery due to lack of placental hormonal influence.. Insulin requirements gradually increase over the next weeks and usually return to pre-pregnancy levels in 2 to 4 months.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** postpartum insulin sensitivity changes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin sensitivity increases dramatically immediately postpartum with placental delivery
- Insulin requirements in the immediate postpartum period are roughly 34% lower than prepregnancy levels
- Insulin sensitivity returns to prepregnancy levels over 1–2 weeks postpartum
- Hypoglycemia prevention is critical for individuals taking insulin during breastfeeding and with erratic sleep/eating schedules
- Individuals with gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) typically do not require diabetes medications postpartum
- Insulin sensitivity may remain elevated for up to 6 weeks postpartum in some individuals, particularly those with preexisting type 1 or type 2 diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals requiring insulin after delivery
- Postpartum period (immediate to 1–2 weeks)
- Breastfeeding status
- Gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) diagnosis

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Postpartum Insulin Sensitivity & Breastfeeding Management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Insulin requirement declines by 30-40% immediately after delivery due to lack of placental hormonal influence.
- Insulin requirements gradually increase over the next weeks and usually return to pre-pregnancy levels in 2 to 4 months.
- Women should be advised to have a meal or snack before or during breastfeeding to decrease the risk of hypoglycemia.
- Breastfeeding should be initiated within 30 minutes after birth and provided every 2 and 3 hours to avoid neonatal hypoglycemia.
- Medicines for the treatment of diabetes related complications and co-morbidities that were discontinued for safety reasons before or during pregnancy should be avoided during breastfeeding period.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be in the postpartum period immediately following delivery.
- Patient must be managing Type 1 Diabetes or insulin-dependent diabetes.
- Patient is breastfeeding or planning to breastfeed.
- Patient requires monitoring for diabetes related complications (especially diabetic retinopathy), thyroid status, and mental status (depression).
- Patient requires contraception counseling to avoid unplanned pregnancies.


### pregnancy counseling and risks

pregnancy counseling and risks in CKD. Review teratogenicity potential of medications for people with CKD of child-bearing potential. Provide regular reproductive and contraceptive counseling aligned with patient values and preferences. Discontinue teratogenic CKD medications (e.g., ACEi/ARBs, mTOR inhibitors) before conception or at conception pregnancy counseling and risks. pregnancy counseling and risks in T1DM. Proactively counsel women of child-bearing age with T1DM on contraception, pregnancy planning, and risks of unplanned pregnancy.. Achieve HbA1c <7% (preferably close to 6%) without significant hypoglycemia before conception.

### Source 1: Chronic Kidney Disease.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pregnancy counseling and risks in CKD

**Clinical Actions:**
- Review teratogenicity potential of medications for people with CKD of child-bearing potential
- Provide regular reproductive and contraceptive counseling aligned with patient values and preferences
- Discontinue teratogenic CKD medications (e.g., ACEi/ARBs, mTOR inhibitors) before conception or at conception
- Continue CKD-specific medications during pregnancy/lactation (e.g., hydroxychloroquine, tacrolimus, cyclosporin, eculizumab, prednisone, azathioprine, colchicine, IVIG)
- Conduct multidisciplinary preconception, pregnancy, and lactation care with obstetrics and subspecialties
- Conduct sex-specific medication dosing adjustments for RAAS-blocking medications in CKD, considering lower doses in women due to potential improved outcomes and lower RAAS activity

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Medication chart review to replace teratogenic medications before conception or establish strict monitoring plan
- Strict monitoring and cessation of teratogenic medications at conception
- Consideration of contraceptive methods with less clear impact on BP (e.g., nonoral hormonal contraceptives)
- Multidisciplinary care coordination before conception and throughout pregnancy and lactation
- Sex-specific medication dosing adjustments due to differences in body weight, composition, and drug metabolism
- Ensure a strict monitoring plan with cessation of potentially teratogenic medications at conception if replacement before conception is not possible

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pregnancy counseling and risks in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Proactively counsel women of child-bearing age with T1DM on contraception, pregnancy planning, and risks of unplanned pregnancy.
- Achieve HbA1c <7% (preferably close to 6%) without significant hypoglycemia before conception.
- Implement basal-bolus insulin regimens or continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) for physiological insulin dosing.
- Start folic acid supplementation (at least 400 µg/day) in the preconceptional period.
- Substitute teratogenic drugs (statins, ACEI, ARB) with safer alternatives (methyldopa, calcium channel blockers, labetalol) before pregnancy.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must have Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM).
- Patient must be of child-bearing age.
- Desired glycemic control must be achieved prior to conception.
- Pre-existing complications (e.g., retinopathy) and lifestyle factors (obesity, smoking) must be addressed.
- Contraception must be practiced until glycemic targets are met.
- Patient must have a past or family history of thromboembolic diseases to consider non-hormonal contraception (barrier methods or IUD) as preferable.


### glyburide safety in gestational diabetes

Glyburide safety and efficacy in gestational diabetes. Associated with higher rates of neonatal hypoglycemia and macrosomia compared to insulin or metformin. Demonstrated failure to meet noninferiority criteria against insulin for preventing large-for-gestational-age infants

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glyburide safety and efficacy in gestational diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Associated with higher rates of neonatal hypoglycemia and macrosomia compared to insulin or metformin
- Demonstrated failure to meet noninferiority criteria against insulin for preventing large-for-gestational-age infants
- Linked to increased risk of maternal hypoglycemia when used in oral glucose-lowering strategies
- Exhibits significant placental transfer with umbilical cord plasma concentrations reaching 50–70% of maternal levels
- Lacks available long-term safety data for offspring exposed in utero
- Increased neonatal abdominal circumference observed with glyburide compared to insulin or metformin

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM)
- Clinical decision must weigh risks of neonatal hypoglycemia and macrosomia against oral medication convenience
- Assessment of maternal hypoglycemia risk prior to initiation
- Consideration of alternative agents (insulin or metformin) due to safety profile concerns
- Absence of long-term follow-up capability for exposed offspring limits recommendation strength
- Prior assessment of maternal hypoglycemia risk before initiating any oral glucose-lowering strategy in GDM

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of glyburide as a first-line oral agent in GDM without insulin comparison
- Reliance on glyburide for GDM management based on noninferiority to insulin
- Assumption of safety equivalence between glyburide and insulin for neonatal outcomes
- Use of glyburide in strategies aiming to prevent large-for-gestational-age infants
- Application of glyburide without acknowledging the 50–70% placental transfer rate
- Use of glyburide as part of an oral glucose-lowering medication strategy without insulin substitution for inadequate glycemic control

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Preference for insulin or metformin over glyburide in GDM management
- Recognition of glyburide's failure in noninferiority trials against insulin
- Incorporation of maternal hypoglycemia risk as a primary concern for oral agents in GDM
- Adoption of composite outcome measures including neonatal hypoglycemia, macrosomia, and hyperbilirubinemia
- Implementation of cautious prescribing due to lack of long-term offspring safety data
- Adoption of composite outcome measures including neonatal hyperbilirubinemia when evaluating glyburide's efficacy against insulin


### metformin dosing and use in T2DM

Start with 500 mg after meals and increase by 500 mg every two weeks to reach maximum 2500 mg. Use sustained release preparations and administer after meals to minimize gastrointestinal side effects. Stop metformin at least 24 hours before elective major surgery or use of radiocontrast media

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** metformin dosing and use in T2DM

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Start with 500 mg after meals and increase by 500 mg every two weeks to reach maximum 2500 mg
- Use sustained release preparations and administer after meals to minimize gastrointestinal side effects
- Stop metformin at least 24 hours before elective major surgery or use of radiocontrast media
- Withdraw metformin immediately if any contraindications occur
- Monitor for vitamin B12 deficiency with prolonged use
- Administer metformin after meals to minimize gastrointestinal side effects
- Use sustained release preparations to reduce gastrointestinal side effects

**Prerequisites:**
- Absence of renal insufficiency
- Absence of hepatic insufficiency
- Absence of respiratory insufficiency and other hypoxemic conditions
- Absence of acute myocardial infarction or congestive cardiac failure
- Absence of alcohol abuse, ketoacidosis, or severe infections
- Absence of lactic acidosis risk factors (e.g., conditions predisposing to hypoxia or tissue hypoperfusion)


### metformin for diabetes prevention

Metformin for Diabetes Prevention and Monitoring. Consider metformin for adults at high risk of type 2 diabetes, particularly those aged 25–59 with BMI ≥35 kg/m², higher fasting plasma glucose (≥110 mg/dL), or higher A1C (≥6.0%).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Metformin for Diabetes Prevention and Monitoring

**Core Recommendations:**
- Consider metformin for adults at high risk of type 2 diabetes, particularly those aged 25–59 with BMI ≥35 kg/m², higher fasting plasma glucose (≥110 mg/dL), or higher A1C (≥6.0%).
- Consider metformin to prevent hyperglycemia in high-risk individuals treated with PI3Kα inhibitors (e.g., alpelisib) or high-dose glucocorticoids.
- Consider periodic assessment of vitamin B12 levels in individuals receiving long-term metformin therapy, especially those with anemia or peripheral neuropathy.
- Monitor vitamin B12 annually in patients taking metformin for more than 4 years or those with risk factors like vegan diets or prior gastric surgery.
- Balance the benefits of glucose lowering against potential adverse effects, treatment burden, and cost, as no pharmacologic agent is FDA-approved solely for diabetes prevention.
- Consider metformin to prevent hyperglycemia in individuals receiving high-dose and/or long-duration glucocorticoid therapy, particularly those with additional risk factors for diabetes (e.g., higher glucocorticoid doses, longer treatment duration).
- Consider metformin for individuals at highest risk of hyperglycemia with PI3Kα inhibitor therapy (e.g., age ≥70 years, BMI ≥30 kg/m², concurrent glucocorticoid use, or baseline hyperglycemia [A1C ≥5.7% or fasting plasma glucose ≥100 mg/dL]).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be an adult at high risk of type 2 diabetes, typified by Diabetes Prevention Program criteria.
- Specific high-risk characteristics include age 25–59 years, BMI ≥35 kg/m², fasting plasma glucose ≥110 mg/dL, or A1C ≥6.0%.
- History of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) is a qualifying condition for consideration.
- For PI3Kα inhibitor users, patients should be high-risk (e.g., older, obese, concurrent glucocorticoid use, baseline hyperglycemia).
- Vitamin B12 monitoring is required for long-term therapy (>4 years) or presence of deficiency risk factors.
- Metformin should be started 1 week prior to initiating PI3Kα inhibitor therapy (e.g., alpelisib) to prevent hyperglycemia and continued for the duration of treatment.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Valsartan for the prevention of diabetes (not recommended due to lack of cardiovascular outcome benefit).
- Testosterone therapy for the prevention of type 2 diabetes in men with hypogonadism (not recommended based on TRAVERSE study).
- Widespread vitamin D therapy for adults with high-risk prediabetes (benefit-to-risk ratio remains uncertain).
- Pharmacologic treatments other than metformin for the sole purpose of preventing type 2 diabetes (lack of long-term data).
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults (now individualized based on patient-specific factors).
- Vitamin D therapy for the prevention of type 2 diabetes in adults with high-risk prediabetes (benefit-to-risk ratio remains uncertain due to lack of statistically significant outcomes and potential adverse effects like hypercalcemia).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consideration of metformin for preventing hyperglycemia in individuals receiving PI3Kα inhibitors (e.g., alpelisib).
- Consideration of metformin for preventing hyperglycemia in individuals treated with high-dose glucocorticoids.
- Recommendation for annual vitamin B12 monitoring in long-term metformin users (>4 years) or those with specific risk factors.
- Recognition of metformin as the only pharmacologic therapy with robust efficacy and safety data for diabetes prevention.
- Use of GLP-1-based therapies for weight management in individuals with overweight or obesity as an essential component of diabetes prevention.
- Metformin is as effective as lifestyle modification in reducing diabetes risk in participants with BMI ≥35 kg/m² and in younger individuals aged 25–44 years.
- Metformin and intensive lifestyle modification led to an equivalent 50% reduction in diabetes risk in individuals with a history of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM).


### metformin in gestational diabetes management

Metformin use in gestational diabetes management. Metformin is associated with lower risk of neonatal hypoglycemia and less maternal weight gain compared to insulin in systematic reviews and RCTs.. Metformin treatment monotherapy failure occurs in 14–46% of individuals.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Metformin use in gestational diabetes management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Metformin is associated with lower risk of neonatal hypoglycemia and less maternal weight gain compared to insulin in systematic reviews and RCTs.
- Metformin treatment monotherapy failure occurs in 14–46% of individuals.
- Metformin does not reduce the risk of GDM in high-risk individuals with obesity, polycystic ovary syndrome, or preexisting insulin resistance.
- Metformin exposure results in smaller neonates with an acceleration of postnatal growth, leading to higher BMI in childhood.
- Metformin should not be used in pregnant people with hypertension, preeclampsia, or those at risk for intrauterine growth restriction.
- Metformin treatment in pregnancy does not reduce the risk of GDM in high-risk individuals with obesity, polycystic ovary syndrome, or preexisting insulin resistance, as demonstrated in a meta-analysis of 11 RCTs (143).
- RCTs comparing metformin with other therapies for ovulation induction in individuals with polycystic ovary syndrome have not demonstrated benefit in preventing spontaneous abortion or GDM (146).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be unable to use insulin safely or effectively due to cost, comprehension, or cultural influences.
- Known risks and the need for more long-term safety data in offspring must be discussed with the patient.
- Continuation of metformin in early pregnancy is considered safe as first-trimester use has not been associated with increased risk of major congenital malformations.
- If used, metformin can be discontinued at the end of the first trimester to avoid sudden deterioration of glycemia and associated increased risk of malformations.
- Patient must not have hypertension, preeclampsia, or be at risk for intrauterine growth restriction.
- Oral agents, including metformin, may be considered as an alternative for individuals with GDM requiring medical therapy who cannot use insulin safely or effectively due to cost, comprehension, or cultural influences, after discussing known risks and the need for long-term safety data in offspring.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Continuation of metformin in individuals with polycystic ovary syndrome for preventing spontaneous abortion or GDM.
- Use of metformin in pregnant people with hypertension or preeclampsia.
- Use of metformin in individuals at risk for intrauterine growth restriction.
- Metformin as a standard alternative for GDM without discussing known risks and need for long-term safety data.
- Metformin for ovulation induction in individuals with polycystic ovary syndrome to prevent GDM.
- Continuation of metformin in individuals with polycystic ovary syndrome for preventing spontaneous abortion or GDM (146, 147–149).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Metformin as an alternative oral agent for GDM when insulin is not feasible due to cost, comprehension, or cultural influences.
- Recommendation to discontinue metformin at the end of the first trimester to avoid sudden deterioration of glycemia and associated increased risk of malformations.
- Recognition that metformin readily crosses the placenta, resulting in umbilical cord blood levels as high or higher than simultaneous maternal levels.
- Evidence that metformin exposure results in smaller neonates with an acceleration of postnatal growth, resulting in higher BMI in childhood.
- Evidence that metformin treatment in pregnancy does not reduce the risk of GDM in high-risk individuals with obesity, polycystic ovary syndrome, or preexisting insulin resistance.
- Metformin can be discontinued at the end of the first trimester (after organogenesis) to avoid sudden deterioration of glycemia and an associated increased risk of malformations due to hyperglycemia (158).
- Continuation of metformin in early pregnancy is considered safe, as first-trimester metformin use has not been associated with increased risk of major congenital malformations (159,160).


### individualized maternal nutrition plan

individualized maternal nutrition plan for T1DM. Create culturally sensitive and socio-economically viable nutrition plans with expert dietitian support. Adopt a less carbohydrate-restricted approach using higher quality carbohydrates, lower fat, and ethnically acceptable foods

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** individualized maternal nutrition plan for T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Create culturally sensitive and socio-economically viable nutrition plans with expert dietitian support
- Adopt a less carbohydrate-restricted approach using higher quality carbohydrates, lower fat, and ethnically acceptable foods
- Structure meals as three major meals and three to four adequately spaced snacks to avoid hypoglycemia and prevent glycemic fluctuations
- Ensure adequate caloric intake for fetal and maternal health while achieving glycemic goals without hypoglycemia
- Monitor attention to timings, content, and quality of meals and snacks
- Incorporate ethnically acceptable foods to enhance adherence and cultural relevance in meal planning

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Involvement of an expert dietician for plan formulation
- Adherence to Dietary Reference Intakes (DRI) for macronutrients
- Maintenance of appropriate gestational weight gain
- Prevention of hypoglycemia events
- Achievement of target glycemic goals
- Ensure adequate caloric intake to support fetal growth and maternal health while achieving glycemic goals


### initial combination therapy

Initial combination therapy in type 2 diabetes. Consider initial combination therapy for patients with A1C levels 1.5–2.0% above individualized goal or those at high risk for cardiovascular disease (CVD) or with established CVD.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Initial combination therapy in type 2 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Consider initial combination therapy for patients with A1C levels 1.5–2.0% above individualized goal or those at high risk for cardiovascular disease (CVD) or with established CVD.
- Incorporate high-glycemic-efficacy therapies (e.g., GLP-1 RAs, dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs, SGLT2 inhibitors) to reduce hypoglycemia and weight gain risks.
- Prioritize GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs over basal insulin for intensive glucose management due to lower hypoglycemia risk and favorable weight/cardiovascular profiles.
- Use fixed-ratio combination products (e.g., IDegLira, iGlarLixi) for patients on GLP-1 RA and basal insulin.
- Avoid combining GLP-1 RAs (or dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs) with DPP-4 inhibitors due to lack of added glucose-lowering benefit.
- Consider switching from evening NPH to a basal analog if hypoglycemia occurs or NPH administration is frequently forgotten, with preference for morning dosing of long-acting basal insulin.
- Consider dosing NPH insulin in the morning for patients with steroid-induced hyperglycemia.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- A1C levels 1.5–2.0% above individualized goal or presence of high CVD risk/established CVD.
- Patient-centered treatment goals aligned with glycemic control, cardiovascular risk reduction, and tolerability.
- Prioritize GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs in patients requiring intensive glucose management unless contraindicated.
- Consider insulin as first injectable if hyperglycemia symptoms, A1C >10%, or blood glucose ≥300 mg/dL.
- Review treatment plans continuously for efficacy, side effects, and treatment burden.
- Consider individual preference, glycemic lowering, weight-lowering effect, and injection frequency when selecting GLP-1 RAs.
- If CVD is present, select a GLP-1 RA with proven cardiovascular benefit (oral or injectable formulations are appropriate).

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Stepwise addition of medications to metformin as the sole initial strategy for all patients.
- Use of DPP-4 inhibitors in combination with GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs.
- Routine initiation of basal insulin without first considering GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized adjustments for patient-specific factors.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Initial combination therapy for rapid glycemic goal attainment in high-risk patients (A1C 1.5–2.0% above goal or CVD risk).
- Preferential use of GLP-1 RAs or dual GIP and GLP-1 RAs for intensive glucose management due to lower hypoglycemia risk and weight benefits.
- Fixed-ratio combination products (e.g., IDegLira, iGlarLixi) for patients on GLP-1 RA and basal insulin.
- Avoidance of GLP-1 RA + DPP-4 inhibitor combinations due to lack of added benefit.
- Continuous review of treatment plans for deintensification based on efficacy, side effects, or changes in patient goals.
- Oral semaglutide is a commercially available GLP-1 RA formulation for patients preferring non-injectable therapy.
- Inhaled human insulin is an option for prandial insulin therapy.


### initial pharmacotherapy guidelines

initial pharmacotherapy and lifestyle management. Provide education regarding the disease and its management to all patients. Lifestyle modification, including healthy dietary practices and sufficient physical activity, should be the first line of management

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** initial pharmacotherapy and lifestyle management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Provide education regarding the disease and its management to all patients
- Lifestyle modification, including healthy dietary practices and sufficient physical activity, should be the first line of management
- Metformin is the initial pharmacotherapy of choice for most patients unless contraindicated or patient has symptomatic hyperglycemia
- Aim to keep HbA1c below 7% in most patients, with individualized treatment targets

**Prerequisites:**
- Specific contraindications or tolerance issues must be considered before initiating metformin
- Symptomatic hyperglycemia may warrant initial pharmacotherapy other than metformin
- Specific contraindications for metformin include renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²), severe hepatic disease, or conditions predisposing to lactic acidosis


### preconception and gestational diabetes telehealth care

telehealth care for preconception and gestational diabetes. team-based care is recommended either through a single specialized center or multiple centers with interprofessional team members. telehealth interventions (e.g., telehealth visits, health apps) combined with in-person visits for gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** telehealth care for preconception and gestational diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- team-based care is recommended either through a single specialized center or multiple centers with interprofessional team members
- telehealth interventions (e.g., telehealth visits, health apps) combined with in-person visits for gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM)
- telehealth care demonstrated reduced incidences of cesarean delivery, premature rupture of membranes, pregnancy-induced hypertension or preeclampsia, preterm birth, neonatal asphyxia, and polyhydramnios compared with standard in-person care alone
- telehealth interventions should include remote blood glucose monitoring with real-time data sharing for GDM management

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- preconception care should be part of the care plan during pregnancy
- telehealth interventions should be used in combination with in-person visits for GDM
- preconception care must include glycemic control optimization prior to conception for individuals with pre-existing diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- telehealth interventions (telehealth visits, health apps) as part of GDM management
- remote blood glucose monitoring with real-time data sharing as a core component of GDM telehealth care


### preconception and early pregnancy assessment

preconception and early pregnancy assessment in T1DM. Ideal booking appointment by 10 weeks of gestation for comprehensive diabetes and complication review.. Conduct retinal and renal assessments for women with pre-existing diabetes if not performed in the last 3 months.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** preconception and early pregnancy assessment in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Ideal booking appointment by 10 weeks of gestation for comprehensive diabetes and complication review.
- Conduct retinal and renal assessments for women with pre-existing diabetes if not performed in the last 3 months.
- Schedule joint diabetes and antenatal clinic visits every 1–2 weeks throughout pregnancy.
- Perform early viability scan at 7–9 weeks to confirm pregnancy viability and gestational age due to higher miscarriage risk.
- Conduct detailed ultrasound and fetal echocardiogram at 18–20 weeks to screen for neural tube defects, cardiac, and structural abnormalities.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Blood pressure measurement, per speculum examination, hemogram, kidney function tests, liver function test, TSH, urine routine microscopy and culture.
- HbA1c measurement in every trimester.
- Retinal assessment unless assessed in the last 3 months.
- Renal assessment if not performed in the last 3 months.
- Neural tube/nasal bone scan with dual screen test for chromosomal anomalies at 11–14 weeks.


### maternal diabetes and offspring risk

Maternal diabetes and offspring risk management. Optimize preconception glycemic control (A1C <6.5% or <7% if at risk of hypoglycemia) to reduce congenital anomalies and adverse pregnancy outcomes.. Implement continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) in pregnancy for type 1 and type 2 diabetes to improve time-in-range and neonatal outcomes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Maternal diabetes and offspring risk management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Optimize preconception glycemic control (A1C <6.5% or <7% if at risk of hypoglycemia) to reduce congenital anomalies and adverse pregnancy outcomes.
- Implement continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) in pregnancy for type 1 and type 2 diabetes to improve time-in-range and neonatal outcomes.
- Use automated insulin delivery (AID) systems in pregnancy complicated by type 1 diabetes to achieve tighter glycemic targets.
- Screen for gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) at 24–28 weeks (or earlier in high-risk women) using IADPSG criteria.
- Administer low-dose aspirin (81 mg/day) from 12–36 weeks in women with pregestational diabetes to reduce preeclampsia risk.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Preconception care must include glycemic optimization, folic acid supplementation (400–800 mcg/day), and evaluation of microvascular complications.
- Women with pregestational diabetes require prepregnancy counseling on contraception, glycemic targets, and fetal risk reduction.
- GDM diagnosis requires fasting plasma glucose ≥92 mg/dL or 1-hour postprandial ≥180 mg/dL or 2-hour ≥153 mg/dL after 75g OGTT.
- CGM use in pregnancy requires healthcare provider training and patient education on data interpretation and insulin adjustments.
- Aspirin prophylaxis for preeclampsia requires initiation between 12–16 weeks in women with diabetes and chronic hypertension.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of glyburide for GDM due to higher neonatal hypoglycemia and macrosomia risk compared to insulin.
- Postprandial glucose targets based solely on fasting glucose without CGM integration.
- Universal first-trimester screening for GDM using HbA1c alone without confirmatory OGTT.
- Routine use of metformin in early pregnancy for GDM prevention without individualized risk assessment.
- Targeting A1C <6.0% in pregnancy without consideration of hypoglycemia risk in type 1 diabetes.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Early GDM screening (before 24 weeks) in high-risk women using HbA1c ≥5.9% (41 mmol/mol) as a threshold.
- Integration of CGM-derived time-in-range metrics (e.g., >70% in target range) as primary glycemic targets in pregnancy.
- Use of AID systems (e.g., hybrid closed-loop insulin delivery) in type 1 diabetes pregnancy to reduce hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia.
- Postpartum screening for type 2 diabetes at 4–12 weeks using OGTT or HbA1c in women with GDM.
- Lifestyle interventions (diet, exercise) and metformin for diabetes prevention in women with prior GDM.


### pregnancy glycemic management strategy

glycemic goals in pregnant individuals are stricter than in nonpregnant individuals. pregnant people with diabetes should consume consistent amounts of carbohydrates to match prandial insulin dosages or use insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pregnancy glycemic management strategy

**Core Recommendations:**
- glycemic goals in pregnant individuals are stricter than in nonpregnant individuals
- pregnant people with diabetes should consume consistent amounts of carbohydrates to match prandial insulin dosages or use insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios
- medical nutrition therapy (MNT) is essential to establish food plans and weight gain goals
- lifestyle modification (nutrition, exercise, weight management) is the first-line treatment after GDM diagnosis
- RDN referral is important for individualized nutrition plans and glycemic management
- avoidance of trans fats and excess saturated fats in the diet
- promotion of n-3 fatty acids from nuts, seeds, and fish to support weight management

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- preexisting diabetes or GDM diagnosis
- RDN involvement for nutrition plan development
- consistent carbohydrate intake or insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio use
- evaluation of carbohydrate quality and macronutrient balance
- adherence to National Academy of Medicine dietary reference intake guidelines
- nutrition plan must provide adequate calorie intake to promote fetal, neonatal, and maternal health
- nutrition plan must achieve appropriate weight gain according to 2009 National Academy of Medicine recommendations

**Previous Recommendations:**
- ketogenic eating patterns due to lack of carbohydrates
- paleo eating patterns due to dairy restriction
- excessive restriction of any macronutrient class
- severe carbohydrate restriction without ketone monitoring
- fixed calorie intake without individualized assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- minimum 175g carbohydrate intake (~35% of 2,000-calorie diet) for all pregnant people
- emphasis on whole foods (fruits, vegetables, legumes, whole grains, lean protein, healthy fats)
- limitation of processed foods, fatty red meat, and sweetened foods/beverages
- use of fasting ketone testing to identify severe carbohydrate restriction
- promotion of higher-quality, nutrient-dense carbohydrates to improve glycemic control and vascular benefits
- minimum dietary reference intake of 71 g of protein for all pregnant people
- minimum dietary reference intake of 28 g of fiber for all pregnant people


### pregnancy glucose management targets

A1C targets in pregnancy should be individualized (<6% to <7% [<42–53 mmol/mol]) to balance fetal outcomes and maternal hypoglycemia risk.. A1C is a secondary measure of glycemia in pregnancy due to physiological changes and limited capture of postprandial hyperglycemia.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pregnancy glucose management targets

**Core Recommendations:**
- A1C targets in pregnancy should be individualized (<6% to <7% [<42–53 mmol/mol]) to balance fetal outcomes and maternal hypoglycemia risk.
- A1C is a secondary measure of glycemia in pregnancy due to physiological changes and limited capture of postprandial hyperglycemia.
