- A1C levels should be monitored more frequently (e.g., monthly) due to altered red blood cell turnover in pregnancy.
- A1C <6% (<42 mmol/mol) in the second and third trimesters is associated with the lowest risk of large-for-gestational-age infants, preterm delivery, and preeclampsia.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be pregnant with or without preexisting diabetes.
- Glycemic goals should account for maternal hypoglycemia risk.
- A1C should be interpreted alongside glucose monitoring data.
- Evidence from the HAPO study links increasing A1C levels within the normal range to adverse outcomes in individuals without preexisting diabetes.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance solely on A1C as the primary measure of glycemia in pregnancy.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of individual patient factors.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- A1C <6% (<42 mmol/mol) as an optimal target in pregnancy if achievable without significant hypoglycemia.
- Monthly A1C monitoring during pregnancy due to physiological changes.
- Emphasis on postprandial glucose monitoring to address macrosomia risk.
- Observational studies indicate the lowest rates of adverse fetal outcomes are associated with A1C <6–6.5% (<42–48 mmol/mol) early in gestation.


### pregnancy diabetes nutrition counseling

Pregnancy diabetes nutrition counseling + lifestyle management. Promote an eating pattern including fruits, vegetables, legumes, whole grains, nuts, seeds, fish, and other lean protein to balance macronutrients and healthy n-3 fatty acids. Lifestyle behavior change is essential for managing GDM and may suffice as treatment for many individuals

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pregnancy diabetes nutrition counseling + lifestyle management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Promote an eating pattern including fruits, vegetables, legumes, whole grains, nuts, seeds, fish, and other lean protein to balance macronutrients and healthy n-3 fatty acids
- Lifestyle behavior change is essential for managing GDM and may suffice as treatment for many individuals
- Telehealth visits combined with in-person visits for pregnant people with GDM can improve outcomes
- Insulin is the preferred agent for managing GDM, type 2 diabetes, and type 1 diabetes in pregnancy
- Automated insulin delivery (AID) systems with pregnancy-specific glucose targets are recommended for pregnant individuals with type 1 diabetes
- Physical activity goals should be included in the management plan for pregnancies associated with diabetes.
- Management must support maternal, fetal, and placental needs to reach glycemic goals regardless of diabetes type.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Pregnant individuals with GDM, type 1 diabetes, or type 2 diabetes
- Glycemic goals must be defined and monitored
- Healthcare team with experience in pregnancy and diabetes management
- Access to telehealth and in-person care resources
- Appropriate diagnostic criteria for GDM or pre-existing diabetes
- Use of AID systems without pregnancy-specific glucose targets requires assistive techniques and collaboration with experienced healthcare teams.
- Metformin use for PCOS requires discontinuation by the end of the first trimester.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Metformin and glyburide as first-line agents for diabetes management in pregnancy
- Use of oral or noninsulin injectable glucose-lowering medications without long-term safety data
- Fixed A1C numeric targets without individualized consideration
- Reliance solely on in-person care without telehealth integration for GDM

**Updated Recommendations:**
- AID systems with pregnancy-specific glucose targets for type 1 diabetes in pregnancy
- Telehealth combined with in-person visits for GDM management
- Discontinuation of metformin for PCOS by end of first trimester
- Emphasis on lifestyle and behavior management as primary treatment for GDM
- Use of multiple daily injections or insulin pump technology for type 1 diabetes in pregnancy


### intraoperative glucose management

intraoperative glucose management in T1DM. Use insulin infusion and intravenous dextrose (5%) running separately during surgery.. Monitor glucose hourly and maintain levels between 72 and 126 mg/dl.. Reduce blood glucose monitoring to every 30 minutes during cesarean section until delivery and maternal consciousness.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** intraoperative glucose management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use insulin infusion and intravenous dextrose (5%) running separately during surgery.
- Monitor glucose hourly and maintain levels between 72 and 126 mg/dl.
- Reduce blood glucose monitoring to every 30 minutes during cesarean section until delivery and maternal consciousness.
- Conduct anesthetic assessment in the third trimester for women with diabetes and comorbidities (e.g., obesity, autonomic neuropathy).

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Separate administration of insulin infusion and intravenous dextrose (5%).
- Hourly glucose monitoring during surgery.
- Third-trimester anesthetic assessment for high-risk patients.


### preconception substance cessation

preconception substance cessation in T1DM. Women with T1DM should be advised to stop smoking, alcohol, and illicit drug use during the preconception period and thereafter.. Referral to a de-addiction clinic or expert may be done if required.. Preconception counseling must include substance cessation advice for women with T1DM.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** preconception substance cessation in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Women with T1DM should be advised to stop smoking, alcohol, and illicit drug use during the preconception period and thereafter.
- Referral to a de-addiction clinic or expert may be done if required.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Preconception counseling must include substance cessation advice for women with T1DM.


### preconception hypertension management

preconception hypertension management in T1DM. Hypertension risk is 2–4 times higher in women with diabetes.. BP <130/80 mm Hg should be achieved before conception.. ACE inhibitors or angiotensin-receptor blockers should be discontinued prior to conception.. Target BP <130/80 mm Hg preconception in women with T1DM.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** preconception hypertension management in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Hypertension risk is 2–4 times higher in women with diabetes.
- BP <130/80 mm Hg should be achieved before conception.
- ACE inhibitors or angiotensin-receptor blockers should be discontinued prior to conception.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Women with T1DM should be screened for hypertension preconception.


### GDM management protocols

Exercise and medical nutritional therapy is the first step and initiated in all women with GDM. Insulin is the preferred drug of choice in diabetes and pregnancy. Monitor fetal growth and maternal blood pressure besides glycemic control. Use fasting, 1-hour, and 2-hour postprandial glucose targets for monitoring

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** GDM management protocols

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Exercise and medical nutritional therapy is the first step and initiated in all women with GDM
- Insulin is the preferred drug of choice in diabetes and pregnancy
- Monitor fetal growth and maternal blood pressure besides glycemic control
- Use fasting, 1-hour, and 2-hour postprandial glucose targets for monitoring

**Prerequisites:**
- Target fasting plasma glucose <90 mg/dl
- Target 1-hour postprandial glucose <140 mg/dl
- Target 2-hour postprandial glucose <120 mg/dl


### automated insulin delivery in pregnancy

AID systems in pregnancy require careful assessment due to non-pregnancy-specific glucose goals and algorithms. Selected individuals with type 1 diabetes may be evaluated for AID systems under expert guidance. Use of insulin delivery technology is an individual choice for people with diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** automated insulin delivery in pregnancy

**Core Recommendations:**
- AID systems in pregnancy require careful assessment due to non-pregnancy-specific glucose goals and algorithms
- Selected individuals with type 1 diabetes may be evaluated for AID systems under expert guidance
- Use of insulin delivery technology is an individual choice for people with diabetes
- Disposable patch-like devices provide basal or bolus insulin in 2-unit increments for type 2 diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Expert guidance is required for evaluating candidates for AID systems in pregnancy
- Capability to safely use the device is necessary for insulin delivery technology adoption

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Disposable patch-like insulin delivery devices for type 2 diabetes


### contraception in diabetes management

Contraception in diabetes management for individuals of childbearing potential. Individuals of childbearing potential with diabetes must use one or two forms of contraception when taking glucose-lowering medications with unknown risks, limited safety evidence, or known risks during pregnancy, regardless of pregnancy intention.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Contraception in diabetes management for individuals of childbearing potential

**Core Recommendations:**
- Individuals of childbearing potential with diabetes must use one or two forms of contraception when taking glucose-lowering medications with unknown risks, limited safety evidence, or known risks during pregnancy, regardless of pregnancy intention.
- Contraception options and their interactions with glucose-lowering medications should be discussed with all individuals of childbearing potential with diabetes.
- Medications affecting gastrointestinal emptying (e.g., GLP-1 RAs, dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs like tirzepatide) may reduce the effectiveness of oral contraception, particularly at initiation or dose increases.
- Individuals starting or increasing tirzepatide doses should use a second form of contraception until the maintenance dose is achieved and used for at least 4 weeks.
- Preconception counseling should be part of routine care for individuals with diabetes of childbearing potential, including medication risks, benefits, and adjustments prior to pregnancy.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals must be of childbearing potential (e.g., not postmenopausal, surgically sterilized, or otherwise unable to conceive).
- Glucose-lowering medications must have unknown risks, limited safety evidence, or known risks during pregnancy.
- Contraception counseling must include interactions between glucose-lowering medications and contraceptive methods.
- For tirzepatide users, a second contraceptive method is required until maintenance dose is achieved and sustained for 4 weeks.
- Preconception counseling must address medication adjustments and glycemic goals prior to planned pregnancy.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Assuming contraception is unnecessary for individuals with diabetes who do not intend to become pregnant.
- Relying solely on oral contraception without considering interactions with glucose-lowering medications like GLP-1 RAs or tirzepatide.
- Not counseling individuals on the impact of gastric-emptying-altering medications on contraceptive efficacy.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Mandatory use of one or two contraceptive methods for individuals of childbearing potential on high-risk glucose-lowering medications.
- Inclusion of contraceptive efficacy discussions in routine diabetes care for childbearing individuals.
- Requirement for dual contraception during tirzepatide initiation or dose escalation until maintenance dose is achieved.
- Integration of preconception counseling into standard diabetes care for individuals of childbearing potential.
- Emphasis on the transient but significant impact of GLP-1 RAs and tirzepatide on oral contraceptive absorption.



## Inpatient Insulin Therapy Management

A comprehensive framework for inpatient insulin therapy management emphasizing individualized glycemic control, hypoglycemia avoidance, and evidence-based protocols across diverse patient populations and clinical scenarios.

**Key Features:**
- Hospitalized hyperglycemia management with integrated CGM and hypoglycemia avoidance (glucose ≤70 mg/dL)
- Individualized glycemic targets via shared decision-making to prevent therapeutic inertia
- Standardized inpatient hypoglycemia protocol with fast-acting carbohydrate treatment and emergency escalation
- Subcutaneous insulin administration technique with 4-mm pen needles to prevent intramuscular delivery
- Perioperative glycemic control with A1C <8% and intraoperative glucose targets of 100–180 mg/dL
- Mandatory patient education on hypoglycemia symptoms, site examination for lipohypertrophy, and risk factor mitigation
- Basal insulin continuation in older adults with type 1 diabetes to prevent DKA despite reduced oral intake
- Insulin therapy indications for type 2 diabetes progression with basal insulin as preferred initial treatment
- Automated insulin delivery (AID) systems preferred over MDI/pumps for type 1 diabetes due to hypoglycemia reduction
- Insulin mixing protocol with immediate administration, vial preparation rules, and cloudy insulin resuspension
- Insulin dose adjustment algorithm for T1DM using ICR (450/TDD or 500/TDD) and 5–10% TDD increases during acute illness
- Annual hypoglycemia risk screening including impaired awareness and fear of hypoglycemia assessments
- Hyperglycemia management in older adults prioritizing hypoglycemia avoidance in PALTC and end-of-life settings

**Notable Details:**
- Avoid hypoglycemia defined as blood glucose ≤70 mg/dL in hospital settings
- Preoperative A1C <8% within 3 months of elective surgery or time in range >50%
- Perioperative glucose target range: 108–180 mg/dL (acceptable 72–216 mg/dL)
- Insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio calculation: 450/TDD (regular insulin) or 500/TDD (rapid-acting analogs)
- Basal insulin dosing for type 2 diabetes: 0.1–0.2 units/kg/day with individualized titration
- AID systems preferred over nonautomated pumps/MDI in type 1 diabetes for improved outcomes
- Emergency services activation for unalert or unable-to-administer hypoglycemia patients
- Lipohypertrophy/lipoatrophy examination required at every clinical encounter for insulin users

Hospitalized hyperglycemia management + Hyperglycemic crises + CGM integration. Promote inclusion of oral glucose in first aid kits for treating hypoglycemia in public settings. Expand criteria for holding specific diabetes medication classes during acute illness hospitalized hyperglycemia management. Hospitalized hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia management. Avoid hypoglycemia defined as blood glucose ≤ 70 mg/dl in the hospital setting. Review patient's overall treatment regimen when hypoglycemia occurs to prevent future episodes. Treat hypoglycemia in alert patients with 15-30 g carbohydrate administration

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hospitalized hyperglycemia management + Hyperglycemic crises + CGM integration

**Core Recommendations:**
- Promote inclusion of oral glucose in first aid kits for treating hypoglycemia in public settings
- Expand criteria for holding specific diabetes medication classes during acute illness
- Revise hyperglycemic crises section to include outpatient prevention and management of diabetic ketoacidosis
- Implement individualized glycemic goals using CGM metrics based on health status and treatment-specific factors
- Use structured insulin order sets to reduce inpatient hypoglycemia and improve glycemic control
- Implement structured insulin order sets to reduce inpatient hypoglycemia and improve glycemic control
- Use HbA1c-guided discharge algorithms to tailor insulin regimens upon discharge

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**

- Admission HbA1c testing is required for glycemic risk stratification
- Basal-bolus insulin therapy is preferred over sliding scale insulin in non-critical care settings
- Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) should be integrated into insulin dosing algorithms
- Diabetes specialist teams should manage high-risk patients to prevent readmissions
- Structured hold protocols must be followed for specific diabetes medications during acute illness
- Individualized glycemic goals should be set using CGM metrics based on health status and treatment-specific factors

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Sliding scale insulin as sole therapy for non-critically ill hospitalized patients
- Routine use of intensive insulin therapy targeting near-normal glucose levels in ICU patients
- Discontinuation of oral diabetes medications without structured hold protocols during acute illness
- Reliance on point-of-care glucose testing alone without CGM validation
- Fixed A1C targets without individualized adjustments for life expectancy or hypoglycemia risk

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of oral glucose in first aid kits for hypoglycemia treatment in public settings
- Expanded criteria for holding diabetes medications during intercurrent illness
- CGM-guided insulin administration protocols in hospitalized patients
- Remote glucose monitoring for hospitalized patients during public health emergencies
- Automated insulin delivery systems with real-time CGM integration in non-critical care
- HbA1c-guided discharge algorithms to tailor insulin regimens upon discharge
- Structured hold protocols for GLP-1 receptor agonists and SGLT2 inhibitors perioperatively

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hospitalized hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Avoid hypoglycemia defined as blood glucose ≤ 70 mg/dl in the hospital setting
- Review patient's overall treatment regimen when hypoglycemia occurs to prevent future episodes
- Treat hypoglycemia in alert patients with 15-30 g carbohydrate administration
- Administer IV dextrose 50% solution and start IV dextrose 5% in water if BG is < 70 mg/dl
- Increase BG monitoring to every 15 min until levels are above 100 mg/dl on two consecutive measurements

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be alert and able to eat or drink for oral carbohydrate administration
- Patient must have blood glucose < 80 mg/dl to start dextrose-containing IV solution at 100-150 ml/h
- Patient must have blood glucose < 70 mg/dl to administer 25 ml dextrose 50% solution IV
- Monitoring frequency must be increased to every 15 minutes during treatment
- Treatment continues until blood glucose levels are above 100 mg/dl on two consecutive measurements


### individualized glycemic management

Individualized glycemic management with shared decision-making. Glycemic goals and management should be individualized using shared decision-making to integrate patient needs, goals, and preferences.. Therapeutic inertia should be avoided to reduce risks of acute/chronic complications and treatment burden. individualized glycemic management. Individualized glycemic management. Establish individualized glycemic targets rather than relying solely on general guidelines.. Implement tight glycemic control in early diabetes stages, particularly for young patients to prevent complications.. Revise glycemic targets based on patient condition when complications arise or in elderly populations.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Individualized glycemic management with shared decision-making

**Core Recommendations:**
- Glycemic goals and management should be individualized using shared decision-making to integrate patient needs, goals, and preferences.
- Therapeutic inertia should be avoided to reduce risks of acute/chronic complications and treatment burden.
- Glycemic goals should be reevaluated over time to balance individualized risks and benefits as clinical complexity increases.
- Severe or frequent hypoglycemia requires modification of treatment plans, including setting higher glycemic goals.
- Deintensification of high-hypoglycemia-risk medications (e.g., insulin, sulfonylureas) should be considered where hypoglycemia risk is high.
- Blood glucose goals should be modified in children with frequent hypoglycemia or hypoglycemia unawareness.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient-specific factors must be considered (e.g., life expectancy, cognitive function, hypoglycemia risk, comorbidities).
- Shared decision-making must integrate individual needs, goals, and preferences.
- Metabolic memory/legacy effect may justify finite periods of intensive glucose lowering even if later deintensified.
- Newly diagnosed individuals or those without limiting comorbidities may benefit from more intensive goals.
- Hypoglycemia risk assessment must guide medication adjustments.
- Consideration of medication burdens beyond hypoglycemia, including tolerability, administration difficulties, impact on education or employment, and financial cost, when balancing benefits of glycemic lowering and disease-specific benefits.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors.
- Rigid application of glycemic frameworks without clinical judgment.
- Failure to deintensify high-hypoglycemia-risk medications despite evidence of risk.
- Therapeutic inertia leading to unnecessary treatment burden or complications.
- Ignoring patient preferences or goals in glycemic management decisions.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of shared decision-making to personalize glycemic goals and treatment plans.
- Consideration of metabolic memory/legacy effect for early intensive glucose lowering.
- Deintensification of diabetes treatment when risks outweigh benefits, including hypoglycemia risk.
- Switching to lower-hypoglycemia-risk therapies (e.g., SGLT2 inhibitors, GLP-1 RAs) for individualized goals or disease-specific benefits.
- Partnering with patients during deintensification to align goals, monitoring, and care plans.
- Use of SGLT2 inhibitors in the setting of heart failure or chronic kidney disease and GLP-1 RAs in the setting of cardiovascular disease or obesity as lower-hypoglycemia-risk therapies for individualized goals or disease-specific benefits.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Individualized glycemic management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Establish individualized glycemic targets rather than relying solely on general guidelines.
- Implement tight glycemic control in early diabetes stages, particularly for young patients to prevent complications.
- Revise glycemic targets based on patient condition when complications arise or in elderly populations.
- Apply strict glycemic control during pregnancy with specific fasting and postprandial targets.
- Monitor using either 1-hour or 2-hour postprandial blood glucose values as clinically indicated.

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient age and stage of diabetes (early vs. late/complicated).
- Presence of established cardiovascular disease, smoking, obesity, hypertension, or family history of premature CVD.
- Pregnancy status requiring specific glucose thresholds.
- Patient-specific factors including life expectancy and hypoglycemia risk.
- Availability of clinical data for blood glucose, blood pressure, BMI, waist circumference, and lipid profiles.


### inpatient hypoglycemia management protocol

Treat hypoglycemia with fast-acting carbohydrates at glucose ≤70 mg/dL (3.9 mmol/L). Recheck glucose 15 minutes after carbohydrate ingestion and repeat treatment if unresolved. Call emergency services for unalert or unable-to-administer patients

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Inpatient hypoglycemia management protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Treat hypoglycemia with fast-acting carbohydrates at glucose ≤70 mg/dL (3.9 mmol/L)
- Recheck glucose 15 minutes after carbohydrate ingestion and repeat treatment if unresolved
- Call emergency services for unalert or unable-to-administer patients
- Use 15 g carbohydrates for most individuals; 5–10 g for automated insulin delivery users unless hypoglycemia is exercise-related or bolus overestimation occurred
- Administer pure glucose for initial treatment; avoid sucrose-containing carbohydrates for patients on acarbose
- Administering oral glucose only to alert individuals who can swallow safely

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Hypoglycemia alert value of ≤70 mg/dL (3.9 mmol/L) triggers treatment
- Individualized plans for preventing and treating hypoglycemia must be developed
- Hypoglycemia treatment embedded into all insulin and insulin infusion orders
- First aid kits in public settings must include oral glucose products
- Blood glucose monitoring before and 15 minutes after treatment
- Patient must be alert and able to swallow safely before administering oral glucose

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Using sucrose-containing carbohydrates for hypoglycemia in patients on acarbose
- Relying on dietary protein to treat hypoglycemia
- Omitting glucose from first aid kits in public settings
- Fixed carbohydrate dosing without consideration of automated insulin delivery systems
- Ignoring recurrent hypoglycemia risk due to ongoing insulin activity or secretagogues
- Using dietary protein to treat hypoglycemia

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Standardized nurse-initiated hypoglycemia treatment protocol for blood glucose <70 mg/dL (<3.9 mmol/L)
- Documentation and tracking of inpatient hypoglycemia episodes in EHR for quality improvement
- Inclusion of glucose tablets or gel in first aid kits for public settings
- Review of treatment plans after any blood glucose <70 mg/dL (<3.9 mmol/L) to prevent level 3 hypoglycemia
- Embedding hypoglycemia treatment into insulin and insulin infusion order sets
- Standardized hospital-wide, nurse-initiated hypoglycemia treatment protocol


### insulin administration technique

Administer insulin into subcutaneous tissue (abdomen, thigh, buttock, upper arm) to avoid unpredictable absorption and hypoglycemia. Use 4-mm pen needles to reduce risk of intramuscular (IM) insulin delivery across all ages and body types insulin administration technique. Insulin administration technique and site rotation. Inject insulin subcutaneously, avoiding intradermal and intramuscular routes to prevent pain and erratic absorption.. Rotate injection sites daily within a specific quadrant (e.g., moving 0.5-1.0 inch) to prevent lipodystrophy.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin administration technique

**Core Recommendations:**
- Administer insulin into subcutaneous tissue (abdomen, thigh, buttock, upper arm) to avoid unpredictable absorption and hypoglycemia
- Use 4-mm pen needles to reduce risk of intramuscular (IM) insulin delivery across all ages and body types
- Rotate injection/infusion sites to prevent lipohypertrophy and ensure consistent insulin absorption
- Educate patients/caregivers on proper injection technique, device use, and site rotation
- Administer rapid-acting insulin immediately post-meal (not too early) to match carbohydrate intake and minimize hypoglycemia risk
- Perform routine examination of injection/infusion sites for lipohypertrophy as part of comprehensive diabetes evaluations
- Provide education on proper care of injection or infusion sites to avoid infection or other complications

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient or caregiver must demonstrate understanding of correct insulin administration technique
- Selection of administration method (pen, pump, patch, inhaled) must align with patient-specific factors, cost, and preferences
- Regular reassessment of administration technique during routine follow-up visits
- Preprandial glucose monitoring should coincide with insulin administration and meal timing
- For older adults in PALTC facilities, meals should be tailored to culture/preferences to ensure adequate nutrition
- Selection of administration method (vial/syringe, pen, patch, inhaled, connected devices, pump) must align with individual-specific factors, cost, coverage, and preferences
- Assessment of administration device use and injection technique as key components of a comprehensive diabetes evaluation and treatment plan

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of longer needles (e.g., >4 mm) without consideration of IM risk, particularly in lean individuals
- Fixed rotation patterns (e.g., always moving clockwise) without assessing for lipohypertrophy
- Routine use of short-acting insulin for postprandial dosing instead of rapid-acting insulin
- Administering insulin too early before meals without balancing hypoglycemia risk
- Ignoring site examination for lipohypertrophy during diabetes evaluations
- Routine use of longer needles (e.g., >4 mm) without consideration of intramuscular (IM) risk, particularly in lean individuals or injections into limbs rather than truncal sites

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Emphasis on 4-mm pen needles as the standard to minimize IM risk across all body types
- Postprandial insulin administration (with rapid-acting insulin) to match carbohydrate intake in older adults
- Integration of FITTER Forward expert recommendations for advanced injection technique education
- Routine site examination for lipohypertrophy as part of comprehensive diabetes evaluations
- Consideration of inhaled insulin as an alternative administration method for eligible patients
- Integration of FITTER Forward expert recommendations for advanced insulin injection technique education

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin administration technique and site rotation

**Core Management Principles:**
- Inject insulin subcutaneously, avoiding intradermal and intramuscular routes to prevent pain and erratic absorption.
- Rotate injection sites daily within a specific quadrant (e.g., moving 0.5-1.0 inch) to prevent lipodystrophy.
- Select abdomen for faster-acting insulin and avoid thigh sites prior to vigorous exercise to prevent hypoglycemia.
- Use smaller, narrow-gauge needles and allow insulin to reach room temperature to reduce injection pain.
- Avoid using alcohol swabs on the injection site to prevent loss of needle silicone coating and subsequent pain.
- Change needles after a single use where possible to reduce pain from blunt tips.
- Take insulin out of the refrigerator approximately 30 minutes before injecting to prevent cold-induced pain.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be able to identify and rotate specific injection sites within defined quadrants.
- For deltoid and buttock injections, assistance from another person is required.
- In young children, deltoid and buttock sites must be avoided due to small muscle mass and nerve proximity.
- Injection sites must be clean and dry; if visibly dirty, clean with soap and water instead of alcohol.
- Thigh injection sites should be avoided if the patient plans to perform running or similar vigorous exercise.
- Injection on the deltoid region and buttocks requires assistance from another person.
- In young children, the deltoid region and buttocks must be avoided due to small muscle mass and proximity to the sciatic nerve.


### perioperative glycemic management

Maintain preoperative A1C <8% (<64 mmol/mol) within 3 months of elective surgery or use 14-day glucose management indicator <8% or time in range >50%. Monitor and maintain blood glucose between 100–180 mg/dL (5.6–10.0 mmol/L) before, during, and after surgery perioperative glycemic management. Perioperative glycemic management and insulin transition. Target perioperative blood glucose between 108-180 mg/dl (acceptable 72-216 mg/dl) to reduce morbidity and mortality.. Prioritize patients with diabetes for the operating list as the first case of the day to avoid prolonged starvation time.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Perioperative glycemic management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Maintain preoperative A1C <8% (<64 mmol/mol) within 3 months of elective surgery or use 14-day glucose management indicator <8% or time in range >50%
- Monitor and maintain blood glucose between 100–180 mg/dL (5.6–10.0 mmol/L) before, during, and after surgery
- Use basal-bolus insulin (basal insulin plus premeal short/rapid-acting insulin) for noncardiac general surgery to improve glycemic outcomes
- Hold metformin and other oral glucose-lowering agents on the day of surgery
- Reduce basal insulin dose by 25% the evening before surgery (or 50% for NPH, 75–80% for long-acting analogs) to lower hypoglycemia risk
- Monitor blood glucose at least every 2–4 hours while the individual takes nothing by mouth (NPO) and administer insulin as needed
- Use insulin as the only recommended glucose-lowering medication in the perioperative period

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Elective surgery patients with diabetes or prediabetes
- Assessment of hypoglycemia risk and surgical type to individualize glycemic goals
- Institutional support for insulin pump use during surgery if applicable
- Interprofessional approach for insulin pump management during surgery
- Availability of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) data for preoperative evaluation
- Consider facility capabilities for diabetes management when discharging individuals not to their home
- Assess surgical stress and counterregulatory hormone release risk for hyperglycemia in major general surgery patients

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Postponing surgery based solely on A1C or glucose management indicator without clinical justification
- Using oral glucose-lowering agents (e.g., metformin) on the day of surgery
- Relying solely on CGM for intraoperative glucose monitoring
- Routine use of correction-only insulin without basal insulin in noncardiac general surgery
- Strict perioperative glycemic goals without individualized risk-to-benefit assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of 14-day CGM glucose management indicator <8% or time in range >50% as an alternative to A1C for preoperative glycemic assessment
- Recommendation to maintain blood glucose 100–180 mg/dL (5.6–10.0 mmol/L) throughout the perioperative period
- Inclusion of SGLT2 inhibitor and GLP-1 RA considerations in perioperative glycemic management (with specific holding protocols)
- Development of preoperative optimization programs to lower A1C before elective surgery
- Reduction of basal insulin doses (25% for evening before surgery) to minimize hypoglycemia risk
- Optimal preoperative A1C goal between 7% and 8% associated with better perioperative glycemic management and decreased length of hospital stay

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Perioperative glycemic management and insulin transition

**Core Management Principles:**
- Target perioperative blood glucose between 108-180 mg/dl (acceptable 72-216 mg/dl) to reduce morbidity and mortality.
- Prioritize patients with diabetes for the operating list as the first case of the day to avoid prolonged starvation time.
- Switch T1DM patients to basal-bolus regimen or initiate IV insulin infusion at least two hours before surgery.
- Monitor blood glucose every 30-60 minutes during the perioperative period, especially under sedation.
- Transition from IV to subcutaneous insulin only after stable BG levels for 4-6 hours and improvement in oral intake.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- HbA1c preferably < 8-8.5% before elective surgery.
- Absence of urine ketones and normal electrolytes and hydration status before surgery.
- Ketone status must be checked whenever blood glucose is > 250 mg/dl.
- Patient must have stable infusion rate and blood glucose in target range for at least 4 to 6 hours before IV-to-subcutaneous transition.
- Patient must be eating scheduled meals or receiving stable enteral/parenteral feeds for transition.
- Comprehensive assessment of associated comorbidities and complications must be performed well in advance of surgery.
- Patients with autonomic neuropathy are at risk for hypoglycemia unawareness, requiring specific vigilance during tight control.


### insulin therapy side effects management

Patient education on hypoglycemia symptoms, management, and risk factors (e.g., exercise, delayed meals) is mandatory.. Regular examination of injection sites by both physician and patient to detect lipohypertrophy or lipoatrophy.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy side effects management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Patient education on hypoglycemia symptoms, management, and risk factors (e.g., exercise, delayed meals) is mandatory.
- Regular examination of injection sites by both physician and patient to detect lipohypertrophy or lipoatrophy.
- Strict adherence to site rotation and periodic needle changes (ideally after each injection) to prevent lipohypertrophy.
- Monitoring for weight gain due to improved glycemic control, recurrent hypoglycemia, or insulin's lipogenic effects.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be informed about hypoglycemia symptoms, management, and contributing factors before insulin initiation.
- Injection sites must be inspected periodically to identify lipohypertrophy or lipoatrophy.
- Needle replacement after each injection is strongly recommended to prevent tissue damage.


### older adults with type 1 diabetes

Insulin is essential for survival; basal insulin must be maintained to prevent DKA even with reduced oral intake.. Insulin delivery options include injections, AID systems, or insulin pumps based on patient preference, capability, and circumstances.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Older adults with type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin is essential for survival; basal insulin must be maintained to prevent DKA even with reduced oral intake.
- Insulin delivery options include injections, AID systems, or insulin pumps based on patient preference, capability, and circumstances.
- CGM should be used with any insulin delivery system to improve A1C and reduce hypoglycemia.
- Treatment goals and strategies are personalized using comprehensive geriatric assessment.
- Special considerations include neurocognitive function, hypoglycemia risk, and end-of-life care.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 1 diabetes diagnosis.
- Age over 65 years or advanced age-related considerations.
- Assessment of life expectancy, cognitive function, and hypoglycemia risk.
- Access to insulin therapy (basal insulin required to prevent DKA).
- Availability of CGM or insulin delivery systems (pump, AID, injections).

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without individualized consideration.
- Discontinuation of basal insulin in older adults with reduced oral intake.
- Routine use of sulfonylureas or other non-insulin therapies in type 1 diabetes.
- Exclusion of CGM due to age or setting limitations.
- One-size-fits-all insulin regimens without personalization.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of CGM with any insulin delivery method for improved outcomes.
- Personalized treatment plans based on geriatric assessment.
- Emphasis on hypoglycemia prevention and neurocognitive function monitoring.
- Consideration of end-of-life care preferences in treatment planning.
- Use of AID systems or insulin pumps tailored to individual capability and circumstances.


### insulin therapy indications

insulin therapy indications and management in type 2 diabetes. Insulin therapy is indicated for progressive type 2 diabetes when oral agents are insufficient to achieve glycemic goals.. Basal insulin alone is the preferred initial insulin treatment, with doses estimated at 0.1–0.2 units/kg/day and individualized titration. insulin therapy indications. Insulin therapy indications and types. Insulin is the mainstay of therapy in type 1 diabetes and indicated for type 2 diabetes patients with significant symptomatic hyperglycemia, weight loss, polyuria, polydipsia, or polyphagia.. Insulin is required for patients with fasting plasma glucose > 270 mg/dl, HbA1c > 9%, severe infections, or presence of ketosis.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin therapy indications and management in type 2 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin therapy is indicated for progressive type 2 diabetes when oral agents are insufficient to achieve glycemic goals.
- Basal insulin alone is the preferred initial insulin treatment, with doses estimated at 0.1–0.2 units/kg/day and individualized titration.
- Prandial insulin may be added if basal insulin is titrated to goal fasting glucose but A1C remains above target or postprandial hyperglycemia persists.
- GLP-1 receptor agonists or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists should be considered before prandial insulin to reduce hypoglycemia and weight gain risks.
- Insulin therapy should be tailored to patient literacy, numeracy, and self-management capabilities with comprehensive education.
- Insulin therapy may be used temporarily for acute situations such as hospitalization, acute illness, or high-dose glucocorticoid therapy, even if oral agents are still effective.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Type 2 diabetes with inadequate glycemic control despite noninsulin therapies.
- Documented progressive hyperglycemia or failure to achieve individualized A1C targets.
- Patient education and involvement in insulin management, including self-titration and hypoglycemia prevention.
- Assessment of hypoglycemia risk, health literacy, and numeracy to ensure safe insulin use.
- Regular glucose monitoring (self-monitored blood glucose or CGM) to guide titration and adjustments.
- Conversion between different basal insulin formulations may require an initial dose reduction of 10–20% when switching from human NPH insulin or U-300 glargine to another insulin, particularly in individuals in very tight management or at high risk for hypoglycemia.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of sulfonylureas, meglitinides, and DPP-4 inhibitors as adjuncts to insulin due to lack of additional benefits and increased hypoglycemia/weight gain risks.
- Routine use of NPH insulin over long-acting basal analogs for reduced nocturnal hypoglycemia risk.
- Fixed numeric A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors such as life expectancy or hypoglycemia risk.
- Reliance on premixed human insulins (e.g., 70/30 NPH/regular) when insulin analogs offer better flexibility and reduced hypoglycemia.
- Use of inhaled insulin in patients with chronic lung disease or those who smoke or recently quit.
- Routine use of sulfonylureas, meglitinides, and DPP-4 inhibitors as adjuncts to insulin due to lack of additional benefits on cardiovascular, kidney, weight, or liver outcomes, and increased risks of hypoglycemia and weight gain.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Emphasis on individualized insulin therapy with avoidance of insulin as a threat or sign of personal failure.
- Use of long-acting basal analogs (e.g., U-300 glargine, degludec) to reduce nocturnal hypoglycemia risk.
- Integration of GLP-1 receptor agonists or dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonists with basal insulin before prandial insulin intensification.
- Consideration of concentrated insulin preparations (e.g., U-500, U-300, U-200) for patients requiring high doses to improve convenience and adherence.
- Use of inhaled insulin with mandatory spirometry screening and contraindication in chronic lung disease or recent smoking.
- Concentrated insulin preparations (e.g., U-500 regular insulin, U-300 glargine, U-200 degludec) may be used for individuals requiring high doses to improve convenience, comfort, and treatment engagement, with specific dosing considerations for U-500 regular insulin (e.g., use of U-500 syringes to minimize dosing errors).
- Inhaled insulin requires mandatory spirometry (FEV₁) testing before and after initiation to screen for chronic lung disease and is contraindicated in individuals with asthma, COPD, or those who smoke or recently quit smoking.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin therapy indications and types

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Insulin is the mainstay of therapy in type 1 diabetes and indicated for type 2 diabetes patients with significant symptomatic hyperglycemia, weight loss, polyuria, polydipsia, or polyphagia.
- Insulin is required for patients with fasting plasma glucose > 270 mg/dl, HbA1c > 9%, severe infections, or presence of ketosis.
- Insulin is indicated for patients not responding to optimal doses of oral anti-hyperglycaemic agents, acute hyperglycemia, DKA/HHS/lactic acidosis, and stressful situations like MI, stroke, or trauma.
- Insulin is recommended for pregnancy, lactation, peri-operative states, and patients intolerant or contraindicated to oral hypoglycaemic agents.
- Insulin is indicated for hepatic and renal failure, renal transplantation, and patients on steroids.

**Prerequisites:**
- Patient must have type 1 diabetes or type 2 diabetes with specific clinical markers such as symptomatic hyperglycemia or failure of oral agents.
- Diagnostic criteria include fasting plasma glucose > 270 mg/dl or HbA1c > 9%.
- Presence of acute conditions like DKA, HHS, lactic acidosis, or severe infections.
- Patient must be intolerant or contraindicated to oral anti-hyperglycaemic agents.
- Clinical context includes pregnancy, lactation, peri-operative state, or specific comorbidities like renal failure.
- Patient must have failed optimal doses of oral anti-hyperglycaemic agents alone or in combination before insulin is indicated for non-acute type 2 diabetes cases.
- Oral agents can be used in renal transplantation patients, distinguishing them from other renal failure cases where insulin is indicated.


### insulin pump therapy complications

insulin pump therapy complications and AID system considerations. AID systems are preferred over nonautomated pumps and MDI in type 1 diabetes due to improved glycemic outcomes and reduced hypoglycemia risk.. Insulin pump therapy can be initiated at diagnosis or at any point in diabetes management, with no age restrictions for adults, adolescents, or children.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin pump therapy complications and AID system considerations

**Core Recommendations:**
- AID systems are preferred over nonautomated pumps and MDI in type 1 diabetes due to improved glycemic outcomes and reduced hypoglycemia risk.
- Insulin pump therapy can be initiated at diagnosis or at any point in diabetes management, with no age restrictions for adults, adolescents, or children.
- Complications include infusion set issues (dislodgement, occlusion) leading to ketosis or DKA, requiring early recognition and management.
- Pump-related skin issues include lipohypertrophy, lipoatrophy, and site infections.
- Discontinuation of pump therapy is uncommon but may occur due to cost, wearability, insurance loss, psychosocial factors, or suboptimal glycemic outcomes.
- Adoption of AID and pump therapy shows geographical variations related to healthcare professional preference or center characteristics.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Assessment of patient readiness and technical understanding for safe pump or AID system use.
- Support from a diabetes care team to guide device selection based on individual needs and preferences.
- Access to resources like Diabetes Wise, Diabetes Wise Pro, or PANTHER Program for device comparison.
- Consideration of socioeconomic factors, as pump therapy is more common in higher-income groups with private insurance.
- Monitoring for infusion set complications and skin issues to prevent ketosis, DKA, or infections.
- Assessment of readiness of the person with diabetes and their caregivers, if applicable, is required before initiation.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Nonintegrated or standard insulin pumps without automation (AID systems are now preferred).
- Fixed A1C targets without individualized consideration of life expectancy, cognitive function, or hypoglycemia risk.
- Historical small, short-duration studies comparing MDI with insulin pump therapy without modern AID system data.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Three AID systems approved for use in type 2 diabetes, expanding beyond type 1 diabetes.
- Early initiation of AID or pump therapy at diagnosis or any point in diabetes management.
- Geographical and socioeconomic disparities in pump therapy adoption, highlighting the need for equitable access.
- Use of CGM alongside insulin pump therapy to enhance glycemic control and safety.
- Focus on psychosocial factors (e.g., anxiety, depression) as reasons for pump therapy discontinuation.
- Three AID systems were recently approved for use by people with type 2 diabetes.


### insulin mixing protocol

Administer mixtures of short and intermediate insulin for better glycemic control in some patients. Inject mixed insulin immediately after preparation. Clean hands and injection site before each injection. Wipe vial tops with spirit before drawing insulin. Roll cloudy insulin vials gently to re-suspend insulin (avoid shaking) insulin mixing protocol. insulin mixing protocol and preparation. Draw regular insulin into the syringe first, followed by NPH insulin to prevent contamination. Gently roll NPH vial between palms to mix; do not shake vigorously. Inject air into NPH vial before drawing insulin, then inject air into regular insulin vial. Remove air bubbles from the syringe after drawing regular insulin

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin mixing protocol and preparation

**Core Management Principles:**
- Draw regular insulin into the syringe first, followed by NPH insulin to prevent contamination
- Gently roll NPH vial between palms to mix; do not shake vigorously
- Inject air into NPH vial before drawing insulin, then inject air into regular insulin vial
- Remove air bubbles from the syringe after drawing regular insulin
- Slowly draw NPH insulin after re-inserting needle into NPH vial
- Remove air bubbles from the syringe after drawing NPH insulin (if present) before finalizing the dose

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Allow insulin vials to reach room temperature before use
- Check insulin vials for clumps or particles before administration
- Clean vial tops with an alcohol swab before puncture
- Use appropriate pre-mixed formulations if available (e.g., Huminsulin 30/70, Novomix 30/70)
- Avoid mixing insulin glargine with any other insulin due to acidic pH
- Ensure the total volume of mixed insulin does not exceed the syringe capacity to avoid dosing inaccuracies

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin mixing protocol

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Administer mixtures of short and intermediate insulin for better glycemic control in some patients
- Inject mixed insulin immediately after preparation
- Clean hands and injection site before each injection
- Wipe vial tops with spirit before drawing insulin
- Roll cloudy insulin vials gently to re-suspend insulin (avoid shaking)
- Use palm rolling (not shaking) to re-suspend cloudy insulin vials before mixing

**Prerequisites:**
- Draw air equal to the dose into the vial before drawing insulin to avoid vacuum
- Inject sufficient air into both vials before drawing mixed doses
- Draw clear rapid or short-acting insulin first when mixing in the same syringe with NPH
- Inject air equal to the dose into the vial before drawing insulin to avoid vacuum formation


### insulin dose adjustment algorithm

insulin dose adjustment algorithm for T1DM. Adjust insulin doses based on daily blood glucose patterns, food consumption, and exercise.. Increase insulin (5-10% of TDD) at 4-6 hourly intervals during acute illness or sick days.. Calculate insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio (ICR) using 450/TDD (regular insulin) or 500/TDD (rapid-acting analogs).

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin dose adjustment algorithm for T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Adjust insulin doses based on daily blood glucose patterns, food consumption, and exercise.
- Increase insulin (5-10% of TDD) at 4-6 hourly intervals during acute illness or sick days.
- Calculate insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio (ICR) using 450/TDD (regular insulin) or 500/TDD (rapid-acting analogs).
- Calculate insulin sensitivity factor (ISF) using 1500/TDD (regular insulin) or 1800/TDD (rapid-acting analogs).
- Adjust pre-meal short-acting insulin based on blood glucose values, carbohydrate content, and exercise.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Daily pattern monitoring of blood glucose levels.
- Frequent blood glucose monitoring during exercise and sick days.
- Urine ketone testing during acute illness.
- Standardized carbohydrate counting for meal-related insulin adjustments.
- Small incremental adjustments for patients with high insulin sensitivity or low insulin doses.
- Increased fluids intake during acute illness
- Frequent monitoring of blood glucose during acute illness


### hypoglycemia risk assessment protocol

Review history of hypoglycemia at every clinical encounter for all individuals at risk for hypoglycemia. Screen individuals at risk for hypoglycemia for impaired hypoglycemia awareness at least annually. Screen individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia for fear of hypoglycemia at least annually

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypoglycemia risk assessment protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Review history of hypoglycemia at every clinical encounter for all individuals at risk for hypoglycemia
- Screen individuals at risk for hypoglycemia for impaired hypoglycemia awareness at least annually
- Screen individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia for fear of hypoglycemia at least annually
- Consider hypoglycemia risk when selecting diabetes medications and glycemic goals
- Prescribe glucagon for all individuals taking insulin or at high risk for hypoglycemia
- Correlate glucose readings from meters and CGM systems with symptoms and treatment to identify discrepancies
- Investigate causes of artifactual hypoglycemia (e.g., CGM sensor compression) when low sensor readings occur without symptoms in low-risk individuals

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals treated with insulin, sulfonylureas, or meglitinides are considered at risk for hypoglycemia
- Individuals with ≥1 major risk factor or multiple other risk factors are considered at high risk for hypoglycemia
- Prior hypoglycemic events, especially level 2 or 3 events, are the strongest risk factors for hypoglycemia recurrence
- Cognitive function should be routinely assessed among older adults with diabetes
- Clinicians should assess social, cultural, and economic risk factors such as food insecurity, low-income status, housing insecurity, and fasting for religious or cultural reasons
- Individuals treated with intensive insulin therapy (including multiple daily injections, CSII, or automated insulin delivery) are at highest risk
- Combining treatment with insulin and sulfonylureas further increases hypoglycemia risk

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Avoid using foods or beverages high in fat and/or protein for initial treatment of hypoglycemia
- Treat normal glucose values as hypoglycemic without confirmation
- Tolerate hypoglycemia without treatment due to lack of symptoms or to avoid hyperglycemia

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use CGM for individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia
- Glucagon preparations that do not require reconstitution are preferred
- Structured education for hypoglycemia prevention and treatment for all individuals taking insulin or at risk for hypoglycemia
- First aid kits should include oral glucose for treating hypoglycemia
- One or more episodes of level 2 or 3 hypoglycemia should prompt reevaluation of the treatment plan, including deintensifying or switching diabetes medications
- Use of CGM is beneficial and recommended for individuals at high risk for hypoglycemia


### hyperglycemia management in older adults

Hyperglycemia management in older adults in PALTC and end-of-life settings. Prioritize avoidance of hypoglycemia and severe hyperglycemia in PALTC residents with type 2 diabetes, using oral agents or basal insulin as alternatives to prandial insulin.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hyperglycemia management in older adults in PALTC and end-of-life settings

**Core Recommendations:**
- Prioritize avoidance of hypoglycemia and severe hyperglycemia in PALTC residents with type 2 diabetes, using oral agents or basal insulin as alternatives to prandial insulin.
- Implement standardized alert systems for blood glucose excursions (e.g., <70 mg/dL, >250 mg/dL, or symptomatic hyperglycemia) with timely notifications to healthcare professionals.
- Conduct assessments at least every 30 days for the first 90 days post-admission and every 60 days thereafter in PALTC, with treatment adjustments via telephone, fax, or in-person visits.
- In palliative care, deintensify or simplify diabetes medications to prioritize comfort, symptom management, and quality of life, avoiding strict glycemic control.
- Use CGM to monitor for hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia in PALTC residents and palliative care patients when frequent blood glucose monitoring is burdensome.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Residents must have documented hypoglycemia risk factors (e.g., impaired cognitive/renal function, polypharmacy, variable nutritional intake) before initiating intensive glycemic management.
- PALTC facilities must have standardized alert systems in place to notify healthcare professionals of blood glucose excursions.
- For palliative care, goals of care discussions must include the individual, care partners, and healthcare team to align treatment intensity with patient preferences.
- CGM use requires training for staff and integration into care plans for residents with insulin-treated diabetes in PALTC.
- Assessments in PALTC must account for life expectancy, functional status, and comorbid conditions to individualize glycemic targets.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults in PALTC or end-of-life settings.
- Routine use of sulfonylureas or insulin regimens with high hypoglycemia risk in frail older adults.
- Daily healthcare professional evaluations in PALTC beyond federal minimum assessment intervals.
- Rigid adherence to strict glycemic control in hospice or palliative care patients.
- A1C monitoring in end-of-life care due to limited utility in guiding treatment.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Oral agents (e.g., DPP-4 inhibitors, SGLT2 inhibitors) as first-line alternatives to basal insulin in PALTC residents with type 2 diabetes.
- CGM-guided insulin administration in long-term care facilities to reduce hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia.
- Deintensification protocols for diabetes medications in older adults with limited life expectancy or high hypoglycemia risk.
- Telehealth programs using CGM and connected insulin devices to improve glycemic management in nursing homes.
- Use of supportive and palliative care tools (e.g., SPICT, Gold Standards Framework) to guide end-of-life diabetes management.


### insulin delivery device recommendations

Insulin pens are preferred for people with insulin-requiring diabetes on multiple daily injections (MDI), though syringes may be used based on individual preference, insulin type, cost, and self-management capabilities. insulin delivery device recommendations. Insulin delivery devices and injection techniques. Pen-like devices and insulin cartridges deliver insulin subcutaneously through a needle and are easy to use. Insulin pumps with integrated algorithms can automatically stop insulin release 30 minutes before hypoglycemia onset

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin delivery device recommendations

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin pens are preferred for people with insulin-requiring diabetes on multiple daily injections (MDI), though syringes may be used based on individual preference, insulin type, cost, and self-management capabilities.
- Insulin pens or injection aids are recommended for people with dexterity issues, vision impairment, or when shared decision-making supports accurate dosing.
- Connected insulin pens are recommended for people with diabetes on multiple daily insulin injections to facilitate dose tracking and data sharing.
- FDA-approved insulin dose calculators/decision support systems may assist in calculating insulin doses.
- Automated insulin delivery (AID) systems are the preferred insulin delivery method over MDI, CSII, and sensor-augmented pumps in people with type 1 diabetes and select adults with type 2 diabetes.
- Insulin pens may allow people with vision impairment or dexterity issues to dose insulin accurately, and insulin injection aids are also available to assist with these issues.
- Connected insulin pens or pen caps can be programmed to calculate insulin doses, synced with select CGM systems, and provide downloadable data reports for real-time insulin dosing and retrospective review by clinicians.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be on multiple daily insulin injections (MDI) for pen or syringe recommendations.
- Consideration of patient dexterity, vision, cost, insulin type, and self-management capabilities for device selection.
- Shared decision-making should guide the choice of insulin delivery device, especially for those with dexterity or vision impairments.
- Patient must be using CSII or AID to ensure continued access across third-party payors.
- Use of FDA-approved insulin dose calculators requires professional input and education for initial dosing and ongoing adjustments.
- Insulin pens, once started, can be kept in use for variable durations based on the type of insulin, usually for 28 days, ranging from 14 to 56 days.
- Needle length considerations: shorter needles (4–5 mm) are recommended to lower the risk of intramuscular injection with erratic absorption and possibly lipohypertrophy.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of inhaled insulin as a primary insulin delivery method (unless preferred by the patient).
- Reliance solely on vial and syringe for insulin delivery without considering pen alternatives.
- Non-FDA-approved insulin dose calculators or decision support systems.
- Routine use of inhaled technosphere insulin as a primary insulin delivery method (unless preferred by the patient).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Connected insulin pens for real-time insulin dose logging and data sharing with clinicians.
- Insulin injection aids for people with dexterity or vision impairments to facilitate accurate dosing.
- AID systems as the preferred insulin delivery method over MDI, CSII, and sensor-augmented pumps in type 1 diabetes and select type 2 diabetes.
- FDA-approved insulin dose calculators/decision support systems for dose calculation assistance.
- Continued access to CSII and AID systems across third-party payors for eligible patients.
- Insulin pen caps placed on existing insulin pens may assist with calculating insulin doses and provide a memory function.
- AID systems are the preferred insulin delivery method over MDI, CSII, and sensor-augmented pumps in people with type 1 diabetes, adults with type 2 diabetes, children and adolescents with type 2 diabetes, and those with other forms of insulin-deficient diabetes.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin delivery devices and injection techniques

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Pen-like devices and insulin cartridges deliver insulin subcutaneously through a needle and are easy to use
- Insulin pumps with integrated algorithms can automatically stop insulin release 30 minutes before hypoglycemia onset
- Injections should be given into subcutaneous tissue at a 90° angle, or 45° angle with skin pinching for thin individuals or children
- Insulin injection sites include abdomen, thighs, arms, and buttocks, with abdomen and thighs being most convenient for self-injection
- Insulin analogues can be administered with or after a meal, whereas short-acting/premixed insulin requires a 30-minute interval before meals
- Used needles must be disposed of in a bio-safe manner to prevent sharps injuries and environmental contamination
- Exercise increases the rate of insulin absorption from injection sites, requiring potential dose adjustments or timing modifications

**Prerequisites:**
- Insulin pump use is restricted to diabetes specialists with relevant experience
- Pump algorithm is indicated for patients with brittle diabetes, multiple dose injections, and recurrent hypoglycemia episodes
- Insulin initiation can be performed on an outpatient basis without hospitalization
- Site rotation within one area is recommended to prevent lipohypertrophy or lipoatrophy
- Patient characteristics such as body habitus (thin individuals or children) determine the need for skin pinching and 45° injection angle
- Insulin pump use requires diabetes specialist oversight with relevant experience
- Site selection must consider variable absorption rates between anatomical locations (abdomen > arms > thighs > buttocks)


### DKA resuscitation protocol

Follow ABCs (airway, breathing, circulation) as the first principle of resuscitation in DKA. Administer isotonic saline bolus (10 ml/kg over 1 hour) before insulin therapy to stabilize cardiovascular status

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DKA resuscitation protocol

**Core Management Principles:**
- Follow ABCs (airway, breathing, circulation) as the first principle of resuscitation in DKA
- Administer isotonic saline bolus (10 ml/kg over 1 hour) before insulin therapy to stabilize cardiovascular status
- Initiate intravenous regular insulin infusion (0.05–0.1 U/kg/h) only after confirming serum potassium >3.3 mmol/L and at least 1 hour after starting fluids
- Start potassium supplementation concurrently with insulin therapy if serum potassium is 3.3–5.3 mmol/L, or delay insulin until potassium >3.3 mmol/L if hypokalemic
- Titrate insulin infusion to achieve blood glucose reduction of 50–75 mg/dl/hour and continue until resolution of DKA (BG <200 mg/dl, pH >7.3, bicarbonate >15 mmol/L)
- Administer a bolus dose of isotonic (0.9%) normal saline at 10 ml/kg intravenously over an hour or less before insulin
- Calculate total fluid requirement as the sum of maintenance requirement (1500 ml/m2/day) and fluid deficit (3%, 6%, or 9% of body weight based on severity)

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Confirm normal serum potassium before initiating intravenous insulin infusion
- Assess degree of dehydration and level of consciousness clinically
- Use isotonic saline for initial 5–6 hours, then switch to 0.45% saline if serum sodium is rising
- Add 5% dextrose to fluids when blood glucose falls below 200–250 mg/dl
- Monitor ECG for surrogate assessment of tissue potassium levels if serum potassium is unavailable
- Confirm normal urine output before initiating intravenous potassium supplementation
- Ensure serum potassium is >3.3 mmol/L before starting intravenous insulin therapy


### hypophosphatemia management in insulin therapy

Hypophosphatemia usually manifests after treatment initiation due to transcellular shift of phosphate from extracellular to intracellular compartment by insulin.. Encouraging early oral intake (<24 hours) may help prevent hypophosphatemia.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hypophosphatemia management in insulin therapy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Hypophosphatemia usually manifests after treatment initiation due to transcellular shift of phosphate from extracellular to intracellular compartment by insulin.
- Encouraging early oral intake (<24 hours) may help prevent hypophosphatemia.
- Severe hypophosphatemia (serum phosphorus <1 mg/dl) may manifest as muscle weakness and rhabdomyolysis.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Monitor serum phosphorus levels in patients receiving insulin therapy.
- Assess for symptoms of severe hypophosphatemia (e.g., muscle weakness, rhabdomyolysis).


### individualized care in older adults

Individualized care in older adults with diabetes. Screening for diabetes complications prioritized based on potential to impair functional status or quality of life. Blood pressure goals individualized (<130/80 mmHg if safe; <140/90 mmHg for those with poor health, limited life expectancy, or high adverse effect risk)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Individualized care in older adults with diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screening for diabetes complications prioritized based on potential to impair functional status or quality of life
- Blood pressure goals individualized (<130/80 mmHg if safe; <140/90 mmHg for those with poor health, limited life expectancy, or high adverse effect risk)
- Cardiovascular risk factor treatment individualized considering time frame of benefit and life expectancy
- Diabetes self-management education and support reassessed after clinical changes, hospitalizations, or functional decline
- Glycemic goals individualized based on life expectancy, cognitive/physical function, hypoglycemia risk, and patient preferences
- Avoidance of hypoglycemia and symptomatic hyperglycemia (>180 mg/dL) in older adults with complex health status to prevent dehydration, weakness, infection, and poor wound healing
- Deintensification of diabetes medications and removal of most agents for type 2 diabetes as organ failure develops in palliative/end-of-life care

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Life expectancy assessment using validated tools (e.g., LEAD) for older adults with diabetes
- Cognitive and physical functional assessment using age-normalized tools when self-care abilities decline
- Evaluation of health literacy, numeracy, and support systems at treatment onset and throughout care
- Consideration of competing mortality and time-to-benefit for intensive management decisions
- Shared decision-making with older adults to align treatment intensity with goals and preferences
- Assessment of oral health, vision, hearing loss, foot care needs, fall risk, and depression status to improve quality of life
- Evaluation of financial cost and difficulties of administration when balancing risks and benefits of diabetes medications

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for all older adults without consideration of individual factors
- Routine use of intensive glycemic management in frail older adults with limited life expectancy
- Reliance solely on A1C for glycemic monitoring in conditions affecting red blood cell turnover (e.g., kidney failure, recent blood loss)
- Aggressive blood pressure targets (<130/80 mmHg) for all older adults regardless of health status
- Routine use of antiplatelet therapy in older adults with diabetes without life expectancy ≥ trial time frames

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of LEAD tool to estimate life expectancy in older adults with diabetes for goal-setting
- CGM or blood glucose monitoring for glycemic management in conditions affecting A1C accuracy
- Deintensification of diabetes medications in palliative/end-of-life care to avoid hypoglycemia
- Prioritization of functional status and quality-of-life outcomes over strict glycemic targets
- Classification of older adults into health status categories (healthy, complex/intermediate, very complex) for tailored care
- Use of Time in Range (TIR) and Time Below Range (TBR) metrics alongside A1C for glycemic goal setting in older adults
- Classification of older adults into specific health status categories (Healthy, Complex/intermediate, Very complex) based on comorbidities, ADL impairments, and cognitive status


### hyperglycemic crisis risk factors

Hyperglycemic crisis risk factors and prevention. Individuals with type 1 diabetes/absolute insulin deficiency are at higher risk for hyperglycemic crises.. Younger age (especially 10–14 years) and adolescence are associated with increased DKA risk.. Prior history of hyperglycemic crises significantly increases recurrence risk.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hyperglycemic crisis risk factors and prevention

**Core Recommendations:**
- Individuals with type 1 diabetes/absolute insulin deficiency are at higher risk for hyperglycemic crises.
- Younger age (especially 10–14 years) and adolescence are associated with increased DKA risk.
- Prior history of hyperglycemic crises significantly increases recurrence risk.
- Psychosocial challenges (e.g., disordered eating, transition periods) contribute to DKA risk.
- Missed or inadequate insulin doses are a primary trigger for DKA.
- Female sex is associated with increased DKA risk.
- Designation as part of an underrepresented group (by race, ethnicity, or migration background) is associated with higher DKA risk.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Hemodynamically and cognitively intact individuals may treat mild DKA with oral hydration, frequent monitoring, and subcutaneous insulin if able to tolerate.
- Individuals with suspected HHS require immediate inpatient evaluation due to greater volume depletion and acute illness triggers.
- Screening for social determinants of health (e.g., insulin access, CGM availability) is required for individuals with prior DKA/HHS.
- Structured diabetes self-management education and support (including problem-solving) is recommended to prevent recurrence.
- Multisystemic behavioral interventions are often necessary for those with recurrent DKA.
- Individuals with suspected HHS require immediate inpatient evaluation due to greater volume depletion and acute illness triggers, even if hemodynamically and cognitively intact.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of insulin pump therapy is not associated with increased DKA risk in the current era (previously debated).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Access to CGM is associated with decreased risk of DKA recurrence.
- Psychological interventions, peer support, and behavioral family systems therapy are effective in reducing DKA admissions.
- Screening for social determinants of health (e.g., inadequate insulin access) and referral to community services are recommended.
- Individualized A1C targets and self-management strategies are emphasized for high-risk groups (e.g., adolescents).
- Access to CGM may decrease risk of DKA recurrence.


### hospital glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia management

Glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia management in hospitalized patients. Consider glucocorticoid type and duration of action when determining insulin treatment. Monitor typical glycemic patterns (normal/mild fasting hyperglycemia, increasing afternoon hyperglycemia peaking in evening) using CGM for patients on daily prednisone/prednisolone

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia management in hospitalized patients

**Core Recommendations:**
- Consider glucocorticoid type and duration of action when determining insulin treatment
- Monitor typical glycemic patterns (normal/mild fasting hyperglycemia, increasing afternoon hyperglycemia peaking in evening) using CGM for patients on daily prednisone/prednisolone
- Administer NPH insulin concomitantly with intermediate-acting steroids (e.g., prednisone) to match steroid-induced hyperglycemic response
- Use long-acting basal insulin for long-acting glucocorticoids (e.g., dexamethasone) or multidose/continuous glucocorticoid use
- Adjust insulin doses (basal, prandial, correction) by 40–60% or more for higher steroid doses, with daily monitoring and adjustments
- Administer NPH insulin in addition to basal-bolus insulin or oral glucose-lowering medications for patients on intermediate-acting steroids (e.g., prednisone) depending on prior diabetes medication history
- Use long-acting basal insulin for long-acting glucocorticoids (e.g., dexamethasone) or multidose/continuous glucocorticoid therapy to manage fasting blood glucose levels

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Glucocorticoid therapy must be ongoing or recently initiated
- Patient must be hospitalized and receiving glucocorticoids
- CGM or POC blood glucose monitoring must be available
- Prior diabetes medication history must be reviewed to determine insulin regimen
- Patient must have documented hyperglycemia or be at risk for glucocorticoid-induced hyperglycemia
- Prior diabetes medication history must be reviewed to determine whether NPH insulin should be added to basal-bolus insulin or oral agents for intermediate-acting steroids

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed insulin dosing without adjustment for glucocorticoid type/duration
- Routine use of sliding-scale insulin alone without basal/bolus components
- Ignoring glycemic patterns specific to glucocorticoid timing
- Using only prandial insulin without basal insulin for long-acting glucocorticoids
- Not accounting for increased insulin requirements with higher steroid doses

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of CGM to track glucocorticoid-specific glycemic patterns
- Concomitant NPH insulin administration with intermediate-acting steroids
- Increased insulin dose adjustments (40–60% or more) for higher steroid doses
- Daily insulin dose adjustments based on glycemia and anticipated glucocorticoid changes
- Emphasis on balancing time in range (70–180 mg/dL) with hypoglycemia risk
- Increasing the ratio of insulin to steroids is associated with improved time in range (70–180 mg/dL) but may increase hypoglycemia risk


### hypoglycemia recurrence risk factors

An episode of severe hypoglycemia increases the risk for subsequent events due to impaired counterregulation.. Hypoglycemia incidence peaks between midnight and 6:00 A.M. in individuals taking basal insulin.. 75% of individuals do not have their basal insulin dose adjusted after hypoglycemia recognition.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypoglycemia recurrence risk factors

**Core Recommendations:**
- An episode of severe hypoglycemia increases the risk for subsequent events due to impaired counterregulation.
- Hypoglycemia incidence peaks between midnight and 6:00 A.M. in individuals taking basal insulin.
- 75% of individuals do not have their basal insulin dose adjusted after hypoglycemia recognition.
- A fasting blood glucose of <100 mg/dL predicts next-day hypoglycemia.
- 84% of individuals with severe hypoglycemia (<40 mg/dL [<2.2 mmol/L]) had a preceding hypoglycemic episode (<70 mg/dL [<3.9 mmol/L]) during the same hospitalization
