**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Documented prior episode of hypoglycemia (<70 mg/dL [<3.9 mmol/L]) increases recurrence risk.
- Severe hypoglycemia defined as <40 mg/dL [<2.2 mmol/L].
- Hypoglycemia defined as <50 mg/dL [<2.8 mmol/L] in relevant studies.
- Hypoglycemia defined as <50 mg/dL [<2.8 mmol/L] in relevant studies for recurrence risk assessment

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Development of algorithms to predict inpatient hypoglycemia episodes.
- Validation of hypoglycemia prediction models for general use.
- Validation of hypoglycemia prediction models for general clinical use (e.g., post-discharge or outpatient settings)


### hospital glycemic management protocol

Individualized glycemic management considering prior insulin/noninsulin therapy, insulin resistance, A1C, current glucose, nutrition, diabetes duration, and treatment goals

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hospital glycemic management protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Individualized glycemic management considering prior insulin/noninsulin therapy, insulin resistance, A1C, current glucose, nutrition, diabetes duration, and treatment goals
- Use validated written/computerized protocols for continuous intravenous insulin infusion in critical care to achieve glycemic goals and avoid hypoglycemia
- Transition from IV insulin to subcutaneous basal insulin 2 hours before discontinuation to prevent rebound hyperglycemia
- Administer low-dose basal insulin analog (0.15–0.3 units/kg) alongside IV insulin in hyperglycemia/DKA to reduce infusion duration and hospital stay
- Use basal-bolus insulin (basal + prandial + correction) as preferred treatment for noncritically ill hospitalized individuals with adequate nutrition
- Provide prandial coverage with rapid-acting subcutaneous insulin for individuals eating but requiring continuous intravenous insulin infusion to prevent large fluctuations in IV insulin requirements and reduce hypoglycemia risk
- Use scheduled subcutaneous insulin orders for hyperglycemia management in hospitalized individuals with diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Prior home insulin/noninsulin therapy and doses must be assessed for individualized dosing
- Continuous IV insulin infusion requires predefined adjustments based on glycemic fluctuations and prior insulin rates
- Subcutaneous basal insulin must be given 2 hours before IV insulin discontinuation to prevent rebound hyperglycemia
- Basal-bolus insulin is preferred for noncritically ill patients with adequate nutritional intake
- Correctional insulin dosing scales should be proportional to total daily insulin dose (low <40 units/day, medium 40–80 units/day, high >80 units/day)
- Total daily insulin dose calculation should account for 60% of the insulin infusion rate to adjust for glucotoxicity when estimating from IV insulin
- Initial total daily insulin dose may be estimated from prior hospitalization insulin dose or 80% of home insulin dose if applicable

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Prolonged use of correction-only insulin (sliding scale) without basal insulin in noncritical care
- Use of insulin mixtures (e.g., 70/30, 75/25) due to increased hypoglycemia risk
- Routine use of concentrated insulins (e.g., U-500) without diabetes team consultation due to dosing errors and hypoglycemia risk
- Fixed A1C targets without individualized consideration of life expectancy, cognitive function, or hypoglycemia risk
- Unvalidated insulin infusion protocols without predefined rate adjustments
- Use of intermediate-acting insulin mixtures (e.g., 70/30 NPH/regular) due to increased hypoglycemia risk compared to basal-bolus therapy
- Prolonged use of correction-only insulin without basal insulin in noncritical care for type 2 diabetes with blood glucose ≥180 mg/dL

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Low-dose basal insulin analog (0.15–0.3 units/kg) added to IV insulin in hyperglycemia/DKA to reduce infusion duration and hospital stay
- Use of insulin pens with safety protocols (nursing education, bar code scanning) to improve safety and nurse satisfaction
- Weight-based total daily insulin dose (0.3–0.6 units/kg/day) as starting dose for hospitalized individuals
- Basal-bolus insulin plan improving glycemic outcomes and reducing complications compared to correction-only insulin in type 2 diabetes
- Transition protocols for IV insulin discontinuation with subcutaneous basal insulin to prevent rebound hyperglycemia
- Use of DPP-4i for mild hyperglycemia in appropriate hospitalized patients as an alternative to insulin
- Consideration of diabetes care team consultation when using concentrated insulins (e.g., U-300, U-200, U-500) to reduce dosing errors and hypoglycemia risk


### transition from IV to subcutaneous insulin

transition from IV to subcutaneous insulin in T1DM. Tapering of IV insulin infusion and initiation of a multiple-dose subcutaneous insulin regimen is recommended when plasma glucose is <200 mg/dl (11.1 mmol/L) and at least two parameters are achieved: AG <12 mmol/L, serum bicarbonate ≥15 mmol/L, venous pH >7.30, beta-hydroxybutyrate <1 mmol/L.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** transition from IV to subcutaneous insulin in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Tapering of IV insulin infusion and initiation of a multiple-dose subcutaneous insulin regimen is recommended when plasma glucose is <200 mg/dl (11.1 mmol/L) and at least two parameters are achieved: AG <12 mmol/L, serum bicarbonate ≥15 mmol/L, venous pH >7.30, beta-hydroxybutyrate <1 mmol/L.
- Insulin infusion should be continued for 1-2 hours after initiation of subcutaneous regular insulin and 30-60 minutes after initiation of subcutaneous rapid-acting insulin analog to prevent recurrence of hyperglycemia or ketoacidosis.
- Sudden discontinuation of IV insulin before the onset of subcutaneous insulin effect is discouraged due to risk of hyperglycemia or ketoacidosis recurrence.
- If the patient is unable to eat, continuation of IV insulin infusion is preferred over transitioning to subcutaneous insulin alone.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Plasma glucose <200 mg/dl (11.1 mmol/L) must be achieved before transitioning.
- At least two of the following must be met: AG <12 mmol/L, serum bicarbonate ≥15 mmol/L, venous pH >7.30, beta-hydroxybutyrate <1 mmol/L.
- Continuation of IV insulin for 1-2 hours (regular insulin) or 30-60 minutes (rapid-acting analog) after subcutaneous insulin initiation.


### sulphonylurea therapy protocol

Initiate therapy with the lowest effective dose and titrate upwards every two weeks until desired control or maximal dosage is reached. Administer sulphonylureas preferably 15 to 30 minutes before meals. Combine sulphonylureas with all other anti-hyperglycaemic agents except glinides and other sulphonylureas

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** sulphonylurea therapy protocol

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Initiate therapy with the lowest effective dose and titrate upwards every two weeks until desired control or maximal dosage is reached
- Administer sulphonylureas preferably 15 to 30 minutes before meals
- Combine sulphonylureas with all other anti-hyperglycaemic agents except glinides and other sulphonylureas
- Monitor for hypoglycaemia, especially with older and long-acting sulphonylureas like glibenclamide in the elderly
- Consider switching sulphonylureas in individual cases if benefit is observed, though it may not be long-lasting
- Monitor for rare but serious skin reactions (e.g., Stevens-Johnson syndrome, photosensitivity reactions, or granulomatous hepatitis) during sulphonylurea therapy

**Prerequisites:**
- Evaluate non-responsiveness factors such as weight gain, non-compliance to diet/exercise/drugs, inadequate dosage, impaired absorption, co-existing endocrine disorders, concomitant medications, infections, or stress
- Avoid sulphonylureas in patients with type 1 diabetes, renal insufficiency, hepatic insufficiency, ketoacidosis, acute myocardial infarction, extensive infections, or history of adverse reactions
- Assess for co-existing endocrine disorders (e.g., thyroid disease) when evaluating non-responsiveness to sulphonylureas


### sulfonylurea safety and selection

Sulfonylureas are associated with hypoglycemia, bone loss, and fracture risk and should be used with caution.. Shorter-acting sulfonylureas (e.g., glipizide) may be preferred, though glipizide has a higher risk of major adverse cardiovascular events.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Sulfonylurea safety and selection

**Core Recommendations:**
- Sulfonylureas are associated with hypoglycemia, bone loss, and fracture risk and should be used with caution.
- Shorter-acting sulfonylureas (e.g., glipizide) may be preferred, though glipizide has a higher risk of major adverse cardiovascular events.
- Glyburide, a longer-acting sulfonylurea with multiple active metabolites, should be avoided in older adults due to hypoglycemia risk.
- Antimicrobials (e.g., fluoroquinolones, sulfamethoxazole-trimethoprim) interact with sulfonylureas to increase hypoglycemia risk; sulfonylureas should be reduced or temporarily discontinued in these cases.
- Insulin secretagogues may need dose reduction or discontinuation during illness, fasting, or impaired eating patterns.
- Sulfonylureas may contribute to bone loss and fracture risk, independent of hypoglycemia, warranting caution in patients with osteoporosis or high fracture risk.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Consistent and adequate eating patterns are essential to reduce hypoglycemia risk.
- Older adults treated with insulin secretagogues should be routinely screened for hypoglycemia.
- Hypoglycemia awareness should be assessed in older adults on sulfonylureas.
- Prevention and treatment strategies for hypoglycemia should be reinforced in older adults.
- Renal function should be assessed before initiating sulfonylureas, as reduced clearance in renal impairment increases hypoglycemia risk.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Glyburide should be avoided in older adults due to its hypoglycemia risk.
- Longer-acting sulfonylureas with multiple active metabolites are discouraged in older adults.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Shorter-acting sulfonylureas (e.g., glipizide) may be preferred despite cardiovascular risk concerns.
- Routine screening for hypoglycemia in older adults on insulin secretagogues is recommended.
- Assessment of hypoglycemia awareness in older adults is emphasized.
- Consider alternative agents (e.g., DPP-4 inhibitors, SGLT2 inhibitors) in patients with a history of hypoglycemia unawareness or recurrent severe hypoglycemia.


### sulfonylurea dosing adjustment

Reduce dose if glucose levels are within individualized goal range and no hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia is present at baseline for new-generation sulfonylureas (glimepiride, gliclazide).. Replace older-generation sulfonylureas (glyburide) with newer-generation agents or reduce dose by 50%.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Sulfonylurea dosing adjustment

**Core Recommendations:**
- Reduce dose if glucose levels are within individualized goal range and no hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia is present at baseline for new-generation sulfonylureas (glimepiride, gliclazide).
- Replace older-generation sulfonylureas (glyburide) with newer-generation agents or reduce dose by 50%.
- Administer sulfonylureas at main mealtime to minimize hypoglycemia risk.
- Split twice-daily doses between two meals to optimize glycemic control.
- Consider dose reduction of newer-generation sulfonylureas (glimepiride, gliclazide) in patients with renal impairment due to prolonged drug half-life and increased hypoglycemia risk.
- Adjust sulfonylurea dosing based on glycemic variability patterns, not just A1C, to minimize hypoglycemia risk in patients with fluctuating glucose levels.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be on sulfonylurea therapy (new or older generation).
- Baseline glucose levels must be assessed to determine individualized targets.
- Absence of hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia at baseline is required for dose reduction.
- Patient must be able to adhere to meal timing for optimal dosing.
- Assess renal function (eGFR) before initiating or adjusting sulfonylurea therapy, with dose reduction or avoidance in patients with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73m².
- Evaluate patient’s meal timing consistency and carbohydrate intake patterns prior to sulfonylurea dosing adjustments to optimize timing and dose.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of older-generation sulfonylureas (glyburide) as first-line therapy due to higher hypoglycemia risk.
- Fixed-dose sulfonylurea regimens without adjustment based on glycemic response.
- Use of glyburide in patients with chronic kidney disease (CKD) due to accumulation of active metabolites and elevated hypoglycemia risk.
- Fixed-dose sulfonylurea regimens without consideration of patient-specific glycemic targets or hypoglycemia risk assessment.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Prefer newer-generation sulfonylureas (glimepiride, gliclazide) over older agents (glyburide).
- Individualized dose reduction based on glycemic control and hypoglycemia risk.
- Meal-based timing adjustments for sulfonylurea administration.
- Incorporate continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) data to guide sulfonylurea dose adjustments, particularly in patients with hypoglycemia unawareness or erratic glucose patterns.
- Prefer once-daily dosing of newer-generation sulfonylureas (e.g., glimepiride) over twice-daily regimens to reduce hypoglycemia risk in patients with adherence challenges.


### stress-induced hyperglycemia management

Stress-induced hyperglycemia management in diabetes. Assess need for more frequent glucose monitoring during stressful events (illness, trauma, surgery). Monitor urine or blood ketones in ketosis-prone individuals during stress. Reevaluate and adjust diabetes treatment during stressful events

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Stress-induced hyperglycemia management in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Assess need for more frequent glucose monitoring during stressful events (illness, trauma, surgery)
- Monitor urine or blood ketones in ketosis-prone individuals during stress
- Reevaluate and adjust diabetes treatment during stressful events
- Hold metformin and SGLT2 inhibitors if oral intake is compromised or acute kidney injury is suspected
- Consider holding GLP-1 receptor agonists during illnesses with significant gastrointestinal symptoms
- Assess for acute kidney injury (AKI) in patients on metformin or SGLT2 inhibitors during stress-induced hyperglycemia
- Evaluate for gastrointestinal symptom severity (e.g., nausea, vomiting, diarrhea) before holding GLP-1 receptor agonists during illness

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Underlying clinical complexity or polypharmacy increases risk for hyperglycemia/hypoglycemia
- Severe stress may precipitate life-threatening hyperglycemic crises requiring immediate care
- Ketosis-prone individuals require ketone monitoring during stress
- Oral intake status must be assessed before holding metformin/SGLT2 inhibitors
- Gastrointestinal symptom severity must be evaluated before holding GLP-1 receptor agonists
- Baseline renal function (e.g., serum creatinine, eGFR) must be assessed prior to holding metformin or SGLT2 inhibitors during stress
- Prior to holding GLP-1 receptor agonists, assess hydration status and risk of dehydration in patients with significant gastrointestinal symptoms

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Urine or blood ketone monitoring for ketosis-prone individuals during stress
- Holding metformin/SGLT2 inhibitors during compromised oral intake or AKI concern
- Holding GLP-1 receptor agonists during significant gastrointestinal illness
- Consider holding DPP-4 inhibitors in patients with severe gastrointestinal illness or acute kidney injury during stress


### inpatient diabetes management

Inpatient diabetes management with EHR decision support. Use of decision support tools and practice advisories in the EHR to guide healthcare professionals in following standardized recommendations. Management of acute illness to mitigate risks of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia in hospitalized patients with diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Inpatient diabetes management with EHR decision support

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use of decision support tools and practice advisories in the EHR to guide healthcare professionals in following standardized recommendations
- Management of acute illness to mitigate risks of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia in hospitalized patients with diabetes
- Integration of personal and hospital-owned diabetes devices and dosing algorithms into care protocols
- Implementation of standardized protocols for glycemic targets tailored to specific patient populations (e.g., ICU vs. non-ICU, surgical vs. medical patients)
- Routine screening for diabetes distress and hypoglycemia awareness in hospitalized patients with diabetes

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Acute illness present in patients with diabetes requiring inpatient management
- Access to EHR-based decision support tools and practice advisories
- Availability of diabetes devices and dosing algorithms for clinical use
- Presence of a dedicated inpatient diabetes care team or endocrinology consultation service for complex cases
- Established protocols for insulin pump and continuous glucose monitor (CGM) management during hospitalization

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of sliding-scale insulin regimens as monotherapy for glycemic control in hospitalized patients

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Enhanced use of EHR decision support tools for inpatient diabetes management
- Adoption of personal and hospital-owned diabetes devices and dosing algorithms
- Focus on transitions from inpatient to outpatient care for diabetes management
- Integration of real-time CGM data into EHR decision support systems for dynamic insulin dosing adjustments
- Mandatory discharge planning protocols for diabetes education and follow-up within 7–14 days post-discharge


### insulin initiation and titration protocol

Initiate insulin on outpatient basis without hospitalization. Start with small doses and titrate every three days based on blood glucose profile. Begin with 0.2 units/kg/day insulin dose, individualized to patient needs

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin initiation and titration protocol

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Initiate insulin on outpatient basis without hospitalization
- Start with small doses and titrate every three days based on blood glucose profile
- Begin with 0.2 units/kg/day insulin dose, individualized to patient needs
- Use two or more insulin injections when single injection plus oral agents fail to maintain control
- Prefer premixed NPH and regular insulin regimens (e.g., 25:75, 30:70, 50:50) before breakfast and dinner
- Modify insulin dose every three days based on blood glucose profile

**Prerequisites:**
- Combination of oral antihyperglycaemic agents no longer maintains desired glycemic control
- Blood glucose profile monitoring for dose adjustment
- Careful monitoring in older persons to avoid hypoglycemia
- Individualize insulin dose based on blood glucose profile and clinical setting


### insulin regimen and action profiles

Short-acting recombinant regular human insulin is the most commonly used bolus (prandial) insulin to prevent post-meal glucose elevation.. Regular insulin should be administered 20-30 minutes before meals for optimum effect; its effect peaks at 3-4 hours and total duration of action is approximately 6-8 hours.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin regimen and action profiles

**Core Management Principles:**
- Short-acting recombinant regular human insulin is the most commonly used bolus (prandial) insulin to prevent post-meal glucose elevation.
- Regular insulin should be administered 20-30 minutes before meals for optimum effect; its effect peaks at 3-4 hours and total duration of action is approximately 6-8 hours.
- Intermediate-acting insulin (NPH) is used as a basal insulin in split-mix, pre-mix, or basal-bolus regimens.
- NPH insulin has a pronounced peak after 6-8 hours and the effect tails off in 12-14 hours, unlike newer basal insulin formulations.
- Regular insulin can be used via the intravenous route in diabetic ketoacidosis, perioperative period, or labor.
- Long-acting insulin analogs (e.g., glargine, detemir) are mentioned as newer basal insulin formulations with flatter, peakless action profiles compared to NPH insulin.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Regular insulin must be administered 20-30 minutes before meals for optimal postprandial glucose control.
- NPH insulin cannot be given by intravenous route or in insulin pumps.
- Basal-bolus regimens require careful titration of intermediate-acting (NPH) or long-acting insulin analogs to avoid nocturnal hypoglycemia due to NPH's pronounced peak.


### insulin storage guidelines

Store insulin not in use in the lower compartment of the refrigerator door at 2-8° C. Avoid freezing insulin or exposing it to direct sunlight/heat. Prevent excess agitation to maintain insulin potency. Use earthenware pot of water (in a plastic bag) for storage if refrigeration is unavailable

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin storage guidelines

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Store insulin not in use in the lower compartment of the refrigerator door at 2-8° C
- Avoid freezing insulin or exposing it to direct sunlight/heat
- Prevent excess agitation to maintain insulin potency
- Use earthenware pot of water (in a plastic bag) for storage if refrigeration is unavailable
- Discard regular insulin if hazy or if cloudy insulin cannot be re-suspended
- Insulin should not be kept in the freezer compartment of the refrigerator

**Prerequisites:**
- Refrigeration required for insulin not in use
- Storage temperature must be maintained between 2-8° C
- Insulin not in use must be refrigerated in the lower compartment of the refrigerator door


### insulin therapy in older adults

Insulin therapy requires good visual, motor, cognitive abilities for dosing, administration, and hypoglycemia management. Insulin doses and plans should be individualized to glycemic goals and hypoglycemia avoidance

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin therapy in older adults

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin therapy requires good visual, motor, cognitive abilities for dosing, administration, and hypoglycemia management
- Insulin doses and plans should be individualized to glycemic goals and hypoglycemia avoidance
- Age alone does not limit technology use; CGM or automated insulin delivery systems should be encouraged if safe and effective
- Once-daily basal insulin is preferred over multiple daily injections in many older adults
- Insulin pens should be preferred over syringes for older adults with functional impairment if affordable
- Simplification of insulin regimens should follow an algorithmic approach to match self-management abilities and support needs
- Shared decision-making should include care partners and social support networks in diabetes management discussions

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Good visual, motor, and cognitive abilities for insulin management or caregiver support
- Individualized glycemic goals based on patient-specific factors
- Assessment of social support and living situation for tailored care planning
- Evaluation of functional status and coexisting chronic illnesses
- Consideration of affordability for insulin pens
- Evaluation of living situation (e.g., assisted living, nursing home, home) to determine medication administration support needs
- Assessment of access to long-term services and support to ensure continuity of care

**Previous Recommendations:**
- NPH insulin for older adults due to higher risk of hypoglycemia-related emergency visits
- Multiple daily insulin injections for older adults with advanced complications or limited support
- Rigid, complex insulin regimens without simplification
- Ignoring social support and living situation in care planning
- Strict metabolic management in palliative care without symptom prioritization

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Encouragement of CGM or automated insulin delivery systems in capable older adults
- Preference for once-daily basal insulin over multiple daily injections
- Simplification of insulin plans to match self-management abilities and support
- Inclusion of care partners and social support networks in diabetes management
- Tailored care plans considering transitions to PALTC and palliative care goals
- Use of algorithmic approaches (e.g., Figure 13.3) to guide insulin regimen simplification in older adults
- Inclusion of care partners in shared decision-making for insulin therapy and glycemic goals


### injectable therapy categories

injectable therapy categories for type 2 diabetes. Use insulin therapy as a key injectable option for type 2 diabetes management. Consider non-insulin injectable therapy (GLP-1 receptor agonists) for patients requiring injectable therapy. Select injectable therapy based on patient-specific factors such as glycemic control, comorbidities, and risk of hypoglycemia

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** injectable therapy categories for type 2 diabetes

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Use insulin therapy as a key injectable option for type 2 diabetes management
- Consider non-insulin injectable therapy (GLP-1 receptor agonists) for patients requiring injectable therapy
- Select injectable therapy based on patient-specific factors such as glycemic control, comorbidities, and risk of hypoglycemia
- Consider initiating injectable therapy (insulin or GLP-1 receptor agonists) in patients with marked hyperglycemia (e.g., HbA1c ≥9% or fasting plasma glucose ≥250 mg/dL) despite optimal oral therapy
- Use GLP-1 receptor agonists as first-line injectable therapy in patients with type 2 diabetes and established cardiovascular disease or high cardiovascular risk, unless contraindicated

**Prerequisites:**
- Prioritize oral anti-hyperglycemic drugs before considering injectable therapy unless marked hyperglycemia or contraindications exist
- Assess patient eligibility for GLP-1 receptor agonists based on cardiovascular risk and renal function
- Assess patient’s renal function (eGFR) before initiating GLP-1 receptor agonists, as some agents require dose adjustments or are contraindicated in severe renal impairment
- Evaluate patient’s body weight and hypoglycemia risk when selecting between insulin and GLP-1 receptor agonists


### insulin deintensification strategy

Insulin deintensification strategy in older adults. Simplify insulin administration plans by reducing complexity (e.g., fewer administration times, fewer blood glucose checks, and eliminating calculations like sliding-scale insulin or insulin-carbohydrate ratios).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin deintensification strategy in older adults

**Core Recommendations:**
- Simplify insulin administration plans by reducing complexity (e.g., fewer administration times, fewer blood glucose checks, and eliminating calculations like sliding-scale insulin or insulin-carbohydrate ratios).
- Deintensify or deprescribe insulin therapy by decreasing dose/frequency or discontinuing treatment entirely when severe/recurrent hypoglycemia occurs, regardless of A1C.
- Individualize glycemic goals based on health status, cognitive/functional capacity, and social circumstances (e.g., A1C <7.0–7.5% for healthy individuals, <8.0% for complex/intermediate cases).
- Use CGM in PALTC settings to monitor hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia, despite staff training requirements, as it provides prompt data for insulin dose adjustments.
- Assess mobility, mentation, medications, and patient preferences to tailor glucose-lowering agents and devices (e.g., CGM, insulin pumps) in post-acute and long-term care settings.
- Incorporate diabetes education/training for staff in post-acute and long-term care settings to improve management of older adults with diabetes, including CGM devices, insulin pumps, and advanced insulin delivery systems (Recommendation 13.16).
- Assess mobility, mentation, medications, and management preferences to tailor glycemic goals and glucose-lowering agents/devices in PALTC settings (Recommendation 13.17).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Severe or recurrent hypoglycemia on insulin or sulfonylureas, regardless of A1C.
- Wide glucose excursions or significant glycemic variability.
- Cognitive or functional decline, polypharmacy, or loss of care partners.
- Residence in PALTC settings (e.g., nursing homes, skilled nursing facilities) where staff may lack familiarity with advanced diabetes technologies.
- Acute illness, weight loss, anorexia, or short-term cognitive/physical decline in community-dwelling individuals receiving short-term care.
- Staff training in diabetes detection, identification of complications, glycemic monitoring, individualized glycemic goal determination, medication and nutritional considerations, oral care, foot assessment, wound and infection concerns, and institutional quality assessment in PALTC settings.
- Development of facility-specific policies and procedures for prevention, recognition, and management of hypoglycemia and symptomatic hyperglycemia in PALTC facilities.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance on fixed numeric A1C targets in older adults without individualized consideration.
- Complex insulin regimens requiring frequent calculations (e.g., sliding-scale insulin, insulin-carbohydrate ratios) in high-risk older adults.
- Uncritical continuation of prehospitalization treatment plans if they increased in complexity during hospitalization.
- Assumption that all older adults can safely manage advanced insulin delivery systems (e.g., pumps, CGM) without staff training.
- Use of sulfonylureas in older adults with high hypoglycemia risk without deintensification.
- Reliance on fixed numeric A1C targets in community-dwelling individuals receiving short-term care in skilled nursing facilities without individualized glucose goals (e.g., glucose 100–200 mg/dL preferred).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Real-time CGM use in PALTC facilities to guide insulin dosing, shown to be safe and effective compared to point-of-care BGM.
- Education and training for PALTC staff on diabetes management, CGM devices, insulin pumps, and hypoglycemia recognition.
- Development of facility-specific policies for hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia management in PALTC settings.
- Prioritization of individualized glycemic goals (e.g., glucose 100–200 mg/dL in skilled nursing facilities) over rigid A1C targets.
- Integration of care partner assessments and social circumstances (e.g., loss of support) into deintensification decisions.
- Real-time CGM use in PALTC facilities for up to 60 days shown to be safe and effective in guiding insulin doses compared with point-of-care BGM in a recent RCT (162).
- High prevalence of hypoglycemia in PALTC facilities among older adults using insulin or sulfonylureas, necessitating CGM for prompt detection despite staff training challenges.


### insulin-related adverse events in hospitals

Insulin dosing errors, missed doses, and administration errors (incorrect type/timing) are common causes of adverse events in hospitalized patients.. Iatrogenic hypoglycemia is often due to improper prescribing of glucose-lowering medications or inadequate management of the first hypoglycemic episode.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Insulin-related adverse events in hospitals

**Core Recommendations:**
- Insulin dosing errors, missed doses, and administration errors (incorrect type/timing) are common causes of adverse events in hospitalized patients.
- Iatrogenic hypoglycemia is often due to improper prescribing of glucose-lowering medications or inadequate management of the first hypoglycemic episode.
- Hypoglycemia risk is elevated in patients with kidney failure, advanced age, comorbidities (heart/liver failure, sepsis, malnutrition), and medication-related factors (e.g., sudden corticosteroid reduction).
- Root cause analysis and aggregation of hypoglycemic episodes are recommended to address systemic issues.
- CGM and AID devices may help mitigate hypoglycemia by providing early alerts to care staff.
- Sudden reduction of corticosteroid dose, reduced oral intake, emesis, or inappropriate timing of short-/rapid-acting insulin doses in relation to meals can precipitate hypoglycemia
- Unexpected interruption of enteral or parenteral feedings or reduced infusion rate of intravenous dextrose may lead to hypoglycemia

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Proactive surveillance of glycemic outliers and an interprofessional, data-driven approach to glycemic management.
- Blood glucose monitoring every 15 minutes during hypoglycemia treatment until stabilization above 70 mg/dL (3.9 mmol/L).
- Patient-specific factors (e.g., kidney failure, comorbidities) must be considered when assessing hypoglycemia risk.
- Root cause evaluation of all hypoglycemic episodes to identify systemic issues.
- Use of fast-acting carbohydrate, intravenous glucose, or glucagon for hypoglycemia treatment based on patient status.
- Blood glucose monitoring every 15 minutes during hypoglycemia treatment until stabilization above 70 mg/dL (3.9 mmol/L) is required for all patients, including those with NPO status receiving intravenous glucose or glucagon

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors in older adults.
- Routine use of sliding-scale insulin without proactive glycemic management.
- Delayed or missed blood glucose checks as an acceptable practice.
- Uninterrupted enteral/parenteral feedings without monitoring for hypoglycemia risk.
- Reliance solely on reactive hypoglycemia management without preventive strategies.
- Routine use of sliding-scale insulin without proactive glycemic management or patient-specific adjustments is no longer endorsed

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Implementation of bundled preventive therapies (e.g., CGM, interprofessional glycemic management) to reduce hypoglycemic events by 56–80%.
- Use of CGM to alert care staff of impending hypoglycemia for early intervention.
- Adoption of AID devices to help avoid hypoglycemia in hospitalized patients.
- Mandatory root cause analysis and aggregation of hypoglycemic episodes for systemic improvement.
- Standardized hypoglycemia treatment protocols (15 g fast-acting carbohydrate, IV glucose, or glucagon) with frequent monitoring.
- Use of CGM to alert care staff of impending hypoglycemia for early intervention in real-time
- Adoption of AID devices in hospitalized patients to help avoid hypoglycemia through automated insulin delivery


### intensive insulin therapy in ICU

Intensive insulin therapy targeting 80–110 mg/dL (4.4–6.1 mmol/L) is no longer recommended due to increased hypoglycemia and mortality risks.. Moderate glycemic goals of 140–180 mg/dL (7.8–10.0 mmol/L) are recommended for most critically ill individuals.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Intensive insulin therapy in ICU

**Core Recommendations:**
- Intensive insulin therapy targeting 80–110 mg/dL (4.4–6.1 mmol/L) is no longer recommended due to increased hypoglycemia and mortality risks.
- Moderate glycemic goals of 140–180 mg/dL (7.8–10.0 mmol/L) are recommended for most critically ill individuals.
- More stringent goals (110–140 mg/dL) may be appropriate for selected patients (e.g., cardiac surgery) if hypoglycemia can be avoided.
- Persistent hyperglycemia ≥180 mg/dL (≥10.0 mmol/L) warrants insulin initiation.
- Clinical judgment must incorporate illness severity, nutritional status, and medication effects (e.g., glucocorticoids).
- Fasting glucose levels <100 mg/dL (<5.6 mmol/L) are predictors of hypoglycemia within the next 24 hours and necessitate basal insulin dose adjustments.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Persistent hyperglycemia ≥180 mg/dL (≥10.0 mmol/L) must be present before initiating insulin therapy.
- Selected patients (e.g., cardiac surgery) may require more stringent glycemic goals if hypoglycemia risk is low.
- Ongoing assessment of glucose measures, illness severity, and nutritional status is required.
- Concomitant medications affecting glucose levels (e.g., glucocorticoids) must be considered.
- Terminally ill patients or those with advanced kidney failure may tolerate higher glycemic levels (up to 250 mg/dL).
- Insulin and/or other therapies should be initiated for the treatment of persistent hyperglycemia ≥180 mg/dL (≥10.0 mmol/L).
- Glycemic goals of 110–140 mg/dL (6.1–7.8 mmol/L) may be appropriate for selected individuals (e.g., critically ill individuals undergoing cardiac surgery) only if they can be achieved without significant hypoglycemia.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Intensive insulin therapy targeting 80–110 mg/dL (4.4–6.1 mmol/L) in all ICU patients.
- Fixed numeric glycemic targets without patient-specific adjustments.
- Routine use of tight glycemic control in medical and surgical ICUs without risk stratification.
- Routine use of tight glycemic control (80–110 mg/dL) in medical and surgical ICUs without risk stratification.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Moderate glycemic goals of 140–180 mg/dL (7.8–10.0 mmol/L) for most critically ill individuals.
- More stringent goals (110–140 mg/dL) for selected patients if hypoglycemia can be avoided.
- Initiation of insulin therapy only for persistent hyperglycemia ≥180 mg/dL (≥10.0 mmol/L).
- Higher glycemic targets (up to 250 mg/dL) for terminally ill or high-risk patients.
- Emphasis on avoiding hypoglycemia, with fasting glucose <100 mg/dL (<5.6 mmol/L) requiring basal insulin adjustments.
- Higher glycemic levels (up to 250 mg/dL [13.9 mmol/L]) may be acceptable in selected populations (terminally ill individuals with short life expectancy, advanced kidney failure [and/or on dialysis], high risk for hypoglycemia, and/or labile glycemic excursions).
- For hospitalized individuals without critical illness, a glycemic goal of 100–180 mg/dL (5.6–10.0 mmol/L) is recommended, whether due to newly diagnosed diabetes, stress hyperglycemia, or pre-existing diabetes.


### DKA and HHS management

Administer intravenous fluids, insulin, and electrolytes with close monitoring during treatment. Ensure timely transition to maintenance subcutaneous insulin administration. Identify and treat the precipitating cause of DKA or HHS

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** DKA and HHS management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Administer intravenous fluids, insulin, and electrolytes with close monitoring during treatment
- Ensure timely transition to maintenance subcutaneous insulin administration
- Identify and treat the precipitating cause of DKA or HHS
- Use clinical judgment for DKA resolution criteria (venous pH >7.3 or bicarbonate >18 mmol/L and plasma/capillary ketones <0.6 mmol/L)
- Use clinical judgment for HHS resolution criteria (serum osmolality <300 mOsm/kg, urine output >0.5 mL/kg/h, and glucose <250 mg/dL)
- Use calculated serum osmolality (2[Na+] + glucose/18 + BUN/2.8) for HHS resolution assessment

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Meet diagnostic criteria for DKA or HHS (Table 16.1)
- Confirm DKA diagnosis with either hyperglycemia or prior history of diabetes (including euglycemic DKA cases)
- Assess for precipitating factors such as reduced food intake, pregnancy, alcohol use, liver failure, or SGLT2 inhibitor therapy
- Monitor for mixed DKA-HHS presentations
- Evaluate patient-specific risk factors for hyperglycemic crises (type 1 or type 2 diabetes)
- Confirm DKA diagnosis with prior history of diabetes even in the absence of current hyperglycemia (euglycemic DKA)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Withholding insulin until glucose stops dropping with fluid administration alone
- Withholding insulin until glucose stabilizes with fluid therapy alone (explicitly discouraged in favor of early insulin initiation)

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of euglycemic DKA in diagnostic considerations (plasma glucose <200 mg/dL [<11.1 mmol/L])
- Emphasis on individualized discharge education for recognition, prevention, and management of DKA/HHS
- Use of clinical judgment over strict numeric thresholds for discharge criteria
- Recognition of rising hyperglycemic crisis rates in both type 1 and type 2 diabetes
- Include calculated serum osmolality in HHS resolution criteria alongside urine output and glucose thresholds


### bedtime insulin addition strategy

Add intermediate, long, or short-acting insulin at bedtime or morning based on blood glucose profile. Continue oral drugs at optimal doses when adding insulin. Reduce or stop secretagogues when premixed or multiple-dose insulin is used

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** bedtime insulin addition strategy

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Add intermediate, long, or short-acting insulin at bedtime or morning based on blood glucose profile
- Continue oral drugs at optimal doses when adding insulin
- Reduce or stop secretagogues when premixed or multiple-dose insulin is used
- Consider reducing or stopping sulfonylureas when adding bedtime insulin to minimize hypoglycemia risk

**Prerequisites:**
- Glycemic control not achieved with maximum tolerable dose of single or combination oral agents
- Blood glucose profile must guide timing (bedtime vs. morning) of insulin addition


### carbohydrate counting in insulin therapy

Carbohydrate counting is essential for patients using continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) and beneficial for those on basal-bolus regimens.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** carbohydrate counting in insulin therapy

**Core Management Principles:**
- Carbohydrate counting is essential for patients using continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) and beneficial for those on basal-bolus regimens.
- Three levels of carbohydrate counting are defined: Level 1 (consistent carbohydrate intake), Level 2 (pattern management), and Level 3 (insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios).
- Level 3 (ICRs) is most recommended for patients on multiple daily injections (MDI) or CSII, with initial ICR calculated as 500/TDD (or 450/TDD for regular insulin).
- Divide daily calorie intake into 6-7 meals (3 major meals and 3-4 snacks), with specific caloric distribution (breakfast 20%, lunch/dinner 25-30%, snacks 10%).
- Bedtime snack should include at least 7-8 g of protein to prevent nocturnal hypoglycemia.
- Use of 10-12 g carbohydrate portions or 15 g carbohydrate exchanges as alternative counting methods (none superior to gram counting)
- Adjustment of insulin doses for food and exercise based on pattern management (Level 2) by monitoring blood glucose responses

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patients on MDI or CSII should use insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios (ICRs) for prandial insulin dose adjustment.
- Major meals and snacks containing >10-15 g carbohydrates must be covered with a bolus insulin dose.
- Meal timing should be consistent with no significant deviations to match insulin regimen.
- Nutritional intake must align with the specific insulin regimen (e.g., fixed vs. flexible eating patterns).
- Fine-tuning of ICRs requires checking blood glucose before and 2 hours post-meal to maintain BG increment <60 mg/dl.
- ICR calculation must account for patient-specific factors (age, sex, pubertal status, duration of diagnosis, and activity)
- Meal timing should have no significant and frequent deviations to align with insulin regimen


### basal-correction insulin protocol

Basal-correction insulin protocol for type 1 diabetes. Correctional insulin alone is contraindicated for type 1 diabetes due to risk of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia. Insulin schedule must include basal and correction components for all hospitalized individuals with type 1 diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Basal-correction insulin protocol for type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Correctional insulin alone is contraindicated for type 1 diabetes due to risk of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia
- Insulin schedule must include basal and correction components for all hospitalized individuals with type 1 diabetes
- Basal insulin must not be held, even for those taking nothing by mouth or during care transitions
- Prandial insulin should be added when individuals are eating
- EHR policies and practice alerts must ensure basal insulin is administered
- Insulin pump or infusion basal insulin must be continued without interruption during procedures or imaging requiring contrast, with appropriate adjustments to prevent hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have type 1 diabetes
- Hospitalized individual status
- Basal insulin requirement must be accounted for
- Prescriber and nursing education must be ongoing
- EHR policies and practice alerts must be implemented
- Basal insulin dose must be individualized based on prior insulin requirements, glycemic targets, and clinical status (e.g., renal function, corticosteroid use)

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Correctional insulin alone as a standalone protocol
- Holding basal insulin during care transitions
- Relying solely on premeal glucose levels for insulin dosing
- Relying on sliding-scale insulin regimens as the primary insulin strategy for hospitalized individuals with type 1 diabetes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Mandatory basal-correction insulin protocol for type 1 diabetes
- EHR policies to prevent basal insulin omission
- Continuous education for prescribers and nursing staff
- Prandial insulin addition when eating
- Basal insulin administration via subcutaneous, pump, or infusion
- Mandatory documentation of basal insulin administration timing and dose in the EHR to ensure accountability and traceability


### diabetic ketoacidosis management

Monitor glucose levels. Check for urine ketones. Start intravenous saline infusion at 1 litre in half hour immediately. Refer to hospital. Administer intravenous insulin after initial fluid resuscitation. Monitor serum potassium levels and correct hypokalemia before starting insulin therapy

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** diabetic ketoacidosis management

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Monitor glucose levels
- Check for urine ketones
- Start intravenous saline infusion at 1 litre in half hour immediately
- Refer to hospital
- Administer intravenous insulin after initial fluid resuscitation
- Monitor serum potassium levels and correct hypokalemia before starting insulin therapy
- Check arterial blood gases (pH, bicarbonate) to confirm DKA diagnosis

**Prerequisites:**
- Ensure continuous cardiac monitoring during initial fluid resuscitation
- Confirm absence of contraindications to insulin therapy (e.g., severe hypokalemia)

## Diabetes Bone Health Management

Diabetes Bone Health Management integrates fracture risk assessment, targeted screening, and glycemic control strategies to mitigate elevated fracture risks in individuals with diabetes, emphasizing proactive bone health interventions alongside diabetes care.

**Key Features:**
- Diabetes-related fracture risk assessment: Quantifies elevated fracture risk (34% overall increase) in type 1 and type 2 diabetes across sexes
- Diabetes-specific bone health screening: Recommends BMD testing for high-risk groups (e.g., postmenopausal women, men ≥50 with diabetes-specific factors)
- Glycemic management for bone health: Integrates physical activity, nutrition (protein/calcium/vitamin D), smoking cessation, and pharmacological treatments to support bone metabolism

**Notable Details:**
- Fracture risk is 34% higher in people with diabetes compared to those without
- BMD testing is advised for individuals aged ≥65 years regardless of sex
- Postmenopausal women and men ≥50 with diabetes-specific risk factors should undergo BMD testing
- Moderate physical activity is prioritized to improve muscle health, gait, and balance
- Daily intake of protein, calcium, and vitamin D is emphasized for bone health
- Smoking cessation is recommended as part of fracture prevention strategies
- Individual fracture risk and medication effects on bone metabolism are considered in type 2 diabetes care plans
- Glycemic management includes pharmacological treatments targeting bone health

**Topics in this group:**

### diabetes-related fracture risk assessment

Fracture risk is significantly increased in people with type 1 or type 2 diabetes in both sexes, with a 34% overall increase compared to those without diabetes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes-related fracture risk assessment

**Core Recommendations:**
- Fracture risk is significantly increased in people with type 1 or type 2 diabetes in both sexes, with a 34% overall increase compared to those without diabetes.
- Type 1 diabetes increases fracture risk by 4.35x for hip fractures, 1.83x for upper-limb fractures, and 1.97x for ankle fractures, occurring 10–15 years earlier than in non-diabetic individuals.
- Type 2 diabetes increases hip fracture risk by 1.79x and vertebral fracture risk by 35%, despite normal or higher BMD, with greater bone loss velocity with age.
- Fracture risk escalates with poor glycemic control (A1C >9% increases risk by 29%), longer diabetes duration (>10 years), hypoglycemia (RR 1.52), and presence of microvascular complications.
- BMD underestimates fracture risk in type 1 diabetes, while type 2 diabetes shows higher BMD but lower bone strength and impaired microarchitecture.
- Fracture risk in type 1 diabetes is independently associated with microvascular complications or neuropathy (RR not specified).
- Fracture risk in type 1 diabetes is increased with average A1C >7.9% (RR 3.57), diabetes duration >26 years (RR 7.6), and family history of fractures (RR 2.64).

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Assess fracture history and risk factors in all people with diabetes.
- Measure BMD via DXA in older adults with type 2 diabetes (≥65 years) or younger individuals (≥50 years) with diabetes-related risk factors (e.g., insulin use, diabetes duration >10 years).
- Consider BMD assessment in type 1 diabetes after age 50 or earlier with low-trauma fractures or additional risk factors.
- Monitor BMD every 2–3 years in type 2 diabetes, or every 2 years in individuals undergoing metabolic surgery.
- Use FRAX with adjustments (e.g., T-score reduction by 0.5 SD, age increase by 10 years) or trabecular bone score to improve fracture risk prediction in type 2 diabetes.
- Assess fracture history and risk factors in all people with diabetes before recommending BMD measurement.
- Consider DXA-assisted vertebral fracture assessment as a low-cost alternative to traditional lateral spine X-rays in type 2 diabetes.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance on FRAX without adjustments for type 2 diabetes, as it systematically underestimates fracture risk in this population.
- Use of standard BMD thresholds without considering diabetes-specific adjustments (e.g., T-score interpretation without -0.5 SD correction).
- Assuming normal BMD in type 2 diabetes indicates low fracture risk, given the disconnect between BMD and actual fracture risk.
- Routine bone densitometry in children/adolescents with type 1 diabetes without additional risk factors (e.g., celiac disease).

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Adjust FRAX inputs for type 2 diabetes (e.g., T-score reduction by 0.5 SD, age increase by 10 years, or trabecular bone score incorporation).
- Recommend DXA-assisted vertebral fracture assessment as a low-cost alternative to traditional lateral spine X-rays.
- Incorporate trabecular bone score (TBS) to enhance fracture risk prediction in type 2 diabetes.
- Monitor BMD more frequently (every 2 years) in individuals undergoing metabolic surgery.
- Consider BMD assessment in type 1 diabetes after age 50 or earlier with low-trauma fractures or additional risk factors.
- Incorporate diabetes-specific adjustments (e.g., T-score reduction by 0.5 SD) into FRAX inputs for type 2 diabetes to improve fracture risk prediction.
- Use rheumatoid arthritis input as a surrogate for type 2 diabetes in FRAX when no other adjustments are available.


### diabetes-specific bone health screening

Diabetes-specific bone health screening and fracture risk management. Perform BMD testing in individuals aged ≥65 years. Perform BMD testing in postmenopausal women and men aged ≥50 years with diabetes-specific risk factors. Consider individual fracture risk and medication effects on bone metabolism in type 2 diabetes care plans

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes-specific bone health screening and fracture risk management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Perform BMD testing in individuals aged ≥65 years
- Perform BMD testing in postmenopausal women and men aged ≥50 years with diabetes-specific risk factors
- Consider individual fracture risk and medication effects on bone metabolism in type 2 diabetes care plans
- Avoid aggressive therapeutic approaches in frail older adults to prevent hypoglycemic events and falls
- Monitor and manage diabetes complications (e.g., neuropathy, retinopathy, nephropathy) that increase fracture risk
- Consider hip fracture risk assessment in individuals with diabetes, given their higher mortality and delayed recovery post-fracture compared to non-diabetic peers

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Age ≥65 years
- Postmenopausal women or men aged ≥50 years with history of adult-age fracture
- Diabetes duration >10 years
- Presence of diabetes-specific risk factors (e.g., frequent hypoglycemia, neuropathy, retinopathy, nephropathy, frequent falls, glucocorticoid use)
- Type 2 diabetes diagnosis for medication-specific risk assessment
- Glucocorticoid use: prednisone at doses >2.5 mg per day for ≥3 months

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of thiazolidinediones (TZDs) in patients at high fracture risk due to doubled fracture incidence
- Long-term sulfonylurea use in patients with high hypoglycemia risk
- Unmonitored insulin therapy in older adults or those with frequent hypoglycemia
- Ignoring fracture risk when selecting glucose-lowering medications
- Assuming all glucose-lowering medications have neutral effects on bone health
- Routine use of sulfonylureas in patients with high hypoglycemia risk due to increased fracture risk (RR 1.30 [95% CI 1.18–1.43])

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Prefer glucose-lowering medications with a good bone safety profile (e.g., metformin, DPP-4 inhibitors, GLP-1 RAs at low doses)
- Discontinue thiazolidinediones in patients at high fracture risk due to reversible fracture risk reduction after discontinuation
- Monitor fracture risk in patients on SGLT2 inhibitors, though overall evidence suggests neutral effects
- Incorporate fall prevention strategies in patients with frequent falls or neuropathy
- Individualize care plans for older adults or frail patients to minimize hypoglycemia and fall risks
- Prefer GLP-1 RAs at low doses over higher doses due to potential counteractive skeletal risks from rapid weight loss
- Monitor fracture risk in patients on insulin therapy, as it is associated with a doubling of hip fracture risk


### glycemic management for bone health

Glycemic management and pharmacological treatment for bone health in diabetes. Advocate for moderate physical activity to enhance muscle health, gait coordination, and balance as part of fracture preventive strategies. Ensure adequate daily intake of protein, calcium, and vitamin D, and cessation of smoking for all individuals with diabetes

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glycemic management and pharmacological treatment for bone health in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Advocate for moderate physical activity to enhance muscle health, gait coordination, and balance as part of fracture preventive strategies
- Ensure adequate daily intake of protein, calcium, and vitamin D, and cessation of smoking for all individuals with diabetes
- Initiate antiosteoporosis therapy and adequate calcium/vitamin D supplementation as soon as possible for individuals with fragility fractures
- Consider osteoanabolic therapy (teriparatide, abaloparatide, romosuzumab) for very-high-risk individuals given diabetes is a low-bone-turnover condition
- Follow osteoanabolic treatments with antiresorptives to preserve BMD gains and prevent rapid loss of newly formed bone
- Individualize vitamin D supplementation targets based on patient risk (e.g., osteoporosis, antiresorptive therapy) rather than rigid thresholds, with serum levels >30 ng/mL recommended for individuals receiving antiresorptive therapy

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Assessment of T-score (≤−2.5) or elevated FRAX score (≥3% for hip fracture or ≥20% for major osteoporotic fracture) for pharmacological treatment consideration
- Evaluation of additional risk factors such as falls and elevated FRAX score for adults with T-score between −2.0 and −2.5
- Pretreatment assessment, supplementation, close monitoring, and specialist consultation for individuals with advanced CKD (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²) receiving denosumab
- Consideration of self-management abilities and ability to maintain injection schedules (e.g., every 6 months for denosumab) prior to drug initiation
- Referral to a bone metabolic specialist for individuals on long-term treatment with antiosteoporosis medications, with multiple fragility fractures, or with multiple comorbidities

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine supplementation with vitamin D (with or without calcium) for the primary prevention of fractures in community-dwelling postmenopausal women and men aged 60 years or older
- Prescription of romosuzumab in individuals who experienced a myocardial infarction or a stroke within the past year
- Use of T-score alone without considering additional risk factors for fracture in type 2 diabetes, as T-score underestimates fracture risk in this population

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Strong consideration of pharmacological treatment for adults with diabetes with a T-score between −2.0 and −2.5 in the presence of additional risk factors for fracture
- Use of osteoanabolic agents (teriparatide, abaloparatide, romosuzumab) for very-high-risk individuals due to the low-bone-turnover nature of diabetes
- Initiation of antiosteoporosis therapy during an inpatient stay for individuals with fragility fractures to reduce care delays
- Recognition that antiosteoporosis treatment reduces the risk of subsequent low-trauma fracture, especially in the first 1–2 years after a fracture
- Individualized selection of bisphosphonate therapy (oral or intravenous) for individuals with poor medication-taking behavior or gaps in access to medical care
- Initiate antiosteoporosis therapy during an inpatient stay for individuals with fragility fractures to reduce care delays



## Insulin Administration Techniques

This guideline focuses on the critical aspects of selecting and using insulin syringes to ensure accurate dosing and safe administration of insulin for subcutaneous delivery.

**Key Features:**
- Syringe-vial concentration matching: Use U-40 syringes exclusively for U-40 insulin and U-100 syringes for U-100 insulin to prevent dosing errors.
- Fixed needle preference: Prefer single-unit fixed needle syringes for conventional insulin administration to ensure consistent dosing and reduce injection pain.

**Notable Details:**
- U-40 syringes are typically marked with a red cap for easy identification.
- U-100 syringes are the most commonly used in clinical practice due to the prevalence of U-100 insulin formulations.
- Never interchange syringe types between different insulin concentrations to avoid severe hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia.
- Insulin syringes are designed for single-use only to prevent contamination and infection.
- The needle length for insulin syringes is typically 6mm to 8mm for standard subcutaneous administration.
- Insulin syringes are calibrated in units (U) to match the insulin vial's concentration, ensuring precise dosing.
- Disposable insulin syringes reduce the risk of needle-stick injuries and cross-contamination in healthcare settings.

**Topics in this group:**

### insulin syringe selection and usage guidelines

Use syringes marked in insulin units (U-40 or U-100) for subcutaneous insulin administration. Ensure syringe-vial concentration match (U-40 syringe for U-40 insulin, U-100 syringe for U-100 insulin). Prefer fixed needle (single unit) syringes for conventional insulin administration insulin syringe selection and usage guidelines. insulin syringe selection and usage guidelines. Use U-40 syringe (red cap) for U-40 insulin vials and U-100 syringe (orange cap) for U-100 insulin vials. Avoid reuse of disposable plastic insulin syringes; if necessary, change syringes daily. Discard used needles and syringes by cutting and placing in a tamper-proof container

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin syringe selection and usage guidelines

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use U-40 syringe (red cap) for U-40 insulin vials and U-100 syringe (orange cap) for U-100 insulin vials
- Avoid reuse of disposable plastic insulin syringes; if necessary, change syringes daily
- Discard used needles and syringes by cutting and placing in a tamper-proof container
- Allow insulin to reach room temperature (~30 minutes) before injection
- Prime insulin pens by removing air bubbles and testing for correct dose delivery
- Clean visibly dirty injection sites with soap and water (not alcohol swabs) before injecting, as alcohol may strip silicone coating from needles

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Ensure correct syringe-insulin vial compatibility (U-40 with U-40, U-100 with U-100)
- Clean visibly dirty injection sites with soap and water before injecting
- Prime insulin pens by dialing 2 units and injecting into air to remove air bubbles
- Mix NPH insulin by inverting the pen 5 times before use
- Use fresh pen needles for each injection to prevent contamination
- Ensure no air bubbles are present in the needle before injection
- Attach a fresh pen needle to the pen before each use

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin syringe selection and usage guidelines

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Use syringes marked in insulin units (U-40 or U-100) for subcutaneous insulin administration
- Ensure syringe-vial concentration match (U-40 syringe for U-40 insulin, U-100 syringe for U-100 insulin)
- Prefer fixed needle (single unit) syringes for conventional insulin administration
- Never share syringes or needles with another person
- Recap needles carefully after each use if reuse is planned; avoid wiping or washing needles
- Use fixed needle (single unit) syringes for conventional insulin administration

**Prerequisites:**
- Syringe concentration must match insulin vial concentration to prevent dosing errors



## Hypoglycemia Management

These guidelines focus on the prescribing, administration, and education around glucagon for managing hypoglycemia in high-risk individuals, particularly those on insulin therapy. The emphasis is on improving access, routine review, and training for caregivers to ensure timely intervention.

**Key Features:**
- Glucagon prescribing for high-risk individuals on insulin or with hypoglycemia history
- Routine review of glucagon access due to historically low prescribing rates
- Education of close contacts (family, staff) on glucagon administration
- Explicit instruction to never administer insulin during hypoglycemia

**Notable Details:**
- Prescribe glucagon to all individuals treated with insulin or at high risk of hypoglycemia
- Routine review of glucagon access is necessary due to low current prescribing rates
- Close contacts must be instructed on glucagon use
- Never administer insulin during hypoglycemia

**Topics in this group:**

### glucagon prescribing for hypoglycemia

Glucagon prescribing and administration for hypoglycemia. Prescribe glucagon to all individuals treated with insulin or at high risk of hypoglycemia. Routinely review access to glucagon due to low current prescribing rates. Instruct close contacts (family, staff, etc.) on glucagon use and explicitly educate them never to administer insulin during hypoglycemia

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Glucagon prescribing and administration for hypoglycemia

**Core Recommendations:**
- Prescribe glucagon to all individuals treated with insulin or at high risk of hypoglycemia
- Routinely review access to glucagon due to low current prescribing rates
- Instruct close contacts (family, staff, etc.) on glucagon use and explicitly educate them never to administer insulin during hypoglycemia
- Prefer intranasal or ready-to-inject glucagon over powder formulations for ease and speed of administration
- Verify insurance coverage and prescribe formulary products whenever possible
- Store glucagon products according to specific product instructions to maintain stability and efficacy
- Replace glucagon products when they reach their expiration date to ensure safe and effective use

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be treated with insulin or be at high risk of hypoglycemia
- Patient must be unable or unwilling to consume carbohydrates by mouth
- Close contacts must be explicitly educated on administration and contraindications (insulin administration)
- Individuals unable or unwilling to consume carbohydrates by mouth must be prescribed glucagon

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine dispensing of glucagon powder requiring reconstitution prior to injection

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of intranasal and ready-to-inject glucagon preparations as preferred options
- Mandatory education for non-healthcare professionals (e.g., family, coaches) on glucagon administration
- Healthcare providers should check insurance coverage and prescribe formulary products to optimize accessibility and affordability



## Edge Cases & Standalone Topics

A collection of specialized clinical and policy considerations for diabetes care, covering pharmacological, device-based, diagnostic, and lifestyle interventions, along with safety and accessibility concerns.

**Key Features:**
- Non-nutritive sweetener (NNS) use in diabetes: Acceptable substitution for nutritive sweeteners when consumed in moderation (≤ acceptable daily intake), with no significant impact on glycemic management.
- Compounded diabetes medication guidance: Avoid non-FDA-approved compounded products due to safety and efficacy concerns; switch to FDA-approved alternatives if unavailable.
- Rapid-acting insulin secretagogues: Include repaglinide, nateglinide, and rapid-acting insulin analogs (e.g., aspart, lispro); administered with meals for postprandial hyperglycemia management.
- Implanted weight loss devices: Minimally invasive options (e.g., gastric balloons, vagus nerve stimulators) approved for short-term weight loss in diabetes care.
- Hypertension diagnostic thresholds: Elevated BP defined as 120–129/<80 mmHg; hypertension as ≥130 or ≥80 mmHg; measure BP at every visit or at least every 6 months.
- Hypoglycemia awareness restoration: Temporary relaxation of glycemic control for 2–3 weeks to restore awareness, with avoidance of hypoglycemia and CGM use.
- Tobacco cessation counseling: Routine screening and complete avoidance advice for tobacco, vaping, and recreational cannabis; provide combination counseling and pharmacologic therapy.
- Exocrine pancreas dysfunction in diabetes: Bidirectional relationship with diabetes; up to half of individuals with diabetes may have impaired exocrine function.
- Thrombosis prevention in pediatric DKA: DKA is prothrombotic; avoid central venous access; consider heparin prophylaxis for high-risk cases.
- Vision rehabilitation care: Counsel and refer patients with diabetes-related vision loss for comprehensive visual impairment evaluations.
- Erectile dysfunction treatments: Treat hypogonadism if present; use PDE5 inhibitors as first-line; consider prostaglandins, vacuum devices, or penile prostheses for refractory cases.
- Standardized insulin preparation labeling: Universal color coding for insulin types; check expiry and inspect for clumps/discoloration; store unopened insulin refrigerated until expiry.
- Repaglinide and nateglinide dosing: Repaglinide 0.5–6 mg/day in 3 divided doses; nateglinide 60–360 mg/day in 3 divided doses; both have short duration (~1–1.5 hours).

**Notable Details:**
- NNS (e.g., saccharin, aspartame, acesulfame K) do not significantly affect glycemic management when consumed in moderation.
- Non-FDA-approved compounded diabetes medications are discouraged due to safety, quality, and effectiveness concerns.

**Topics in this group:**

### non-nutritive sweetener use in diabetes

NNS may be an acceptable substitute for nutritive sweeteners in people with diabetes accustomed to sugar-sweetened foods or beverages, when consumed in moderation (no more than acceptable daily intake).. NNS do not significantly affect glycemic management. non-nutritive sweetener use in diabetes. non-nutritive sweetener use in diabetes. Non-nutritive sweeteners (e.g., saccharin, neotame, aspartame, acesulfame K, stevia, alitame, sucralose) are used to improve sweetness and palatability in low-sugar or 'diet' products.. They may replace table sugar in home cooking.. Intake not exceeding acceptable daily intakes (ADI) is considered safe.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** non-nutritive sweetener use in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- NNS may be an acceptable substitute for nutritive sweeteners in people with diabetes accustomed to sugar-sweetened foods or beverages, when consumed in moderation (no more than acceptable daily intake).
- NNS do not significantly affect glycemic management.
- NNS can reduce overall calorie and carbohydrate intake if individuals do not compensate with additional calories from other food sources.
- Healthcare professionals should promote water as the healthiest beverage option but may recommend no-calorie or low-calorie sweetened products for people with overweight or obesity and diabetes to avoid deprivation.
- Healthcare professionals should regularly assess individual NNS use based on acceptable daily intake and recommend moderation.
- NNS have undergone extensive safety evaluation by regulatory agencies and are continually monitored for harm.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- People with diabetes accustomed to regularly consuming sugar-sweetened foods or beverages may consider NNS as substitutes.
- NNS use should not exceed the acceptable daily intake levels established by regulatory agencies.
- Individuals should not compensate for NNS use with additional calories from other food sources to achieve calorie reduction.
- Healthcare professionals should encourage reductions in foods and beverages with added sugars and promote reducing overall sugar intake and calories with or without the use of NNS.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- NNS are recommended as potential substitutes for nutritive sweeteners in moderation for people with diabetes.
- Healthcare professionals are encouraged to regularly assess NNS use and promote moderation based on acceptable daily intake.
- No-calorie or low-calorie sweetened products may be used by people with overweight or obesity and diabetes to avoid deprivation, with direction of benefit similar to water.
- A meta-analysis and systematic review of RCTs found no evidence that NNS raise liver enzymes.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** non-nutritive sweetener use in diabetes

**Core Management Principles:**
- Non-nutritive sweeteners (e.g., saccharin, neotame, aspartame, acesulfame K, stevia, alitame, sucralose) are used to improve sweetness and palatability in low-sugar or 'diet' products.
- They may replace table sugar in home cooking.
- Intake not exceeding acceptable daily intakes (ADI) is considered safe.
- Potential to replace caloric or carbohydrate intake if substituted for caloric sweeteners.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient education on ADI compliance for NNS use is recommended to ensure safe consumption.


### compounded diabetes medication guidance

Use of compounded products not FDA-approved is not recommended due to safety, quality, and effectiveness concerns.. Switch to an FDA-approved medication with similar efficacy if a glucose-lowering medication is unavailable (e.g., in shortage). compounded diabetes medication guidance. compounded diabetes medication guidance. Physicians should be aware of herb-drug interactions when patients use indigenous diabetes medications. Herbal products like Pterocarpus marsupium, Gymnema sylvestre, Mormordica chirantica, and Eugenia jambolana may have blood glucose-lowering effects

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** compounded diabetes medication guidance

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use of compounded products not FDA-approved is not recommended due to safety, quality, and effectiveness concerns.
- Switch to an FDA-approved medication with similar efficacy if a glucose-lowering medication is unavailable (e.g., in shortage).
- Reassess the appropriateness of resuming the original FDA-approved medication upon resolution of unavailability.
- Counsel individuals of childbearing potential on contraception options and the impact of glucose-lowering medications on contraception efficacy.
- Use a person-centered shared decision-making approach for preconception planning addressing glycemic goals and medication adjustments.
- Educate patients on the risks and signs of diabetic ketoacidosis when treated with SGLT inhibitors, including methods of risk mitigation and discouragement of ketogenic eating patterns.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have a confirmed need for glucose-lowering medication.
- Unavailability of the prescribed medication must be documented (e.g., shortage).
- Patient must be assessed for hyperglycemia risk during immunotherapy or other high-risk treatments.
- Individuals of childbearing potential must be counseled on contraception and glycemic management before pregnancy.
- Post-transplantation patients must have individualized glycemic goals based on organ type and treatment plan.
- Assess and adjust glycemic management based on specific glucocorticoid treatment plans with frequent reassessment of glucose levels.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of compounded diabetes medications not FDA-approved.
- Routine use of non-FDA-approved medications for glucose management.
- Fixed A1C targets without consideration of patient-specific factors in older adults.
- Routine use of noninsulin therapies in postoperative hyperglycemia without preference for insulin.
- Ignoring the impact of glucose-lowering medications on contraception efficacy.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Switching to FDA-approved alternatives during medication shortages.
- Shared decision-making for preconception planning in individuals of childbearing potential.
- Immediate insulin initiation for immunotherapy-associated hyperglycemia or diabetic ketoacidosis risk.
- Metformin as first-line treatment for hyperglycemia due to mTOR or PI3K inhibitors.
- Use of GLP-1 receptor agonists for long-term glycemic management in post-transplantation diabetes mellitus.
- Consider DPP-4 inhibitors for mild hyperglycemia in postoperative management of adults with posttransplantation diabetes mellitus (PTDM) or preexisting type 2 diabetes.

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** compounded diabetes medication guidance

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Physicians should be aware of herb-drug interactions when patients use indigenous diabetes medications
- Herbal products like Pterocarpus marsupium, Gymnema sylvestre, Mormordica chirantica, and Eugenia jambolana may have blood glucose-lowering effects
- There is a common misconception that all herbal drugs are safe and non-toxic, which is not necessarily true
- Physicians should assess the potential for contamination or adulteration in indigenous diabetes medications, as these may pose additional safety risks beyond herb-drug interactions
- Clinicians should document the use of indigenous diabetes medications in patient records to monitor for adverse effects or interactions with conventional therapies

**Prerequisites:**
- Physicians must evaluate the safety and efficacy of indigenous diabetes medications in patient management
- Careful assessment of herb-drug interactions is required before recommending or continuing indigenous medications
- Physicians must verify the source and preparation method of indigenous diabetes medications to ensure quality and consistency, as variability in formulations can affect efficacy and safety
- A thorough patient history should include inquiry about the use of indigenous diabetes medications, even if not disclosed voluntarily, to prevent unrecognized interactions


### rapid-acting insulin secretagogues

rapid-acting insulin secretagogues (repaglinide, nateglinide). Administer with each meal due to rapid absorption (0.5-1 hr) and short half-life (<1 hr). Enhance insulin secretion via non-sulphonylurea receptor binding sites on β-cells. Useful for managing postprandial hyperglycemia rapid-acting insulin secretagogues. rapid-acting insulin secretagogues (aspart, lispro, glulisine) and ultra-rapid analogs (Fiasp, Lyumjev). Administer 5-10 minutes before meals due to quicker onset than regular insulin. Useful for reducing immediate post-prandial hyperglycemia due to earlier and higher peak. Shorter duration decreases risk of delayed hypoglycemia compared to regular insulin

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** rapid-acting insulin secretagogues (aspart, lispro, glulisine) and ultra-rapid analogs (Fiasp, Lyumjev)

**Core Management Principles:**
- Administer 5-10 minutes before meals due to quicker onset than regular insulin
- Useful for reducing immediate post-prandial hyperglycemia due to earlier and higher peak
- Shorter duration decreases risk of delayed hypoglycemia compared to regular insulin
- Ultra-rapid analogs (e.g., Fiasp) can be given immediately before or up to 20 minutes after meals
- Preferred in patients with unpredictable meal timing (e.g., toddlers, school/college students, office workers)
