- Ultra-rapid acting insulin lispro (Lyumjev) is approved for use in adults with T1DM and T2DM by the US FDA

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Must be used in conjunction with meal timing adjustments for optimal efficacy
- Not suitable for patients unable to monitor or respond to hypoglycemia risks
- Ultra-rapid analogs require careful dose titration to avoid post-meal hyperglycemia
- Ultra-rapid acting insulin analogs (e.g., Fiasp) may be administered immediately before meals in selected cases, not just post-meal

### Source 2: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** rapid-acting insulin secretagogues (repaglinide, nateglinide)

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Administer with each meal due to rapid absorption (0.5-1 hr) and short half-life (<1 hr)
- Enhance insulin secretion via non-sulphonylurea receptor binding sites on β-cells
- Useful for managing postprandial hyperglycemia
- Produce fewer and milder hypoglycemic episodes and side effects compared to sulfonylureas

**Prerequisites:**
- Must be taken with meals to align with rapid insulin release
- Use in mild renal insufficiency requires supervision
- Elderly patients require supervision when using these agents
- Must be used with caution in patients with hepatic impairment due to potential altered drug metabolism


### implanted weight loss devices

Implanted weight loss devices in diabetes care. Gastric banding devices have fallen out of favor due to limited long-term efficacy and high complication rates.. Minimally invasive devices such as implanted gastric balloons, vagus nerve stimulators, and gastric aspiration therapy are approved for short-term weight loss.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Implanted weight loss devices in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Gastric banding devices have fallen out of favor due to limited long-term efficacy and high complication rates.
- Minimally invasive devices such as implanted gastric balloons, vagus nerve stimulators, and gastric aspiration therapy are approved for short-term weight loss.
- High cost, limited insurance coverage, and limited efficacy data in diabetes treatment have created uncertainty for current use.
- Several medical devices have been voluntarily removed from the U.S. market due to safety and efficacy concerns.
- Minimally invasive devices are approved for short-term weight loss but lack long-term efficacy data in diabetes care.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- FDA approval for short-term weight loss indication.
- Assessment of patient-specific factors regarding cost and insurance coverage.
- Evaluation of limited efficacy data specifically for individuals with diabetes.
- Patient-specific evaluation of cost and insurance coverage is required before considering implanted weight loss devices.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Gastric banding devices
- Devices with high complication rates and limited long-term efficacy

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Implanted gastric balloons
- Vagus nerve stimulator
- Gastric aspiration therapy


### hypertension diagnostic thresholds

Hypertension diagnostic thresholds and management in diabetes. Elevated blood pressure is defined as systolic 120–129 mmHg with diastolic <80 mmHg.. Hypertension is defined as systolic ≥130 mmHg or diastolic ≥80 mmHg.. Blood pressure should be measured at every routine clinical visit or at least every 6 months.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Hypertension diagnostic thresholds and management in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Elevated blood pressure is defined as systolic 120–129 mmHg with diastolic <80 mmHg.
- Hypertension is defined as systolic ≥130 mmHg or diastolic ≥80 mmHg.
- Blood pressure should be measured at every routine clinical visit or at least every 6 months.
- Individuals with elevated blood pressure should have confirmation using multiple readings on separate days to diagnose hypertension.
- A systolic blood pressure goal of <120 mmHg should be encouraged in individuals with high cardiovascular or kidney risk.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Blood pressure must be measured in a seated position with feet on the floor and arm supported at heart level after 5 minutes of rest.
- Cuff size must be appropriate for the upper-arm circumference.
- Diagnosis of hypertension requires an average of two or more measurements obtained on two or more occasions.
- Individuals with CVD and blood pressure ≥180/110 mmHg may be diagnosed with hypertension at a single visit.
- Postural changes in blood pressure and pulse may require checking lying, sitting, and standing measurements to assess autonomic neuropathy.
- Blood pressure measurements should be taken by a trained individual following general population guidelines for accurate readings.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults (now individualized).
- Diastolic blood pressure goals of <90 mmHg or <85 mmHg (HOT trial showed no significant difference compared to <80 mmHg in diabetes).
- Standard systolic blood pressure goal of <140 mmHg for all individuals with diabetes (now discouraged in favor of <130/80 mmHg or <120 mmHg for high risk).
- Exclusion of people with diabetes from intensive blood pressure lowering trials (SPRINT excluded diabetics, but evidence supports intensive goals).
- Lack of high-quality data guiding blood pressure goals in people with type 1 diabetes (now recommends similar goal of <130/80 mmHg).
- Diastolic blood pressure goals of <90 mmHg or <85 mmHg without consideration of achieved systolic blood pressure or cardiovascular risk stratification.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recommendation to support a blood pressure goal of <130/80 mmHg in people with diabetes.
- Encouragement of a systolic blood pressure goal of <120 mmHg in individuals with high cardiovascular or kidney risk based on BPROAD and ESPRIT trials.
- Use of home blood pressure self-monitoring and 24-h ambulatory blood pressure monitoring to detect white coat or masked hypertension.
- Shared decision-making process for individualizing blood pressure goals addressing cardiovascular risk, adverse effects, and patient preferences.
- Recommendation of ACE inhibitors or ARBs for treatment of hypertension in people with coronary artery disease or elevated urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio.
- Recommendation to use ACE inhibitors or ARBs for hypertension treatment in people with coronary artery disease or elevated urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (≥30 mg/g).
- Use of home blood pressure self-monitoring and 24-h ambulatory blood pressure monitoring to confirm hypertension diagnosis and monitor treatment efficacy.


### hypoglycemia awareness restoration

Temporary relaxation of glycemic control to restore awareness. Scrupulous avoidance of hypoglycemia for 2-3 weeks. Breaking the vicious cycle of recurrent hypoglycemia due to blunted sympathoadrenal flow. Use of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) during the avoidance period to detect asymptomatic hypoglycemia

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** hypoglycemia awareness restoration

**Core Management Principles:**
- Temporary relaxation of glycemic control to restore awareness
- Scrupulous avoidance of hypoglycemia for 2-3 weeks
- Breaking the vicious cycle of recurrent hypoglycemia due to blunted sympathoadrenal flow
- Use of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) during the avoidance period to detect asymptomatic hypoglycemia

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Patient must be under supervision during temporary glycemic relaxation
- Duration of avoidance period must be 2-3 weeks
- Monitoring of sympathoadrenal flow response is implied
- Patient education on recognizing early autonomic symptoms of hypoglycemia before the avoidance period
- Regular follow-up with healthcare provider during the 2-3 week avoidance period


### tobacco cessation counseling

Tobacco and Cannabis Cessation Counseling. Ask people with diabetes routinely about the use of tobacco or vape products at every visit. Advise complete avoidance of tobacco, vaping, and recreational cannabis in any form. Provide or refer for combination treatment consisting of cessation counseling and pharmacologic therapy for users

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Tobacco and Cannabis Cessation Counseling

**Core Recommendations:**
- Ask people with diabetes routinely about the use of tobacco or vape products at every visit
- Advise complete avoidance of tobacco, vaping, and recreational cannabis in any form
- Provide or refer for combination treatment consisting of cessation counseling and pharmacologic therapy for users
- Consider hyperglycemic ketosis cannabis hyperemesis syndrome in type 1 diabetes patients with specific lab criteria
- Utilize diabetes education programs to systematically deliver cessation interventions
- Advise against using smokeless tobacco products (e.g., dip, chew) due to increased cardiovascular disease and oral cancer risks

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Routine assessment of all types of tobacco use at every visit with every person with diabetes
- Presence of elevated A1C, macrovascular complications, or microvascular complications in smokers
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes for specific cannabis-related hyperglycemic ketosis risk assessment
- Patient presentation of symptoms such as severe nausea, abdominal pain, and vomiting in cannabis users
- Laboratory confirmation of blood glucose ≥250 mg/dL, anion gap >10, and serum β-hydroxybutyrate >0.6 mmol/L
- Consider cannabis hyperemesis syndrome in type 1 diabetes patients with pH ≥7.4 and bicarbonate >15 mmol/L in the presence of ketosis

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Perception that e-cigarettes are less harmful than combustible cigarettes
- Use of e-cigarettes as a primary cessation strategy without concurrent avoidance of combustible cigarettes
- Fixed numeric A1C targets for older adults without individualization based on life expectancy and hypoglycemia risk
- Reliance on unregulated CBD products for diabetes management without monitoring for psychoactive contaminants
- Failure to provide evidence-based pharmacologic therapy to more than two-thirds of patients attempting to quit
- Perception that smokeless tobacco products are safer than combustible cigarettes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Recommendation of varenicline as first-line pharmacotherapy for tobacco dependence in adults with diabetes
- Emerging research suggesting GLP-1 receptor agonists may improve smoking cessation and reduce postcessation weight gain
- Specific diagnostic criteria for hyperglycemic ketosis cannabis hyperemesis syndrome including pH ≥7.4 and bicarbonate ≥15 mmol/L
- Integration of cessation interventions into diabetes education clinics to increase quit rates and long-term abstinence
- Advisory against using both combustible and electronic cigarettes simultaneously or using e-cigarettes as a recreational drug
- Recommendation to avoid delta-8 THC and other psychoactive cannabinoids due to unregulated formulations and adverse effects
- Consideration of fluoxetine as a potential intervention to prevent postcessation weight gain


### exocrine pancreas dysfunction in diabetes

Exocrine pancreas dysfunction and bidirectional diabetes relationship. Diabetes is linked to exocrine pancreas diseases like pancreatitis, disrupting global architecture and physiology.. Up to half of individuals with diabetes may have impaired exocrine pancreas function.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Exocrine pancreas dysfunction and bidirectional diabetes relationship

**Core Recommendations:**
- Diabetes is linked to exocrine pancreas diseases like pancreatitis, disrupting global architecture and physiology.
- Up to half of individuals with diabetes may have impaired exocrine pancreas function.
- People with diabetes are at an approximately twofold higher risk of developing acute pancreatitis.
- Prediabetes and/or diabetes develops in approximately one-third of individuals after an episode of acute pancreatitis.
- Postpancreatitis diabetes may include either new-onset disease or previously unrecognized diabetes.
- Incretin-based therapies for diabetes may be associated with a higher frequency of pancreatitis, though causality remains unestablished and results are mixed

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals requiring total pancreatectomy for medically refractory chronic pancreatitis.
- Presence of postsurgical diabetes risk following total pancreatectomy.
- Careful consideration of personal factors for the individual with diabetes.
- Careful consideration of disease factors when deciding indications and timing of surgery.
- Surgery must be performed in skilled facilities with demonstrated expertise in islet autotransplantation.
- Consideration of incretin-based therapy risks in patients with a history of pancreatitis or exocrine pancreas dysfunction

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Islet autotransplantation should be considered for individuals requiring total pancreatectomy for medically refractory chronic pancreatitis to prevent postsurgical diabetes.
- Observational studies report islet graft function persisting up to a decade post-total pancreatectomy with islet autotransplantation in some individuals


### thrombosis prevention in pediatric DKA

DKA is considered a prothrombotic state, increasing venous thrombosis risk.. Central venous access should preferably be avoided to prevent thrombosis.. Heparin prophylaxis may be considered for children requiring central venous catheters.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** thrombosis prevention in pediatric DKA

**Core Management Principles:**
- DKA is considered a prothrombotic state, increasing venous thrombosis risk.
- Central venous access should preferably be avoided to prevent thrombosis.
- Heparin prophylaxis may be considered for children requiring central venous catheters.
- Heparin prophylaxis may be considered for children likely to remain immobile for more than 24–48 hours.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Children requiring central venous catheters placement.
- Children likely to remain immobile for more than 24–48 hours.


### vision rehabilitation care

Vision rehabilitation care for diabetes-related vision loss. Counsel patients with diabetes-related vision loss on the availability and scope of vision rehabilitation care. Provide or refer for a comprehensive evaluation of visual impairment by a practitioner experienced in vision rehabilitation

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Vision rehabilitation care for diabetes-related vision loss

**Core Recommendations:**
- Counsel patients with diabetes-related vision loss on the availability and scope of vision rehabilitation care
- Provide or refer for a comprehensive evaluation of visual impairment by a practitioner experienced in vision rehabilitation
- Deliver educational materials and resources for eye care support alongside self-management education
- Evaluate patients to determine potential benefits from comprehensive vision restoration
- Address physical, psychological, behavioral, and social consequences of vision loss
- Coordinate with multidisciplinary teams (e.g., endocrinologists, occupational therapists, social workers) to deliver integrated vision rehabilitation care

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes-related vision loss must be confirmed
- Patient must be evaluated by a practitioner experienced in vision rehabilitation
- Educational materials and resources for eye care support must be provided
- Self-management education (e.g., glycemic management and hypoglycemia awareness) must be included
- Assessment of potential benefits from vision restoration must be conducted
- Vision loss must be assessed for its impact on activities of daily living (ADLs) and instrumental ADLs before initiating rehabilitation

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Comprehensive evaluation by a vision rehabilitation specialist
- Integration of educational materials and resources for eye care support
- Focus on functional independence and quality of life outcomes
- Holistic approach addressing psychological and social impacts of vision loss
- Evaluation for potential benefits from vision restoration therapies
- Evaluation of potential benefits from low-vision assistive technologies (e.g., magnifiers, screen readers, adaptive lighting)


### erectile dysfunction treatments

Erectile dysfunction treatments in diabetes. Treat hypogonadism if present as part of erectile dysfunction management. Use phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors as first-line therapy. Consider intracorporeal or intraurethral prostaglandins for non-responders. Use vacuum devices as a non-invasive alternative. Consider penile prostheses for severe or refractory cases

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Erectile dysfunction treatments in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Treat hypogonadism if present as part of erectile dysfunction management
- Use phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors as first-line therapy
- Consider intracorporeal or intraurethral prostaglandins for non-responders
- Use vacuum devices as a non-invasive alternative
- Consider penile prostheses for severe or refractory cases

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Presence of erectile dysfunction symptoms
- Evaluation for hypogonadism if clinically indicated
- Assessment of contraindications to phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors
- Patient preference and willingness to try non-invasive options
- Specialist referral for complex or refractory cases
- Documentation of erectile dysfunction severity and duration as part of baseline assessment


### standardized insulin preparation labeling

standardized insulin preparation labeling and storage. Use universal color coding for insulin preparations, with fixed colors per insulin type regardless of manufacturer.. Check expiry date and inspect for clumps or discoloration before purchasing insulin.. Store unopened insulin in the refrigerator shelf until expiry date.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** standardized insulin preparation labeling and storage

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use universal color coding for insulin preparations, with fixed colors per insulin type regardless of manufacturer.
- Check expiry date and inspect for clumps or discoloration before purchasing insulin.
- Store unopened insulin in the refrigerator shelf until expiry date.
- Store opened insulin vials/cartridges at room temperature (≤25–30°C) for up to 1 month in cooler climates or in the refrigerator in hot climates.
- Avoid freezing insulin and exposure to direct sunlight or heat.
- Inspect electrical supply adequacy and storage conditions at medical shops where insulin is procured.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Maintain extra vials/cartridges of both short/rapid-acting and intermediate/long-acting insulin at home.
- Store insulin in personal luggage during travel; never in check-in luggage.
- Use cooled thermos flasks or earthenware pots with wet sand in hot climates without refrigeration.
- Select needle length (4–6 mm) based on body mass index to avoid intramuscular injections.
- Lift a skin fold and inject at 45–60° angle in lean individuals to minimize intramuscular risk.
- Ensure unopened insulin cartridges/disposable pens are stored in the refrigerator until their expiry date.
- Store insulin pens without needles attached to prevent air entry.


### repaglinide and nateglinide dosing

Administer repaglinide 0.5–6 mg/day in 3 divided doses with meals. Administer nateglinide 60–360 mg/day in 3 divided doses with meals. Both drugs have a short duration of action (~1–1.5 hours). Repaglinide is predominantly excreted via feces (90%). Nateglinide is predominantly excreted via urine (83%)

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** repaglinide and nateglinide dosing

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Administer repaglinide 0.5–6 mg/day in 3 divided doses with meals
- Administer nateglinide 60–360 mg/day in 3 divided doses with meals
- Both drugs have a short duration of action (~1–1.5 hours)
- Repaglinide is predominantly excreted via feces (90%)
- Nateglinide is predominantly excreted via urine (83%)

**Prerequisites:**
- Must be taken with meals to optimize postprandial glucose control
- Dose adjustments may be required based on renal function (especially for nateglinide)
- Dose adjustments for repaglinide may be required in severe hepatic impairment (not just renal function)


### sweetener use guidelines

Avoid nutritive sweeteners (e.g., fructose, honey, corn syrup, molasses, fruit juice concentrates, dextrose, maltose, mannitol, sorbitol, xylitol). Use non-nutritive sweeteners (e.g., aspartame, acesulfame K, stevia, sucralose, saccharin) in moderation. Avoid non-nutritive sweeteners during pregnancy

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** sweetener use guidelines

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Avoid nutritive sweeteners (e.g., fructose, honey, corn syrup, molasses, fruit juice concentrates, dextrose, maltose, mannitol, sorbitol, xylitol)
- Use non-nutritive sweeteners (e.g., aspartame, acesulfame K, stevia, sucralose, saccharin) in moderation
- Avoid non-nutritive sweeteners during pregnancy


### frailty phenotype in diabetes

frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes. Frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes often includes obesity alongside physical frailty. Frailty may present at an earlier age in people with type 2 diabetes compared to the general population. Functional exercise capacity in middle-aged adults with type 2 diabetes resembles that of non-diabetic individuals over a decade older

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes often includes obesity alongside physical frailty
- Frailty may present at an earlier age in people with type 2 diabetes compared to the general population
- Functional exercise capacity in middle-aged adults with type 2 diabetes resembles that of non-diabetic individuals over a decade older
- Frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes may include cognitive impairment alongside physical frailty
- Frailty in type 2 diabetes is associated with higher risk of hypoglycemia due to impaired counterregulatory responses

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Screening for frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes should incorporate both physical performance measures (e.g., gait speed, grip strength) and metabolic markers (e.g., HbA1c, insulin resistance)
- Frailty assessment in diabetes requires evaluation of sarcopenic obesity using dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry (DXA) or bioelectrical impedance analysis

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of intensive glycemic control targets (e.g., HbA1c <6.5%) in frail older adults with type 2 diabetes is no longer recommended due to increased hypoglycemia risk

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Implementation of structured resistance training programs as first-line therapy for frailty phenotype in type 2 diabetes, with progressive overload principles
- Use of GLP-1 receptor agonists (e.g., semaglutide) as preferred agents in frail patients with type 2 diabetes due to weight loss benefits and lower hypoglycemia risk


### genetic diabetes treatment strategies

Genetic diabetes treatment strategies for MODY and neonatal diabetes. Individuals with HNF1A or HNF4A mutations (MODY) are treated with low-dose sulfonylureas but may require insulin therapy.. For HNF1A mutations, adding a DPP-4 inhibitor to sulfonylurea may improve glycemic variability and goal attainment.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Genetic diabetes treatment strategies for MODY and neonatal diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Individuals with HNF1A or HNF4A mutations (MODY) are treated with low-dose sulfonylureas but may require insulin therapy.
- For HNF1A mutations, adding a DPP-4 inhibitor to sulfonylurea may improve glycemic variability and goal attainment.
- Neonatal diabetes due to KCNJ11 or ABCC8 mutations is treated with high-dose sulfonylureas.
- Neonatal diabetes due to INS, GATA6, EIF2AK3, or FOXP3 mutations requires insulin therapy.
- Individuals with HNF1B mutations (MODY) may respond to sulfonylureas but often require insulin therapy over time due to progressive beta-cell dysfunction.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Confirmation of specific genetic mutations (e.g., HNF1A, HNF4A, KCNJ11, ABCC8, INS, GATA6, EIF2AK3, FOXP3) via genetic testing.
- Diagnosis of maturity-onset diabetes of the young (MODY) or neonatal diabetes based on clinical and genetic criteria.
- Monitoring for sulfonylurea response and glycemic variability in HNF1A mutation carriers.
- Genetic testing for HNF1B mutations in suspected MODY cases with renal cysts or genital tract abnormalities.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Empirical insulin therapy as first-line treatment for HNF1A or HNF4A MODY without trial of sulfonylureas.
- Routine use of metformin as primary therapy in KCNJ11 or ABCC8 neonatal diabetes without sulfonylurea trial.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Genetic testing to guide targeted therapy selection in suspected MODY or neonatal diabetes.
- Use of DPP-4 inhibitors as adjunctive therapy in HNF1A MODY with sulfonylurea.
- High-dose sulfonylureas as first-line therapy for KCNJ11 or ABCC8 neonatal diabetes.
- Consider genetic testing for HNF1B mutations in patients with MODY phenotype and extra-pancreatic features (e.g., renal cysts, genital tract abnormalities) to guide treatment selection.


### medication reconciliation

medication reconciliation at care transitions. Cross-check home and hospital medications to ensure safety of new and old prescriptions. Verify no chronic medications are stopped during transitions. Detect and resolve duplication of medications under different brand names

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** medication reconciliation at care transitions

**Core Recommendations:**
- Cross-check home and hospital medications to ensure safety of new and old prescriptions
- Verify no chronic medications are stopped during transitions
- Detect and resolve duplication of medications under different brand names
- Fill and review new or changed prescriptions with the patient and care partners at or before discharge

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have an active medication list from home and hospital settings
- Access to patient's current medication history and discharge orders
- Presence of care partners or patient at discharge for review
- Patient's medication list must include dosage, frequency, route, and indication for each medication


### pramlintide adjunct therapy

Pramlintide adjunct therapy in type 1 diabetes. Pramlintide is approved for use in adults with type 1 diabetes as an adjunct to insulin therapy.. Modest reduction in A1C (0.3–0.4%) and modest weight loss (~1–2 kg) observed with pramlintide.. Pramlintide is based on the naturally occurring β-cell peptide amylin.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Pramlintide adjunct therapy in type 1 diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Pramlintide is approved for use in adults with type 1 diabetes as an adjunct to insulin therapy.
- Modest reduction in A1C (0.3–0.4%) and modest weight loss (~1–2 kg) observed with pramlintide.
- Pramlintide is based on the naturally occurring β-cell peptide amylin.
- Pramlintide requires administration as a subcutaneous injection immediately prior to major meals (pre-meal dosing)
- Gastrointestinal side effects (e.g., nausea, vomiting) are common with pramlintide and may limit adherence

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Must be used in adults with type 1 diabetes.
- Requires concurrent insulin therapy as an adjunct treatment.
- Pramlintide should be initiated at a low dose (15 mcg) and titrated gradually to reduce gastrointestinal adverse effects
- Pramlintide is contraindicated in patients with confirmed gastroparesis or other significant gastrointestinal motility disorders

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Sotagliflozin is contraindicated in type 1 diabetes due to an eightfold increased risk of DKA compared with placebo


### mitochondrial diabetes characteristics

Mitochondrial diabetes presents with maternal inheritance and bilateral hearing impairment in most carriers.. Average age of onset is between 35 and 40 years, later than typical T1DM or other monogenic diabetes.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** mitochondrial diabetes characteristics

**Core Management Principles:**
- Mitochondrial diabetes presents with maternal inheritance and bilateral hearing impairment in most carriers.
- Average age of onset is between 35 and 40 years, later than typical T1DM or other monogenic diabetes.
- May initially resemble T2DM, treated with diet or sulfonylureas, but progresses to insulin dependence due to insulinopenia.
- Metformin is contraindicated due to risk of lactic acidosis.
- Diagnosis requires genetic analysis, often linked to A3243G mutation in mitochondrial DNA.
- Mitochondrial diabetes is also known as maternally inherited diabetes and deafness (MIDD).
- It can initially present with phenotypes resembling T1DM or T2DM.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Genetic testing for confirmation of mitochondrial DNA mutations.
- Family history assessment for maternal transmission pattern.
- Audiological evaluation for bilateral hearing impairment.
- Avoidance of metformin in confirmed or suspected cases.
- Transition to insulin therapy as beta-cell function declines.
- Robust familial clustering assessment for maternal transmission pattern.
- Audiological evaluation for bilateral hearing impairment in suspected cases.


### monofilament testing protocol

monofilament testing protocol for diabetic foot assessment. Apply the monofilament perpendicular to the skin's surface. Apply sufficient force to cause the filament to bend or buckle. Maintain total duration of approach, contact, and departure at approximately 1-2 seconds. Avoid sliding the filament across the skin or making repetitive contact at the test site

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** monofilament testing protocol for diabetic foot assessment

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Apply the monofilament perpendicular to the skin's surface
- Apply sufficient force to cause the filament to bend or buckle
- Maintain total duration of approach, contact, and departure at approximately 1-2 seconds
- Avoid sliding the filament across the skin or making repetitive contact at the test site
- Do not test on ulcer sites, calluses, scars, or necrotic tissue

**Prerequisites:**
- Use a 10 gram force monofilament
- Test along the perimeter of the foot, not directly on lesions
- Test selected sites must include plantar surface of the great toe, first, third, and fifth metatarsal heads, and the plantar surface of the heel


### monogenic diabetes diagnosis

Consider monogenic diabetes in children and adults diagnosed in early adulthood with atypical features (e.g., negative diabetes-associated autoantibodies, no obesity, strong family history). Genetic testing is recommended for individuals diagnosed with diabetes under 6 months of age, regardless of current age

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Monogenic diabetes diagnosis

**Core Recommendations:**
- Consider monogenic diabetes in children and adults diagnosed in early adulthood with atypical features (e.g., negative diabetes-associated autoantibodies, no obesity, strong family history)
- Genetic testing is recommended for individuals diagnosed with diabetes under 6 months of age, regardless of current age
- Diagnose MODY in individuals with hyperglycemia onset before age 25, impaired insulin secretion, and autosomal dominant inheritance
- Consult a diabetes genetics specialist for interpretation of genetic mutations and further evaluation
- Provide genetic counseling to affected individuals and family members regarding inheritance patterns and treatment implications
- Consider monogenic diabetes in individuals with transient neonatal diabetes (e.g., due to 6q24 overexpression) who may be treatable with medications other than insulin
- Consider monogenic diabetes in individuals with INS gene mutations causing permanent neonatal diabetes, where insulin therapy is preferred

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes diagnosed within the first 6 months of life (neonatal diabetes)
- Stable, mild fasting hyperglycemia (100–150 mg/dL [5.6–8.5 mmol/L]) or A1C between 5.6 and 7.6% (38–60 mmol/mol)
- Negative diabetes-associated autoantibodies and lack of typical type 1 or type 2 diabetes features
- Strong family history of diabetes with atypical presentation
- Absence of obesity or metabolic features inconsistent with type 2 diabetes
- Presence of autoantibodies does not definitively exclude monogenic diabetes, as rare cases of monogenic diabetes with positive autoantibodies have been reported

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine insulin therapy for all neonatal diabetes cases without genetic testing
- Assuming all early-onset diabetes is autoimmune type 1 diabetes
- Fixed treatment plans for MODY without genetic confirmation
- Ignoring family screening for monogenic diabetes
- Delaying genetic testing in suspected monogenic diabetes cases

**Updated Recommendations:**
- High-dose sulfonylurea therapy for KATP-related neonatal diabetes instead of insulin
- Genetic testing via next-generation sequencing for suspected monogenic diabetes
- Personalized therapy based on MODY subtype (e.g., no therapy for GCK-MODY, sulfonylureas for HNF1A/HNF4A-MODY)
- Genetic counseling for affected individuals and family members
- Screening for extrapancreatic complications in diagnosed MODY cases
- Genetic screening via next-generation sequencing is increasingly recommended as a cost-effective first-line approach for suspected monogenic diabetes


### orthostatic hypotension management

orthostatic hypotension management in diabetes. Therapeutic goal is to minimize postural symptoms rather than restore normotension. Encourage physical activity and exercise to avoid deconditioning. Use nonpharmacologic measures such as adequate salt intake, avoiding aggravating medications, and compressive garments

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** orthostatic hypotension management in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Therapeutic goal is to minimize postural symptoms rather than restore normotension
- Encourage physical activity and exercise to avoid deconditioning
- Use nonpharmacologic measures such as adequate salt intake, avoiding aggravating medications, and compressive garments
- Volume repletion with fluids and salt is critical
- Treat supine hypertension at bedtime with shorter-acting drugs affecting baroreceptor activity
- Encourage small-particle eating pattern to improve symptoms in diabetic gastroparesis associated with orthostatic hypotension

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have symptomatic orthostatic hypotension
- Prior evaluation to exclude reversible causes (e.g., medications, volume depletion)
- Assessment of supine hypertension before bedtime treatment
- Consideration of patient tolerance and comorbidities before initiating pharmacologic therapy
- Monitoring for adverse effects of pharmacologic agents
- Assess risk-benefit balance before withdrawing GLP-1 receptor agonists due to their potential benefits despite adverse gastrointestinal motility effects

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of metoclopramide beyond 12 weeks due to risk of extrapyramidal effects
- Long-term use of erythromycin due to tachyphylaxis
- Routine use of gastric electrical stimulation for diabetic gastroparesis
- Fixed numeric blood pressure targets in orthostatic hypotension management
- Uncritical continuation of GLP-1 receptor agonists without assessing gastrointestinal motility impact
- Use of gastric electrical stimulation for diabetic gastroparesis due to limited data supporting efficacy in diabetic populations

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Use of midodrine and droxidopa (FDA-approved for orthostatic hypotension)
- Bedtime treatment of supine hypertension with shorter-acting agents (e.g., guanfacine, clonidine, isradipine, atenolol, metoprolol tartrate)
- Small-particle eating pattern for symptom relief in diabetic gastroparesis
- Withdrawal of drugs with adverse gastrointestinal motility effects (e.g., opioids, anticholinergics, TCAs, GLP-1 RAs, pramlintide)
- Limited use of metoclopramide reserved for severe gastroparesis unresponsive to other therapies
- Use of enalapril as an alternative shorter-acting agent for bedtime treatment of supine hypertension when preferred agents are not tolerated


### refugee diabetes care models

Refugee/migrant diabetes care models. Refugee status and migrant farmworker status are independent risk factors for cardiovascular disease and type 2 diabetes.. Nontraditional care delivery models (e.g., mobile integrated health, telehealth) should be leveraged to improve access to diabetes care for migrant populations.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Refugee/migrant diabetes care models

**Core Recommendations:**
- Refugee status and migrant farmworker status are independent risk factors for cardiovascular disease and type 2 diabetes.
- Nontraditional care delivery models (e.g., mobile integrated health, telehealth) should be leveraged to improve access to diabetes care for migrant populations.
- Healthcare professionals should initiate referrals to social workers and community resources to address barriers to care (e.g., cost, language, transportation, food insecurity).
- Diabetes care must account for unique barriers such as migration frequency, work hours, cultural/linguistic differences, and systemic healthcare complexity.
- Refugee status is an independent risk factor for cardiovascular disease, compounding diabetes management challenges in humanitarian crises.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must be identified as a refugee, migrant farmworker, or agricultural worker with diabetes.
- Assessment of barriers to care (e.g., food insecurity, language, transportation) must be conducted.
- Referral pathways to social services and community resources must be established or accessible.
- Healthcare professionals must assess pesticide exposure history in migrant agricultural workers as part of diabetes risk stratification.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- One-size-fits-all diabetes care models without consideration of migrant/refugee-specific barriers.
- Reliance solely on traditional clinic-based care without leveraging mobile or telehealth solutions.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Integration of mobile integrated health and telehealth to overcome geographic and logistical barriers.
- Targeted screening for diabetes in migrant farmworker populations due to elevated risk.
- Collaboration with community resources to address social determinants of health (e.g., food access, housing).
- Establish partnerships with agricultural worker health centers to facilitate care continuity during seasonal migration.


### pre-pregnancy weight optimization

pre-pregnancy weight optimization in T1DM. Optimize weight before pregnancy to reduce adverse outcomes for mother and offspring.. Women with excess weight should preferably lose weight before pregnancy.. Strongly advise and encourage weight loss in women with BMI >27 kg/m² before conception.. BMI assessment before pregnancy planning.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pre-pregnancy weight optimization in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Optimize weight before pregnancy to reduce adverse outcomes for mother and offspring.
- Women with excess weight should preferably lose weight before pregnancy.
- Strongly advise and encourage weight loss in women with BMI >27 kg/m² before conception.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- BMI assessment before pregnancy planning.
- Individualized weight loss counseling tailored to pre-pregnancy health status.
- BMI assessment must be conducted as part of pre-pregnancy planning for women with T1DM


### prenatal and postpartum exercise guidelines

At least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity aerobic activity per week during pregnancy and postpartum, preferably spread throughout the week. Consult a healthcare professional before making significant changes to physical activity intensity

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** prenatal and postpartum exercise guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- At least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity aerobic activity per week during pregnancy and postpartum, preferably spread throughout the week
- Consult a healthcare professional before making significant changes to physical activity intensity
- Regular exercise reduces odds of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM), gestational hypertension, and preeclampsia by 38%, 39%, and 41%, respectively
- Acute and long-term prenatal exercise improves glycemia, with larger effects in people with diabetes
- Avoid supine exercises after the first trimester due to potential compression of the inferior vena cava

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Generally healthy individuals
- Consultation with a healthcare professional for activity adjustments or significant changes
- Avoid exercise in individuals with absolute contraindications such as significant heart or lung disease, incompetent cervix, multiple gestation with risk of preterm labor, persistent second- or third-trimester bleeding, placenta previa after 26 weeks, or ruptured membranes

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Exercise interventions improve glucose outcomes and reduce need to start insulin or insulin dose requirements in GDM
- Insufficient evidence to recommend a specific type of exercise program (aerobic, resistance, or combined) for diabetes-related outcomes in pregnancy
- Consider supervised exercise programs for individuals with GDM or pre-existing diabetes to optimize glycemic control and safety


### pregnancy-safe antihypertensive therapy

Avoid ACE inhibitors, ARBs, direct renin inhibitors, MRAs, SGLT2 inhibitors, and neprilysin inhibitors in pregnancy due to fetal harm risk. Switch to pregnancy-safe antihypertensives (e.g., methyldopa, labetalol, long-acting nifedipine) prior to conception if using contraindicated medications

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** pregnancy-safe antihypertensive therapy

**Core Recommendations:**
- Avoid ACE inhibitors, ARBs, direct renin inhibitors, MRAs, SGLT2 inhibitors, and neprilysin inhibitors in pregnancy due to fetal harm risk
- Switch to pregnancy-safe antihypertensives (e.g., methyldopa, labetalol, long-acting nifedipine) prior to conception if using contraindicated medications
- Use hydralazine only for acute management of hypertension in pregnancy or severe preeclampsia
- Avoid diuretics for routine blood pressure management in pregnancy, except for volume management in late-stage pregnancy
- Monitor blood pressure for 72 hours postpartum and at 7–10 days postpartum in individuals with gestational hypertension, preeclampsia, or superimposed preeclampsia

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Sexually active individuals of childbearing potential not using reliable contraception must avoid kidney-protective medications harmful in pregnancy
- Individuals intending to become pregnant should switch from contraindicated antihypertensives to pregnancy-safe alternatives prior to conception
- Pregnancy-safe antihypertensives (methyldopa, labetalol, long-acting nifedipine) are preferred for chronic management
- Hydralazine is reserved for acute hypertensive crises or severe preeclampsia
- Long-term cardiovascular risk follow-up is recommended for individuals with gestational hypertension, preeclampsia, or superimposed preeclampsia
- Special consideration should be taken for individuals of child-bearing potential

**Previous Recommendations:**
- ACE inhibitors
- ARBs
- Direct renin inhibitors
- MRAs
- Neprilysin inhibitors

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Methyldopa as a first-line pregnancy-safe antihypertensive
- Labetalol as a preferred pregnancy-safe antihypertensive
- Long-acting nifedipine for chronic hypertension management in pregnancy
- Postpartum blood pressure monitoring for 72 hours in hospital and 7–10 days postpartum
- Long-term cardiovascular risk follow-up for individuals with hypertensive disorders of pregnancy


### FCPD diagnostic criteria

FCPD diagnostic criteria and clinical profile. FCPD is characterized by idiopathic non-alcoholic chronic pancreatitis with recurrent abdominal pain, steatorrhea, and pancreatic calcification.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** FCPD diagnostic criteria and clinical profile

**Core Management Principles:**
- FCPD is characterized by idiopathic non-alcoholic chronic pancreatitis with recurrent abdominal pain, steatorrhea, and pancreatic calcification.
- Diagnosis requires tropical country origin, presence of diabetes, evidence of chronic pancreatitis (pancreatic calculi or ≥3 of: abnormal pancreatic morphology, recurrent abdominal pain since childhood, steatorrhea, abnormal pancreatic function test), and exclusion of other causes of chronic pancreatitis.
- Most patients are lean adolescents or young adults (10–30 years) with asymptomatic or incidental detection despite hyperglycemia.
- Patients require multiple insulin doses for glycemic control but rarely develop ketosis due to endogenous insulin sufficiency, reduced glucagon reserve, or limited non-esterified fatty acids.
- FCPD is predominantly reported from Kerala and Tamil Nadu, with prevalence declining due to improved socioeconomic status and nutrition.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Evidence of chronic pancreatitis (pancreatic calculi on X-ray or ≥3 diagnostic features: abnormal morphology, recurrent abdominal pain since childhood, steatorrhea, abnormal pancreatic function test).
- Exclusion of other causes of chronic pancreatitis.
- Tropical country origin (e.g., India, particularly Kerala/Tamil Nadu).
- Presence of diabetes mellitus.
- Detailed evaluation for pancreatic calcification and morphology (sonography/CT scan).


### carbohydrate exchange tables for Indian foods

Use standardized 15 g carbohydrate exchanges for common Indian foods to guide meal planning and insulin dosing.. Refer to specific portion sizes for breads, cereals, starchy vegetables, snack foods, fruits, biscuits, and sweets.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** carbohydrate exchange tables for Indian foods

**Core Management Principles:**
- Use standardized 15 g carbohydrate exchanges for common Indian foods to guide meal planning and insulin dosing.
- Refer to specific portion sizes for breads, cereals, starchy vegetables, snack foods, fruits, biscuits, and sweets.
- Apply portion-based exchanges (e.g., 1 slice white bread = 15 g carbs, 1 small apple = 15 g carbs) for consistent carbohydrate counting.
- Differentiate carbohydrate exchanges based on specific food categories including breads, cereals, starchy vegetables, snack foods, fruits, biscuits, and sweets.
- Specify distinct portion sizes for regional staples like Bajra, Makai, and Jowar rotis compared to wheat bread.
- Account for variations in carbohydrate density by assigning smaller volumes (e.g., ¼ cup) to high-sugar fruits like blueberries versus larger volumes for other fruits.
- Include specific counts for snack items such as Papad (2), Panipuri (6), and Bhelpuri (½ cup) to standardize exchange values.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Portion sizes must be measured accurately to ensure correct carbohydrate estimation.
- Exchange tables should be tailored to regional Indian dietary patterns and food availability.
- Patients must accurately measure specific portion sizes (e.g., 1 slice, ¼ roti, ½ cup) rather than estimating loosely.
- Food preparation methods must be considered, as the tables assume standard preparations (e.g., boiled potatoes) and may require adjustment for fried or mashed variants.
- Selection of foods must align with the specific regional dietary patterns and availability represented in the table categories.


### adverse drug reaction symptoms

Monitor for gastrointestinal problems including nausea, diarrhoea, and stomach pain. Assess for flu-like symptoms such as headache, runny nose, and sore throat. Evaluate for skin reactions characterized by painful skin followed by a red or purple rash. Check for joint pains as a potential adverse reaction

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** adverse drug reaction symptoms

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Monitor for gastrointestinal problems including nausea, diarrhoea, and stomach pain
- Assess for flu-like symptoms such as headache, runny nose, and sore throat
- Evaluate for skin reactions characterized by painful skin followed by a red or purple rash
- Check for joint pains as a potential adverse reaction


### diabetes-cancer comorbidity risk

Diabetes-cancer comorbidity risk management. Encourage age- and sex-appropriate cancer screenings for people with diabetes, coordinated with primary care. Promote reduction of modifiable cancer risk factors (obesity, physical inactivity, alcohol use, smoking)

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes-cancer comorbidity risk management

**Core Recommendations:**
- Encourage age- and sex-appropriate cancer screenings for people with diabetes, coordinated with primary care
- Promote reduction of modifiable cancer risk factors (obesity, physical inactivity, alcohol use, smoking)
- Monitor for atypical diabetes presentation (lean body habitus, negative family history) in middle-aged or older adults as potential pancreatic cancer precursor
- Avoid routine pancreatic cancer screening in absence of symptoms (e.g., weight loss, abdominal pain)

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diabetes diagnosis confirmed
- Patient-specific risk assessment for cancer based on age, sex, and comorbidities
- Coordination with primary healthcare professional for screening plans

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine pancreatic cancer screening in asymptomatic diabetes patients
- Reliance on fixed cancer risk reduction strategies without addressing modifiable factors

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consider postpancreatitis diabetes mellitus as a high-risk state for pancreatic cancer (2.4-fold increased risk)
- GLP-1 receptor agonists not associated with increased thyroid, pancreatic, or other cancer incidence in humans
- Metformin and sulfonylureas may have potential anticancer properties (evidence limited)
- Pioglitazone use requires caution due to mixed evidence regarding bladder cancer risk


### diabetes-periodontal disease coordination

Diabetes-periodontal disease coordination + Disability assessment in diabetes. Coordinate medical and dental teams to adjust glucose-lowering medications before and after dental procedures, especially for patients on insulin, sulfonylureas, or meglitinides.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes-periodontal disease coordination + Disability assessment in diabetes

**Core Recommendations:**
- Coordinate medical and dental teams to adjust glucose-lowering medications before and after dental procedures, especially for patients on insulin, sulfonylureas, or meglitinides.
- Include dental professionals in the diabetes care team using a collaborative interprofessional approach.
- Assess patients with diabetes for disability at initial visit and monitor for functional decline at subsequent visits.
- Conduct psychosocial assessments to screen for depression, anxiety, and social support issues in patients with diabetes-related disabilities.
- Perform functional capacity evaluations and use standardized disability questionnaires (e.g., WHODAS 2.0, Diabetes Distress Scale) to measure disability and emotional burden.
- Conduct a dental history and oral examination as part of the clinical assessment for people with diabetes.
- Ensure dental professionals are informed about hepatic, cardiovascular, kidney, and pulmonary conditions to assist in appropriate dosing of antibiotics.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patient must have diabetes with confirmed periodontal disease or risk factors for periodontal disease.
- Dental professionals must be informed about the patient’s glycemic goals, medications, and comorbid conditions.
- Blood glucose monitoring equipment, carbohydrates, and glucagon must be available during dental procedures for patients at risk of hypoglycemia.
- Patient must consent to interprofessional coordination between medical and dental teams.
- Disability assessment requires evaluation of activities of daily living (ADLs) and instrumental activities of daily living (IADLs).
- Dental professionals must be aware of high A1C levels in patients, as this population may have lower oral healing capacity.
- Dental professionals must know about comorbidities including hepatic, cardiovascular, kidney, and pulmonary conditions.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Routine use of fixed A1C targets without considering periodontal disease severity or dental procedure risks.
- Excluding dental professionals from the diabetes care team.
- Assuming all patients with diabetes have equal access to dental care without addressing barriers.
- Ignoring the bidirectional relationship between periodontal disease and diabetes in treatment planning.
- Not adjusting glucose-lowering medications prior to invasive dental procedures.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Mandatory coordination between medical and dental teams to adjust diabetes medications before and after dental procedures.
- Inclusion of dental professionals in the diabetes care team for collaborative interprofessional management.
- Standardized disability assessments at initial and follow-up visits for patients with diabetes.
- Use of functional capacity evaluations and standardized disability questionnaires (e.g., WHODAS 2.0) to guide rehabilitation referrals.
- Emphasis on addressing social determinants of health in patients with diabetes-related disabilities.
- Inclusion of dental health professionals in the diabetes care team using a collaborative interprofessional care approach.
- Emphasis on early detection of oral health problems by clinicians to promote prompt referral and mitigate extensive procedures.


### dietary fiber and protein recommendations

Fiber intake of 40 g/day (2000 Kcals) is recommended for the general population. Traditional Indian diets with whole grains, whole pulses, green leafy vegetables, and fruits (e.g., papaya, guava, apples) are recommended. All fruit juices should be avoided

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** dietary fiber and protein recommendations

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Fiber intake of 40 g/day (2000 Kcals) is recommended for the general population
- Traditional Indian diets with whole grains, whole pulses, green leafy vegetables, and fruits (e.g., papaya, guava, apples) are recommended
- All fruit juices should be avoided
- Proteins should provide 12-15% of total energy intake for people with diabetes
- Protein sources should include vegetable proteins (pulses, soy, grams, peas), low-fat dairy, fish, and lean meats

**Prerequisites:**
- Fruit intake should be in moderation
- Saturated fats (SFA) should be ≤10% of energy intake, or 7% in those with raised blood lipid levels
- Polyunsaturated fats (PUFA) should be 10% of energy intake with n6: 3-7% and n3: >1% energy
- Monounsaturated fatty acids (MUFA) should be 10-15% of energy intake
- Trans fats should be <1% of energy intake and preferably avoided

Copyright and usage guidelines for ADA clinical guidelines. Readers may use this work for educational, noncommercial purposes if properly cited and unaltered..
