### diabetes medication cost trends

Diabetes medication cost trends and financial screening. Costs for noninsulin and insulin diabetes medications have increased dramatically over the past two decades, with a growing proportion of costs passed to patients and families.. Medication costs can cause significant stress and negatively impact medication-taking behavior.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes medication cost trends and financial screening

**Core Recommendations:**
- Costs for noninsulin and insulin diabetes medications have increased dramatically over the past two decades, with a growing proportion of costs passed to patients and families.
- Medication costs can cause significant stress and negatively impact medication-taking behavior.
- Cost-reducing strategies may improve medication adherence in some cases.
- Screen all people with diabetes for financial concerns and cost-related barriers to care.
- Engage healthcare team members (e.g., pharmacists, social workers) to identify cost-saving opportunities.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients with high-deductible health plans or no insurance may incur very high out-of-pocket expenses.
- Financial screening should include assessment of out-of-pocket costs for medications, medical equipment, and glucagon.
- Cost-saving strategies must be tailored to individual patient circumstances and insurance coverage.
- No out-of-pocket cost caps currently exist for diabetes medical equipment (e.g., glucose monitoring devices, insulin administration supplies) or for noninsulin medications.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Reliance on average wholesale prices (AWP) or National Average Drug Acquisition Costs (NADAC) as actual patient costs, as these do not account for discounts, rebates, or price adjustments.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Implementation of insulin cost caps ($35 per prescription/month) for Medicare and state-regulated commercial insurance plans in 26 states and D.C.
- Introduction of insulin manufacturer cost reductions and copayment assistance programs, though with variable eligibility and coverage.
- Emphasis on screening for financial barriers and engaging multidisciplinary teams to address cost-related challenges.
- Cost caps for insulin include a tiered structure for state-regulated commercial insurance plans, offering either $35 per prescription per month or $100 per total monthly insulin payment.


### acute complications of hypoglycemia

Hypoglycemia can lead to severe systemic complications including hypokalemia, cardiac arrhythmias, and neurological emergencies.. Monitor for pulmonary edema, aspiration pneumonia, and acute gastric dilatation in severe hypoglycemic episodes.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** acute complications of hypoglycemia

**Core Management Principles:**
- Hypoglycemia can lead to severe systemic complications including hypokalemia, cardiac arrhythmias, and neurological emergencies.
- Monitor for pulmonary edema, aspiration pneumonia, and acute gastric dilatation in severe hypoglycemic episodes.
- Assess for acute pancreatitis, upper gastrointestinal bleeding, and rhabdomyolysis as potential complications.
- Neurological complications such as cortical venous thrombosis, basilar artery thrombosis, and subarachnoid hemorrhage require urgent evaluation.

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Immediate correction of hypoglycemia with glucose administration is mandatory.
- Electrolyte and cardiac monitoring are essential in managing hypokalemia and arrhythmias.
- Neurological imaging (e.g., CT/MRI) should be performed if thromboembolic complications are suspected.


### MASH fibrosis screening protocol

Screen adults with type 2 diabetes or prediabetes (especially with obesity, cardiometabolic risk factors, or cardiovascular disease) for MASH-related cirrhosis risk using FIB-4 index, even with normal liver enzymes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** MASH fibrosis screening protocol

**Core Recommendations:**
- Screen adults with type 2 diabetes or prediabetes (especially with obesity, cardiometabolic risk factors, or cardiovascular disease) for MASH-related cirrhosis risk using FIB-4 index, even with normal liver enzymes.
- Use FIB-4 ≥1.3 as threshold for additional risk stratification with liver stiffness measurement (LSM) via transient elastography or ELF test if unavailable.
- Refer adults with type 2 diabetes or prediabetes at higher risk for significant liver fibrosis (FIB-4 ≥1.3, LSM ≥8.0 kPa, or ELF ≥9.8) to gastroenterology/hepatology for further evaluation and management.
- Implement lifestyle changes (weight loss ≥10%, structured nutrition, physical activity) and consider GLP-1 RAs or pioglitazone for glycemic management in adults with type 2 diabetes and MASLD.
- Use resmetirom for adults with MASLD and moderate (F2) to advanced (F3) fibrosis, initiated by specialists within an interprofessional team.
- Evaluate adults with diabetes or prediabetes with persistently elevated plasma aminotransferases (>30 units/L for >6 months) and low FIB-4 for other causes of liver disease.
- Follow individuals with prediabetes or type 2 diabetes and LSM <8.0 kPa in nonspecialty clinics with repeat surveillance testing every ≥2 years.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Adults must have type 2 diabetes or prediabetes, particularly with obesity or cardiometabolic risk factors, for initial FIB-4 screening.
- FIB-4 requires age, ALT, AST, and platelet count for calculation.
- LSM via transient elastography or ELF test is required for FIB-4 ≥1.3 to stratify fibrosis risk.
- Resmetirom treatment requires MASLD with moderate (F2) to advanced (F3) fibrosis confirmed by histology or validated imaging/blood-based tests.
- Specialist referral (gastroenterology/hepatology) is required for FIB-4 ≥1.3, LSM ≥8.0 kPa, or ELF ≥9.8.
- FIB-4 calculation requires age, ALT, AST, and platelet count.
- Resmetirom treatment requires MASLD with moderate (F2) to advanced (F3) liver fibrosis confirmed by liver histology or validated imaging/blood-based test, excluding cirrhosis.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of elevated plasma aminotransferases (>40 units/L) as sole screening criterion for MASH-related fibrosis.
- Reliance on nonproprietary diagnostic panels (e.g., MASLD fibrosis score) over FIB-4 for initial risk stratification.
- Vitamin E monotherapy for MASH in adults with type 2 diabetes.
- Metformin for histological improvement of steatohepatitis in MASH.
- Statin therapy in decompensated cirrhosis without close monitoring.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Replacement of NAFLD terminology with MASLD, emphasizing cardiometabolic risk factors and insulin resistance.
- FDA approval of semaglutide for MASH treatment with moderate to advanced fibrosis.
- FDA approval of resmetirom for MASLD with moderate (F2) to advanced (F3) fibrosis.
- Use of FIB-4 ≥1.3 as threshold for additional fibrosis risk stratification (LSM or ELF).
- Recommendation for GLP-1 RAs (including dual GIP/GLP-1 agonists) or pioglitazone for glycemic management in adults with type 2 diabetes and MASLD.


### MASH management with SGLT2 inhibitors

SGLT2 inhibitors are considered weight-neutral or modestly beneficial for weight loss in diabetes with obesity.. SGLT2 inhibitors are not prioritized for MASH management due to limited histological evidence compared to GLP-1 RAs or pioglitazone.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** MASH management with SGLT2 inhibitors

**Core Recommendations:**
- SGLT2 inhibitors are considered weight-neutral or modestly beneficial for weight loss in diabetes with obesity.
- SGLT2 inhibitors are not prioritized for MASH management due to limited histological evidence compared to GLP-1 RAs or pioglitazone.
- SGLT2 inhibitors may be used as add-on therapy in type 2 diabetes with obesity if more effective weight-loss agents are not tolerated or contraindicated.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Presence of type 2 diabetes or type 1 diabetes with obesity.
- Consideration of MASLD or MASH when selecting glucose-lowering therapies.
- Biopsy-proven MASH or high risk of clinically significant liver fibrosis identified via noninvasive tests for combination therapy with pioglitazone plus GLP-1 RA.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Insulins, sulfonylureas, and thiazolidinediones for weight management in obesity due to promotion of weight gain.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Semaglutide approved by the FDA for MASH with moderate to advanced liver fibrosis based on phase 3 histological outcomes.
- Tirzepatide and semaglutide prioritized for glucose lowering and weight loss in type 2 diabetes with obesity.
- Combination therapy with pioglitazone plus GLP-1 RA recommended for hyperglycemia in type 2 diabetes with biopsy-proven MASH or high fibrosis risk.
- Semaglutide is the only agent with FDA approval for MASH with moderate to advanced liver fibrosis based on phase 3 histological outcomes.


### A1C test standardization guidelines

A1C test standardization and diagnostic use. A1C testing must use NGSP-certified methods traceable to the DCCT reference assay for diagnosis and monitoring.. Point-of-care A1C testing for diagnosis is restricted to FDA-approved devices in CLIA-certified laboratories with trained personnel.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** A1C test standardization and diagnostic use

**Core Recommendations:**
- A1C testing must use NGSP-certified methods traceable to the DCCT reference assay for diagnosis and monitoring.
- Point-of-care A1C testing for diagnosis is restricted to FDA-approved devices in CLIA-certified laboratories with trained personnel.
- Confirmatory testing is required for diagnosis unless classic hyperglycemic symptoms or hyperglycemic crisis are present.
- Discordant glucose and A1C results require evaluation for analytical problems, interferences, or alternative biomarkers (e.g., fructosamine, glycated albumin).
- A1C results near diagnostic thresholds (e.g., 6.5–7.0%) should prompt repeat testing in 3–6 months and patient education.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- NGSP certification or IFCC traceability for A1C assays.
- CLIA certification and annual competency assessments for point-of-care testing personnel.
- Three times per year proficiency testing participation for CLIA-certified sites.
- Confirmation of diagnosis via two abnormal tests (same or different) unless symptomatic hyperglycemia or crisis is present.
- Use of plasma glucose criteria in conditions altering A1C-hemoglobin relationship (e.g., hemoglobin variants, pregnancy, G6PD deficiency).
- Use of plasma glucose criteria for diagnosis in conditions altering the A1C-hemoglobin relationship (e.g., anemia, erythropoietin treatment, hemodialysis, HIV treatment, thalassemia, folate deficiency).
- Requirement for two abnormal test results (same or different tests) for diagnosis unless classic hyperglycemic symptoms or hyperglycemic crisis are present.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Non-NGSP-certified or non-traceable A1C assays for diagnosis.
- Point-of-care A1C devices not FDA-approved for diagnostic use.
- Reliance on single A1C tests for diagnosis without confirmatory testing.
- Use of race/ancestry as proxies for genetic differences in A1C interpretation.
- A1C assays known to be affected by hemoglobin variants without interference checks.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- IFCC-certified assays as acceptable alternatives to NGSP-certified methods outside the U.S.
- Updated list of A1C assays with hemoglobin variant interferences (available at ngsp.org/interf.asp).
- Recommendation to use alternative biomarkers (fructosamine, glycated albumin) for monitoring in cases of A1C discordance.
- Emphasis on individualized diagnostic confirmation based on test concordance and clinical context.
- AABBCC approach proposed as a tool to aid diabetes type classification.
- Use of alternative validated biomarkers (fructosamine, glycated albumin) for monitoring in cases of discordant glucose and A1C results.


### blood glucose meter accuracy standards

Blood glucose meter accuracy standards and usage. Glucose meters must meet FDA or ISO 15197:2013 accuracy standards, but postmarketing accuracy is monitored by manufacturers, not independent sources.. People with diabetes should use only unopened and unexpired test strips to ensure accuracy.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Blood glucose meter accuracy standards and usage

**Core Recommendations:**
- Glucose meters must meet FDA or ISO 15197:2013 accuracy standards, but postmarketing accuracy is monitored by manufacturers, not independent sources.
- People with diabetes should use only unopened and unexpired test strips to ensure accuracy.
- Optimal BGM use requires proper data review and interpretation by both the person with diabetes and healthcare professionals.
- Frequent BGM use (6–10 times daily for intensive insulin therapy) is associated with lower A1C levels and fewer acute complications.
- BGM data should be used to adjust food intake, physical activity, or pharmacologic therapy to achieve treatment goals.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Glucose meters must meet FDA or ISO 15197:2013 accuracy standards for reliable diabetes management.
- BGM is particularly important for people with diabetes treated with insulin therapy to detect and prevent hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia.
- For basal insulin users, assessing fasting glucose with BGM informs dose adjustments to achieve blood glucose goals.
- BGM should be reevaluated at each routine visit to ensure its effective use.
- CGM with BGM backup is preferred for individuals on intensive insulin therapies.
- For individuals using basal insulin, assessing fasting glucose with BGM is required to inform dose adjustments and achieve blood glucose goals.
- BGM monitoring frequency should be reevaluated at each routine clinical visit to ensure continued effective use.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Assuming glucose meters are accurate solely because they are FDA cleared without verifying postmarketing performance.
- Using preowned or secondhand test strips, which may give incorrect results.
- Relying solely on BGM without integrating data into clinical and self-management treatment plans.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Meters with integrated speech for visually impaired individuals to read aloud glucose levels.
- Meters with features like reapplying blood to a strip with an insufficient initial sample to reduce user burden.
- Real-time advice from meters when monitoring glucose levels to guide user actions.
- Integration of BGM with health platforms for comprehensive glucose management.
- Evidence supporting CGM use in people with type 2 diabetes for glycemic management and cost-effectiveness.
- Meters with integrated speech features to read aloud glucose levels for visually impaired individuals.
- Meters with the ability to reapply blood to a strip with an insufficient initial sample to reduce user burden.


### diabetes progression monitoring guidelines

Monitor progression from prediabetes to all types of diabetes, not only type 2 diabetes.. Consider continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) data when monitoring for disease progression among individuals with presymptomatic type 1 diabetes.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes progression monitoring guidelines

**Core Recommendations:**
- Monitor progression from prediabetes to all types of diabetes, not only type 2 diabetes.
- Consider continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) data when monitoring for disease progression among individuals with presymptomatic type 1 diabetes.
- Refer individuals with overweight or obesity at high risk for type 2 diabetes to a diabetes prevention program with the goal of achieving and maintaining weight reduction of at least 5–7% of initial body weight.
- Focus on eating patterns with the strongest evidence base for preventing type 2 diabetes, including Mediterranean and low carbohydrate eating patterns.
- Deliver certified technology-assisted diabetes prevention programs through smartphones, web-based applications, and telehealth modalities.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals with overweight or obesity at high risk for type 2 diabetes.
- High-risk individuals treated with a PI3Kα inhibitor (e.g., alpelisib and inavolisib).
- High-risk individuals treated with high-dose glucocorticoids.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Consider using metformin to prevent hyperglycemia in high-risk individuals treated with a PI3Kα inhibitor.
- Consider using metformin to prevent hyperglycemia in high-risk individuals treated with high-dose glucocorticoids.


### SMBG indications and frequency

Perform SMBG in all people with diabetes on insulin. Perform SMBG in patients with brittle diabetes. Perform SMBG in patients prone to ketosis or recurrent hypoglycemia. Perform SMBG in patients with hypoglycemia unawareness. Perform SMBG whenever tight control is indicated (e.g., pregnancy, acute illness, complications)

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** SMBG indications and frequency

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Perform SMBG in all people with diabetes on insulin
- Perform SMBG in patients with brittle diabetes
- Perform SMBG in patients prone to ketosis or recurrent hypoglycemia
- Perform SMBG in patients with hypoglycemia unawareness
- Perform SMBG whenever tight control is indicated (e.g., pregnancy, acute illness, complications)

**Prerequisites:**
- Individualize SMBG frequency based on clinical need
- Frequency of SMBG should be individualised based on clinical need, with greater frequency specifically recommended during pregnancy


### 2019 WHO diabetes classification

Classifies diabetes into distinct types: Type 1 Diabetes, Prediabetes and Type 2 Diabetes, Diabetes Induced by Systemic Anti-Cancer Therapy, Pancreatic Diabetes or Diabetes in the Context of Disease of the Exocrine Pancreas, Posttransplantation Diabetes Mellitus, Monogenic Diabetes Syndromes, and Gestational Diabetes Mellitus. 2019 WHO diabetes classification. 2019 WHO diabetes classification updates. Removed subtypes for T1DM and T2DM in classification. Introduced 'hybrid forms of diabetes' (e.g., slowly evolving immune-mediated diabetes of adults, ketosis-prone type 2 diabetes). Added 'unclassified diabetes' as a temporary label for unclear diagnoses near onset

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** 2019 WHO diabetes classification

**Core Recommendations:**
- Classifies diabetes into distinct types: Type 1 Diabetes, Prediabetes and Type 2 Diabetes, Diabetes Induced by Systemic Anti-Cancer Therapy, Pancreatic Diabetes or Diabetes in the Context of Disease of the Exocrine Pancreas, Posttransplantation Diabetes Mellitus, Monogenic Diabetes Syndromes, and Gestational Diabetes Mellitus.
- Emphasizes the use of diagnostic tests to differentiate between diabetes types.
- Encourages tailored management strategies based on the specific diabetes classification.
- Encourages the use of C-peptide testing and autoantibody assays to differentiate between Type 1 Diabetes and other forms of diabetes.
- Recommends genetic testing for suspected monogenic diabetes syndromes when clinically indicated.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Diagnostic tests must be performed to confirm diabetes classification.
- Patient history and clinical presentation must be considered for accurate classification.
- C-peptide levels must be measured in patients with unclear diabetes classification to distinguish between insulin-deficient and insulin-resistant states.
- Autoantibody testing (e.g., GAD65, IA-2, ZnT8) is required for confirming autoimmune Type 1 Diabetes in ambiguous cases.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Inclusion of Diabetes Induced by Systemic Anti-Cancer Therapy as a distinct classification.
- Recognition of Pancreatic Diabetes or Diabetes in the Context of Disease of the Exocrine Pancreas as a separate category.
- Formal classification of Posttransplantation Diabetes Mellitus.
- Acknowledgment of Monogenic Diabetes Syndromes as a distinct entity.
- Updated criteria for Gestational Diabetes Mellitus.
- Inclusion of 'Diabetes Induced by Systemic Anti-Cancer Therapy' as a distinct category, recognizing chemotherapy-induced hyperglycemia as a separate entity.
- Formal classification of 'Posttransplantation Diabetes Mellitus' as a distinct type, acknowledging its unique pathophysiology post-organ transplant.

### Source 2: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** 2019 WHO diabetes classification updates

**Core Management Principles:**
- Removed subtypes for T1DM and T2DM in classification
- Introduced 'hybrid forms of diabetes' (e.g., slowly evolving immune-mediated diabetes of adults, ketosis-prone type 2 diabetes)
- Added 'unclassified diabetes' as a temporary label for unclear diagnoses near onset
- Included 'Diabetes mellitus in Pregnancy' as a distinct entity, with Gestational Diabetes Mellitus under 'Hyperglycemia first detected during pregnancy'
- Renamed 'Latent autoimmune diabetes in adults' (LADA) to 'slowly evolving immune-mediated diabetes of adults'

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Diagnosis must consider multiple factors (e.g., age, BMI, autoimmunity, insulin sensitivity) rather than isolated features
- Autoantibody testing required for differentiation in ambiguous cases
- Genetic testing recommended for neonatal diabetes or suspected monogenic diabetes (e.g., MODY)
- C-peptide measurement and insulin dose assessment may aid in revising misdiagnosed T1DM/T2DM cases
- Differentiation from T2DM requires evaluation of insulin resistance features (e.g., acanthosis nigricans, central obesity)
- Consider 'unclassified diabetes' label for provisional diagnoses in the 20-40 age group due to high heterogeneity and misclassification risk
- Genetic testing for neonatal diabetes recommended for all cases diagnosed in infancy (e.g., KCNJ11, ABCC8, INS mutations)


### alcohol consumption guidelines

alcohol consumption in type 2 diabetes. Avoid alcohol consumption if possible. If alcohol is consumed, take it in moderation. Do not count alcohol as part of the meal plan. Monitor for hypoglycemia in the fasting state. Be aware that alcohol provides 7 kcal/g of empty calories. Avoid alcohol if you have advanced liver disease, neuropathy, or dyslipidaemia

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** alcohol consumption in type 2 diabetes

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Avoid alcohol consumption if possible
- If alcohol is consumed, take it in moderation
- Do not count alcohol as part of the meal plan
- Monitor for hypoglycemia in the fasting state
- Be aware that alcohol provides 7 kcal/g of empty calories
- Avoid alcohol if you have advanced liver disease, neuropathy, or dyslipidaemia
- Avoid alcohol if you are pregnant or planning pregnancy

**Prerequisites:**
- Assess liver function (ALT, AST, GGT) and lipid profile before advising alcohol consumption


### antipsychotic-induced metabolic monitoring

antipsychotic-induced metabolic monitoring in serious mental illness. Monitor body weight, glycemia, and lipids every 12–16 weeks in patients prescribed second-generation antipsychotics. Adjust treatment plans based on metabolic changes. Include nonmedical caretakers in treatment decision-making for patients with impaired judgment

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** antipsychotic-induced metabolic monitoring in serious mental illness

**Core Recommendations:**
- Monitor body weight, glycemia, and lipids every 12–16 weeks in patients prescribed second-generation antipsychotics
- Adjust treatment plans based on metabolic changes
- Include nonmedical caretakers in treatment decision-making for patients with impaired judgment
- Provide enhanced care and self-management support tailored to individual capacity and needs
- Coordinate diabetes care with mental health professionals for holistic management
- Monitor glycemia, body weight, and lipids every 12–16 weeks in adolescents prescribed second-generation antipsychotics
- Provide diabetes training for community support personnel and healthcare aides involved in the care of patients with serious mental illness

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Prescription of second-generation antipsychotic medications
- Diagnosis of serious mental illness (e.g., schizophrenia, thought disorders)
- Established diabetes or prediabetes status
- Assessment of patient's ability to engage in self-management behaviors
- Inclusion of a nonmedical caretaker for patients with impaired judgment
- Established diagnosis of type 2 diabetes or prediabetes in adolescents prescribed second-generation antipsychotics
- Assessment of psychological distress levels in patients with serious mental illness to tailor enhanced care and self-management support

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Routine monitoring of glycemia, body weight, and lipids every 12–16 weeks for antipsychotic-treated patients
- Incorporation of nonmedical caretakers in treatment planning for patients with serious mental illness
- Enhanced care models including remote monitoring and diabetes training for caregivers
- Collaborative care between diabetes and mental health teams
- Use of group support and educational interventions for behavioral change
- Systematic review evidence showing nonpharmacologic interventions reduce psychiatric symptoms, total cholesterol, and LDL cholesterol in patients with type 2 diabetes and serious mental illness


### diabetes risk prediction

Diabetes risk prediction + Weight management interventions. Risk stratification for type 2 diabetes incorporates BMI thresholds, with lower cutoffs for Asian populations (e.g., ≥23 kg/m²) due to higher diabetes risk at lower BMIs.

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes risk prediction + Weight management interventions

**Core Recommendations:**
- Risk stratification for type 2 diabetes incorporates BMI thresholds, with lower cutoffs for Asian populations (e.g., ≥23 kg/m²) due to higher diabetes risk at lower BMIs.
- Lifestyle interventions (diet, exercise, behavioral therapy) are prioritized for diabetes risk reduction in individuals with prediabetes or obesity.
- Pharmacotherapy (e.g., GLP-1 receptor agonists, SGLT2 inhibitors, orlistat, phentermine/topiramate) is considered for high-risk individuals when lifestyle changes are insufficient.
- Bariatric/metabolic surgery is recommended for patients with severe obesity (BMI ≥35 kg/m²) and type 2 diabetes or prediabetes, with evidence of durable diabetes remission.
- Weight loss maintenance strategies include long-term pharmacotherapy (e.g., semaglutide, tirzepatide) and structured follow-up to prevent relapse.

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- BMI ≥23 kg/m² for Asian populations or ≥25 kg/m² for other groups as screening thresholds for diabetes risk.
- Confirmed prediabetes (A1C 5.7–6.4%, fasting glucose 100–125 mg/dL, or 2-hour OGTT 140–199 mg/dL) or obesity (BMI ≥30 kg/m², or ≥27 kg/m² with comorbidities).
- Exclusion of contraindications for pharmacotherapy (e.g., history of pancreatitis for GLP-1 agonists, uncontrolled hypertension for phentermine).
- Multidisciplinary team involvement (e.g., dietitians, endocrinologists, surgeons) for comprehensive risk management.
- Baseline assessment of comorbidities (e.g., cardiovascular disease, sleep apnea) to tailor interventions.

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Fixed A1C targets (e.g., <7%) without individualized adjustments for life expectancy, hypoglycemia risk, or patient preferences.
- Routine use of orlistat as monotherapy for long-term weight management due to modest efficacy and gastrointestinal side effects.
- Unsupervised very-low-calorie diets (<800 kcal/day) without medical monitoring, given risks of micronutrient deficiencies and muscle loss.
- Glucocorticoid use for weight loss in diabetes, as it exacerbates hyperglycemia and insulin resistance.
- Relying solely on BMI for risk stratification without considering visceral adiposity or metabolic syndrome components.

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Tirzepatide (dual GIP/GLP-1 agonist) approved for chronic weight management in adults with obesity or overweight with comorbidities, including diabetes prevention.
- Semaglutide 2.4 mg (Wegovy) for long-term weight management in individuals with obesity or overweight, including those with prediabetes.
- Bariatric surgery outcomes data supporting durable diabetes remission and cardiovascular risk reduction in patients with BMI 30–34.9 kg/m² (if uncontrolled diabetes).
- Remote monitoring and digital health tools (e.g., app-based interventions) to enhance adherence to lifestyle modifications.


### travel management protocols

travel management protocols for T1DM. Increase blood glucose monitoring frequency during travel due to variable eating, activity, and sleep patterns. Adjust insulin doses based on travel-related changes in routine. Transport insulin and glucagon at 2-25°C in an insulated carrycase, never in check-in baggage

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** travel management protocols for T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Increase blood glucose monitoring frequency during travel due to variable eating, activity, and sleep patterns
- Adjust insulin doses based on travel-related changes in routine
- Transport insulin and glucagon at 2-25°C in an insulated carrycase, never in check-in baggage
- Carry all diabetes supplies including insulin, syringes, pen needles, BG strips, meter, sensor, disposables, hypoglycemia prevention items, and concentrated calorie sources
- Carry 50% extra supplies as spares for the duration of travel
- Carry concentrated calorie sources like dry fruits specifically to manage hypoglycemia during travel

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Parents must ensure accompanying teachers can handle routine care and emergencies during school trips
- Emergency contact details must be provided to accompanying teachers
- Discarded sharps must be stored safely for later disposal
- Ensure teachers are trained to handle routine diabetes care and emergencies during school trips


### travel preparation for insulin-dependent diabetes

Inform physician 4-6 weeks before travel for education on insulin storage, dose adjustment, hypoglycemia care, and sick day rules. Carry medications and supplies in hand baggage with reserve supplies for 2-4 weeks

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** travel preparation for insulin-dependent diabetes

**Core Management Principles:**
- Inform physician 4-6 weeks before travel for education on insulin storage, dose adjustment, hypoglycemia care, and sick day rules
- Carry medications and supplies in hand baggage with reserve supplies for 2-4 weeks
- Use two glucose meters and adequate supplies (strips, batteries, ketone strips, logbooks, glucose tablets, glucagon injection)
- Keep insulin between 4°C-30°C, avoid extreme temperatures, and carry insulin in hand baggage during air travel
- Check blood glucose every 4 hours during travel and adjust insulin doses based on time zone changes
- Inform airline in advance about diabetes status, medications, and materials to facilitate check-in, security checks, and hypoglycemia care
- Arrive at least 3 hours before departure to navigate check-in and security smoothly

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Adequate glycemic control before travel with appropriate insulin type/dose adjustments
- Legible prescription with all medicines and blood testing materials
- Medical identification bracelet with disease, insulin use, and allergies
- Valid travel insurance for international travel
- Carry snacks and supplies to treat hypoglycemia, and inform fellow passengers/crew
- Carry a prescription for common ailments (fever, emesis, diarrhea) and a first aid kit
- Ensure specific vaccines are obtained based on the region of travel


### medication shortage management

medication shortage management in diabetes care. Use FDA Drug Shortages Database to identify medications in shortage. Avoid use of non-FDA-approved compounded products due to safety and efficacy concerns. Switch to a different FDA-approved medication when available during shortages

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** medication shortage management in diabetes care

**Core Recommendations:**
- Use FDA Drug Shortages Database to identify medications in shortage
- Avoid use of non-FDA-approved compounded products due to safety and efficacy concerns
- Switch to a different FDA-approved medication when available during shortages
- Reassess patients to determine appropriateness of resuming original medication once supply is restored
- Report adverse events or safety concerns related to compounded or alternative medications to FDA MedWatch or other pharmacovigilance systems

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Medication must be listed on the FDA Drug Shortages Database to qualify for compounding or alternative strategies
- Patient-specific clinical appropriateness must guide switching decisions
- Pharmacy compounding must comply with regulatory standards for safety and quality
- Compounding pharmacies must be registered with the FDA and comply with current good manufacturing practices (cGMP) for outsourcing facilities during shortages

**Previous Recommendations:**
- Use of non-FDA-approved compounded incretin products (e.g., GLP-1 RAs, dual GIP/GLP-1 RAs)
- Incorporation of untested additional ingredients (e.g., vitamin B12, vitamin B6) into compounded products
- Use of salt forms of active ingredients not proven safe or effective for human use in compounded products
- Administration of compounded products in nonstandard concentrations, multidose vials, or prefilled syringes without proper education or labeling

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Switching to alternative FDA-approved medications when shortages occur
- Reassessment of patients after shortage resolution to determine resumption of original therapy
- Use of FDA-approved compounding facilities for copies of marketed products during shortages
- Adherence to FDA guidance for compounding during drug shortages to ensure safety and efficacy
- FDA determination of 'critical supply gap' as a prerequisite for allowing compounding of marketed product copies during shortages


### air travel diabetes management

Carry diabetes supplies (insulin, syringes, glucose tablets) in hand luggage with a doctor's note and prescription. Monitor blood glucose more frequently due to stress, activity changes, and time zone shifts. Adjust insulin timing and dosing based on meal schedules and time zone changes

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** air travel diabetes management

**Core Management Principles:**
- Carry diabetes supplies (insulin, syringes, glucose tablets) in hand luggage with a doctor's note and prescription
- Monitor blood glucose more frequently due to stress, activity changes, and time zone shifts
- Adjust insulin timing and dosing based on meal schedules and time zone changes
- Stay hydrated and avoid alcohol/caffeine to prevent dehydration and blood sugar fluctuations
- Wear a medical alert bracelet or carry identification indicating diabetes
- Avoid prolonged sitting without movement; perform calf raises, ankle circles, or short walks in the aisle every 1-2 hours to improve circulation and reduce risk of deep vein thrombosis (DVT) in T1DM patients
- Consume a balanced meal with complex carbohydrates before boarding to stabilize blood glucose during the initial phase of the flight

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Carry at least double the required insulin and supplies in case of loss or delays
- Have a written prescription and medical certificate for insulin and supplies
- Check airline policies regarding carrying diabetes medications and sharps
- Plan for time zone adjustments by consulting a healthcare provider for insulin schedule modifications
- Ensure travel insurance covers diabetes-related medical emergencies
- Ensure insulin vials/pens are stored in an insulated travel pouch or Frio cooling wallet to maintain temperature between 2°C and 30°C during transit
- Carry a letter from the healthcare provider on official letterhead specifying the medical necessity of carrying diabetes supplies, including sharps, to facilitate security checks


### driving safety and hypoglycemia prevention

driving safety and hypoglycemia prevention in T1DM. Ensure blood glucose (BG) is not low or trending low before driving. Carry non-perishable snacks and simple sugar sources in the vehicle for emergencies. Monitor BG trends using CGMS and avoid even mild hypoglycemia. Avoid driving during rapid BG changes that cause vision blurring

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** driving safety and hypoglycemia prevention in T1DM

**Core Management Principles:**
- Ensure blood glucose (BG) is not low or trending low before driving
- Carry non-perishable snacks and simple sugar sources in the vehicle for emergencies
- Monitor BG trends using CGMS and avoid even mild hypoglycemia
- Avoid driving during rapid BG changes that cause vision blurring
- Refrain from consuming alcohol or intoxicants before or while driving

**Clinical Prerequisites:**
- Minimum age of 16 years for a learner’s license, 18 years for a regular license, and 20 years for a commercial license
- Normal vision before driving
- Frequent BG testing initially
- Normal vision must be maintained for driving


### diabetes hospital readmission

Diabetes hospital readmission prevention. Monitor insulin adjustments for individuals admitted with A1C >9% or DKA. Follow a transitional care model to prevent readmissions. Prescribe glucagon for individuals hospitalized with severe hypoglycemia, impaired awareness, or high risk for hypoglycemia

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** Diabetes hospital readmission prevention

**Core Recommendations:**
- Monitor insulin adjustments for individuals admitted with A1C >9% or DKA
- Follow a transitional care model to prevent readmissions
- Prescribe glucagon for individuals hospitalized with severe hypoglycemia, impaired awareness, or high risk for hypoglycemia
- Initiate CGM prior to discharge to prevent acute diabetes-related complications and readmission
- Implement collaborative person-centered medical homes for people with diabetes and chronic kidney disease
- Implement scheduled home health visits for high-risk individuals post-discharge
- Ensure timely ambulatory follow-up care within 7-14 days post-discharge for all hospitalized diabetes patients

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Individuals admitted with A1C >9% or DKA require insulin adjustment monitoring
- High-risk individuals for hypoglycemia (e.g., kidney failure, intensive insulin management) require glucagon prescription
- People with diabetes and chronic kidney disease require collaborative person-centered medical homes
- CGM initiation prior to discharge requires follow-up facilitation
- Transitional care model requires proactive care transitions from inpatient to outpatient settings
- High-risk individuals for readmission require identification based on male sex, longer prior hospitalization duration, multiple previous hospitalizations, number/severity of comorbidities, or lower socioeconomic/educational status
- Timely ambulatory follow-up care requires proactive scheduling prior to discharge

**Previous Recommendations:**
- No standardized protocol to prevent readmissions (previously lacked structured approach)

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Initiate CGM prior to discharge to prevent acute diabetes-related complications and readmission
- Collaborative person-centered medical homes for people with diabetes and chronic kidney disease
- Prescribe glucagon for high-risk hypoglycemia patients hospitalized with severe hypoglycemia or impaired awareness
- Initiate CGM prior to discharge to facilitate structured follow-up and prevent acute complications (newly emphasized mechanism beyond general prevention)


### ICMR NCD leadership

ICMR NCD leadership profile. Leads development and dissemination of national non-communicable disease (NCD) guidelines. Oversees implementation of evidence-based clinical practice guidelines for type 2 diabetes management. Coordinates with Ministry of Health and Family Welfare for policy integration. Drives research prioritization in NCD prevention and control

### Source 1: ICMR Type 2 Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** ICMR NCD leadership profile

**Therapeutic Actions:**
- Leads development and dissemination of national non-communicable disease (NCD) guidelines
- Oversees implementation of evidence-based clinical practice guidelines for type 2 diabetes management
- Coordinates with Ministry of Health and Family Welfare for policy integration
- Drives research prioritization in NCD prevention and control
- Ensures alignment of ICMR guidelines with global health standards
- Publishes periodic updates to national NCD guidelines based on emerging evidence and epidemiological trends
- Facilitates multi-stakeholder consultations (e.g., clinicians, patient groups, NGOs) during guideline development

**Prerequisites:**
- Scientific expertise in NCD management
- Regulatory authority under Department of Health Research
- Collaboration with state health departments
- Evidence-based guideline development process
- National health policy integration framework
- Mandatory inclusion of cost-effectiveness analyses for all recommended interventions in guideline development
- Requirement for state-level adaptation of ICMR guidelines to local epidemiology and healthcare infrastructure


### insulin formulation availability in India

Human regular and NPH insulin are available in strengths of 40 units/ml and 100 units/ml in vials.. All insulin (regular, NPH, analogs) in pen cartridges have a strength of 100 units/ml.

### Source 1: ICMR_Guidelines_for_Management_of_Type_1_Diabetes.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** insulin formulation availability in India

**Core Management Principles:**
- Human regular and NPH insulin are available in strengths of 40 units/ml and 100 units/ml in vials.
- All insulin (regular, NPH, analogs) in pen cartridges have a strength of 100 units/ml.


### glucagon formulation options

Glucagon is available in multiple formulations: injection powder with diluent for reconstitution, nasal powder, and prefilled pen/syringe.. Dosage varies by formulation: 1 mg (injection), 3 mg (nasal), and 0.5 mg or 1 mg (prefilled pen/syringe).

### Source 1: American Diabetes Association Care Guidence.pdf

**Clinical Recommendation:** glucagon formulation options

**Core Recommendations:**
- Glucagon is available in multiple formulations: injection powder with diluent for reconstitution, nasal powder, and prefilled pen/syringe.
- Dosage varies by formulation: 1 mg (injection), 3 mg (nasal), and 0.5 mg or 1 mg (prefilled pen/syringe).
- Median costs differ by formulation: injection ($303 AWP, $242 NADAC), nasal ($357 AWP, $285 NADAC), and prefilled pen/syringe ($391 AWP, $303 NADAC).
- Median AWP and NADAC prices include minimum and maximum ranges for some formulations (e.g., injection: AWP $180–$337, NADAC $207–$242)
- NADAC for prefilled pen/syringe is $303–$304, indicating slight price variability within this formulation class

**Patient Eligibility Criteria:**
- Patients with severe hypoglycemia or recurrent hypoglycemic episodes may benefit from glucagon formulations.
- Formulation selection should consider patient-specific factors such as ease of administration and cost.
- Clinicians should consider formulation selection based on patient-specific factors such as age (e.g., pediatric dosing for 0.5 mg pen), caregiver capability, and likelihood of successful administration during emergencies

**Updated Recommendations:**
- Nasal glucagon powder (3 mg) is a newly introduced formulation option.
- Prefilled pen and prefilled syringe formulations (0.5 mg, 1 mg) are newly available options.
